Its memory versions will therefore render three of the union's four arms and never the fourth, and the `service_account_id` list filter that arrived beside the arm is honored and matches nothing, since no such actor is ever written (slice 2). The divergence is an absent arm rather than a wrong one — a client decoding with the SDK's union meets only variants it already handles — and it is registered here, at the bump that pinned the arm, rather than at the slice that writes the rows, because the pin is what makes the claim checkable. (`user_actor`'s id shape diverges for the separate reason the `principal`/`api_key` actor entry above gives: there is no `user_` id here either.) *Evidence: BetaManagedAgentsActorUnion, its doc naming all four members and the `AsAny` that dispatches on them (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betamemorystorememoryversion.go BetaManagedAgentsActorUnion and BetaManagedAgentsActorUnion.AsAny), BetaManagedAgentsServiceAccountActor, its doc comment and the `svac_...` id (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betamemorystorememoryversion.go BetaManagedAgentsServiceAccountActor), the `service_account_id` list filter (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betamemorystorememoryversion.go BetaMemoryStoreMemoryVersionListParams.ServiceAccountID); the same union with three variants (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.63.1 — betamemorystorememoryversion.go BetaManagedAgentsActorUnion); internal/identity (no service-account lane), internal/domain/id.go knownPrefixes (no `svac_`); docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 6 and "Out of scope"*
- **`/v1/dreams` is served (plan 41)** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#475).** This entry replaces one recording the opposite — "No `/v1/dreams` — the memory-consolidation surface is not served", written when plan 36 scope decision 3 left dreams out — corrected rather than deleted so the convergence stays auditable. All five paths now answer: `POST|GET /v1/dreams`, `GET /v1/dreams/{id}`, `POST …/archive` and `POST …/cancel`, carrying the fourteen-key `dream` resource (the guide's illustrative create response shows thirteen — it omits `output_behavior` — while the spec's `required` list and the API reference's 200 example both carry it, and they govern). `drm_` joins `knownPrefixes` as `PrefixDream`, so a dream id is a well-formed id everywhere. The list takes the parameters the SDK and CLI send — `limit` (default 20, max 100), `page`, `include_archived` (default false), `statuses[]`, and the **exclusive** `created_at[gt]`/`[lt]` bounds this surface takes where the agent, deployment and memory-store lists take `[gte]`/`[lte]` alone — and pages newest-first behind a `{data, next_page}` envelope. The lifecycle rules are the guide's: cancel moves a `pending` or `running` dream to `canceled`, is an idempotent no-op on an already-`canceled` one and a 400 on a `completed` or `failed` one; archive sets `archived_at` on a terminal dream, is a no-op when it is already set, and is a 400 on `pending` or `running`. **A dream now runs.** The runner claims a `pending` dream, renders its input sessions' transcripts, clones the input store — or, under `update_existing`, mounts it — and drives a pipeline session whose id the dream reports, then settles `completed`, `failed` or `canceled` and archives that session — so `pending` is a state a dream passes through rather than the one it stays in, and the lifecycle above is the one a client observes. **Both output behaviors are served.** `create_new` clones; `update_existing` consolidates the caller's own store in place, held against a second live in-place dream by a partial unique index, and a create that collides answers the spec's own `conflict_error` — see the two entries below. *Evidence: the five paths (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamService.New and BetaDreamService.Get and BetaDreamService.List and BetaDreamService.Archive and BetaDreamService.Cancel), the fourteen `api:"required"` fields (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDream), the status union (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamStatus), the list filter and its bracketed array format (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamListParams.Statuses and BetaDreamListParams.URLQuery); platform.claude.com managed-agents dreams guide (the cancel and archive rules, the list defaults, and "a `running` dream can briefly report an empty `outputs[]`"); docs/plan/41_dreams.md §2.2, §2.3, §2.5, §2.6, §4.1, §5.1 and §5.3; internal/api/dreams.go and internal/api StartDreamRunner (the sweep behind the routes), internal/domain/id.go (PrefixDream in knownPrefixes), internal/store/migrations/0034_dreams.sql* *Landed for #475.*
- **`model` at create — no catalogue, no provider route, and no id-length bound on any surface (plan 41 slice 1, decision 8)** — The spec bounds a model id at 1-256 characters and the dreams guide names the six models its research preview supports; this platform enforces neither, and not only on dreams: `parseModel` is shared with `POST /v1/agents` and with a session's `agent_with_overrides`, so all three take any non-empty string (or an object with a non-empty `id`) of any length, unchecked against a catalogue and unresolved against `model_providers`. The rejected alternative is what makes this deliberate rather than an omission: `provider.Registry` is built in `cmd/brain/main.go` alone and the controlplane has never imported `internal/provider`, so a create-time route check means handing the controlplane `MODEL_PROVIDERS_PATH` and a registry for one `Describe` call — a new cross-process coupling for a check `POST /v1/agents` has never made either, and one worth filing for all three surfaces at once if it is ever wanted. An unroutable model therefore surfaces where the model is first called: for a dream, at the pipeline session's first turn once the runner slice lands, which the runner settles as `failed{internal_error}`. The 256-character bound is registered once here for all three surfaces rather than enforced on the newest of them alone, which would make one shared parser answer differently depending on which route reached it. *Evidence: "1-256 characters", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamModelConfig.ID, and `minLength` 1 and `maxLength` 256 on `id`, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaDreamModelConfigParams.properties.id; platform.claude.com managed-agents dreams guide (the six preview models); internal/api/wire.go parseModel, internal/api/dreams.go, cmd/brain/main.go (the only `provider.Registry`); docs/plan/41_dreams.md §5.2 and decision 8*
- **`model.speed` — accepted, echoed, and never checked against a model catalogue (plan 41 slice 1, decision 11)** — The SDK's own doc for the field says `fast` "provides significantly faster output token generation at premium pricing. Not all models support `fast`; invalid combinations are rejected at create time." This platform rejects no combination: `speed` must be `standard` or `fast`, and is then stored and echoed on the resource unchanged, exactly as it is on an agent — so a request the reference refuses at create succeeds here, and any mismatch surfaces at the provider instead. Which combinations are invalid is the reference's model catalogue and not this platform's: design principle 4 makes a provider any endpoint speaking the protocol, gateways and self-hosted models included, whose speed modes nobody here can enumerate — so a create-time check could only be a hardcoded table that is wrong for the deployments this platform exists for. *Evidence: the sentence this diverges from, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamModelConfig.Speed; internal/api/wire.go parseModel (shared with the agent and session surfaces), internal/api/dreams.go; docs/plan/41_dreams.md decision 11* *Tracked: #78 (a `speed: fast` create against one model that supports it and one that does not would settle which combinations the reference refuses and with what status. Plan 41's dream checklist lapsed with the plan, and the dream half is unobservable while the preview is closed: the agent surface carries the same field behind no preview gate, but nothing shows the two share a model catalogue, so a probe there settles the agent half alone. This platform refuses none of them on any surface).*
- **The session and dream lists accept a bare `statuses` beside `statuses[]` (plan 41 slice 1)** — Both the SDK and the `ant` CLI marshal the filter with `ArrayQueryFormatBrackets`, and the spec names the parameter `statuses[]` with `explode: true`, so every real client sends `statuses[]=pending&statuses[]=running`. The public list reference writes it bare — "Filter by lifecycle status. Repeat the parameter to match any of multiple statuses" — so this platform takes both spellings and unions them, on `GET /v1/sessions` and on `GET /v1/dreams` alike. It is a lenient parse of ours, the `session_` id alias's sibling: a client that followed the prose gets the page it asked for rather than a silently unfiltered one, and no client following the SDK can tell the difference. Whether the reference accepts the bare spelling is unobserved, and nothing here turns on the answer — accepting a spelling it rejects costs a caller nothing. *Evidence: the doc sentence, the `statuses` query tag and the bracket format, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamListParams.Statuses and BetaDreamListParams.URLQuery; the CLI's same format, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betadream.go handleBetaDreamsList; internal/api/sessions.go (the `statuses[]`/`statuses` union) and internal/api/dreams.go; docs/plan/41_dreams.md §2.5* *Tracked: #78 (`statuses[]` bracketed against bare, on the reference's own list. The sessions half is takeable now; the dream half waits on an endpoint this account cannot reach, plan 41's checklist having lapsed with the plan, and nothing shows the two lists parse the filter in one place).*
- **`memory_store_org_limit_exceeded` is never produced (plan 41 slice 2)** — The dreams guide lists six dream `error.type` values and calls the list non-exhaustive; one of them reports that an organization has run out of memory stores. No dream here can carry it, because this platform enforces no organization store cap — the clone a `create_new` dream writes is counted against nothing. The other five are all produced: `timeout` by the runtime budget measured from the dream's creation, `internal_error` by a terminated pipeline session, a stage over its turn cap, an unroutable model and the other faults the plan's table names, and the three `input_*` types by the checks the start arm and every later tick make against the input store and the input sessions. This is not an inference a recording could settle: whatever the reference answers when an organization is full, there is nothing here to be full of. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents dreams guide (the non-exhaustive error-type list); `BetaDreamError` types `type` as a bare string, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadream.go BetaDreamError.Type, so the set is prose alone; docs/plan/41_dreams.md §5.2 and its out-of-scope list; internal/api StartDreamRunner*
- **`POST /v1/dreams` answers 500 `api_error` on a deployment that runs no dream runner (plan 41 slice 2)** — The reference has no such state: where dreams are served, they run. Here the runner is a controlplane sweep an operator can switch off — `DREAM_TICK_INTERVAL=0`, the chart's `controlplane.dreamTickInterval: "0"` — and a create accepted on such a deployment would sit `pending` forever with nothing to say why. So the create refuses, with `500`, `error.type: "api_error"` and the message `the dream runner is disabled on this deployment`, while list, retrieve, cancel and archive keep answering for the dreams a runner once ran. The status is 500 rather than a 4xx because the fault is the deployment's, not the request's, and `api_error` is the type the reference reserves for a fault on its own side. *Evidence: cmd/controlplane/main.go (the runner option installed only for a positive interval, and the log line that says so at boot); internal/api/dreams.go (the create's refusal); docs/plan/41_dreams.md §4.7; internal/api TestDreamRunnerDisabled*
- **A dream's pipeline session, and the transcript files it mounts, are read-only to the public API while the dream owns them (plan 41 slice 2)** — The guide exposes the session — "You can stream that session's events" — and says nothing about writing to it. Here every mutation a developer key could make on it is refused with a 400 `invalid_request_error` naming the dream that owns it: `POST …/events`, `POST /v1/sessions/{id}`, `DELETE`, the session and thread archives, and the three resource routes; `DELETE /v1/files/{id}` on one of that dream's transcript rows answers the same 400. Reads, the list and the stream answer as they do for any session, and the gate lifts the moment the dream closes, after which the session is an ordinary archived one. The reason is this platform's own and no recording can move it: the pipeline runs on a hidden agent whose toolset is `always_allow`, so a session anyone could steer would run whatever a caller liked under the platform's own agent — and, under `update_existing`, write it into the store the caller asked to consolidate. That a `create_new` session also has `bash`, and an `update_existing` one does not, changes the size of the hole and not whether it is one. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents dreams guide (the session is streamable, its mutability undocumented); docs/plan/41_dreams.md §4.3, §4.4 and §4.5; internal/api (the dream-ownership check each session-mutating handler makes, and the same check on the file delete)*
- **What runs inside a dream's session — the pipeline, its caps and its redaction — is this platform's design, not the reference's wire (plan 41 slice 2)** — The reference publishes a dream's inputs, its lifecycle and its outputs, and nothing whatever about what the session it exposes does. So everything between them is ours, argued in the plan rather than inferred from a source: the transcript renderer's filter (thinking blocks, images and the session's own lifecycle events dropped, a delegated child's report kept), its 24 KiB cap per transcript and 2 KiB per tool result, the best-effort shape redaction — `sk-`, `AKIA`, `Bearer` and assignment-shaped secrets — applied to every rendered byte before it leaves the controlplane, the `INDEX.md` beside the transcripts, the in-place run's narrower shape — no `bash`, so the write jail around memory is the file tools' own refusal rather than the prompt's, and a memory it retires becomes a tombstone because nothing left can delete — and the four staged instructions the runner posts as `user.message`s — orient, digest, merge, index and audit — each under its own turn cap (30 / 300 / 60 / 30 model turns, every thread's counted; the first, third and fourth measured against live runs, the second the fan-out's arithmetic) and each after the first opening by checking the previous stage's artefact, because the runner cannot read the sandbox and a container recreated after a crash starts with an empty scratch. A recording would show the reference's pipeline; it could not make this one wrong, which is what puts the whole area here rather than among the inferences. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents dreams guide and the API reference (the session exposed, its contents undocumented), with docs/plan/41_dreams.md §2.7 listing what is unpublished; docs/plan/41_dreams.md §3 and §3.4 (the two harnesses the design is informed by, read as design references only per CLAUDE.md); internal/transcript, internal/api StartDreamRunner*
- **A dream's digest threads are one per batch of eight transcripts, where the reference runs one per transcript (plan 41 slice 3)** — This is the single place a public artifact shows the reference's own pipeline shape, and this platform deliberately differs. The `anthropics/claude-quickstarts` knowledge-wiki notebook keeps two saved dream runs whose `session.thread_created` events number exactly one per input session — six for six inputs, four for four. Here the coordinator spawns one thread per **batch** of `dreamDigestBatch` (8) transcripts, so a hundred transcripts fan out to thirteen threads rather than a hundred. The reason is a cap of this platform's own: `maxLiveThreads` is 25 counting the primary, so one thread per transcript would refuse the twenty-fifth spawn on any dream past two dozen inputs, and the plan's bound is a hundred; batching also keeps the coordinator clear of the separate 25-turn settlement chain its spawn-and-wait round would otherwise walk. What a batch costs is context — eight capped transcripts is roughly 50k tokens in one thread — which is why the batch size is a package `var` rather than a constant. Nothing on the wire shows the difference: a dream's threads belong to the pipeline session, and the reference publishes no contract about them. *Evidence: anthropics/claude-quickstarts knowledge-wiki notebook at commit 40a7a14 (the saved runs' thread counts), the observation docs/plan/41_dreams.md §2.7 records; docs/plan/41_dreams.md §3.3 (the batch of eight, and the thirteen threads it implies at the hundred bound) and §9's wall-clock risk; internal/brain/delegate.go (maxLiveThreads, maxSettlementChain), internal/api (dreamDigestBatch and the stage-2 message); docs/HISTORY.md § "Dreams — the hundred-transcript bound" (the fan-out measured at the bound: thirteen threads for a hundred transcripts, not thirteen intended)*
- **`memory_store.created` / `.archived` / `.deleted` webhooks are never delivered (plan 36 slice 1)** — Creating, archiving or deleting a store fires nothing outbound, because this platform has no webhook subsystem at all — the absence [#261](https://github.com/OpenSDLC-Dev/managed-agent-platform/issues/261) was filed for when plan 21 shipped outcomes with polling and SSE as its only notification lanes. The store lifecycle is that gap's sibling, not a second one: whatever delivers `session.outcome_evaluation_ended` will deliver these three by the same machinery. Three is also the whole memory webhook surface — the reference states outright that individual memories and memory versions emit no webhook events — so slice 2's rows add nothing here. Until then the store lifecycle is observed the way every other resource's is, by `GET /v1/memory_stores` and `GET /v1/memory_stores/{id}`. *Evidence: the three `memory_store.*` event types, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betawebhook.go BetaWebhookEventDataUnion.Type; platform.claude.com managed-agents/webhooks ("Individual memories and memory versions emit no webhook events"); internal/api/memorystores.go (no emission path); docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md § Ground truth, "Webhooks"* *Tracked: #261 (the webhook subsystem itself; the outcome event in its title is the first emitter it needs, not the only one).*
- **Memory-version actors — `user_actor.user_id` is a `principal_` id, `api_actor.api_key_id` an `apikey_` id (plan 36 slice 1; the actor object renders from slice 2 on)** — The reference's `user_actor` attributes a write to "a human user through the Anthropic Console" and carries a `user_…` value; this platform has no `user_` id to give, because nothing here is bound to an end user (design principle 5) and its console is an IdP's, not an account store of its own. The human's stable identifier is the `principal_…` bookkeeping row the identity lane provisions, and a machine caller's is its `apikey_…` row id — the two lanes `principalFrom` already resolves. Slice 1 is where that choice is made, in the audit-only `created_by` column a store write records (never on the wire, the `sessions.created_by` rule); slice 2 is where the same value becomes visible, inside a memory version's `created_by`/`redacted_by` actor, the union arm following the lane that wrote it. So the arms, their `type` values and their field names are the reference's verbatim — only the id shapes inside them are ours, which is the shape difference the `service_account_actor` entry above already pointed at. *Evidence: the user actor's "a `user_...` value" and the API actor, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betamemorystorememoryversion.go BetaManagedAgentsUserActor and BetaManagedAgentsAPIActor; internal/api/auth.go principalFrom (the two lanes and which wins); internal/domain/id.go (`principal_`/`apikey_` held out of knownPrefixes, being off-wire ids); internal/store/migrations/0028_memory_stores.sql created_by; internal/api/memorystores_test.go TestMemoryStoreRecordsItsCreator; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 6*

- **Memory-version retention keeps the newest five — the count the reference withholds (plan 36 slice 2, scope decision 5; implemented for #476)** — **OURS in its count, the reference's in its shape.** The reference retains a memory's versions for 30 days and adds that "the recent versions are always kept regardless of age, so memories that change infrequently might retain history beyond 30 days" — publishing the window and withholding how many "recent versions" survive. This platform sweeps at startup and then hourly for versions past the 30-day window that are not among their memory's newest **five**, and five is a number nobody can read off the wire: retention is a server-side background policy, so no recording against the reference can reveal it, which is why plan 36 deferred it rather than guessing at the time. One row is exempt whatever its age: a live memory's head, because `memories.memory_version_id` is not a foreign key and a swept head would be a 404 on a memory that is fine — the keep window already covers it, so the guard is belt-and-braces on the one statement that could ever dangle it. Nothing else is. A deleted memory's lineage prunes by the same rule, which leaves it listable rather than immortal: five rows of it always survive, so the lineage a client lists is never empty, and its `deleted` marker is normally among them — no version is ever *inserted* for a memory id after that one, since ids are random and never reused and a re-create at the same path mints a new memory. That is insert order rather than timestamp order (`created_at` defaults to `now()`, which is transaction start, and the delete's transaction begins before it takes the store lock), so ordering the marker out of the window is possible in principle and would take five such interleavings inside one delete's begin-to-lock gap; the listable promise holds either way. The reference's own "versions … persist after the memory is deleted" is about the delete not cascading, not about exemption from retention, and its retention rule names no exception. A redacted version is no exception either, so `redacted_at`/`redacted_by` — the only record that an erasure happened and who performed it — age out with the row they annotate. A store's hard delete still removes everything at once, through the `ON DELETE CASCADE` migration 0029 gives `memory_versions`. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/memory (the 30-day retention and "however, the recent versions are always kept"); internal/api/memoryretention.go (the sweep, its window and count, the head guard, and the index the per-candidate lookup rests on); internal/api/memoryretention_internal_test.go (the newest-five rule, the recent-and-few exemptions, a head the keep window does not reach, a deleted memory's lineage still ending in its `deleted` version, and several memories of one store settled by one statement); cmd/controlplane/main.go (where it runs); internal/store/migrations/0029_memories.sql (no retention column; ON DELETE CASCADE from the store)* *Landed for #476; the count is a standing choice, revisable only by evidence a recording cannot produce.*
- **A `self_hosted` session accepts `file` and `github_repository` resources, a superset of the reference's memory-store-only rule (plan 36 slice 6, scope decision 2)** — The reference documents that "self-hosted environments accept only `memory_store` resources"; this platform accepts `file` (plan 08), `github_repository` (plan 25) and `memory_store` (this plan) on `self_hosted` alike — a standing superset at the create surface, whatever the worker then does with each. The BYOC worker materializes `file` and, since slice 6, `memory_store` the same way its cloud executor does; a `github_repository` it still accepts and mounts nothing for, the deliberate no-mount of #322 (unchanged here). Slice 6 completes the memory arm: it landed the worker's memory sync and lifted slice 3's interim 400 (`memory_store resources are not supported on self_hosted environments yet`, judged from the environment's `kind`). A `self_hosted` session's store is mounted from the per-item sessions token its work items carry (`secret`, decision 15), and the worker fails the item with the reference's own `ErrSessionMemoryNoToken` when a store is attached and no token arrives — so the two workers agree on what a memory-less session looks like, and the platform never accepts a store it cannot serve. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/self-hosted-sandboxes ("self-hosted environments accept only `memory_store` resources"); the item failed with `ErrSessionMemoryNoToken`, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem, and the store download and the run-loop sync, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.Download and SessionMemoryStores.SyncIfDue; internal/worker/memory.go (memoryRefs, materialize, sync, ErrSessionMemoryNoToken); internal/api/sessions.go createSession (no env-kind gate on resources); internal/api/sessionmemory_test.go TestSessionMemoryStoreAttachment; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 16 and scope decision 2* *Tracked: #52 (delivered; the superset is a standing choice, settled when plan 36 archived — the repository arm's own no-mount is #322).*
- **Memory stores sync at the run boundary, not through a live mount (plan 36 slice 4, decision 11)** — The reference describes a cloud session's store as a mount whose writes other sessions see "almost immediately"; here a `cloud` sandbox's store directory is reconciled with the store when a tool run ends, before a run's tools read a mount the sandbox already held, and by the reaper before it acts on a sandbox — so a change made by one session reaches another at that other's next run, and a change another session or the API made reaches a running session at its next run — one run of freshness, not seconds. Two consequences follow and are this platform's: a `bash` write into a `read_only` store's directory is not refused by the filesystem — only the file tools refuse it — and is never pushed, so it is overwritten by the store's next change; and a session's write is attributed to `session_actor` only once its run has ended, never mid-run. The run-end sync is the reference's own self-hosted model at a coarser cadence: its worker syncs from inside its tool-call loop, at most once every 15 seconds, and once more at teardown. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/memory (the cloud mount's freshness); the 15-second cadence, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.SyncIfDue and SessionMemoryStores.syncStore and DefaultMemorySyncInterval, and the call inside the tool loop, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem; internal/executor/memory.go readMemory / settleMemory / applyMemory and reaper.go; internal/toolset/toolset.go Runner.MemoryRoots; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decisions 11-12 and scope decision 7* *Tracked: #52 (delivered; settled with plan 36 — the live mount stays out of scope).*
- **No delete corroboration window, no per-sync delete cap, no deletions mode — the wipe guard alone (plan 36 slice 4, decision 11)** — The reference's self-hosted worker guards a local deletion three ways before deleting the memory it mirrors: a 30-second corroboration window, a per-sync cap on deletions, and a `deletions` mode a store can be mounted with; plus a wipe guard that re-downloads an emptied directory rather than deleting everything. This platform keeps only the wipe guard: a file the agent deleted, against a store that has not changed it since, is deleted from the store at the run's end with a compare-and-set on the head's digest, and appends a `deleted` version — a deliberate, attributed, reversible-by-history act rather than a delayed one. The three other guards exist in the reference for a filesystem an operator's own tools may be sweeping between syncs; a platform-managed sandbox's directory is written by the agent alone. *Evidence: corroboratedDelete, the deletion cap and the mode, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.corroboratedDelete and deletePass.takeSlot and MemoryDeleteMode; internal/memsync/plan.go Plan (the wipe guard and the DeleteRemote arm); internal/executor/memory_test.go TestMemorySyncPushesLocalChanges / TestMemorySyncWipeGuard; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 11* *Tracked: #52 (delivered).*
- **A directory with files and a missing or altered marker is pulled into, where the reference leaves it alone (plan 36 slice 4, decision 12)** — Every mounted store directory carries the reference worker's marker, `.anthropic-memory-store` with the bytes `version 1\n<memory_store_id>`, and both halves stop trusting a directory whose marker is missing or altered. What each then does differs: the reference worker, finding files under a marker it cannot vouch for, leaves the directory as found and does not sync it at all; this platform keeps syncing it pull-only — the store's changes and deletions still land, nothing is pushed from it — so an agent that damaged the marker still reads a current view, and the file tools are not told (they refuse a `read_only` or archived store's files, and any path under `/mnt/memory` that is inside no store — never a marker's state). A wiped mount is the case both agree on: an empty directory against a baseline of more than one file is re-downloaded with a fresh marker (the reference's `stampAndPull`, our rebuild) and pushes again afterwards. *Evidence: scanMarker, markerSHA, the marker-mismatch return at the sync's start and stampAndPull, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.scanMarker and markerSHA and SessionMemoryStores.syncStore and SessionMemoryStores.stampAndPull; internal/memsync/tree.go MarkerBytes; internal/executor/memory.go materializeStore / settleStore / applyMemory; internal/executor/memory_test.go TestMemoryStoreMissingOrUntrusted / TestMemorySyncWipeGuard* *Tracked: #52 (delivered).*
- **The BYOC worker reads the store's archived and occupancy 400s specially, where the reference worker remembers every 400 as a per-file refusal (plan 36 slice 6, decision 16)** — The reference's self-hosted worker treats every `400`/`413` a push draws the one way: it records the file's SHA and leaves the file un-synced until its bytes change (`refusedSHAs[rel] = localSHA`, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.syncPath and SessionMemoryStores.upload). This worker keeps that rule for an unrecognized 400 or a 413 and reads two of the store's own 400s out of it. An archived store's 400 — `cannot modify archived resource: memory store …`, the reference's words since #540, or the older `memory store … is archived` a control plane before it answers — turns the rest of that sync pull-only — the same end state the cloud executor reaches from the store row up front (decision 12), reached here lazily because the worker's sessions token cannot read the row and learns the archival only from the first write the store refuses; nothing is remembered, so the writes resume once the store is unarchived. A worker built before #540 knows only the older wording, so against a control plane at or past #540 it reads the archived 400 as an unrecognized one: every changed file of that store is pushed, refused and remembered, and stays un-synced after an unarchive until its bytes change — the reference worker's own outcome, and why the changelog says to upgrade self-hosted workers before or with the control plane. A local deletion is the exception: an archived store admits a delete, as the reference's does (#685), so the worker's deletions — settled before its pushes — land on it, where the cloud executor's pull-only sync keeps them local. An occupancy-cap 400 (`memory store … holds 2000 memories`) is refused for that run but not remembered, so the create is retried on a later sync once deletions make room rather than pinned until its content changes. Both are safe-degrade: any 400 the worker does not recognize falls through to the reference's remember-until-the-bytes-change rule. *Evidence: `refusedSHAs[rel] = localSHA` for every 400/413, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.syncPath and SessionMemoryStores.upload; internal/api/memories.go lockMemoryStoreForWrite and createMemory (the `cannot modify archived resource` and `holds %d memories` 400 messages the worker matches on, beside the pre-#540 `is archived`); internal/worker/memory.go refusalKind / settleStore (the archived and occupancy carve-outs and the pushRefused fallthrough); internal/worker/memory_test.go TestMemoryStoreRefusalsOverTheWire / TestMemoryDeleteReachesAnArchivedStore / TestMemoryPushRefusedIsRememberedUntilBytesChange; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 16* *Tracked: #52 (delivered).*
- **POST /v1/deployments and the update route reject `budget` (plan 37 slice 1)** — The reference stamps a deployment's spend ceiling onto each session it starts; this platform has no session budgets, so `budget` is absent from both allow-lists and a request carrying it is a 400 unknown field, exactly as POST /v1/sessions answers today. This one breaks a real client path rather than an obscure field: the `ant` CLI exposes --budget, --budget.max-list-cost and --budget.type on both beta:deployments create and beta:deployments update, so those invocations 400 against this platform. Storing and echoing an unenforced cap would be worse, because an operator would then believe a ceiling exists. The Deployment object never emits the key either, which is the reference's own shape for an unset budget — the one property outside its 16-entry required list, "absent when no budget is set". *Evidence: the spec's `BetaManagedAgentsCreateDeploymentParams.budget` and `BetaManagedAgentsUpdateDeploymentParams.budget`, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCreateDeploymentParams.properties.budget and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsUpdateDeploymentParams.properties.budget, and `BetaManagedAgentsDeployment` (17 properties, 16 required, `budget` the exception), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeployment; the create and update budget flags, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betadeployment.go betaDeploymentsCreate and betaDeploymentsUpdate; internal/api/deployments.go deploymentCreateKeys, internal/api/deployments_test.go TestDeploymentRejectsBudget.* *Tracked: #432 (the deployment half — a create carrying budget, and whether the Deployment object omits the key or renders it null when unset).*
- **Deployment and deployment-run webhooks — the nine `deployment.*` / `deployment_run.*` events are never delivered (plan 37 slice 1)** — The reference emits deployment.created / .updated / .paused / .unpaused / .archived / .deleted and deployment_run.started / .succeeded / .failed, each carrying only {type, id, organization_id, workspace_id}; this platform has no webhook subsystem at all, so none of the nine is delivered. Whatever delivers session.outcome_evaluation_ended will deliver these nine by the same machinery. *Evidence: the spec's nine deployment webhook data schemas — BetaWebhookDeploymentCreatedEventData, Updated, Paused, Unpaused, Archived and Deleted, plus BetaWebhookDeploymentRunStartedEventData, Succeeded and Failed — each a four-field payload of type, id, organization_id and workspace_id with all four required, and all nine carried in BetaWebhookEventData's discriminator mapping, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaWebhookEventData.discriminator.mapping; the same nine data types, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betawebhook.go BetaWebhookDeploymentCreatedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentUpdatedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentPausedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentUnpausedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentArchivedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentDeletedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentRunStartedEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentRunSucceededEventData and BetaWebhookDeploymentRunFailedEventData; a full-tree grep of this repo finds no webhook code.* *Tracked: #261 (the webhook subsystem itself; the deployment family is the second event group waiting on it, not the first).*
- **The 1,000-scheduled-deployments-per-organization cap is not enforced (plan 37 slice 1)** — The reference caps a customer at 1,000 scheduled deployments and directs the rest to support. A self-hosted operator owns their own capacity, and a limit we cannot tune for them is a limit that only ever fails a legitimate request. The cap appears in neither the spec nor the SDK. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/scheduled-deployments (fetched 2026-08-27 — "A maximum of 1,000 scheduled deployments is supported per organization."); the spec, which sets no maximum on any deployment count: none on the create params, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCreateDeploymentParams, and none on the list operation, whose one bound is a page size, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeployment.go BetaDeploymentService.List and BetaDeploymentListParams.Limit.*
- **A deployment configuring resources against a self_hosted environment is accepted and fires (plan 37 slice 1)** — The reference pauses such a deployment with self_hosted_resources_unsupported_error, because a self-hosted environment "cannot mount them". This platform already accepts a superset at session level — file and github_repository alongside memory_store on self_hosted — so the precondition the reference pauses on is not one this platform holds, and the type is produced by nothing. The consequence is real and worth stating: a github_repository on a self_hosted deployment materializes nothing and the agent is not told, so a schedule can produce a session whose repository is absent, every night. That is the standing no-mount of #322 under a timer, not a new gap. *Evidence: the spec's BetaManagedAgentsSelfHostedResourcesUnsupportedRunError and BetaManagedAgentsSelfHostedResourcesUnsupportedDeploymentPausedReasonError (both described as "The deployment configures resources, but its environment is self-hosted and cannot mount them"), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsSelfHostedResourcesUnsupportedRunError and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsSelfHostedResourcesUnsupportedDeploymentPausedReasonError, each carried in its union — BetaManagedAgentsRunError and BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsRunError.discriminator.mapping.self_hosted_resources_unsupported_error and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError.discriminator.mapping.self_hosted_resources_unsupported_error; docs/DIVERGENCES.md (the accepted superset, and the silent no-mount).* *Tracked: #322 (the deployment half — the same no-mount under a timer, so a schedule produces a session whose repository is absent every night rather than once).*
- **Archiving an agent is refused while a live deployment pins it, where the reference cascades (plan 37 slice 1, decision 7)** — A docs-page sentence says an archived agent archives its deployments in the same operation. This platform refuses instead: `POST /v1/agents/{agent_id}/archive` answers 400 naming the deployments whenever one with `archived_at IS NULL` pins the agent, and the operator archives those first. What we diverge from rests on that one sentence and nothing else — the archive operation carries `summary: "Archive Agent"` and no description at all, and a search of the whole spec for a cascade from an agent returns nothing — while where the reference *does* cascade it says so, the tunnels archive being the only operation whose description spells one out, at its current path and again at the deprecated Admin API path it supersedes. The spec's deployment types point the other way outright: `agent_archived_error` sits in both the run-error and the paused-reason union, described as "The deployment's agent was archived", which is a state a universal cascade would never leave anything in. So what is confirmed here is our behavior, not theirs. The trade is deliberate: a cascade destroys every schedule pinning an agent in one unrecoverable step, since this platform ships neither unarchive nor `DELETE /v1/deployments`, while a refusal is recoverable by retrying in the right order. Two carve-outs keep the refusal from building a dead end of its own. An *archived* deployment never blocks, being terminal and unable to fire — blocking on one would leave an agent no operator could ever archive. And an agent that is already archived skips the check and re-archives idempotently, because the refusal guards the transition rather than the state — refusing there would be a 400 no operator could clear by archiving the agent, since it already is. What the skip tolerates is a live deployment pinning an archived agent, and no route can produce that pairing: create and update both refuse an archived agent, so a *new* pin is impossible, and the archive of a pinned agent is what this entry refuses. It survives only as a row written before this rule, or by direct SQL. That is also what makes `agent_archived_error` unreachable for a deployment's own agent by construction rather than by convention, which slice 4's entry on that error type rests on. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/scheduled-deployments (fetched 2026-08-27 — "If a deployment's agent has been archived, the deployment is automatically archived in the same operation."); the spec's BetaArchiveAgent operation (summary only, no description), its BetaManagedAgentsAgentArchivedRunError and BetaManagedAgentsAgentArchivedDeploymentPausedReasonError (both "The deployment's agent was archived"), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsAgentArchivedRunError and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsAgentArchivedDeploymentPausedReasonError, and its tunnels archive operation (the only one whose description spells a cascade out, repeated at the deprecated `/v1/organizations/tunnels/{tunnel_id}/archive` it supersedes), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betatunnel.go BetaTunnelService.Archive, and the same bare "Archive Agent", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaAgentService.Archive; internal/api/agents.go archiveAgent and refuseDeploymentsPinningAgent (the FOR UPDATE the check runs under, against the FOR SHARE deployment create and update take on the agent they pin), internal/api/agents_test.go TestAgentArchiveRefusedWhileADeploymentPinsIt.* *Tracked: #78 (archive an agent that a deployment pins, and read both the agent and the deployment).*
- **Scheduled fires apply no jitter (plan 37 slice 4, decision 6)** — The reference offsets each fire by a small per-schedule jitter to spread a multi-tenant fleet's load. A self-hosted single-organization deployment has nothing to spread, so we fire at the first tick that sees an occurrence due, which lands 0-30 seconds after it and satisfies the reference's own published claim that a run starts "at or shortly after its listed time". scheduled_at still records the exact pre-jitter cron match, so the wire value is identical either way. *Evidence: "Each fire is offset by a small per-schedule jitter, so a run will actually start at or shortly after its listed time", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCronSchedule.properties.upcoming_runs_at, scheduled_at is the pre-jitter instant, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsScheduleTriggerContext.properties.scheduled_at; platform.claude.com managed-agents/scheduled-deployments (fetched 2026-08-27 — the 15% / 5-second / 9-minute quantification).*
- **session_rate_limited_error is never recorded (plan 37 slice 4)** — The reference throttles session creation and records the rejection as a failed run that does not pause the schedule. This platform imposes no session-creation rate limit at all, so the type's only cause never arises and nothing produces it; it is admitted by the store so a future emitter needs no migration. An earlier draft reused the type to mark a scheduled fire skipped for overlap, which decision 10 declined — this platform installs no overlap brake, so there is no second cause either. *Evidence: "Session creation was rejected due to rate limiting. The schedule keeps firing; subsequent runs may succeed.", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsSessionRateLimitedRunError, the type is one of the two the pausing union omits, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsRunError.discriminator.mapping.session_rate_limited_error and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError.discriminator.mapping; no rate limiter exists in internal/api.* *Tracked: #46 (per-org rate limiting on the management API; when it lands, this type gains its reference cause).*
- **mcp_egress_blocked_error at fire time (plan 37 slice 4)** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#571).** The reference fails a run when an MCP server host the agent uses is blocked by the environment's network policy, and so does this platform now: session creation refuses such an agent ("MCP-client egress and the tool catalog", below), and a deployment fire goes through the same create, so its run settles on `mcp_egress_blocked_error` and a scheduled fire pauses the deployment on it. This entry replaces one recording that the type was never produced — the check then ran only at dial time, inside the executor — corrected rather than deleted so the convergence stays auditable. *Evidence: "An MCP server host used by the deployment's agent is blocked by the environment's network policy", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsMcpEgressBlockedRunError; internal/api/mcpadmission.go (admitMCPServers); internal/api/mcpadmission_test.go (TestDeploymentRunRecordsABlockedMCPHost).*
- **workspace_archived_error and organization_disabled_error are never recorded (plan 37 slice 4)** — Both describe a scope this platform reserves but does not enforce: org_id / workspace_id / project_id carry single-tenant defaults on every top-level table, deployments included, and no query filters on them. The store admits both values so a future emitter needs no migration. *Evidence: the two run-error schemas, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsWorkspaceArchivedRunError and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsOrganizationDisabledRunError, both in the pausing fourteen, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError.discriminator.mapping.workspace_archived_error and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError.discriminator.mapping.organization_disabled_error; internal/store/store.go:34-42 (reserved, not enforced).* *Tracked: #56 (the emitter — plan 42 slice 6 pauses a deployment and fails a run with workspace_archived_error, and re-argues organization_disabled_error on one organization per deployment).*
- **skill_not_found_error is never recorded (plan 37 slice 4)** — Unlike the multi-tenant variants this one has a live surface here: skills are implemented, so an agent's skill reference can genuinely be missing. The fire validates exactly what POST /v1/sessions validates and no more, and session create does not resolve an agent's skill references — so a missing skill fails inside the running session's materialization rather than as a failed deployment run, and the type is produced by nothing. *Evidence: BetaManagedAgentsSkillNotFoundRunError, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsSkillNotFoundRunError, in the pausing fourteen, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentPausedReasonError.discriminator.mapping.skill_not_found_error; internal/api/sessions.go createSession (no skill resolution); internal/executor/telemetry.go (skills materialization outcomes, which is where a missing skill surfaces).*
- **environment_not_found_error — recorded, and unreachable here while an environment's deployments hold it (plan 37 slice 4)** — The reference fails a run when the deployment's environment row is gone, and a recording shows how it gets there: it deleted an environment a deployment still pinned (200, 2026-09-02 batch2 idx 462 `env.delete.with-deployment`), and the deployment's next manual run settled `environment_not_found_error`, "session creation rejected: environment `env_…` not found" (idx 465 `deployment.run.after-env-archived`, named for an archive that answered 404 because the delete had already landed). Here the row cannot go: deployments.environment_id carries a foreign key, and DELETE /v1/environments refuses while any deployment references it — with a message naming them, which is the other half of this plan's change to that handler. That refusal is where this platform and the recording part: the reference took the delete and failed the run afterwards, this platform refuses the delete, so the recorded type and sentence are unreachable while `deployments_environment_id_fkey` holds, and #540 adds no code for them. If the row were gone regardless, a fire takes the generic unclassified path (§5.2's last row): its transaction rolls back, leaving no run row and no pause; a scheduled occurrence is retried on later ticks while it is still the most recent due one, and a manual run answers the 404 (`environment env_… not found`). The reference instead settles the run with `environment_not_found_error`, an arm of its paused-reason union, so a scheduled fire there pauses the deployment. An archived environment is a different type and is reachable. The store admits the value so a future emitter needs no migration. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (batch2 idx 462 and 465); the spec's BetaManagedAgentsEnvironmentNotFoundDeploymentPausedReasonError, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsEnvironmentNotFoundDeploymentPausedReasonError; internal/api/environments.go environmentStillReferenced (the delete refusal this plan makes name deployments); migration 0031_deployments.sql (the FK); internal/api/sessions.go errSessionEnvironmentNotFound (unclassified).* *Landed for #540.*
- **unknown_error is never recorded, and an unclassified fire failure records nothing at all (plan 37 slice 4)** — Both published unions carry unknown_error as an explicit fallback variant — "An unknown or unexpected error caused the run to fail" on the run side, "An unrecognized error auto-paused the deployment" on the pause side — so the reference's answer to an unclassified fire failure is a recorded run that pauses the schedule. We roll the whole transaction back instead: the claim is released and a later tick retries the same occurrence — but only while it is still the most recent due one, which on a */5 schedule is the five minutes until the next occurrence falls due, not the hour the catch-up window suggests. After that the occurrence is counted as collapsed rather than retried, so a persistent fault abandons every occurrence it touches. The trade is deliberate. unknown_error is one of the fourteen pausing types, so emitting it would auto-pause a healthy deployment on a transient database blip, and this platform ships no auto-resume — an operator would have to unpause by hand. A rollback recovers unattended — the deployment, at least, if not always the occurrence. The cost is that the failure leaves no database trace whatever: no run row, last_run_at unmoved, no paused_reason, nothing in either list surface. The only record is telemetry: deployment.fires increments with outcome="abandoned" and the deployment.fire span is emitted either way. The store admits the value under the same CHECK as the other thirteen, so a future emitter needs no migration. *Evidence: both fallback variants, both documented as fallbacks, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeploymentrun.go BetaManagedAgentsUnknownRunError and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeployment.go BetaManagedAgentsUnknownDeploymentPausedReasonError; no auto-resume exists — of the eight routes on /v1/deployments only POST /unpause clears the pause columns, and there is no timed or automatic counterpart.* *Tracked: #78 (make a fire fail unclassifiably on the reference, then read the run and the deployment).*
- **A fired session is titled with the deployment's name (plan 37 slice 4)** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#678).** The entry this replaces left a fired session's title unset, because no source said otherwise and because it was bundled with the metadata call now recorded as the `A fired session's metadata is empty` note. Two recording waves settled the title the other way: each fired a deployment, read the session back, and found its `title` equal to the deployment's `name` — `"rec78 nightly"` on 2026-09-02, `"rec84 two-adjacent"` on 2026-09-05 — beside a `metadata` of `{}`. The ownership-hook argument that keeps the metadata empty never reached the title, which is a nullable, human-readable label rather than a seam an application owns. Both fire paths, `POST /v1/deployments/{id}/run` and the scheduler, now pass the deployment's name as the session's title. What survives is one reading of ours: the name is read at the fire, so a renamed deployment's next session takes the new name, where both waves fired deployments that had never been renamed. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`deployment.run.session.get`, `deployment.create.cron`) and the 2026-09-05 recording (`rec84.session.get.before-delete`, `rec84.adjacency.two-adjacent`); session title is nullable on create, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCreateSessionParams.properties.title; internal/api/deploymentruns.go deploymentSessionIn; internal/api/deploymentruns_test.go TestDeploymentRunCreatesASessionAndRendersTheRun and internal/api/deploymentscheduler_test.go TestSchedulerFiresTheMostRecentDueOccurrence.* *Tracked: #78 (rename a deployment, fire it, and read the session's title); landed for #678.*
- **A success run outlives its session with its session_id intact (plan 37 slice 5)** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#663).** The run object publishes "Exactly one of session_id or error is non-null", and the runs list publishes has_error=false as "runs with non-null session_id". Sessions are deletable on both sides, and the reference never nulls the link: a recorded run read byte-identically before and after its session was deleted, still naming a session that now answered 404, with `error` null — so the invariant holds for the run's whole life. The same wave answered the filter: `has_error=true`, `false` and a non-boolean value answered 200, 200 and 400. The entry this replaces recorded the opposite, because 0031 declared deployment_runs.session_id ON DELETE SET NULL and a deleted session left both members null; migration 0042 drops that foreign key, so the column is plain text that may name a session which no longer exists. The has_error=false arm still filters on succeeded_at IS NOT NULL — the durable marker migration 0032 added (#520), which the schedule.last_run_at entry rests on too — and on every run settled since 0042 that agrees with the published session_id predicate. **What survives is history**: a run whose session was deleted before 0042 lost the id, which is recorded nowhere, so it still renders session_id null beside a null error, and succeeded_at still counts it a success. *Evidence: the 2026-09-05 recording (`rec84.run.get.before-session-delete`, `rec84.session.delete.fired`, `rec84.run.get.after-session-delete`, `rec84.session.get.after-delete`, and `rec84.runs.list.has_error.true` / `.false` / `.bogus`); the has_error sentence, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeploymentrun.go BetaDeploymentRunListParams.HasError, and "no session lifecycle tracking", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsDeploymentRun; internal/store/migrations/0042_deployment_runs_session_link.sql; internal/api/deploymentruns.go scanRun and listDeploymentRuns; internal/api/deploymentruns_test.go TestDeletingTheSessionDoesNotUnsettleTheRun; internal/store/store_test.go TestDeploymentRunKeepsItsSessionIDPastTheSession.* *Landed for #663; the success marker landed for #520.*
- **A deployment echoes a resource's `mount_path` as given, and mounts the path it resolved at create — files and repositories (confirmed 2026-09-05 and 2026-09-12, #78; aligned for #849)** — **Converged, except on deployments stored before #849.** The resource config "echoes the input minus write-only credentials", and the recordings show what that means for a mount path: a file's `/uploads/rec141-input.txt` came back as sent from create (2026-09-12-console-141 api-fixtures idx 15) and from every later answer for that deployment — the list (ui-network idx 232 and 305), pause (250), unpause (254), an update that sent no resources (255), archive (api-fixtures idx 23) and, archived, a later list (2026-09-12-console-followups ui-network idx 502) — while the session its manual run created mounted `/mnt/session/uploads/rec141-input.txt` (ui-network idx 266, the session read at idx 268); a repository's `/workspace/sdk` came back as sent, and an omitted one as no key at all (2026-09-05 batch3 idx 20 and 19). This platform echoed the path it resolved at create or update. It now keeps two things for each file and repository: the resolved path in the stored `mount_path`, which the fire mounts as before — so neither a fire nor a binary from before #849, reading the same column during a rolling update or after a rollback, ever resolves a spelling — and the caller's spelling in a storage-only `given_mount_path`, which the echo renders in `mount_path`'s place, with no key when the caller sent none. Create and update still refuse up front, in session create's words, any path that cannot resolve — a file's that resolves to no path under the uploads directory, past 1024 bytes or to unstorable text, a repository's relative, unclean or reserved, or its derived default too long. **A mixed-version window**: during a rolling update from a release before #848, a deployment read from a new pod echoes `/uploads/x`, and saved back through an old pod is resolved by that pod to `/mnt/session/uploads/uploads/x`, which then stands as a pre-#849 element; finishing the rollout before editing deployments, or re-sending the spelling afterwards, avoids it. **What survives is history**: an element stored before #849 has no `given_mount_path`, so it echoes its resolved path — rooted under `/mnt/session/uploads`, doubled for an `/uploads/<name>` stored before #848, a repository's default derived — what it was sent being recorded nowhere, and its fire mounts that path as before. Sending such a deployment's echoed resources back keeps the same paths: a file's as echoed, a repository's only once its write-only `authorization_token` is restored, since the echo never carries it and an update refuses a repository without one. Four readings are ours. (1) **Where two resources meet is judged at create and update, on the resolved paths**, with session create's rules: `/uploads/x` beside `/x` — two spellings of one mount — is refused up front, as are one spelling twice, two repositories at one default and a resource above a repository's mount. No recording shows a deployment holding two resources that meet, and a reference that keeps spellings until the fire might judge them only there; refusing up front keeps a deployment that could never fire from being stored. The refusal is in the deployment routes' own words, naming both spellings sent — or the default an omitted one took — and what they resolve to (`mount_path "/uploads/x" and mount_path "/x" both resolve to "/mnt/session/uploads/x"`); session create keeps its own, an overlapping repository's being the reference's recorded sentence (2026-09-03 batch1 `session.create.repo-same-repo-twice` and `repo-nested-mounts`), which no deployment recording holds (#540). (2) A file's omitted `mount_path`, and either kind's null or empty one, echoes no key, as a repository's omitted one was recorded to. (3) The spelling is echoed as decoded and uncleaned, so `/uploads//x/` echoes as sent; re-encoding can change how a character is escaped (a lone surrogate comes back as U+FFFD), never the path. (4) **The spelling is bounded too**: resolution bounds only its result, so `/` + `a/../` repeated thousands of times + `x` resolves to `/mnt/session/uploads/x`, and a deployment, which stores and echoes the spelling, refuses one over 1024 bytes ("mount_path must be at most 1024 bytes as sent"); session create, which stores only the resolved path, takes it. *Evidence: "Echoes the input minus write-only credentials", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsSessionResourceConfig; `mount_path` nullable and not required on both configs, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsFileResourceConfig.properties.mount_path and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsGitHubRepositoryResourceConfig.properties.mount_path; the recordings 2026-09-12-console-141 (api-fixtures idx 15 and 23; ui-network idx 232, 250, 254, 255, 266, 268 and 305), 2026-09-12-console-followups ui-network idx 502, 2026-09-05 batch3 idx 19 and 20 and 2026-09-03 batch1 idx 100-102; internal/api/deploymentresources.go deploymentResource and config, internal/api/sessionresources.go parseFileResource and mountWords, pinned by TestDeploymentEchoesMountPathsAsGiven, TestDeploymentStoresTheResolvedPathBesideTheSpelling, TestDeploymentBoundsTheStoredSpelling, TestDeploymentValidatesMountPathsUpFront and TestDeploymentRefusesResolvedMountOverlapsUpFront (internal/api/deployments_test.go), TestDeploymentRowsStoredResolvedFireWhereTheyDid (internal/api/deploymentruns_test.go) and TestDeploymentFireResolvesTheUploadsAlias (internal/api/sessionresources_test.go).* *Tracked: #78 (a deployment created with two file resources whose spellings resolve alike, `/uploads/x` and `/x` — refused, or stored and then fired; one whose file omits `mount_path`, read back; and a spelling over 1024 bytes that resolves short); landed for #849.*

- **POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — `expected_last_heartbeat` is required here, optional on the reference (confirmed 2026-09-02, #78)** — A recording answered the question the inference this replaces asked, and the answer moves the entry rather than closing it: the reference accepts a heartbeat that omits the field, and omitting it is not "beat without checking" but a distinct, weaker operation — **claim if unclaimed, else extend** — which stamps `acknowledged_at` on the way. This platform still answers an absent field with 400, and that is now a deliberate divergence rather than an unconfirmed guess. The argument is the one the entry always made, and the recorded meaning strengthens it: requiring the precondition makes every heartbeat a compare-and-swap against the beat the caller last saw, so two workers cannot both believe they hold one item, where the reference's omitted form is a claim path that bypasses exactly that check. Requiring it rejects no real worker — the automated worker, the only real consumer, always sends it. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (the heartbeat probes, including the omitted-field form and the `acknowledged_at` it stamped); docs/history/2026-07.md § "work API — the work-item lifecycle: get / ack / heartbeat / stop (slice 8, second part)"; omitzero, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaEnvironmentWorkHeartbeatParams.ExpectedLastHeartbeat.* *Tracked: #545 (whether to accept the reference's omitted form, which claims-or-extends with no precondition at all).*
- **POST …/work/{work_id}/ack — idempotent here, a 409 on the reference (confirmed 2026-09-02, #78)** — Only the queued-to-starting edge stamps `acknowledged_at`, so re-acking an already-advanced item returns it unchanged rather than erroring, which is what makes a worker's retry of a lost ack response safe. The recording settles the reference's half of that, in the opposite direction: both the ack of an already-active item and a repeated one answered `409 invalid_request_error`, "Work item sesn_… must be in QUEUED state to acknowledge. Current state: active." So the mismatch is real, and the retry-safety property is ours alone — a worker written against the reference must already tell "my ack never landed" apart from "another worker holds it", which is precisely the distinction our 200 spares it. Kept on that reasoning, and recorded here rather than deleted because the entry it replaces asserted the reference's behavior was unknown. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`work.ack` and `work.ack.again`, both 409 with that message); docs/history/2026-07.md § "work API — the work-item lifecycle: get / ack / heartbeat / stop (slice 8, second part)".* *Tracked: #541 (whether to adopt the reference's 409 and give up the retry-safety property).*
- **POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — the default lease TTL is 30 s here, 300 s on the reference (recorded 2026-09-02 and 2026-09-19)** — A beat that sends no `desired_ttl_seconds` is granted, and answered, a 30 s lease here (`ttl_seconds: 30`), and Ack's startup lease matches it. The reference grants 300 to every recorded beat that took or extended a lease without asking — the 10 claims and 31 echoes of the four 2026-09-19 self-hosted sets, and 2026-09-02's two beats that sent no precondition (batch2 idx 254 and 258) — and 120 to the one echo that asked for 120 (idx 262), so both honour a smaller request; the one claim that answered a stop instead has its own entry (*POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — the stopping claim's `ttl_seconds`*). The owner kept 30 rather than copying the default (2026-09-26, on #810). It is what lets a dead worker's item be reclaimed in half a minute rather than five, and `queue.WindDown` and the sessions token's window, a minute each, are sized to it: the reference worker beats at half the answered TTL, capped at 30 s, so here it learns of a stop within 15 s, and it bounds its tool-result send retries by the answered TTL (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go runHeartbeat), which here gives it 30 s where the reference gives 300. Both workers adapt to whatever TTL is answered, so neither breaks on the difference. *Evidence: internal/api/workapi.go (defaultHeartbeatTTLSeconds, heartbeatTTL), internal/queue/lifecycle.go (ackStartupLeaseSeconds), internal/queue/queue.go (WindDown); internal/api/workapi_test.go TestWorkHeartbeatClaimsLeaseAndExtends; every heartbeat answer in the `worker-network.json` of the 2026-09-19 `-self-hosted-latest-cli`, `-self-hosted-docker`, `-custom-mixed-tools` and `-custom-order-followup` sets, and 2026-09-02 batch2 idx 254, 258 and 262.* *Landed for #810.*
- **POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — the stopping claim's `ttl_seconds`: the TTL here, 120 on the reference (recorded 2026-09-19, #810)** — The claim that learns of a stop (*POST …/work/{work_id}/stop — graceful vs force semantics*) answers with the claim's effective TTL — its `desired_ttl_seconds`, clamped, or the 30 s default — as every beat here that does not extend the lease does. The one recorded such claim answered 120 (custom-mixed-tools #44), though it sent no `desired_ttl_seconds` and the same set's ordinary claims answered 300 (#56). One sample does not say where 120 comes from. The reference's non-extending beats appear to report the TTL the lease was last granted, not a requested one — 2026-09-02's heartbeat on stopped work answered the 120 an earlier echo had asked for (batch2 idx 262, then 297) — so 120 may be a startup lease its ack installs, but no recording shows one. The owner chose to echo rather than copy one sample (2026-09-26, on #810). Neither worker acts on the value: ours returns on the reported state before reading it, and the reference's records it as its send window before reading the state that then cancels the run the window would bound (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go runHeartbeat). *Evidence: internal/queue/lifecycle.go Heartbeat, internal/api/workapi.go heartbeatWork; internal/api/workapi_test.go TestWorkGracefulStopOfStartingWorkWindsDownThroughTheClaim, internal/worker/lease_test.go TestSDKTypedHeartbeatDecodesAStoppingClaim; the 2026-09-19 `-custom-mixed-tools` `worker-network.json` #44 and #56, and 2026-09-02 batch2 idx 262 and 297.* *Tracked: #78 (where the reference's 120 comes from — whether its ack installs a 120 s startup lease); landed for #810.*
- **Optimistic-version conflicts (agent updates) — the reference's 409, words included (confirmed 2026-09-02, #78; aligned for #540)** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#540).** The inference this entry first held picked 409 with `invalid_request_error` because the reference SDK defines no dedicated conflict type, and flagged the reference's real shape as unobserved. The recording confirmed the envelope outright — a stale `version` answered `409 {"type":"invalid_request_error","message":"Concurrent modification detected. Please fetch the latest version and retry."}` — and left one difference, which this entry kept deliberately: ours named the two versions (`agent version mismatch: expected 1, currently 2`), on the argument that a client branches on the type and an operator reads the versions. The owner's decision on #540 reverses that: where a recording holds the reference's message, the wire carries it verbatim, so the 409 is now that sentence, byte for byte, and the versions it withholds move to the operator's log — an Info line `agent update refused: stale version` carrying the request id, `agent_id`, `expected_version` and `current_version`, written where the conflict is raised. Nothing on the wire survives the convergence; what survives is the log line, which no client sees. The title once read "agent/session updates": a session update takes no `version` here, so the agent update is the one route this answers. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`agent.update.stale-version`, batch2.json idx 126, request `{"version":1,"description":"stale"}`); internal/api/agents.go updateAgent (the errConflict and the log line beside it), internal/api/errors.go; internal/api/agents_test.go TestAgentUpdateOptimisticVersioning (the message and the log line, both pinned).* *Landed for #540.*
- **Error envelope — the reference's nested `details` member, emitted where we raise the errors it was recorded on (#664)** — **Converged on the errors named here.** No schema declares `details` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.InvalidRequestError), so the recordings are the whole source: 23 error bodies carry it, always as a member of `error`. We now answer as recorded, details included: the files 400 for an upload (`{error_code: file_not_downloadable}`); the environment kind change 400 (`{error_code: invalid_config_type_change}`); the work heartbeat 412 (`{current_state, error_visibility: user_facing, error_code: heartbeat_precondition_failed}`); on `/api/oauth/…/tokens`, an unknown environment's 404 (`{error_visibility: user_facing, error_code: environment_not_found}`), a malformed environment id's 400 `invalid_request_error` (`{error_visibility: user_facing, error_code: invalid_request}`), an unknown or another environment's key's 404 (`{error_visibility: user_facing}`) and a malformed key id's 400, which carries none; and on `/api/console/…`, an unknown workspace's or API key's 404 and the 400 for an id without the `apikey_` prefix (each `{error_visibility: user_facing}`). Every recorded id gets its recorded answer — the malformed `not-an-env-id`, `not-a-uuid` and an API key id given as a UUID; the well-formed `env_000000000000000000000000`, `env_01MQbDnwtRB9MBhtuxWAHq1M`, `apikey_01ABCDEFGHJKMNPQRSTVWXYZ` and the recorded key UUID (batch2 idx 18, `rec83.edge4.revoke.unknown-uuid`) — and the rule that sorts ids no recording reaches is an inference (*Console namespace ids — what makes one malformed beyond the recorded inputs*). An API key update looks the key up before it judges the body, as the recorded `{"nonexistent_field":1}` on an unknown key answered the 404 (batch5 idx 12). Every other error renders as before, with no `details`, and the memory path conflict's two members stay flat in `error`, as its schema declares (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsMemoryPathConflictError). Since #540 every error named here carries the reference's message too, byte for byte — the heartbeat 412's naming the precondition the beat sent and the item's last heartbeat (`NULL` for none, as the SDK's fixture renders it) — save one arm no recording reaches: a key id or organization segment the reference's uuid parser would refuse for its length or grouping rather than for a stray character keeps ours. Member order differs and carries no meaning: `details` renders first in `error`, sorted as the whole envelope always has been, where the reference lists it last; inside it the order is the recorded one. **`current_state` is the item as the refused beat found it** — the heartbeat response's members less `type`, `lease_extended` false, plus `lease_updated_at` — and the pinned SDK's own worker reads it: it decodes `current_state` as an untyped map and logs its `state`, `ttl_seconds` and `last_heartbeat` when a beat is refused (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — lib/environments/worker.go serverLeaseState). Three of its values rest on less than a recording. Once a beat has claimed the item, `lease_updated_at` is its `started_at`: the one recorded item's three 412s carry a value equal to its `started_at` to the microsecond. Before one has — never claimed, or re-queued, which clears the last heartbeat here — `last_heartbeat` and `lease_updated_at` are both null, as in the SDK's own fixture for a 412 on re-queued work (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — lib/environments/worker_test.go leaseLostBody); no recording holds one. `ttl_seconds` is the refused beat's effective TTL, as every beat here that extends nothing reports it; the recorded 300 is both that beat's default and its lease's, ours is 30 (*POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — the default lease TTL is 30 s here, 300 s on the reference*), and the SDK's fixture says 120, the figure *POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — the stopping claim's `ttl_seconds`* cannot yet explain. A malformed `expected_last_heartbeat` on an absent item is now the 404 any absent item gets, since a 412 reports an item. **Emitted beyond what was probed:** the malformed-environment 400 on list and revoke, recorded on issuance only, and for an `env_` id with unstorable bytes, where the recorded id lacked the prefix; the unknown-environment 404 on revoke, recorded on issuance and list; and the unknown-workspace 404's details on issuance and update, recorded on the listing. Each is the same check, in the same function, as the probe that was recorded. **Emitted since #820**, which made the errors they were recorded on ours too: the 401 `authentication_error` `{error_visibility: user_facing}` for a foreign organization, on both console prefixes; the 404 `{error_visibility: user_facing}` on `GET /api/console/organizations/{org}/workspaces/{id}`; and the 400 `{error_visibility: user_facing}` for a malformed `principal_id` on API-key creation. The 400 for an organization segment that is not a UUID carries none, as recorded (*The console-API dialect's edges*). *Evidence: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02 batch2 `file.content.download`, `work.heartbeat.NO_HEARTBEAT`, `work.heartbeat.wrong-expected`, `work.heartbeat.NO_HEARTBEAT.again` and `env.archive.with-deployment` (a /v1 environment 404, no details); 2026-09-03 batch2 `cookie.files.content-original`; 2026-09-05 batch2 `rec83.edge3.*`, `rec83.edge4.revoke.*` and `rec83.edge6.foreign-org-uuid`, batch5 `rec86.keys.update.*` and `rec86.create.principal_id.bogus-string`, batch8 `setup.envkeyA.mint.foreign-workspace-env` and `item6.after-archive.workspaceB.*`; 2026-09-12 batch1 `rec91.kind.*`; internal/api/errors.go withDetails, internal/api/workapi.go mapWorkErr, internal/queue/lifecycle.go HeartbeatMismatchError, internal/api/consoleapi.go consoleEnvironmentID; TestFileDownloadGate, TestFileNotDownloadableDetailsReachAnSDKClient, TestEnvironmentKindIsImmutable, TestWorkHeartbeatRefusalReportsTheItem, TestConsoleKeyErrorsCarryTheRecordedDetails, TestConsoleKeyRevokeRejectsIdsItDoesNotOwn, TestAPIKeyErrorsCarryTheRecordedDetails, TestAPIKeyRoutesRejectUnknownScopesAndIDs, TestAnErrorWithoutDetailsRendersAsBefore, TestDetailsNestInsideTheError, TestConsoleKeyRoutesRejectOtherOrganizations, TestAPIKeyPrincipalIDIsJudgedAsRecorded, TestConsoleWorkspaceReadIsTheRecordedNotFound.* *Tracked: #78 (a recorded 412 on work no beat has claimed, or re-queued since, would settle the null forms and whether `lease_updated_at` follows `started_at`); landed for #664 and #820.*
- **DELETE /v1/environments/{id} — the self-hosted queue refusal, the sessions refusal and force=true (confirmed 2026-09-02 and 2026-09-03, #78; implemented for #546 and #841)** — The reference refuses to delete a `self_hosted` environment whose work queue still holds items — `409 invalid_request_error`, "Cannot delete self-hosted environment with work in the queue. Either archive the environment first to allow the queue to drain, or use force=true to delete immediately." — and deletes it on the same request with `?force=true`. This platform now answers that refusal in that sentence: an undrained `tool_exec` item on a `self_hosted` environment — the work API's own scope, an item drained once `stopped` — is the 409. A delete the sessions refuse is the reference's other 409, recorded on 2026-09-03 (batch2 #250 `sweep2.del.environments.pWX3jb`): "Environment has 4 active sessions.
