Use force=true to delete anyway." — and since #841 this platform answers it in that sentence and with its recorded `x-should-retry: false`, which keeps the SDK from retrying the 409 twice, but what it counts differs. There it counted four sessions the recording had already deleted through the API (#189, #205–#207) on a `self_hosted` environment holding no queued work, while every `cloud` environment whose sessions had been deleted the same way was deleted with a 200 (#242–#248 and #252), and so was a `cloud` one holding five sessions nobody had deleted or archived (2026-09-05 batch7 #30 `rec88.env.delete.on`): on the reference a session refuses only a `self_hosted` delete, and one deleted through its API still counts there. Here a deleted session is gone, and `sessions.environment_id` refuses on every other one — archived and terminated ones included, on either kind — so N is that count, and a `cloud` environment holding sessions is refused, in the same 409 and sentence, where the reference deletes it; the refusal is the one foreign key whatever the kind. N renders as the recorded template has it, so one session reads "1 active sessions" (INFERRED: only 4 is recorded). With a deployment in the way the refusal stays this platform's 400 naming the deployments, since a deployment refuses nothing on the reference (*environment_not_found_error — recorded, and unreachable here while an environment's deployments hold it*, above). **What diverges is what force reaches.** On the reference the forced delete returned 200 with the queue live, and a live item there is itself a session, so no session stood in the way; here every work item carries a session row and `sessions.environment_id` has a foreign key with no cascade, so a delete forced past the queue is refused by the sessions — the same 409 and header, in this platform's words, "environment env_… still has sessions; delete them first", since the recorded sentence's one piece of advice is the force the caller just sent — as one is by a deployment (its 400). `force=true` therefore exchanges one refusal for another rather than deleting, and that is deliberate: a session is the event log, and no resource delete here takes one down as a side effect of another's. It also means the 409 guards no loss the sessions' key had not already prevented — the delete raised 23503 before any cascade could run, and what the pre-#546 handler got wrong was the words, since clearing the sessions it named is precisely what cascades the queue away. The row the key cannot cover is an item whose environment differs from its session's, which the schema permits and no enqueue path produces. The archive path the sentence recommends drains the queue as described — an archived environment refuses new sessions, existing ones run on and their items drain — but draining is not by itself enough to delete here: the sessions that held those items still hold the environment, so they have to go too, which is precisely what draining first makes safe. Both sentences are kept verbatim rather than corrected for this platform, so a reader following one literally can still reach a dead end — an already-archived environment is told to archive, and the force both offer never deletes. Wire fidelity wins there because a client branches on the status and the type, never on the prose. Neither the SDK nor the `ant` CLI types a `force` parameter on the environment delete; a client sends it as a raw query option. Three readings the recordings do not settle: whether the reference refuses a `cloud` environment's in-flight queue too (no `cloud` delete recorded held queued work — this platform refuses `self_hosted` only, as the sentence says); which lifecycle states it counts as in the queue (the recorded item was re-queued, so no other state is observed — this platform counts every state but `stopped`, the boundary `queue.Stop`'s own force arm draws, so a worker's in-flight item refuses too; that is deliberately wider than what `…/work/stats` reports as `depth`, which counts the unreserved backlog a worker could poll next and so reads 0 for the leased item whose loss costs most); and what it makes of a `force` value other than `true` (this platform reads the parameter with `parseBoolParam`, the shared helper behind every `include_archived`, so Go's own `strconv.ParseBool` spellings — `1`, `t`, `TRUE` — force as well, and a malformed value is a 400 where before #546 any `force` value was ignored). *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 teardown, quoted in #546 — `DELETE /v1/environments/env_012x9DcYikKh1WcxFVA8evah` → 409 with the sentence above, then `?force=true` → 200, outside the exported arrays as the issue's provenance comment records; the SDK's BetaEnvironmentDeleteParams, which it generates from the spec's delete operation — headers only, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironment.go BetaEnvironmentDeleteParams, and no force field, absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironment.go BetaEnvironmentDeleteParams.Force; the CLI's environments delete, with no force flag, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betaenvironment.go betaEnvironmentsDelete, and work stop's `force`, the only one the CLI sends to the API, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betaenvironmentwork.go betaEnvironmentsWorkStop, beside `ant apply`'s, which overrides drift locally, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — pkg/cmd/apply.go applyCommand; the 2026-09-03 recording (batch2 #189, #205–#207, #242–#252) and the 2026-09-05 one (batch7 #30); internal/api/environments.go deleteEnvironment and environmentStillReferenced, internal/api/environments_test.go TestEnvironmentDeleteBlockedBySessions and TestEnvironmentDeleteQueueRefusalIsSelfHostedAndUndrainedOnly, internal/api/page.go parseBoolParam, internal/queue/lifecycle.go (HasUndrainedWork, which owns the scope, over workAPIScope and Stop's state boundary), internal/queue/stats.go (depth); migration 0001_init.sql (work_items ON DELETE CASCADE from environments; sessions.environment_id without one).* *Tracked: #78 (whether the reference refuses a cloud environment's in-flight queue, which lifecycle states it counts as in the queue, what it makes of a force value other than true, and how it renders one session's count); landed for #546 and #841.*
- **A memory attachment's element — `access` echoed as `"read_write"`, `instructions` only when supplied (confirmed 2026-09-02, #78; aligned for #666)** — **Converged, except on elements stored before #666.** The spec says `access` "Defaults to read_write" and types both fields nullable, and the entry this replaces read that two ways: the default stored and echoed as the string, which the recording confirms, and an `instructions` omitted or null rendered as `null`, which it refutes — the reference omits the key. `session.create.store-access-null` sent `{"access": null, "instructions": null}` and came back with six keys, `access` among them as `"read_write"`; of the 44 memory elements across the recording's two files, 8 carry `instructions` and none carries a null one. The same elements show `mount_path` never null and a store without a description echoing `""`, both as here. This platform now omits the key too. What survives is the old shape on old rows: an element is written into `sessions.resources` once and re-served verbatim by the session `GET` and the resources list, so a session created before #666 keeps `"instructions": null` on every read. No migration rewrites it — the SDK types the field nullable, so a client written against it accepts either form, and the brain's decoder reads both as absent. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`session.create.store-access-null`, `session.create.five-stores`, `session.get.five-stores`, `session.resources.list`); the spec's BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResource and …ResourceParam (`access` "Defaults to read_write", nullable), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResource.properties.access and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResourceParam.properties.access; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionresource.go BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResource and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResourceParam and BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStoreResourceParamAccess; internal/api/sessionresources.go parseMemoryResource / memoryResourceJSON; internal/brain/memory.go; internal/api/sessionmemory_test.go TestSessionMemoryStoreAttachment / TestSessionMemoryStoreAttachmentRejections; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 7*
- **The memory mount-path slug — an ASCII alphabet, hyphens trimmed, and the whole store id's slug for a name without alphanumerics (confirmed 2026-09-02, #78; aligned for #671)** — **Converged, except on sessions created before #671.** Documented: the directory name is "the store's display name sanitized to a filesystem-safe slug (lowercased; non-alphanumeric runs become a single hyphen)". The entry this replaces inferred the three things that sentence leaves open, and one recorded create settles all three: "alphanumeric" means ASCII ("Ünïcode" mounts at `/mnt/memory/n-code`), a leading or trailing hyphen is trimmed ("(Notes)" slugs to `notes`, which is why it collided with "notes" there and landed at `notes-2` — the collision entry under INFERRED), and a name with no alphanumerics at all takes the slug of the **whole store id**, prefix included: the reference mounted "!!!" at `/mnt/memory/memstore-01d7kbgb3pwxq5cqhyg2tk8e`. That third reading was wrong here — the bare token, its case kept — and matches now. What survives is the old path on old rows: a session's element is stored once, `mount_path` included, and re-served and mounted verbatim, so an all-symbol store attached before #671 keeps its old path in that session; a deployment stores no mount path and derives the new one at its next fire. The slug is computed from the name snapshotted at attach time, so a rename changes later sessions only, which the reference documents. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`session.create.five-stores` — "rec78 Notes renamed", "notes", "(Notes)", "Ünïcode" and "!!!" in one create); platform.claude.com managed-agents/memory (the slug rule); the spec's `mount_path` ("Derived from the store's name. The entry this replaces answered 400 `invalid_request_error`, on the `vault_ids` precedent, and the recording confirms it: the reference refused `session.create.archived-store` with 400 `invalid_request_error`, "Memory store memstore_… is archived.", which this platform answers in those words since #540 (it said `memory store … is archived`). The store row is read `FOR SHARE` inside the create transaction, so an archive cannot slip between the check and the insert, and a deployment fire records the refusal as `memory_store_archived_error`, in the run's own recorded sentence (*A missing memory store at fire time*). That entry also answered an unknown store with the same 400, which the recording contradicts; that arm is an inference now, under INFERRED beside the other memory-attachment entries. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`session.create.archived-store`); the spec's `archived_at` ("Archived stores are read-only and cannot be attached to new sessions"), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsMemoryStore.properties.archived_at; internal/api/sessionresources.go snapshotMemoryStore; internal/api/sessionmemory_test.go TestSessionMemoryStoreAttachmentRejections / TestSessionMemoryAttachLocksTheStoreRow; internal/api/deploymentruns_test.go TestDeploymentRunRecordsAClassifiedFailure; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 7*
- **A deleted store's attachment is kept as snapshotted, and the Memory-stores block hedges it where the reference does not (plan 36 slice 3, decision 7; confirmed 2026-09-02, #78; kept for #672)** — Deleting a store cascades its memories and versions and leaves every session's element in place, as the reference does: its session `GET` and resources list, read after the store's delete, still carry the element unchanged. Here `GET /v1/sessions/{id}` renders it and the `memory_store_id` filter matches it, exactly as a deleted file's mount stays in `resources` (plan 08 decision 2's accepted dangling reference). The next run's prompt is where this platform diverges, on purpose: the reference renders the deleted store as an ordinary bullet (` - "!!!" → /mnt/memory/memstore-01d7kbgb3pwxq5cqhyg2tk8e/ (read-write)`), and ours adds a line after the store's instructions, so the platform has the last word on it — "NOT AVAILABLE: the memory store no longer exists, so nothing you save there persists; the path may still hold what was mounted before." The hedge is true here and the bare bullet would not be: neither the executor nor the BYOC worker syncs a store whose row is gone, so a write into that directory never reaches a store, and an unqualified `(read-write)` would have the model take an unsaved write as saved. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`store.delete.attached`, `session.get.after-attached-store-deleted`, `session.resources.list.after-store-deleted`, `sessA.events.after-turn2`); internal/store/migrations/0028_memory_stores.sql and 0029_memories.sql (the cascades end at the store); internal/executor/memory.go settleStore and internal/worker/memory.go settleStore (a gone store is skipped); internal/brain/memory.go renderMemoryBlock; internal/brain/memory_test.go TestRenderMemoryBlock; internal/api/sessionmemory_test.go TestSessionMemoryStoreAttachment / TestSessionListFiltersByMemoryStore; docs/plan/36_memory-stores.md decision 7*
- **The Memory-stores block — the reference's recorded structure, its guidance rewritten for the run-boundary sync (plan 36 slice 4, decision 9; confirmed 2026-09-02, #78; ported for #672)** — A session asked to print every part of its system prompt that mentions `/mnt/memory` returned the reference's block, and the brain now renders its structure: the persistence paragraph, the heading `Available stores (write only inside the directories listed below):`, and per store ` - "<name>" → <mount_path>/ (read-write|read-only)`, then `: <description>` when there is one and the instructions on the next line, indented three spaces. The leading space and the indents are the model's transcription — instructed to be verbatim, but model-mediated. Five of the guidance's seven bolded sections are the recording's bytes. The rest is rewritten where it is false here (see "Memory stores sync at the run boundary, not through a live mount"): the stores are local copies, not "mounted … from a shared remote service"; a write is saved by a sync, normally when the tool calls finish, not as it lands, and nothing is said of context compaction, which this platform does not do; the FUSE paragraph's per-operation latency and transient errors go; "Handling write failures" trades `Stale file handle`, `EIO` and a retry after ~300 ms for the ways a change goes silently unsaved here — the store winning a both-sides edit, a deferred sync, the wipe guard, the 2,000-changed-files skip, the 102,400-byte cap, content that is not UTF-8, a path the store refuses, a path collision, the 2,000-memory cap, a read-only store (whose archived form still takes a `self_hosted` deletion, #685), a disturbed marker; and "Check memory first" names `grep`, told to skip `/mnt/memory/.sync` and the markers, and `ls -R` where the reference names `rg`, which the default `debian:stable-slim` sandbox image does not ship, and says prior sessions "may already have" saved findings, without "on this exact environment" — a store is a workspace's, may be empty, and is bound to no environment. `memory_test.go` holds the guidance's figures to memsync's constants. Ours, beyond the rewrite: the deleted store's hedge (the entry above); a line for a store whose state the brain could not look up this turn; with no writable store, a lead saying nothing written is saved and no write sections; and three guards on callers' text, which the reference's multi-line bullet makes matter more — each of name, description and instructions flattened onto one line, the name quoted Go-style so an embedded `"` cannot close the platform's quote (the recorded names render unchanged), and the instructions behind an `Instructions: ` label so they cannot pass for a bullet or a hedge. #581 asks the same question of the skills block's template, and this decision is its precedent: port the recorded structure, keep the reference's words where they are true here, rewrite what is not, and register what stays ours. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`session.create.five-stores`, `sessA.send.memory-prompt-echo`, `sessA.events.after-turn2`); internal/brain/memory.go renderMemoryBlock and its constants; internal/brain/testdata/memoryblock.golden and memoryblock-readonly.golden; internal/brain/memory_test.go TestRenderMemoryBlock, TestRenderMemoryBlockNothingWritable and TestRenderMemoryBlockRecordedBullets; internal/brain/memoryinject_test.go; internal/memsync/memsync.go and plan.go Plan; internal/executor/memory.go and internal/worker/memory.go (readStore, settleStore); internal/executor/executor.go and internal/worker/lease.go (the default image)*
- **POST /v1/sessions/{id}/events — a message while the primary awaits a response (confirmed 2026-09-12, #842)** — With the session idle on `requires_action` for one `agent.custom_tool_use`, the reference refused a lone `user.message` with 400 ``Invalid user.message event at events[0]: waiting on responses to events [sevt_…]; only `user.tool_confirmation`, `user.custom_tool_result`, `user.tool_result`, or `user.interrupt` may be sent (a `system.message` may trail a tool result)``, naming the call, and took the custom result next. That is the whole of what is recorded. This platform queued such a message behind the call until #842 and now refuses it in those words (events.CheckWhileAwaiting), **in the recorded state only: the primary resting idle on responses it awaits.** A primary still running — its model turn under way, or a platform call (a cloud command) running beside a custom call the turn also made — is no state any recording refused in, so a message to it is queued as before, and so is one to an idle primary whose head call awaits nothing from outside (a platform call allowed and not yet run). Everything else is inferred from the sentence, which names the four events a client may send while the session waits, and is read as one gate over every wait those four answer. (1) **The waits:** a custom call's result, a `self_hosted` worker's result for a built-in, and the confirmation an ask-gated call needs — an allow leaving a custom or worker call still awaiting its result, a denial answering the call. A call whose answer arrived but is queued behind an earlier one is not awaited and not named — a denial included, before the walk writes its result — though the re-idle's `stop_reason.event_ids` still lists it. (2) **What is refused:** a `user.message`, and a `user.define_outcome` alike, the sentence leaving it out of the four; a `system.message` is never refused itself, since it may follow only a message or a result. (3) **A send is judged with its own answers in**, wherever they sit in it, and an interrupt reaching the primary answers every call, so a message beside the answers that free the primary — before them or after — or behind the documented interrupt-and-redirect is read as before; only what the primary would still await afterwards is refused, the first message or outcome named at its index. (4) **Only the primary's waits count**, it being the thread a message and an outcome address: a child parked on its own call does not refuse one, though the session's `requires_action` unions the child's ids. (5) Several ids render as Go renders a slice, `[sevt_a sevt_b]`, in log order — only a single id was recorded. (6) The gate runs after the send's own refusals (an answer naming no pending call, a management key's tool result) and before the outcome checks; the reference's order among them is unrecorded. The reference's budget gate, recorded beside this (`session budget reached: …`), has no counterpart here. *Evidence: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-12-followups budget-runtime.json [3] (the session idle on `requires_action` naming the call), [4] `rec.budget.message-over-budget` (the refusal), [6] (the custom result accepted after it), [28] (a `self_hosted` call idling the session on `requires_action` too); internal/events/awaiting.go ThreadWaits and CheckWhileAwaiting, internal/api/events.go sendSessionEvents; internal/api/awaiting_test.go TestAMessageWhileACustomCallAwaitsItsResultIsRefused, TestWhatTheSendWaitsOn and TestAMessageBehindACallAwaitingNothingExternalQueues, processing_order_test.go TestAnAnswerQueuedBehindAnEarlierCallGoesToTheTail (a message read while a child waits).* *Tracked: #78 (a recording of a message beside a pending confirmation or a worker's call, of one in a send that also answers the call, of one while the primary runs beside a pending call, of one while only a child waits, and of a refusal naming several calls); landed for #842.*
- **`x-should-retry: false` — on the refusals recorded carrying it (confirmed 2026-09-02 to 2026-09-19, #842)** — The reference marks many of its 4xx answers `x-should-retry: false` and not others, by condition rather than by status or route: the memories' 409s carry it where their 400s and 404s do not, and `Environment env_… not found.` carries it on an environment's delete and archive and not on a session create. The header is not decoration — the SDK reads it ahead of the status, and without it spends two retries on a 409 (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — requestconfig.go shouldRetry). Until #842 this platform sent it on two refusals; it now sends it wherever a recording shows it and this platform answers the same status (noRetry): an unknown path's `Not found`; `x-api-key header is required`; an environment key that is not live (the reference's `Invalid bearer token`); the skills' 403 scope refusals to an environment key; an environment create's `name: Field required`, `…: Extra inputs are not permitted` and unknown `config` tag, and an environment delete's and archive's 404; the work API's cloud-poll 400, cloud-list 404 and `Token not authorized for this environment` 403; a file's 404, the not-downloadable 400, and the environment-key lane's content 404; both memory 409s; and on the skills `Skill not found`, `Skill version not found`, the two list 400s, `source must be one of …`, `Invalid skill_id format: …`, the version-name 400, the `'latest' is not accepted …` and `Invalid version id` 400s, the only-version 400 and the upload refusals. Recorded 4xx captured without it get none: an agent's stale version 409, a credential's duplicate 409, a revoked environment key's 401, the agents list's 401 to an environment key, `Not Found` under /v1/dreams/ and /v1/deployments/, and every recorded agent, session, deployment, vault and memory-route 400 and 404 but an unknown path's. Absence counts only where the capture could hold the header: the 2026-09-02 batch2 requests made with the worker's environment key (`auth: env-key-a`), whose captures kept `content-type` alone, every 2026-09-12 session and most 2026-09-19 ones captured no `x-should-retry` at all — where the same 2026-09-02 batch did hold it on its other lanes, the work list's and poll's 401s to a console credential (#292, #294) among them — so a refusal recorded only in such a capture — the work ack's 409 (#255) and the heartbeat's 412 (#259) among them — is unrecorded for this purpose and keeps none. Recorded ones this platform answers with another status are untouched (the beta-header 404s, the numeric and `skillver_` version refusals, the console cookie's content 403). Three extensions are inferred (the catalogue below): the console namespace marks every refusal, every one recorded there carrying it; a helper whose every recorded answer carries it marks every input it answers; and a malformed id's 404 takes it wherever the absent id's does, so the two stay indistinguishable (#135). *Evidence: every response header in managed-agents-wire-recordings, the conditions above cited at their noRetry sites — internal/api/errors.go (noRetry, writeError's console rule, errMemoryPathConflict, errMemoryPrecondition), server.go errUnknownPath, auth.go, envauth.go, environments.go, workapi.go, files.go, skills.go, skillsupload.go; internal/api/noretry_test.go TestXShouldRetryFollowsTheRecordings.* *Tracked: #78 (a capture that holds the header for the work ack, the heartbeat and every refusal this entry leaves unmarked would confirm or correct each); landed for #842.*
- **POST /v1/sessions/{id}/resources — a file needs a usable read tool (confirmed 2026-09-12, #842)** — The reference refuses a file added to a session whose agent cannot read it: against an agent whose `agent_toolset_20260401` disabled every tool by `default_config` and enabled bash alone, the add was 400 ``Missing required tool: file resources require the read tool to be usable (enabled and not always_deny) on the session's `agent_toolset` ``, and the same file was accepted on a session whose agent enabled read by name. **Converged since #842**, in those words (requireReadTool), with three edges of this platform's own. (1) *Usable* is the sentence's: an `agent_toolset_20260401` entry on the session's agent leaving read enabled. Its `always_deny` half is vacuous here, no toolset accepting that policy, and an agent with no such entry at all — MCP and custom tools only — has no read tool and is refused, which the recording, whose agent had a toolset, does not show. The agent is the session's snapshot, the coordinator's on a multiagent session, whose members' toolsets are not consulted. (2) After a session update enabled read on the same session's agent (the update echoed it enabled), the reference still refused the add, and the recording leaves why unresolved — its console kept showing Read denied. This platform reads the session's current snapshot, so the add succeeds once an update enables read — and the agent's own update does not, a session keeping the agent version it was created with. The same reading leaves a bypass this platform does not close: an update that disables read after files are mounted leaves them mounted, and nothing recorded says the reference refuses that update or unmounts them. A stored toolset entry that no longer resolves offers no usable read, so its session's add is this refusal, not a 500. (3) Session create is not held to the rule: every recorded create that attached a file named an agent with read usable, so whether create refuses the rest is unrecorded and create keeps accepting it — which is also what a deployment run's session create does. The check runs after the session's own refusals (absent, archived, a dream's) and before the mount path's and the file's, an order the recording does not settle. *Evidence: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-12-console-141 ui-network.json [92] `session.file.attach-by-id` and [112] `session.file.retry-after-read-enabled` (the refusals), [288] `session.readable-agent.attach-file` (the acceptance), api-fixtures.json [11] (the session update enabling read) and [13] (a create attaching a file to an agent with read enabled), ISSUE_141_COVERAGE.md (the unresolved #112); 2026-09-02 batch2 [388, 389] and 2026-09-03 batch2 [17] (creates attaching files, read usable); internal/api/sessionresources.go requireReadTool; internal/api/sessionresources_test.go TestAddingAFileNeedsAUsableReadTool.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording of a session create attaching a file to an agent without read, of an add to an agent with no toolset, of the add after a session update that stays unexplained, and of an update disabling read beside mounted files); landed for #842.*
- **GET /v1/files/{id}/content — the environment-key content lane, wider than the reference's (confirmed 2026-09-03)** — A BYOC worker reads a mounted file's bytes with the same environment key it polls work with, over `GET /v1/files/{id}/content` — the **single** file route on the dual-auth set. It is deliberately narrower than skills' read set: the metadata GET `/v1/files/{id}`, the collection list, and every mutation stay management-only. On this lane the `downloadable` gate is skipped (a user upload is `downloadable:false`, yet a session that mounts it must read it), but admission to the lane is not sufficient: the key is **environment-scoped, and further gated on the file being mounted**. The download handler authorizes an environment key for a file only if some session in that environment references it in `resources[]` — one jsonb containment lookup (`resources @> [{"file_id": …}]`) filtered on `environment_id` — and a file no session in the env mounts answers 404, indistinguishable from absent. The scope is the environment, not the single session a worker happens to be servicing (an environment key is not tied to one session; it polls all of its environment's work), so the readable set is the union of `resources[]` across the environment's sessions — still bounded to the environment. Since #578 what a session's `resources[]` name is its own copies, so an upload is readable here only through a session created before then that still names it, and a copy's bytes are served from the object it shares with its upload. Unlike skills (workspace-global shared assets), file content can be sensitive, so a leaked environment key is never a workspace-wide file-exfiltration credential and cannot even probe file existence across environments. The reference worker has no file path at all, so the lane and its auth scope are entirely ours to define and record. *Evidence: docs/plan/08_files.md decision 10; internal/api/server.go (isFileReadPath, dispatchAuth), internal/api/files.go (downloadFile, fileMountedInEnvironment), internal/worker/files.go (SetupFiles)* **The absence is confirmed, so the lane and its scope are now wholly ours — this is a divergence, not an inference.** An environment key was answered **404 `not_found_error` "Not found"** on `GET /v1/files/{id}/content` in all three recorded arms: the file as uploaded, the session-scoped copy the resource actually holds, and the same file under a key for a *different* environment. The middle arm is the one that settles it — a session in that key's own environment had mounted the file, and the reference still refused, so it is not a scoping rule we failed to satisfy but a route the environment key does not reach at all. The console cookie on the same file got a **400** instead: ``File `file_…` is not downloadable. Only files generated by a tool (for example, the code execution tool) can be…`` — the credential allowed to know gets the reason, the one that is not gets a flat, uninformative status. That asymmetry is deliberate existence-hiding: a 404 in every case means an environment key cannot tell "wrong environment" from "not downloadable" from "no such file", and so cannot use status codes to enumerate file ids or probe downloadability. **This platform's lane is strictly more permissive** — it serves a mounted file's bytes to the environment key with the downloadable gate skipped (decision 10) — and the mount entry says why the reference can afford to refuse: its workers never need an HTTP download, because files arrive as a per-session FUSE mount. #840 weighed it again under the owner's rule that a recorded route takes its recorded answer unless that breaks a flow of our own, and it stays: this platform's worker downloads each mounted file over this route with its environment key (SetupFiles), so the recorded 404 would leave a BYOC session without its files. A `cloud` environment's key, which has no worker to serve, gets the recorded "Not found" (*A `cloud` or archived environment's key*). The scoping this platform does apply is the right shape for what it chose to serve: unmounted and cross-environment reads are 404, matching the reference's own refusal to distinguish them, and a store error reads as "not mounted" so a transient blip denies rather than leaks. The cookie's 400 records one more thing for free — **only tool-generated files are downloadable at all**, an uploaded file never is — which bears on the file-download-headers entry, whose test assumes a downloadable upload. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `envkey.files.content-original`, `envkey.files.content-session-copy`, `envkey.files.content-wrong-environment` and `cookie.files.content-original`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §A.* *Landed for #55.*
- **Expired-file metadata — "up to 30 days" implemented as a floor, where the published phrase is a ceiling (plan 49 slice 2, #655)** — The docs publish both halves of what `expires_at` does: the content stops being retrievable immediately, while "Its metadata (GET /v1/files/{file_id}) remains readable for up to 30 days, with expires_at in the past". That sentence settles the anchor — the window runs from the expiry, not from the upload — so a file uploaded with a 90-day lifetime is readable for 120 days in total here, and that arithmetic is the published sentence's rather than a reading of ours. What the sentence leaves open is the direction of the bound, and the direction is where this platform diverges: **"up to 30 days" is a ceiling, and the sweep implements a floor.** The row stays readable for *at least* 30 days past the expiry and is then removed on the hour, so its real life is the window plus up to one interval — a backlog no longer stretches that, the sweep draining until a batch comes back short, though a transcript an open dream owns is held past the window entirely, until its dream closes (#698) — where the phrase as published would equally admit a reference that drops the row a day after the expiry. No recording can turn this into agreement, which is why it belongs here rather than among the inferences: if the reference removes the row promptly or lazily, ours outlives it by guaranteeing the window that sentence caps; if it keeps the row forever behind an expired marker, ours removes what it keeps. A recording sizes the gap, it cannot close it — a file uploaded with a short lifetime and read back past the window answers how the reference behaves, at the cost of waiting out the window. *Evidence: platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/files ("remains readable for up to 30 days"); internal/api/fileretention.go (the window, measured from `expires_at` against the database's own clock); internal/api/fileretention_test.go TestExpiredFilePurge (a file one minute either side of the window); docs/plan/49_file-expiration.md.* *Tracked: #78 (how promptly the reference removes expired metadata, which sizes this gap without closing it).*
- **Skill reads on the environment-key lane — the version subtree, as recorded (confirmed 2026-09-03)** — **Converged for the environment key (#840).** The SDK's SetupSkillsFromSession doc comment prescribes the environment Bearer key for the versions list/get and `/content` download it performs, and the reference serves the key that subtree and no more. One key was answered route by route in one pass: `…/versions`, `…/versions/{vid}` and `…/versions/{vid}/content` **200**; `GET /v1/skills` and `GET /v1/skills/{id}` **403 `permission_error`** `OAuth token does not meet scope requirement any_of(org:skills, user:developer, user:managed_agents, user:skills, workspace:developer, workspace:skills)`; `GET /v1/agents` **401 `authentication_error` "Authentication failed"**. Under the console cookie both refused skill routes answer 200, so the split belongs to the credential, not the route — admission is per route, the same shape #550 tracks on the work API. This platform used to serve the bare `GET /v1/skills/{id}` to the key as well, for a coherent read-lane rule: one route wider than the reference, a divergence taken knowingly, on the argument that the key already reads every version and archive the skill object names, so the wider lane withheld nothing (a 2026-09-04 recording confirmed the split: the version routes served the key, [134, 135] `/content` bare and with `?beta=true`, [143, 144, 145] a version, `latest` and the version list, and [146] the skill object refused it). Under the owner's rule for #840 — a recorded route gets its recorded answer unless following it would break a flow of our own — it now follows the recording. `isSkillReadPath` admits the version subtree alone, and the management lane answers a live environment key on the collection and on a skill's own read with the reference's 403, words and all (`environmentKeyRefusals`; the later recordings `rec81.envkey.skills.get.custom` and `rec82.skills.list.envkey` answer the same). Nothing of ours depended on the wider lane: the reference worker's SetupSkills and this platform's worker each make the version get and the download, never a skill's own read. The 403 names the reference's OAuth scopes, a vocabulary this platform does not have; it is answered as recorded on these two routes, and the scope model itself stays #550's. **Two things stay open.** The sessions-token lane keeps the bare read (`isWorkTokenSkillPath`): it serves the environment key's skill reads, and the reference was never recorded answering that token on `GET /v1/skills/{id}`, so the lane keeps its own answer there. And whether a management key is also accepted on the version routes is unobserved. *Evidence: the doc comment's WithHeaderDel("X-Api-Key") + WithAuthToken(environmentKey), checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — skills.go AgentToolContext.SetupSkillsFromSession; internal/api/server.go (isSkillReadPath, isSkillResourcePath, dualAuth), internal/api/envauth.go (environmentKeyRefusals), internal/api/worktokenauth.go (isWorkTokenSkillPath), internal/worker/skills.go (resolveSkill, materializeSkill); internal/api/skillsauth_test.go TestSkillReadsEnvironmentKeyLane, internal/api/envkeyrefusals_test.go TestTheManagementLaneAnswersALiveEnvironmentKeyAsRecorded.* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `envkey.skills.*`, `envkey.agents.list-should-refuse` and `cookie.skills.*`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §A; 2026-09-04/batch2.json idx 146; 2026-09-05/batch1.json idx 51.* *Tracked: #78 (whether a management key is accepted on the skill version routes — the console-cookie refusal names "a workspace API key" among the credentials `/content` accepts, but no probe sent one — and what the reference answers a sessions token on a skill's own read); landed for #54; landed for #840.*
- **Environment `config.packages` — installed per sandbox at every first provision, never cached across sessions (plan 40 decision 2)** — The reference documents the install as cached: "Packages are installed by their respective package managers and cached across sessions that share the same environment." This platform caches nothing across sessions. The lists install into each session's sandbox at its first provision, so a second session on the same environment pays the same `apt-get` again — and so does a restored sandbox after a reap, since the sentinel recording what a sandbox has settled (`/tmp/.map-packages`) lives outside the checkpoint roots on purpose and a restore is a fresh container. Inside one sandbox the pass is idempotent: a manager whose list is unchanged and installed is skipped, so the repeat is per sandbox, not per tool call. The reference's mechanism is unobserved; the cache it describes could be a shared package store or a per-environment image keyed on the packages hash, and it is the latter — the shape this plan weighed and declined — that is the delivery cost: Kubernetes has no image-build primitive, Docker's would need a registry both the executors and the chart trust, and the image would join the fields `sandbox.Spec` compares when it adopts a running sandbox. The divergence is therefore in cost rather than in contents — the same packages arrive, they just arrive more often — and it is registered because an operator sizing a package list against install time is sizing it per session here. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/environments § "Packages" (fetched 2026-09-05 — "cached across sessions that share the same environment"); docs/plan/40_environment-packages.md decisions 1 and 2; internal/executor/packages.go (packagesSentinelPath and its comment, installPackages), internal/executor/packages_test.go TestASettledManagerIsNotReinstalled and TestTheSentinelIsNotPreservedAcrossACheckpoint.* *Tracked: #595 (the per-environment install cache the reference's sentence describes).*
- **Session threads — a child's resume of an idle session (ours, deliberately; the recorded shape differs)** — When a child thread's move to running moves an idle session — a confirmation or result answering a child parked on `requires_action` while every other thread is idle — this platform appends, after the answer, `session.status_running` and then that child's own cross-posted `session.thread_status_running`, written by the send itself (#793) and, when the answer denies, after the denial's result, as the one recorded child denial lists the two on the child's own list (2026-09-12-console-followups/approvals-network.json, the child thread's events, idx 7–9: the confirmation, the result, the child's running event), and the primary stays idle: the fold over live threads ("Session status as a fold over threads", among the inferences below) runs the session because the child runs. The reference does neither. Eleven resumes are recorded across six recordings, answering a child's ask with five `user.tool_confirmation`s (one a deny), four `user.tool_result`s and two `user.custom_tool_result`s. In every one `session.status_running` is paired 1 µs later with the **primary's** (the coordinator's) `session.thread_status_running`, ahead of the answer; the primary idles again (`end_turn`, with no model request between and no change to usage), and the child's own `session.thread_status_running` lands 0.14 to 0.57 s after the session event, never with a `session.status_running` of its own. In six of the eleven the child's running comes before the primary's idle, in five after it. In ten a `session.usage` and a `session.status_idle` follow the primary's idle, so the child runs under a session its event list shows idle, and that idle's `stop_reason` is `requires_action` still naming the ask just answered — stale; in the eleventh, where a sibling was already running, no session idle follows. The threads lists read just after five of the resumes show the answered child `running` and the primary `idle`. **This platform keeps its shape on purpose** (decided on #793, 2026-09-25): a session's `status` is a truthful fold that answers whether any work is happening in the session, and the executor's liveness check (a `tool_exec` item runs only on a running session), the MCP work check and the archive/delete guards all read it that way. Idling the session under a working child, as the reference's list does, would let an executor drop a confirmed call unrun and let an archive or delete end a session mid-work. The reference's shape is also inconsistent with itself — an idle written after the child's running in six resumes, a stale stop reason, an idle that depends on whether a sibling runs — which reads as an artefact of how it forwards the answer rather than a contract a client could rely on. The pair this platform emits is ordered by its session event, as the primary's is. The same fold decides what a wake of the coordinator writes while a child works, and that residual is deliberate too: a `user.message` that wakes the coordinator while a child keeps the session running writes the coordinator's `session.thread_status_running` alone, with no `session.status_running`, because the session never left running — where the reference, whose session reads idle while a child works, would write the pair. A consequence carries into delegation the same way: a child's report later wakes the coordinator without a `session.status_running`, because the reporting child still holds the fold at running, where nine of the ten recorded report wakes carry one ("Coordinator delegation", below). *Evidence: internal/events/status.go (TransitionThread, foldSession); internal/executor/executor.go and mcpwork.go (a live-work item runs only on a running session); internal/api/sessions.go (requireNotRunning); TestTransitionThreadFoldsOverChildren (the child resume, asserted as this platform's behavior); TestAChildsDenialIsListedAheadOfItsResume (a child's denial); TestAWakeUnderARunningChildWritesTheThreadEventAlone (the coordinator's wake under a running child).* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings, each resume read off a session's event list resolved by probe name — 2026-09-02/batch2.json `sessK2.events.after-archived-subagent-message` (one, the confirmation at 00:09:51.927739Z); 2026-09-12-console-followups/setup.json `before-cleanup-main-events` (two), its threads list in approvals-network.json at 14:18:49.579Z (`alpha-approve-response`); 2026-09-12-child-lifecycle/setup.json `persisted-session-events-after-interrupt` (two, the second with no session idle), the same list the console reads in ui-network.json at 22:37:36.851Z (`old-viewer-idle-alpha`) and 22:38:12.412Z (`primary-idle-after-global-interrupt`) — confirmation `sevt_01UFLuBbv8ffy3jRmHG77BQB` at 22:36:33.010389Z behind `session.status_running` `sevt_015Nc7gNkrySJgYmYzYsnKzV` and the coordinator's `sevt_016y5YwnebNYAaHuZ3rYFXAc`, the session's `session.status_idle` `sevt_01AynFm4Z2f3vfTEQa3n6saK`, then the child's running `sevt_01DQnE1ivkB96LkYvkrzHDcG` at 22:36:33.346349Z — and its threads list at 22:36:34.647Z (`both-child-approvals`); 2026-09-19-self-hosted-docker/setup.json `multiagent.events.after-child-results` (two, tool results), its threads list `multiagent.threads.during-worker-execution`; 2026-09-19-self-hosted-latest-cli/setup.json `multiagent.events.after-child-results` (two, tool results), its threads list in ui-network.json at 15:22:18.779Z (`coordinator-threads-during-tools`); 2026-09-19-custom-mixed-tools/setup.json `custom-children.completed.events` (two, custom tool results), its threads list `custom-children.alpha-returned-beta-pending.threads`.* *Landed for: #793 (item 2, registered as deliberate).*
- **The environments list — one envelope on every beta lane, where the reference answers two dated lanes with others (#676)** — The reference's `GET /v1/environments` envelope follows the `anthropic-beta` header: `{data, next_page}` under `managed-agents-2026-04-01` and with no header, `{data, has_more, next_page}` under the console's `environments-2026-03-01`, and `{data, has_more, first_id, last_id}`, with no `next_page` at all, under the dated `environments-2025-11-01`. This platform answers `{data, next_page}` on all three, because it ignores the header: selecting a response shape from a request header is what the accept-and-ignore rule of the All endpoints entry forbids. A client of either dated lane that reads `has_more`, or pages on `first_id` and `last_id`, finds neither here. The console reads only `next_page` on this list, and the SDK's `PageCursor` reads nothing else, so neither notices. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings, every one a last page: 14 bodies `{data, next_page}` on `managed-agents-2026-04-01` or no header; 6 `{data, has_more, next_page}` on `environments-2026-03-01` (2026-09-12-console-141/ui-network.json idx 31 and 264 among them); 9 `{data, has_more, first_id, last_id}` on `environments-2025-11-01` (2026-09-04/batch2.json idx 162 and 2026-09-05/batch7.json idx 0, 34 and 41 among them).* *Evidence: internal/api/environments.go listEnvironments (one `nullPageJSON`, whatever the header); internal/api/page_test.go TestListTerminalPageEnvelope.*
- **The memories list — the recorded `prefixes` key, rendered as an empty array (confirmed 2026-09-02, 2026-09-05 and 2026-09-12, #78; aligned for #676)** — **Converged, no longer a divergence (#676).** Every memories list body the recordings hold carries a top-level `"prefixes": []` beside `data`: 48 bodies of 48 — 7 in 2026-09-02's `free_batch1.json`, 9 in 2026-09-05's `batch4.json` and `batch6.json`, 32 in 2026-09-12-console-141's `ui-network.json` — on pages with a cursor and without, under every filter probed, and at `depth=1`, where rolled-up paths arrive as `memory_prefix` items in `data` rather than in `prefixes`. The published result schema has no such key and closes the object on `data` and `next_page` (absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsListMemoriesResult.properties.prefixes). This platform rendered the published two until #676, which decided for the recording as #680 did for the work object's undeclared `actor: null`: the memories list now carries `"prefixes": []` on every page and at every depth, never null or omitted, while its `next_page` follows #676's rule. A typed client finds the key only under `JSON.ExtraFields`, where the SDK files a key it does not model (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — pagination.go PageCursor). What survives is the constant: the empty array is written, not derived, so **when the reference's `prefixes` would be non-empty is an inference** — no recording shows it, and 2026-09-05/FINDINGS6.md found no parameter that fills it — and a reference body that ever carried a value would differ again. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-12-console-141/ui-network.json idx 11 (a `depth=1` page whose one item is a `memory_prefix`), 2026-09-05/batch4.json idx 7 (a cursor page over a store past 2,000 memories) and batch6.json idx 7–13 (nested paths under every filter probed).* *Evidence: internal/api/page.go memoryPageJSON; internal/api/memories.go listMemories; internal/api/memories_test.go memoryRows and wantEmptyPrefixes (every memories list read in that file); internal/api/page_test.go TestListTerminalPageEnvelope (the exact keys on every page of a walk) and TestSDKPagersEndOnEitherTerminalEnvelope (the `[]` under `JSON.ExtraFields`).* *Tracked: #78 (a recorded memories list whose `prefixes` is not empty, and the request that produced it); landed for #676.*

## Inferences to confirm (INFERRED)

Assumptions about reference behavior, each awaiting the recording that would settle it. An
entry no recording could settle is not an inference — it is a divergence, and belongs above.

A recording that lands does not always empty an entry. Where it confirms the assumption and
this platform already matches, the entry leaves for the notes section above, titled "no
longer an inference" as `agent_toolset default permission policy` is. Where it confirms the
assumption and we differ **by choice**, it moves up to the divergences. Where it shows we
are simply wrong, the entry stays here until the alignment lands, with `Tracked:` naming the
issue that will land it rather than the recording that already arrived — the question is
answered, the code is not, and a bug is not a divergence to be argued.

- **grep — what the reference's searches find and how they answer (#827)** — The 2026-09-02 echo fixes the twelve properties' names, types and descriptions and grep's tool description, and no recorded call shows grep answering. Those texts are Claude Code's GrepTool's word for word, so where the recording is silent that tool is the closest evidence of what the reference runs, read from Claude Code 2.1.288's published binary: its GrepTool builds rg's argv as `--hidden`, a `--glob !<dir>` for each of `.git`, `.svn`, `.hg`, `.bzr`, `.jj` and `.sl`, then `--max-columns 500`, and its rg wrapper keeps the lines rg printed when rg exits 2. Two of those are adopted as inferred: grep searches hidden files and directories with the six version-control directories globbed out (`.gitignore` and rg's other ignore files still honoured, and a path that names an ignored file, or one inside `.git`, still searched); and rg's exit 2 beside matches — one unreadable file among them — answers with the matches and rg's messages after them, not a tool error, and so does one whose matches an `offset` paged away, as `no matches` and the messages, while an exit 2 with nothing found stays one. Ours beside the hidden walk: the memory sync's own files are left out, as the memory block's `grep -ri` line leaves them out — each store's `.anthropic-memory-store` marker by name, wherever it lies, and the sync's baselines in `/mnt/memory/.sync` wherever a search reaches them from a root above (one rooted at or inside that directory searches it, as rg does a hidden directory it is handed). That keeps a search that meets them in passing clean and is no boundary: a path that names them — the marker file, the baselines' directory, or the tree by another name, `/proc/self/root/mnt/memory` or a link to `/mnt/memory` — is searched, as bash reads it. rg 15.2.0 anchors a glob that begins with `/` at its working directory, not at the search root — its override matcher strips the working directory's bytes from each walked path — so that glob is spelled from the sandbox workdir, which the search enters, cleaned, before rg runs: `!/memory/.sync` from `/mnt`, `!//mnt/memory/.sync` from `/workspace`. Both come after the model's own glob, which cannot bring them back. `--max-columns 500`, which replaces a long line with rg's omission notice, is not adopted: the output caps and the spill already bound an answer. The descriptions name rg's flags, and grep passes each as the flag named, so the answers' shapes are rg's own — `-l`'s paths, `-c`'s `path:count`, content's `path:line:text` with `-`-marked context lines and `--` between groups, a single file's lines without its name, the binary notice. What rg does not settle is ours: rg is given the resolved path, so paths print absolute, as the SDK's client-side rg prints them; every call searches with `--sort=path`, paged or not, so an answer lists in one order every time, its pages are disjoint and together the whole list, and a page continues an unpaged answer the output cap cut short — at the cost of rg's parallel search: sorting runs rg's walk and search on one thread, so a large tree takes longer to search than it would unsorted; `--no-config` keeps an image's `RIPGREP_CONFIG_PATH` out of it; an answer without matches — rg's exit 1, or a page past the last line, which the search's own exit reports — is the text `no matches`, the SDK's, while a lone empty line is an answer, of no text; `head_limit` and `offset` count rg's output lines, `--` separators included, as the descriptions' `| tail -n +N | head -N` does, and say nothing when they cut; given both, `context` is the `-C` passed, and `-A`/`-B` go to rg beside it, which lets each override it for its own side; `head_limit` and `offset` must each be a whole number from 0 to 2³¹−1 in every mode, and the context counts are held to the same only in content mode, going unread outside it as their descriptions say; a call whose pattern, path, type and glob make a command past 120 KiB is refused before it runs, the command being one exec argument, which Linux caps near 128 KiB; and a failed search is a tool error carrying what rg printed, while a message rg printed beside an answer follows it. Only what the search's script printed is read. It prints a begin line before anything else and an end line as it exits, on stdout and on stderr, each carrying a 64-bit nonce of its own; what reaches the streams outside that frame — the banner an image's `BASH_ENV` file prints before it, an `EXIT` trap's words after it — is in no answer and no message, and a step of the script's own that stops it, such as a paging tool missing, says why inside the frame. Output with no begin line, or a stream the output cap did not cut with no end line, never came from the script whole: a tool error carrying what the sandbox printed, a banner included. The frame is no boundary against the sandbox's own processes, which can read the script, nonce and all, from the exec's argv. It keeps out what an image's startup prints, not what it changes: the startup file still runs for the script, as it runs for every exec the platform makes, and the platform's other scripts whose answers it parses read them from inside a frame of their own the same way (`sandbox.Frame`, #860; docs/self-hosted-security.md names them, and what stays unframed). `type`'s recorded description calls it "more efficient than include", a property the schema does not have; it is kept as recorded, and a model that sends `include` is refused with the list of the properties grep takes, so it can recover in one turn. The tool description is the echo's with one reading settled: its regex examples carry one backslash each (`function\s+\w+`). The echo spells two — the recording file holds eight per backslash, two JSON layers over the model's two — in prose whose quotes it had unescaped, which is the JSON spelling of one; Claude Code's binary carries one; and only one makes the examples the regexes they describe. Trailing whitespace the echo cannot show is left off, as `web_fetch`'s is. The reference's rg version, and so its type table, is unknown. *Evidence: internal/toolset/grep.go grepInput.query, vcsDirs, memoryGlobs, grepQuery.script and grepAnswer; internal/toolset/search.go newSearchFrame; internal/sandbox/frame.go Frame; internal/sandbox MaxCommandBytes and CheckCommand; internal/toolset/definitions.go grepDescription; TestGrepParameters, TestGrepAnswersBesideAnUnreadableFile, TestGrepKeepsWhatRipgrepSaidBesideTheAnswer, TestSearchesThroughAnImageBanner, TestSearchesFrameEverySearchWithANonceOfItsOwn, TestGrepLeavesOutTheMemorySyncState, TestGrepSearchesWhatAPathNamesInTheMemoryTree, TestMemoryGlobs, TestGrepSortsEveryCallByPath, TestSearchesRefuseACommandPastOneExecArgument, TestGrepDescriptionMatchesTheRecording, TestUnknownInputPropertiesAreRefused; the recorded schema and description, managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02/batch2.json probe `sessA.events.after-turn2`; Claude Code 2.1.288's binary, read with `strings` (the GrepTool's argv and its rg wrapper's exit-2 branch); the SDK's answers (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — search.go runRipgrep).* *Tracked: #78 (a recording of the reference answering grep in each output mode — its output shapes, path spelling and order, hidden and version-control files, an exit 2 beside matches, paging and the rg it runs).*

- **Work API auth — whether the wrong-environment 403 is uniform across the work routes** — The recording that settled the refusal itself (2026-09-02; the note above) exercised **`/work/poll` alone**: a live key aimed at another environment, and the same key aimed at an unknown environment id, both answering 403 `permission_error` "Token not authorized for this environment". This platform answers the other seven work routes — list, stats, get, update, ack, heartbeat and stop — identically, because all eight run one shared `workScope` check, and the tests pin that uniformity. **That the reference is uniform too is an inference**, and the recording cannot settle it even for the routes it touched, because the reference does not admit an environment key to all of them: `work.get` and `work.update` refused **the environment key itself** with a different 403 — an OAuth scope failure, "OAuth token does not meet scope requirement any_of(…)" — which lands before any environment comparison could, while `poll`, `stats` and `ack` admitted the same key. For those two routes the wrong-environment question therefore does not arise upstream at all. **That admission split is a mismatch of its own and belongs to #550**: the reference reaches `work.get` and `work.stats` with console org-auth where this platform reaches all eight with the environment key and none with a management or console lane, and a reviewer must not clear it on this entry's authority. What stays inferred here is correspondingly narrower — whether the routes the reference *does* admit an environment key to answer a wrong-environment key the way poll does. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording (`work.poll.wrong-env.cloud` and `work.poll.wrong-env.unknown-id` are its only wrong-environment probes; `work.get.*` and `work.update.*` carry the scope-requirement message against the environment key, while `work.get.via-console-org-auth` answers 200); internal/api/workapi.go workScope and its eight call sites; internal/api/workapi_test.go, internal/api/worklist_test.go.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference answers a wrong-environment key the same way on the work routes beyond poll that it admits an environment key to at all).*

- **Which console role may issue an environment key — `admin` here, unobserved there (plan 31 slice 2)** — The reference console offers a "Generate environment key" action, but which of its seven roles gate it is undocumented and was not captured in the 2026-08-10 recording that fixed the endpoint's path and payloads (plan 30). For **identity-authenticated callers**, this platform gates the whole environment-key surface — issue, revoke, **and list** — at `admin`; a management `x-api-key` is unaffected, because it is the deployment's root credential and carries no role at all (the role model exists for humans, and gating a key lane that has always meant full authority would be a different, unasked-for change). The gate rests on the local judgment that which hosts hold live credentials, and what they are named, is itself an inventory worth withholding from a read-only role. The reference's published role descriptions do say its *Developer* role can manage API keys, so a future recording may well justify relaxing this to `developer`; until one exists, the tighter gate is the one that fails safe. Both halves are testable against a recording: the roles the action is offered to, and whether the listing is gated separately from issuance. *Evidence: internal/api/server.go (the three consoleTokensPath/consoleRevokePath registrations at identity.RoleAdmin); internal/api/identitylane_test.go (TestIdentityLaneEnvironmentKeyRoutesRequireAdmin); docs/plan/30_environment-keys-console-issuance.md (the 2026-08-10 observation), docs/plan/31_console-sso-rbac.md* *Tracked: #78 (the reference's own gate stays unobserved — which of its seven roles are offered the key action, and whether listing is gated separately from issuance); landed for #56's SSO/RBAC half (plan 31 slice 2).*

- **The sessions token — when `secret` is populated, what it admits, and its scope (plan 36 slice 5, decision 15)** — Five readings of the reference the worker's behavior implies, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker, but no recording confirms — the first the kind recording item 10 settles, the rest readings of the worker's code no recording against the platform reaches. (1) `secret` is populated only for a work item whose session attaches a memory store: the SDK's field comment says "may be populated when polling for work" and the worker falls back to the environment key "otherwise" — a hedge compatible with always-on-poll and conditional alike, naming no condition; this platform mints only where the token buys something (scope decision 6). (2) The environment key is refused on the memory routes with a **401**: the worker's comment says those endpoints "reject the environment key" and names no status; here the memory routes take a management key or the sessions token, and an environment key gets the management lane's 401. (3) The token's admission matrix is the set of calls the worker makes with it — its own item's heartbeat and stop, its own session's read and events list/stream/send, the skill reads, and of the memory routes only the five its sync calls (the memories' list and create; a memory's get, update and delete) on stores its session attaches — and nothing else; since #804 a stop answers the item as GET renders it, `secret` null, so the token reads its own item through a stop although `GET …/work/{work_id}` refuses it, an asymmetry the reference shows for the environment key too, whose GET it refuses on scope (2026-09-02 `work.get.*`, `batch2.json` idx 253, 257 and 263) while that key's stops answer the full object (idx 295, 299 and 300); beyond the poll body that carried it, the token learns that way only the item's state and lifecycle stamps and any metadata patched since, less than the session read and events it is already admitted to; and what the reference answers a stop sent with a sessions token is unrecorded, since the recorder omits the `Authorization` header; whether the reference's token reaches more (the store's own lifecycle, other sessions in the environment, the rest of the work API) is unrecorded, and this platform refuses all of it (a sibling session and an unattached store 404 in the environment-key lane's shape). (4) One token per work item, superseded by a re-hand-out, rather than one per session: the worker reads it as "the bearer for this item's work lifecycle". (5) `read_only` is not enforced against the token: the worker enforces it itself (`ReadOnlyRoots`), as this platform's executor does on `cloud`, and the token's holder is the environment's own worker either way. A write through the token is attributed as `session_actor` (decision 6's sync lane, which the reference's actor union admits). How long a token outlives its item's stop request is *ours*: while the item is `stopping`, because its worker still has the stop to finish and may learn of it only about a minute later (the one recorded claim on `stopping` work was sent 58.9 s after its stop, and the worker's force stop at 60.1 s: 2026-09-19 custom-mixed-tools #23, #44 and #47), but past a minute from the request only on the item's heartbeat and stop, every other route answering 401: the item settles at a force stop, or at a poll of its environment once its lease and that minute have passed, and never if nothing polls, so stranded, the token can still finish the stop and a decommissioned worker's token cannot keep the session and its memories. The reference worker reads its session once its heartbeat has started and flushes its memory writes (`Cleanup`) before its force stop (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem), so a worker whose claim lands past that minute has its session read refused, which ends the item before its run or its memory stores are set up — there is nothing to flush — and its force stop admitted. It loses nothing: the refused read fails the item before its run starts, so no tool has written anything to flush. Once the item is `stopped`, a minute from the request, which the worker's post-stop flush (`Cleanup`, 30 s on a context of its own) needs, and which the finalizer waits out before it settles a wind-down, so its re-arm never runs beside the abandoned item's token; a force stop or a session-wide interrupt (`queue.CancelSession`) settles at once, so for the rest of that minute the old item's token can work beside the session's next item, as it could before #810. The reference token's lifetime is not observable from this side. *Evidence: the calls the token authorizes, the fallback, "reject the environment key" and ReadOnlyRoots, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem, the five `MemoryStores.Memories` calls, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.listMemories and SessionMemoryStores.pullAll and SessionMemoryStores.upload and SessionMemoryStores.deleteRemote, and Cleanup, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — memories.go SessionMemoryStores.Cleanup; internal/api/worktokenauth.go (the lane and its matrix), internal/worktoken/worktoken.go (Authenticate's join conditions); internal/api/worktoken_test.go TestSessionsTokenAdmissionMatrix, TestSessionsTokenJoinConditions, TestSessionsTokenOutlivesWindDownWhileStopping* *Tracked: #78 (recording item 10 — a `secret` from a real poll: its key set beyond `sessions_token`, and whether a storeless session's item carries one; and a stop sent with a decoded `sessions_token`).*
- **Which role each management route requires — the whole matrix is a local judgment (plan 31 slice 3)** — The reference documents that it has console roles and that they gate its UI, but not which role gates which API route; no recording captures it, and the entry above already records the environment-key case separately. So the matrix this platform enforces is inferred throughout, and three judgments in it are the ones a recording could most plausibly overturn. **Vault-credential reads are `viewer`** on the argument that they render sealed metadata only — the get and list queries select no `secret_ciphertext`, and the secret fields are split out of the auth document at parse time — where a reference that treats the whole credential resource as privileged would put them at its equivalent of `admin`. **The `admin` tier bounds secret access, not secret destruction**: a `developer` may archive or delete a vault, which nulls or cascades away every credential inside it, and may delete an environment, which cascades its keys — the tier gates who may read, write, mint or validate a secret, on the reasoning that destroying a container you own is ordinary resource ownership and discloses nothing, but a reference that gated the parent mutations too would be defensible and this platform would then be the looser of the two. **Session-resource create and rotation stay `developer`** although both seal a `github_repository` `authorization_token`, because mounting a repository into your own session is the session lifecycle that tier exists for and gating it at `admin` would put the platform's primary workflow behind the credential tier. All three are testable against a recording that exercises the reference console as each role. *Evidence: internal/api/server.go (the annotated route table); internal/api/vaultcredentials.go (the read queries), internal/api/vaults.go (deleteVault's ON DELETE CASCADE, archiveVault's secret_ciphertext = NULL), internal/api/sessionresources.go (the sealed authorization_token); internal/api/rolelane_test.go, internal/api/rolematrix_test.go; docs/plan/31_console-sso-rbac.md* *Tracked: #78 (the reference's own matrix stays unobserved — whether it gates vault-credential reads, the deletes that cascade a secret away, and session-resource create and rotation more tightly); landed for #56's SSO/RBAC half (plan 31 slice 2).*
- **MCP credential matching — what the normalization sentence does not reach (plan 29 slice 5)** — The documented rule is implemented verbatim: "scheme and host lowercased, default ports and trailing slashes stripped … a different path, subdomain, or non-default port does [prevent a match]". Everything the sentence does not name — path case, query, fragment, userinfo — is compared as given. Three readings it leaves open are ours, all settled toward *not* matching, since a false match sends a token to a server the credential was not registered for while a false miss dials unauthenticated and surfaces as an error: exactly one trailing slash is stripped; an IPv6 zone identifier is not folded, and two hosts are one when their IDNA A-labels agree — which still keeps `İ` and `i` apart, since they punycode to `xn--i-9bb` and `i` and Go resolves the two to different DNS names, while joining the spellings UTS46 defines as one name (`Ä`/`ä`, U+212A and `k`, `ſ` and `s`) that an ASCII fold left as two; and a tie between two spellings inside one vault is broken by credential id. Two normalizations the sentence does not name are ours and go the other way, because net/http applies them to the request itself so the two spellings reach one origin: an empty port (`host:`) is dropped, and a default port is compared as a number, so `:0443` is `:443`. Also ours: a vault that matched but whose credential cannot be used does not fall through to a later vault — the documented rule is about which vault *matches*. And the "no match dials unauthenticated" wording has one exception this platform does not close: an `mcp_servers` URL written with userinfo authenticates with the Basic header net/http derives from it, matched credential or not; a matched credential replaces that header rather than joining it, so the configured, rotatable one wins. *Evidence: platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/mcp-connector ("Provide authentication at session creation") and /vaults ("Runtime behavior"), fetched 2026-08-14; internal/vaultresolve/mcpurl.go, internal/vaultresolve/mcp.go and their tests.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle how the reference normalizes what its matching sentence leaves unnamed — path case, userinfo, a second trailing slash); landed for #45.*

- **Which failures are authentication failures (plan 29 slice 5)** — The two error types and their causes are documented; the **test** is not. This platform reads an authentication failure as **401** on the operation's most recent exchange, and nothing else — 403 is a decision about the request rather than about the credential, and 407 is a proxy's status, not the server's. A recording settled that matrix **at the dial**, and the 403 arm was wrong here until #572 narrowed it: five servers pointed at `httpbin.org/status/{code}` in one turn answered 401 → `mcp_authentication_failed_error` and 403, 407, 500 and 502 → `mcp_connection_failed_error`, so only 401 is an authentication failure and 403 is a connection failure, whose message *there* is `access forbidden`. **The wording is the reference's and not ours**: this platform stores the go-sdk's prose for the exchange that failed, cut to `scheme://host`, so a 403 reads as a failed `initialize` rather than as `access forbidden`. Only the type is matched, and deliberately — 407's message shows the reference's wording is not a thing to copy. What the reference does with a non-2xx on the **call** rather than the dial is unobserved, the probe having varied the status on servers that answered it to every request; this platform applies the dial's split there too — 401 an authentication failure, every other non-2xx a connection failure whether or not its body carries a JSON-RPC error (#641; the answering-an-MCP-call entry says why). 407's exclusion holds for a different reason than assumed — not "not authentication" but a generic connection error, whose message leaks an internal type name (`ToolboxUnavailableError`). Three further facts arrived with it: `retry_status` is `retrying` for all five, the 401 included, so an authentication failure is retried rather than terminal (which this platform already does); an upstream 500 is reported as `upstream server error (HTTP 502)`, identically to a real 502, so a client cannot recover the origin's status; and `session.error.error`'s key set is exactly `mcp_server_name, message, retry_status, type` — there is no `http_status` field. The 401's message also states that the dial finds its credential **by URL**, which cross-confirms the `mcp_server_url` normalization entry. Two rules make "most recent" precise, both ours: the streamable transport's teardown `DELETE` is excluded, since it is sent after the operation has already failed and its success would erase the refusal that caused it; and an exchange that produced no response at all clears the flag, since a transport failure after a refused probe is a connection that failed. Both matter because the go-sdk opens every connection with a `server/discover` it recovers from on any error. That covers the reference's first two causes together, since a server answers alike whether a token was sent or not. It is observed in this platform's own transport chain because the go-sdk carries the status only as prose (checked against go-sdk v1.7.0 — mcp/streamable.go streamableClientConn.checkResponse), which a version bump may reword. A fourth cause is ours with no wire counterpart: a credential that matched and could not be resolved is an authentication failure and the dial is not made — an anonymous dial the operator believes was authenticated would hide the misconfiguration. `retry_status` is `retrying` for both types, per the connection-failure entry. *Evidence: platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/mcp-connector ("Handle connection and authentication failures", fetched 2026-08-14); internal/mcp/authstatus.go, internal/executor/mcpcred.go, internal/executor/mcpexec.go (mcpFailure) and their tests.* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probe `session.events.list.mcp-httpbin-statuses`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §D.* *Tracked: #78 (which exchange of an operation the reference reads, and what it reports for a non-2xx on the call, are still unobserved, since the probe varied the status at the dial and not the exchange); landed for #45, #572, #641.*

- **OAuth refresh — when it runs and what a failed one means (plan 29 slice 5b)** — That refresh is platform-performed, and its grammar (RFC 6749, the three `token_endpoint_auth` arms, RFC 8707 `resource`), are documented; when it runs and what happens when it fails are not. Ours: at the dial, and only there — a credential is refreshed when its `expires_at` is past or within a minute of it, never in reaction to the server's 401, so one expiry costs one exchange rather than a refused call and a retry. The minute absorbs both the skew that makes `expires_at` approximate and the flight time of the dial it is about to authorize. An expired credential that *cannot* refresh — no refresh block, no refresh token — still sends the token it has, since `expires_at` is the issuer's estimate and not its decision; that is the reference's `no_refresh_token` shape carried onto the dial path. A refusal from the token endpoint is `mcp_authentication_failed_error`, and so is every other way a 2xx fails to yield a grant — an error document, a body that never finished, one past this platform's 1 MiB cap — and an endpoint at an address the dial guard will not dial. None of those is a retry away from working, and a retryable verdict on them has nowhere to surface: the work item faults, is reclaimed, writes no catalog row, and repeats with the operator told nothing. What does retry is the issuer having a moment: unreachable, 5xx, or one of the three 4xx that are about timing rather than the grant (408, 425, 429). The shape is the `invalid`/`unknown` split the `mcp_oauth_validate` probe makes, with two deliberate departures: those three named 4xx, because a probe's verdict is advice an operator reads while this one settles a tool call for good, and the guard refusal, which the probe reports as a plain connection failure on purpose so it cannot become an internal-host oracle.
