Rotation is stored under a compare-and-set on the ciphertext the resolution read, best-effort: the dial uses the token it was issued whether or not the write lands, and a refusal that follows another resolution's rotation — the row's sealed bytes having changed under this dial — reads that rotation back instead of reporting a failure, which is what keeps an issuer's single-use refresh tokens from turning a concurrent dial into a permanent error. **One dead end is accepted**: a grant that names no lifetime — or one so long this platform will not believe it — stores a null expiry rather than keeping one already in the past, which would refresh on every later dial; that credential then gets no further proactive refresh, and recovering after the server refuses its token needs the operator, because nothing here refreshes in reaction to a 401. *Evidence: platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/vaults ("Runtime behavior", fetched 2026-08-14); internal/oauthrefresh, internal/vaultresolve/mcprefresh.go and their tests.* **One arm is recorded, and the method this entry implied for the other turns out to be impossible.** A credential cannot be seeded with a past `expires_at` at all: `POST /v1/vaults/{v}/credentials` with an `mcp_oauth` credential dated 2020 is a **400 `auth.expires_at must be in the future.`**, so the refresh window has to be reached by dating the credential seconds ahead and waiting. **This platform accepts what that 400 refuses**: `parseMCPOAuthCreate` reads `expires_at` for its format alone, so a credential can be created here already expired, which then refreshes on its very first dial (#589). Reached that way, with the access token past `expires_at` and the credential's token endpoint answering 400, the MCP call **still succeeded**: the platform sent the stale access token, the server answered on its own terms, `is_error` was `false`, and **no `session.error` was emitted**. So a failing token endpoint is silent on the wire, and the credential is matched by URL and its token does reach the server. **Ours is loud instead**: the same shape answers `mcp_authentication_failed_error`, because `refreshIfDue` marks a token-endpoint refusal `ErrCredentialUnusable` and the executor answers the call rather than attempting it with the token in hand — the argument for which is above, and it is worth noting that the can-not-refresh path here already does what the reference did, sending the token it has. Whether the failed-refresh path should agree with it, and so with the reference, is #590. What the probe cannot separate — whether a refresh was attempted and failed quietly, or never attempted at all — needs a token endpoint whose request log can be read, which is the same missing piece that blocks the MCP client-transport entry. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `cred.create.oauth-te-*` and `sess.create.oauth-dial-te400`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §D.* *Tracked: #589 (refuse a past `expires_at` at create, as the reference does), #590 (decide whether a failed refresh falls through to the stale token as the reference did, or keeps answering `mcp_authentication_failed_error`), #78 (whether the reference refreshes at the dial or in reaction to a 401, and which token-endpoint refusals it retries — both need a readable token endpoint, not another recording of this shape); landed for #45.*

- **Resolved toolset-config echo — what `configs[]` holds, and when it resolves (plan 29 slice 1)** — That the echo *is* resolved is documented, not inferred: the SDK marks `configs`/`default_config` and every `enabled`/`permission_policy` under them required on both toolset kinds' response types (three it calls "Resolved"), and the MCP-connector guide gives the two kinds one shape. Two readings around it are **ours**. **What `configs[]` contains:** the entries the client supplied, each resolved — not one entry per tool (impossible for the MCP kind, so both kinds share one rule); whether the reference instead lists every built-in on an `agent_toolset_20260401` echo is **unobserved**. It binds every surface shipping one of these objects, including a `session.updated` event's `agent` (`internal/api/sessions.go`, `materializedAgentSnapshot`). **When it resolves:** at render, not at write — unobservable from the wire. Stored rows keep the client's own bytes, so no backfill was needed, updates merge against what the client actually sent, and the default policies stay flippable constants (`toolset.DefaultAgentToolsetPolicy` / `DefaultMCPToolsetPolicy` — see the agent_toolset-default entry) rather than values frozen into old rows. **One place resolution is persisted, deliberately:** the `session.updated` payload is built before the event is appended, so that `events` row holds the resolved agent forever — that is what an event is, the record of what the API answered at that moment, and without it the same update answers twice in two shapes. Two consequences are accepted, not denied: an event appended before this slice keeps its sparse agent, and flipping a default-policy constant later leaves old events showing the policy in force when they were appended while the session resource re-renders under the new one. The invariant is therefore "resolution never reaches the agent and session **resources**", not "resolution is never written down". Wire-visible: a supplied value is echoed **as stored** (omissions filled, nothing validated), except an unknown *key* nested inside `default_config`/`configs[]`, which the rebuild drops (one at the toolset object's own level is kept) — both reach only rows written before this slice, since validation now rejects an unknown key at either level; and duplicate `configs[]` entries naming one tool merge field-by-field, last winning, echoing once in first-appearance order. **Since the SDK bump (plan 36 slice 0) a built-in entry also echoes `type`, unconditionally — request or no request.** That bump replaced the single resolved-config struct, absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfig, with an eight-variant union that dispatches on `type`, since anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfigUnion, so an echo carrying only `name` leaves a typed client with no variant at all; rendering it is the response schema's own shape rather than a guess. The two web variants' new `allowed_domains`/`blocked_domains`/`max_content_tokens`/`user_location` are *not* echoed, because they cannot be stored — those keys are refused on input, which is the web-tools entry above. *Evidence: BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfigUnion and its `AsAny()`, which switches on `u.Type` and returns nil without it, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfigUnion and BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfigUnion.AsAny, and the bash variant: `name` and `type` are both `constant.Bash` discriminators the generator always marshals, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsBashToolConfig; the claim this clause used to make, that the resolved-config types came through the SDK bump of plan 35 slice 0 byte-identical, the inter-pin diff touching no toolset type, held at that bump's pin (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.63.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfig) and is what the split ended (absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfig); platform.claude.com managed-agents/mcp-connector ("supports the same `default_config` and `configs` shape as the built-in agent toolset", fetched 2026-08-08); internal/toolset/materialize.go (its Materialize / materializeConfigs comments carry the derivations cut from here); internal/toolset/materialize_test.go TestMaterializeResolvesBothToolsetKinds (merge, malformed leaf, dropped nested key); internal/api/mcptoolset_test.go TestToolsetConfigsEchoResolved, TestResolvedEchoDoesNotReachTheStore (the three stored reads) and TestSessionUpdatedEventCarriesResolvedAgent* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether an agent_toolset echo lists every built-in or only the entries the client supplied); landed for #45.*

- **`mcp_toolset` nested-shape rejection — the statuses and messages (plan 29 slice 1)** — An `mcp_toolset` entry gets the eager shape check its built-in twin has had since #26: an unknown key anywhere in the `default_config` / `configs[]` / `permission_policy` nest, or a `permission_policy.type` outside the two-value enum, is a 400 rather than a stored entry carrying the #26 fail-open into the kind whose default gates on a human. That the reference rejects any of this is unobserved; its statuses and strings certainly are. Ours name the field's path where the raw walk reaches it — the unknown-key and leaf-shape refusals; the `permission_policy.type` refusal and the typed-decode backstop for a member repeated within one object name none. Rejecting that conflict at all is ours, as is treating a repeated member as last-wins everywhere it is not one. Three further refusals are ours, each closing a shape that could be stored and then echoed against the response schema: a `configs[]` entry that is not an object; one whose `name` is absent, empty or null; and an explicit `null` for `enabled` or `permission_policy`. A name's **length** is unchecked, so the SDK's documented 1–128 bound binds only at its lower edge — the treatment #66 gives the MCP server name's 1–255. The rung binds **stored** entries too (`validateAgentSpec` re-runs it on every resolve), so an `mcp_toolset` written before it existed now fails at session create. **Two asymmetries with the built-in kind are recorded rather than harmonized**: policies validate eagerly here and lazily there (only for tools a config leaves enabled), so a *disabled* built-in tool may still carry a policy type the wire has no arm for and still show it in that entry's echo (unchanged from before materialization, which echoed the same bytes raw); and the null and `name` refusals bind the MCP arm only — the built-in arm has accepted both since #26, and extending them there would change shipped accept/reject behavior outside #45's scope, so that convergence waits for its own change and recording. No per-tool `name` is checked against a known set on either arm; for the built-in kind, whose eight names the SDK spells out one params variant at a time, an unrecognized name is therefore accepted while `resolveToolset` ignores it — pre-existing, and materialization is what makes that laxity newly visible, so it declines to speak for them: a `configs[]` element with an unknown name, no name, or one that is not an object is echoed exactly as supplied, in place. **One key joined the built-in arm's accepted set with the SDK bump of plan 36 slice 0: `type`, and only when it equals `name`.** The SDK gives every built-in tool config a `type` beside `name` (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsBashToolConfigParams.Type and BetaManagedAgentsEditToolConfigParams.Type and BetaManagedAgentsReadToolConfigParams.Type and BetaManagedAgentsWriteToolConfigParams.Type and BetaManagedAgentsGlobToolConfigParams.Type and BetaManagedAgentsGrepToolConfigParams.Type and BetaManagedAgentsWebFetchToolConfigParams.Type and BetaManagedAgentsWebSearchToolConfigParams.Type and BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfigUnion.AsAny) — optional on the request (`json:"type,omitzero"`, only `name` being required) and the discriminator the response union dispatches on — so a client shaped by that SDK may send it: the SDK's own generated example does, and `ant` passes `--tool` JSON through raw, so it sends whatever the operator's value carries. Accepting it is the minimum that keeps such an `agents create` from a 400; requiring equality is **INFERRED**, because nothing in the SDK or the docs says what the reference does when the two disagree, and the two fields are one discriminator in the schema — a config claiming to be two tools has no resolution here. A mismatch answers with the unknown-key 400's shape. *Evidence: the request types the accepted key set comes from (`BetaManagedAgentsMCPToolsetParams`, `MCPToolConfigParams`, whose `name` reads "1-128 characters", `MCPToolsetDefaultConfigParams`, the two policy params; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsMCPToolsetParams and BetaManagedAgentsMCPToolConfigParams and BetaManagedAgentsMCPToolsetDefaultConfigParams and BetaManagedAgentsAlwaysAllowPolicyParam and BetaManagedAgentsAlwaysAskPolicyParam) and, for the built-in arm, the eight `BetaManagedAgents<Tool>ToolConfigParams` variants of `BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfigParamsUnion` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfigParamsUnion) with their `name`/`type` constants, e.g. for bash (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsBashToolConfigParams) — the split deleted the single `AgentToolConfigParams` whose `Name` enum this clause used to cite as the source of the eight-name set (absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolConfigParams); internal/toolset/materialize.go, whose comments hold the derivations cut from here (`ValidateMCPToolset`: raw walk versus typed backstop; `checkMCPConfigFields`: null; `materializeConfigs`: echoing in place); internal/toolset/definitions.go `resolveToolset` (the lazy built-in policy check); internal/api/wire.go `parseTools`, `validateAgentSpec`; internal/toolset/materialize_test.go TestValidateMCPToolset, TestMaterializeResolvesBothToolsetKinds; internal/api/mcptoolset_test.go TestMCPToolsetShapeValidated, TestStoredSpecsAreRevalidatedOnResolve* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference rejects these nested shapes at all and with what status and message, and what it does with a built-in config whose `type` disagrees with its `name`); landed for #45.*

- **Oversized-output spill — path convention and preview text** — The spill itself is reference-documented ("written to a file in the sandbox … a truncated preview with the file path"), but neither the path convention nor the preview wording is: ours are `/tmp/tool_outputs/<tool_use_id>.txt` and `[output truncated; full output written to …]`. The full record — including the deliberate no-web-spill and no-read-spill halves and the 1MiB Exec-retention bound — lives in the "Oversized tool output" entry in the Deliberate-divergences section above; needs a recording against a real managed-agents endpoint. *Evidence: internal/toolset/toolset.go (spillDir, Runner.spill); platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/tools (fetched 2026-08-01)* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the reference's own spill path convention and the wording of its truncated preview).*

- **user.interrupt — what it writes for the calls it abandons, and where** — The docs pin the interrupt's outcome (it "stops the agent mid-execution", and the interrupted turn "ends with a `session.status_idle` event whose `stop_reason` is `end_turn` … there is no stop reason specific to interruption") but say nothing about the `tool_use` events the ended turn leaves outstanding. Ours answers each with an `is_error:true` result carrying a fixed text, in the family that matches the call — `agent.tool_use` → `agent.tool_result`, `agent.custom_tool_use` → `user.custom_tool_result`, `agent.mcp_tool_use` → `agent.mcp_tool_result` — stamped `processed_at` at synthesis. Answering them at all is forced (the log is append-only, and an unanswered `tool_use` makes every later replay a request the model protocol rejects). **The `agent.tool_use` arm is now recorded rather than inferred** (2026-09-02): interrupting a live `bash` call, the reference answered it with an `agent.tool_result` carrying `is_error: true`, exactly one text block, a `tool_use_id` naming the tool-use *event* id, and a populated `processed_at` — all four of which this platform already wrote — and with the text "Tool execution was interrupted before completion. Please retry.", which `events.InterruptResultText` now copies verbatim in place of its own wording. What stays inferred is the other two arms, which no recording covers and which reuse that one constant, plus the two consequences below. Two consequences are also ours: a gated call answered this way stops blocking its `requires_action` gate, and a `user.tool_confirmation` naming an already-answered call is rejected 400 (allowing it would let a denial write a second result for one call). **Ordering is recorded too, and now matched** (landed for #539): the reference wrote the synthesized `agent.tool_result` *before* the `user.interrupt` that caused it, by list position and by `processed_at` alike (`agent.tool_use` 00:05:42.722212Z, `agent.tool_result` 00:05:45.383003Z, `user.interrupt` 00:05:45.426111Z), and the live 2026-09-12-child-lifecycle/streams/ui-01.sse shows the same. This platform now writes an interrupt's commit in processing order (docs/plan/56_processing-order.md): the results it synthesizes and any `span.outcome_evaluation_end` it writes, then `user.interrupt`, then the idle pair, where it used to append every event the client posted first and its own reaction after. Replay is unaffected either way (the results and a message merge into one user turn with results first, as the Messages API requires). What the recording still does not reach is the redirect batch specifically, since the interrupt it captured carried no following message. This platform writes a one-send `[interrupt, message]` redirect as the results, `user.interrupt`, the idle pair, `session.status_running`, `session.thread_status_running`, then `user.message`, whichever order the two were posted in: that is the rule's prediction, the message following its running pair as every recorded message wake does, so where the reference puts that message against the results is INFERRED. The rest of a send's layout is the rule applied where nothing is recorded, so it is ours: a client's own answers stay where they were received, among the send's interrupts in receipt order, being consumed on receipt — an answer precedes the results only of an interrupt received after it, and an `[interrupt, answer]` send writes the interrupt's results, the interrupt and its idle before the answer — though one thread's answers fill those places in the order its calls are processed, and a denial's result follows its confirmation, ahead of the answers the denial lets the ordered tool flow go on to, unless the send resumes the primary, whose denial results follow its running pair as recorded ("Session threads — the primary thread …", below); a denial and an interrupt of the denied call's thread settle the call in receipt order — a denial received first answers it, with its `deny_message`, and the interrupt answers only the thread's other calls, while a confirmation received after the interrupt, or any allow beside one, is consumed on receipt with nothing left to do, since the interrupt answers its call (the denial used to be swallowed in either order); a thread two interrupts of one send reach is ended by the first received, its results and idle around that one; a notice to a coordinator follows the coordinator's running event whichever arm woke it, in receipt order with a message its woken turn also reads; and what nothing in the commit consumes follows everything the send processes, wherever it was posted: a message to a primary already running, a notice to a coordinator left parked, and an answer the ordered tool flow does not reach, queued behind an earlier call of its thread that still waits. That last one sits where the reference keeps it, since a result for a later custom call stays unprocessed at the tail of the reference's list until the earlier call's answer arrives (2026-09-19-custom-order-followup `setup[80]` and `[83]`); only its place beside a wake in the same send is ours. The stamps agree with that order within the commit: every interrupt is stamped on receipt once its arms have synthesized its results, as the reference stamps one (#793), no append lets a batch's stamps run backwards, and an answer the settlement processes, stamped after the append, is held inside its list slot, while a queued one stays unstamped. The POST echo keeps the posted order throughout. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/events-and-streaming (interrupt + redirect in one `events[]` batch, `end_turn` outcome); internal/events/toolflow.go (toolUseAnswer, InterruptResults, UnconfirmedAskEvents, ValidateToolConfirmations); internal/events/log.go (AppendInTx); internal/api/events.go (processingOrder, interruptThreadInTx, sendSessionEvents); internal/api/interrupt_test.go, processing_order_test.go (TestTheFirstInterruptReceivedEndsTheThread, TestAnAnswerQueuedBehindAnEarlierCallGoesToTheTail, TestAnAnswerBehindAConfirmedCallIsPlacedByWhatTheConfirmationSays, TestADenialIsListedAheadOfTheAnswersItUnlocks, TestAnAnswerBesideAnInterruptOfItsThreadIsConsumedInItsSlot, TestADenialAndAnInterruptOfItsThreadAnswerTheCallInReceiptOrder), answerclamp_test.go, interruptstamp_test.go; docs/plan/13_user-interrupt.md* *Tracked: #78 (the agent-toolset arm's family, shape, wording and ordering are all settled now; a recording would still settle the custom-tool and MCP arms, and the ordering inside a one-send interrupt-plus-message redirect batch; #429 tracks the family choice specifically); landed for #68, and for #539's ordering.*

- **Resource lifecycle facts nobody had recorded, because nobody had needed to destroy this much (recorded 2026-09-03)** — Tearing down a recording wave reached endpoints and refusals no earlier pass had, and four are worth keeping. **A `terminated` session is hidden from the default `GET /v1/sessions` listing** — not archived, simply excluded by status — so anything that reconciles resources by listing them misses it, which is how that session's own teardown leaked; this platform now hides one too: its default listing drops the `terminated` rows beside the archived ones, so `include_archived=true` reaches them — on a parameter whose name then covers a status that is not archival, the reference's own oddity adopted along with its behavior. **That an explicit `statuses[]=terminated` replaces that restriction rather than being narrowed by it is INFERRED**: the sweep probed a default list and an `include_archived` one, never a status-filtered list. The alternative, a scope no filter reaches past the way `include_archived=false` is scoped, was weighed and rejected — it would leave a value both the SDK's types and the spec it bundles admit for `statuses[]` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaSessionListParams.Statuses; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsSessionStatus.enum) matching nothing without a flag neither description mentions, and would collapse *terminated by failure* and *archived* into one answer only `include_archived=true` can ask, where the reference publishes no rule against combining the two for sessions as it does for deployments. Since #710 every session this platform calls `terminated` is an archived one the archive clause already excludes, so the alignment bites only on a stored `terminated`, which nothing writes until #577 lands. **Agents cannot be deleted, only archived**: `DELETE /v1/agents/{id}` is a **404 `not_found_error` "not found"** — lower-case, where the files route says "Not found" — while `POST /v1/agents/{id}/archive` is a 200 with `archived_at` set; sessions, environments, vaults, memory stores and files all accept `DELETE`, and this platform likewise serves no agent-delete route. **Revoking an environment key is `POST /api/oauth/organizations/{org}/environments/{env}/tokens/{id}/revoke` → 204** with an empty body: `DELETE` on the token's own path is a 404, as are `DELETE /api/oauth/organizations/{org}/tokens/{id}` and `/api/oauth/tokens/{id}`, and the listing `GET …/tokens` → `{data, pagination}` returns rows of `created_at, expires_at, id, name` — **the token value is returned only at issuance, never on a list**, which is what the key-expiry entry assumes and now has evidence for. And **the reference's refusal for an environment key is route-dependent**: one key drew four answers in one pass — **403** `permission_error` "OAuth token does not meet scope requirement any_of(…)" on `GET /v1/skills` and `GET /v1/skills/{id}`, **401** `authentication_error` "Authentication failed" on `GET /v1/agents`, and **200** on `GET /v1/skills/{id}/versions` and on a version's content, so admission is per route rather than per resource family. A key revoked at teardown then answered **401** `authentication_error` "Invalid bearer token" on that versions route. What the bytes do **not** support is the reading this wave was first written up with, that a revoked key is distinguishable on the wire from a live one out of scope: the two states were never probed on one route, and the revoked key cannot be tied to the live one, three keys having been revoked and the archive redacting which answered. The route-dependence is the fact; the distinguishability is not. Where each stands against our own source, since recorded is not compared: **the token value returned only at issuance** matches, over a revoke route answering 204 (`handleNoContent`) and a listing rendering exactly `id`, `name`, `created_at` and `expires_at`. **Agents undeletable** matches in capability and not on the wire — `DELETE /v1/agents/{id}` answers a **405** `invalid_request_error` here, that path sitting in the method-less fallback whose handler answers `errMethodNotAllowed`, against the reference's 404 on the one route the wave probed sixteen times. No typed client can reach the difference: neither the SDK's `BetaAgentService` (absent at anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaAgentService.Delete) nor the `ant` CLI's `beta:agents` command (absent at anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — betaagent.go betaAgentsDelete) carries a delete, so only a hand-rolled request sees it — which is an argument about how much the mismatch costs, not a reason to leave it untracked, and #592 now owns choosing between matching the 404 and keeping the 405 on purpose. Neither side has a house rule about it — the same wave recorded the reference answering a console route's unsupported `DELETE` a 405 `invalid_request_error` — so this is one route's difference and not a style. **The route-dependent refusal** now matches on all four routes (#840). On the revoked key's own route the envelope matches: `isSkillReadPath` admits the versions subtree, and a dead key answers **401** `authentication_error` — `invalid environment key` for an expired or unknown key, and for a revoked one "OAuth access token has been revoked.", the sentence later recordings give a key known to be revoked (*Environment key revocation and expiry*). Which dead state drew "Invalid bearer token" here the archive does not say, so that sentence is adopted for none of them. `/v1/agents` falls to the management lane, which now recognises a live environment key and answers it the reference's **401** `authentication_error` "Authentication failed"; `GET /v1/skills` and `GET /v1/skills/{id}` answer it the reference's **403** `permission_error` "OAuth token does not meet scope requirement any_of(…)", the second no longer admitted (*Skill reads on the environment-key lane*). Those two 403s are the one place this platform speaks the reference's scope vocabulary, as recorded words with no scope model behind them; the model is #550's gap, here as on the work API. What the reference answers a revoked key on `/v1/agents` was never recorded, so the revoked sentence answered there is inferred (*A revoked environment key on a management route*). What this platform *does* separate by status — a live key naming another environment on the work API, 403 `permission_error` — it separates **in order to match**, at the cost of an anti-oracle property the work-API-auth entry above records giving up; `authenticateEnvironmentKey` now tells a revoked key apart as well, and still folds an expired key with an unknown one. **Terminated sessions absent from the default listing** was the mismatch that kept this entry here and the alignment has landed; what holds it in this section now is #592's undecided 405 and the `statuses[]` inference above; the other facts stay beside them because one recording's facts are worth more together than split three ways, and each is named rather than left for a reader to clear on this entry's authority. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `envkey.revoke.*`, `envkey.dead-key-verify`, `sweep*.*` and `verify.final.*`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §K.* *Tracked: #592 (`DELETE /v1/agents/{id}`: match the reference's 404, or keep the fallback's 405 deliberately), #78 (a recording would settle whether an explicit `statuses[]=terminated` replaces the default listing's status restriction or is narrowed by it); landed for #574's alignment; landed for #840.*

- **/v1/vaults + credentials — statuses and semantics the docs imply but no recording confirms** — The documented caps reject as this platform's own 400 messages (the reference's exact statuses/strings are unobserved); the ≤20-credential cap counts *active* credentials only (archiving frees capacity, matching "archived keys are freed"); the `secret_name` / `mcp_server_url` uniqueness namespaces are per-field — the two MCP variants share the `mcp_server_url` namespace, environment variables their own — with a duplicate active key a 409; a credential create on an archived vault is a 400. Two of those are recorded now, with their words, which this platform answers since #540: an MCP credential at a URL another active one holds is a 409 `invalid_request_error` "A credential already exists for this MCP server URL.", and a create in an archived vault a 400 "Vault is archived." (2026-09-03 batch1 `cred.create.mcp-oauth-with-refresh-basic`, `cred.create.in-archived-vault`); the recorded 409s for a URL differing only by a trailing slash or a host's case are *MCP credential matching*'s, and an environment variable's duplicate keeps ours; vault delete is a hard delete that cascades to the vault's credentials (a credential cannot outlive its vault — whether the reference instead refuses to delete a non-empty vault is unobserved); vault archive purges and archives every credential with it. *Evidence: internal/api/vaults.go, internal/api/vaultcredentials.go; migration 0011_vaults.sql (partial-unique index, ON DELETE CASCADE); internal/api/vaults_test.go TestCredentialUniquenessAndCap and TestCredentialWritesInAnArchivedVault* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the caps' reject statuses and whether the reference refuses to delete a non-empty vault); landed for #50.*
- **allowed_hosts wildcard — the matching rule is recorded; what stays inferred is whether the runtime guard shares it (plan 12 slice 4)** — The shared egress host matcher admits `*.example.com` for a subdomain but never the apex `example.com`, which **matches** the SDK's recorded behavior — "a `*.`-prefixed entry matches any subdomain of the named domain but not the domain itself" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betavaultcredential.go BetaManagedAgentsLimitedCredentialNetworkingResponse.AllowedHosts), so apex exclusion is reference-recorded, not our inference. Depth was the residual and is one no longer: the SDK's word "subdomain" does not pin single-label against any-depth, and the 2026-09-03 recording answered it directly (below). What keeps this entry in the INFERRED section is narrower and stated at the end — every probe reads the *create-time* admission matcher, and that the reference's runtime sandbox egress guard applies the same rule is likely and unproven (INFERRED, #78). Request-side hardening the entry-grammar doc does not cover: a request host carrying an empty label (`.example.com`, `a..example.com`) never matches, so the `*.` boundary cannot be slipped by an out-of-grammar host — the API validates *entries*, this guards the *query* side. The same matcher decides both a credential's `allowed_hosts` and an environment's networking allow-list. The SDK's response schema adds "An entry matches the request host exactly", which plan 43 (#609) reads as *matches its canonical form*: entry and request host are both converted to their IDNA A-label before comparison, so one name in two spellings is one host and two names are never merged. What "exactly" excludes — a different subdomain, an extra label — is unchanged. *Evidence: the entry grammar (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betavaultcredential.go BetaManagedAgentsLimitedCredentialNetworkingParams.AllowedHosts), the wildcard/apex semantics (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betavaultcredential.go BetaManagedAgentsLimitedCredentialNetworkingResponse.AllowedHosts); internal/egress/hostset.go (HostSet.Match, hasEmptyLabel); internal/egress/hostset_test.go TestHostSetMatch* **The depth reading is confirmed, at no model cost.** The create-time MCP host gate is a pure host-pattern test — a session create either is or is not admitted — so it interrogates the matcher without a turn. Under `allowed_hosts: ["*.deepwiki.com"]` the reference admits `mcp.deepwiki.com` (one label), `a.b.deepwiki.com` (two) and `a.b.c.deepwiki.com` (three), and refuses the apex `deepwiki.com`. So `*.X` spans **any** label depth and excludes the apex, which is what this platform's matcher does. The load-bearing row is the fifth: `mcp.notdeepwiki.com` is **refused** even though that string does end in `deepwiki.com`, which proves the reference requires the dot boundary rather than testing a naive suffix — the same property `hasEmptyLabel` and the entry grammar defend here. One caveat is kept deliberately rather than glossed: what these probes read is the **create-time admission matcher**. That the reference's runtime sandbox egress guard applies the same rule is likely and unproven, and it is the only reason this entry is not simply closed. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `sess.create.mcp-host-wildcard-gate` and the four host variants beside it, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §E.* *Tracked: #78 (whether the runtime sandbox egress guard shares the create-time matcher's wildcard rule); landed for #50.*
- **POST /v1/sessions (create) — vault_ids attachment error shape** — Session create accepts top-level `vault_ids` (plan 12 slice 3); each id must name an existing, unarchived vault. A `vault_ids` entry that is malformed, names a missing vault, or names an archived one fails the create with a 400 `invalid_request_error` in the standard envelope — the docs say only that a session "referencing" such a vault fails in the future, not the create-time status/shape, so the 400 is ours (INFERRED). Attachment is create-time-only; `vault_ids` on update stays rejected, which is wire-faithful: the SDK's update params carry the field but document it "Not yet supported; requests setting this field are rejected. Reserved for future use." We reject *any* presence of the key, including an explicit `null`; since the SDK's `omitzero` never transmits a null/empty `vault_ids`, the reference's response to a bare `null` is unobserved and rejecting it is the conservative reading of "unsupported" (INFERRED). Duplicate ids within `vault_ids` are preserved and echoed as given — we do not mutate the client's list, and whether the reference dedupes is unobserved; it is harmless either way, since resolution is first-vault-wins. Read-time credential resolution has since landed (`internal/vaultresolve`, first-vault-wins) — see the resolution entry below. *Evidence: "credentials the agent can use" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasession.go BetaSessionNewParams.VaultIDs), "Not yet supported; … rejected" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasession.go BetaSessionUpdateParams.VaultIDs); internal/api/sessions.go parseVaultIDs/validateAttachedVaults; internal/api/sessions_test.go TestSessionVaultAttachment* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the create-time reject status, the response to an explicit null on update, and whether the reference dedupes); landed for #50.*
- **Vault credential resolution — unsafe `secret_name` skipped at injection, not rejected at create (plan 12 slice 4/D5)** — An `environment_variable` credential's `secret_name` is validated at create only as a non-empty string; whether the reference restricts it is unobserved, so we add no create-time 400 (staying wire-permissive). Instead, read-time resolution injects a `secret_name`→placeholder pair only when the name is safe to set in the sandbox; an unsafe name is skipped rather than injected — the "a bad credential surfaces [as a session error] and does not block the session" arm of the docs' resolution model (the session-error emission itself lands with the gate). Two cases are skipped: a name that is not a valid env-var name (`sandbox.ValidEnvName`, `[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*`), because an invalid `Spec.Env` key would fault every provision and reclaim-loop; and a platform-**reserved** name (`sandbox.ReservedEnvName`: `PATH` and the loader/shell hooks `LD_PRELOAD`/`LD_LIBRARY_PATH`/`LD_AUDIT`/`BASH_ENV`/`ENV`/`IFS`), because `PATH` is a valid env-var name yet injecting an opaque value over it would clobber binary resolution and break the bootstrap and every tool exec — a reclaim-loop reachable through a credential the create API accepts. When several attached vaults carry the same `secret_name`, the first vault in `vault_ids` order wins (the docs' "first vault with a match"). An archived vault contributes nothing (the query guards the vault's own `archived_at`, not only the credential's). *Evidence: internal/vaultresolve/vaultresolve.go (Bindings); internal/executor/executor.go (sandboxEnv); internal/sandbox/sandbox.go (ValidEnvName, ReservedEnvName); internal/vaultresolve/vaultresolve_test.go, internal/executor/vaults_test.go, internal/sandbox/env_test.go; plan 12 D5* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference restricts secret_name at create at all); landed for #50.*
- **Egress gate — `unrestricted`'s address floor covers the sessions that get a gate, and `limited`'s package-registry widening is a thirty-host list reaching past package registries (plan 12 slice 4; carved down by plan 29 slice 6a and #591, sized by #594; the floor landed with plan 45)** — The gate's request-level networking policy (`internal/gate`) admits **every host** under `unrestricted` and, since plan 45, judges **every resolved address** underneath it. What stays INFERRED is the floor's *contents*: the reference describes an unrestricted "safety boundary point" and publishes no blocklist, so which classes it refuses is still ours to infer from one probe. A recorded sandbox on an `unrestricted` environment was refused `http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/` with an **HTTP 403** whose body is `Destination IP is in a private/reserved range`, while `https://example.com/` answered 200: the reference keeps an address floor under `unrestricted` — its body naming a "private/reserved" class, though link-local is the only case the wave probed — and the cloud metadata endpoint, the one host that matters most, is behind it. The refusal actually recorded is link-local, and `dialguard.IPAllowed` does cover it — a floor this platform already holds under the vault probe, the MCP client's `DefaultClient` and the token-endpoint refresh, and it now holds under sandbox egress on this mode too, for the sessions an egress gate is provisioned for (plan 45, #570): every host is still admitted and every resolved address is judged, with the refusal carrying the reference's own 403 and body. The half left open is the deployment shape rather than the policy — a session no gate is provisioned for networks directly, so nothing in `internal/gate` runs for it, which is #620: every `unrestricted` session with no vault attached, and every session at all on a deployment that configures no gate image. The reference's *message* names a wider class than its probe demonstrated, and the wider half is one we would differ on by choice rather than by omission: `IPAllowed` refuses loopback, link-local, the unspecified address and multicast and **deliberately admits RFC 1918**, because on-prem MCP servers and token endpoints legitimately live on the operator's own private network — `internal/dialguard/dialguard.go`'s doc comment argues it in place. So whether the reference refuses 10/8, 172.16/12 and 192.168/16 too is unrecorded, only `169.254.169.254` having been probed, and if it does that is a divergence to keep rather than a gap to close. The refusal's *shape* records a second thing, and it is a divergence rather than a gap: the 403 arrives with a plain-text body and curl exiting 0, so a proxy answered the request, yet `http://127.0.0.1:1/` fails at the socket (curl exit 7) and `env` carries no proxy variable at all — **the reference intercepts transparently, at the network layer.** `cmd/gate` is *reached* through `HTTP_PROXY` but is not *enforced* by it: the sidecar installs owner-match iptables rules first — egress permitted only for the gate's own UID and loopback, everything else dropped — verifies they took, and only then drops to that UID and serves the proxy. A **gated** sandbox process that clears its own proxy variables is therefore dropped by the firewall, not let out — the proxy variables and the shared network namespace are injected by one branch, and the sandbox is admitted only by the gate's own healthcheck, which its listener, and so the verified firewall, precede. What differs is what a refused request looks like, not how strong the refusal is: theirs answers an HTTP 403 a client can read and log, ours is a dead socket. The residual that does exist is narrower and already owned — a sandbox image running **as** the gate UID bypasses the owner-match rule (#196). All of that presumes a gate, and one is provisioned only for a `limited` or vault-attached session on a deployment that set a gate image (the chart default is empty) — so **here**, a session of the shape this probe recorded (`unrestricted`, no vault attached) is given no gate at all: no proxy variable, no firewall, and it reaches the network directly. The reference intercepted that same shape, which is the divergence. #570 therefore had two halves: the `unrestricted` policy needed the floor for the sessions that do get a gate, which plan 45 delivered, and the ungated shape still needs a chokepoint before any floor can apply to it at all. `limited` admits `allowed_hosts` **plus the endpoints the session's agent declares MCP servers at** under `allow_mcp_servers`, which the SDK documents as permitting "outbound access to MCP server endpoints configured on the agent, beyond those listed in the `allowed_hosts` array". That widening rule is therefore the reference's; the plumbing is ours (the reference says nothing about how a sidecar proxy learns an agent's endpoints — INFERRED): the control plane resolves them and sends them beside the policy (`gateconfig.Config.MCPServerEndpoints`), not folded into `allowed_hosts`; they match on **`host:port`**, where an `allowed_hosts` entry matches by name and opens every port on its host; and a dial only they admit is held to the platform's address floor (`dialguard.IPAllowed`, on the resolved address), which `allowed_hosts` deliberately is not. Every declaration that would widen the gate past the server it names is refused (wildcard hosts, IPv6 literals, undialable schemes or no host at all, floor-refused literals, impossible ports), and so is anything `egress.ValidateHostEntry` refuses for an operator's list — which also excludes **an underscore in a label**, **a trailing-dot FQDN**, and a **non-ASCII (IDN) hostname**. Those three the platform's own MCP dial would reach (`egress.MCPServerAdmitted` checks no grammar under the flag), so each is a reachability gap rather than a safety one and each fails closed here; the IDN gap costs little, since a client punycodes a Unicode host before the gate sees it and a punycode declaration works. A refusal is silent on the wire — the sandbox sees the ordinary 403 — so only the control plane's log names its count. One consequence: a credential whose own arm is `unrestricted` substitutes on any admitted host, so it now reaches hosts an agent author declared — and, under `allow_package_managers`, the registry hosts *neither* author named — not only ones an operator listed; nothing separates the two authors' privileges (one management API key), and `unrestricted` reach is already the environment's call. `allow_package_managers` is **honored, on thirty hosts**. Under `{"type":"limited","allowed_hosts":[]}` the flag opens a curated host allowlist **and nothing else**, and a host it does not name answers 403 through the proxy — `Host not in allowlist: <host>` — rather than failing at the socket. A second recording probed **eighty** hosts across three `limited` configurations (the flag on, the flag off, and the flag on with `packages.npm` declared) and settled what the first wave's three Python probes could not. Thirty answered: `pypi.org`, `files.pythonhosted.org`; `registry.npmjs.org`, `registry.yarnpkg.com`, `nodejs.org`; `crates.io`, `index.crates.io`, `static.crates.io`; `rubygems.org`, `index.rubygems.org`; `proxy.golang.org`, `sum.golang.org`; `archive.ubuntu.com`, `security.ubuntu.com`, `ppa.launchpad.net`; `packagist.org`; `repo.maven.apache.org`, `repo1.maven.org`, `plugins.gradle.org`; `github.com`, `api.github.com`, `raw.githubusercontent.com`, `codeload.github.com`, `objects.githubusercontent.com`, `gitlab.com`, `bitbucket.org`; `ghcr.io`, `registry-1.docker.io`, `auth.docker.io`, `download.docker.com`. Each was observed admitted with the flag on **and** gate-refused with it off, across two control rounds, so none is attributed to the flag without its own control. **Two of those groups are the finding, because neither is a package registry**: the flag opens **source forges** and **container registries**, reach that neither its name nor the published wording suggests (the docs' "public package registries (such as PyPI and npm)", the SDK's "(PyPI, npm, etc.)", checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironment.go BetaLimitedNetwork.AllowPackageManagers) — an agent under `limited` reaches those forge hosts, and can open a tunnel to any port on any of the thirty, the class being host-matched rather than endpoint-matched. Read the forge half as measured host reach rather than a demonstrated `git clone`: GitHub's pack host `codeload.github.com` and object host `objects.githubusercontent.com` are admitted beside the apex, so a clone is very likely whole, but no probe ran one, and for GitLab and Bitbucket only the apex was probed at all. The container half is narrower than it looks: `ghcr.io` and `registry-1.docker.io` answer manifests, but `pkg-containers.githubusercontent.com` — GHCR's layer host — was probed and refused and Docker Hub's blob hosts are absent, so how far a real `docker pull` gets is unmeasured, no probe having run one. `sourceforge.net` was probed and refused and `quay.io` is absent entirely. It covers **PHP and Java** too, neither among the six managers `BetaPackages` names, while refusing NuGet, Dart, Hex, CocoaPods, Deno, conda, Alpine, CRAN, CPAN, Clojars, jsDelivr, unpkg and SourceForge — so the list tracks that enum in neither direction. Matching is by **exact host**, and this wave proves it rather than assuming it: `test.pypi.org`, `test-files.pythonhosted.org`, the `pythonhosted.org` apex, `npmjs.org`, `www.npmjs.com`, `registry.npmjs.com`, `golang.org`, `gist.githubusercontent.com`, `pkg-containers.githubusercontent.com` and `index.docker.io` were each refused while a sibling one label away was admitted. apt is **distribution-specific**: Debian's `deb.debian.org`, `ftp.debian.org` and `cdn-aws.deb.debian.org` are refused where Ubuntu's mirrors are admitted, and even within Ubuntu `ports.`, `azure.archive.`, `esm.`, `changelogs.` and `keyserver.ubuntu.com` are refused. One methodological note for whoever probes next, because it is the reason this wave is trustworthy where a status-only one would not be: the probes captured a body snippet each, since on this candidate set a status code lies in both directions — `crates.io` answers 403 from its own origin to a request with no `User-Agent` and `static.crates.io` 403 on a bucket listing, both while admitted — and the gate's own refusal is self-identifying where an origin's is not. **Thirty is still a lower bound, not the reference's list**: eighty probed, thirty answered, so `egress.PackageRegistryHosts` holds exactly these and a host nobody has probed stays out. That keeps the divergence in the **narrow** direction, deliberately — guessing an entry would widen a `limited` sandbox *past* the reference on the strength of a name that merely looked obvious, whereas a host this set omits is refused and never leaked. The set is opened by the flag **alone**, never by what `config.packages` lists: the first recording's `pkg-on` environment declared no packages at all (its stored block was the six empty lists the reference synthesizes on every cloud environment) and `pypi.org` opened anyway, and the second confirms the converse, its `packages.npm: ["left-pad"]` arm being classification-identical to the plain flag-on arm across the seventeen hosts it probed. And it matches by **host**, like an operator's entry rather than like the port-scoped MCP set — the reference's own distinction rather than an inference from the probes, since it widens by "MCP server **endpoints** configured on the agent", where an endpoint is what carries a port, but by "public package **registries** … beyond those listed in the `allowed_hosts` array", whose unit is the domain that array itself takes. But like the MCP set, and unlike `allowed_hosts`, a dial only it admits is held to the platform's address floor — the dividing line being not who typed the hostname but whether an operator vouched for it by listing it. One thing it gets that the MCP set does not: the gate **roots the name for the dial** (a trailing dot on the dial address alone, leaving the `Host` header, the CONNECT authority and the TLS server name spelled as the sandbox sent them), so a resolver's `search` list cannot answer a curated name between the admission check and the connection — a sidecar inheriting a pod's `ndots:5` would otherwise try `pypi.org.<search-domain>` first, and an internal record there lands on an RFC 1918 address the floor admits by design. Where the sets overlap a precedence settles it, and it runs strongest-treatment-first: `allowed_hosts` before either widening (an operator who listed a host, `*.` entry included, has vouched for it, so it is dialled unfloored and unrooted), then the registry set before the MCP set, so a curated host an agent also declares an endpoint at keeps the rooting rather than losing it to the class that does not root. Only this class is rooted, because only this list is one the platform itself authors: an `allow_mcp_servers` declaration may legitimately be a *relative* in-cluster name that only a resolver's search list completes (`nexus.infra:8080`, the Kubernetes `<service>.<namespace>` spelling), which telling apart from a public name is what `ndots` exists to guess at, and `allowed_hosts` is the operator's own list resolving through the operator's own resolver, so rooting either would narrow reach without buying a boundary. What that leaves standing on the MCP class is a residual rather than nothing, and #601 carries it — narrowed, since plan 44, to its policy half alone. Every dial this platform makes to a customer-supplied or agent-declared name now goes through one dialler (`dialguard.Dialer`): the gate's two handlers, the executor's own MCP dial, the vault-validate probe, the token-endpoint refresh and the JWKS fetch each resolve their name **once**, hold every address that came back to the floor before any connect, and connect to those addresses — so the address a policy judged is the address the socket uses, and nothing below the check can re-resolve the name. What one resolution cannot change is which answer a resolver gives, and that is the whole of what is left — and it is wider than the `search` list, which is only one way to produce it: a declared name may resolve to a private address by search-list completion, by split-horizon DNS, or by a controlled zone publishing an RFC 1918 record for it, and `IPAllowed` admits every one of those deliberately. So the rooting question #601 asks narrows the residual rather than ending it. A credential is the sharp end. Two paths carry one and both choose it by **name** while the socket goes to an address: the gate substitutes on plain HTTP when the request host matches a credential's own `allowed_hosts` (a CONNECT tunnel is opaque, so HTTPS keeps its placeholder), and the executor dials the declared URL itself under a bearer matched by the credential's `mcp_server_url`. Either way the secret is chosen for the declared host and then delivered wherever the search list sends the dial, where declaring the internal host outright would not have obtained it, neither matching rule having matched. Beside that, an operator reading a declaration's public-looking spelling as where the session will connect can still be crossed. The flag has a **create-time** half this platform now enforces too, confirmed from the public docs rather than this recording: the reference rejects an environment that lists `packages` under `limited` networking without `allow_package_managers`, with a 400, and `normalizeEnvConfig`'s `checkPackagesNetworking` does the same (plan 40, folding in #576). An unknown networking type fails closed whatever widening flags ride beside it. *Evidence: both flags' wording (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaenvironment.go BetaLimitedNetwork.AllowMCPServers and BetaLimitedNetwork.AllowPackageManagers); internal/api/gateconfig.go (mcpGateEndpoints, mcpEndpoint, endpointPort — their comments carry the per-refusal derivations and the refusal log this row no longer restates); internal/gate/policy.go (newPolicy, admit, the admission classes and their floored/rooted questions, endpointKey, packageRegistries); internal/egress/registries.go (the set, its grouping, and why thirty is a floor rather than the list); internal/gate/gate.go (Config, newDialer, rootedName, rootedDial — whose comments carry the per-shape argument for what rooting leaves alone); internal/gaterun/gaterun.go (the conversion that cannot lose the floor); internal/egress/mcphost.go (MCPServerAdmitted); internal/egress/engine.go:52-55; internal/domain/environment.go:29-30; internal/gate/gate_test.go (TestGateAdmitsADeclaredMCPEndpointOnlyUnderItsFlag, TestAnMCPOnlyDialIsHeldToTheAddressFloor, TestGateAdmitsAPackageRegistryOnlyUnderItsFlag, TestAPackageRegistryOnlyDialIsHeldToTheAddressFloor, TestGateTunnelsAPackageRegistryOnlyUnderItsFlag, TestGateUnrestrictedAdmitsAnyHost, TestGateUnknownNetworkingFailsClosed); internal/gate/helpers_internal_test.go (TestThePackageRegistrySetOpensOnlyUnderItsFlag, TestThePackageRegistrySetStillRefusesWhatTheRecordingSawRefused, TestWhatEachAdmissionClassAsksOfTheDial, TestRootedNameLeavesEverythingThatIsNotADNSNameAlone, TestOnlyARegistryDialIsRooted, TestBothDialPathsCarryTheRooting); internal/egress/registries_test.go (TestPackageRegistryHostsAreWhatTheRecordingSized, TestNoPackageRegistryEntryIsAWildcard); internal/api/gateconfig_internal_test.go (TestTheGatesMCPEndpointsRefuseWhatWouldWiden — every refusal but the underscore and the IDN host; TestACredentialOnADeclaredMCPHostIsNotUnreachable, TestACredentialOnAPackageRegistryHostIsNotUnreachable — the conflict detection is told each widening the gate is told, or it reports a credential scoped to one of those hosts as permanently unreachable); plan 12 slice 4* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `session.events.list.blocklist-unrestricted`, `session.events.list.blocked-status`, `sess.create.pkg-on` and `sess.create.pkg-off`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §E; 2026-09-05/batch7.json with GROUND_TRUTH7.txt's 139 host-arm rows, analysed in 2026-09-05/FINDINGS7.md.* *Tracked: #620 (the shape no gate covers: a session the executor provisions none for, which is any `unrestricted` session with no vault attached and every session on a deployment that configures no gate image), #601 (the policy half — whether a declared MCP endpoint should resolve absolutely too; its mechanical half, one resolution per dial judged before any connect on every path including the executor's, landed as plan 44); landed for #50, the MCP-endpoint widening for #45, the package-registry widening for #591, its rooted dial for #596, its thirty-host sizing for #594, the create-time 400 for #576 (plan 40), and `unrestricted`'s address floor for #570 (plan 45) — held wherever a gate is actually provisioned.*
- **Credential get/update/delete — nesting and immutability edges** — A `{vault_id}` path segment that mismatches the credential's actual vault 404s on every nested verb; update on an archived credential rejects (400, like archived environments — its secrets were purged); the auth union's variant cannot change on update; a refresh block cannot be introduced after create (the update union structurally lacks its client_id/token_endpoint anchors); switching `token_endpoint_auth` arms requires a client_secret (the prior arm's secret is not inherited), while restating the same arm may omit it. All follow from the SDK's update-param structure and sibling conventions, and four have since been recorded refusing as this platform does, so since #540 they answer in the reference's words: a credential addressed under another vault is a 404 "Credential not found." on get, update and delete (2026-09-03 batch1 `cred.get.wrong-vault`, `cred.update.wrong-vault`, `cred.delete.wrong-vault`); a variant switch is a 400 "auth.type: does not match this credential's stored type" (`cred.update.switch-variant-to-static-bearer`), the stored and requested types going to the operator's log; a refresh block on a credential created without one is a 400 "auth.refresh: this credential was created without refresh capability" (`cred.update.refresh-fields-on-no-refresh-cred`), an unknown key inside that block refused first, as recorded (`cred.update.add-refresh-post-create`); and an update in an archived vault is a 400 "Vault is archived." (`cred.update.in-archived-vault-C`) — the vault judged before the credential its archive cascaded to, while a credential archived on its own keeps ours. The rest is unrecorded. *Evidence: update unions dropping the immutable fields and the `none` arm (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betavaultcredential.go BetaVaultCredentialUpdateParamsAuthUnion and BetaManagedAgentsMCPOAuthUpdateParams and BetaManagedAgentsStaticBearerUpdateParams and BetaManagedAgentsEnvironmentVariableUpdateParams and BetaManagedAgentsMCPOAuthRefreshUpdateParams and BetaManagedAgentsMCPOAuthRefreshUpdateParamsTokenEndpointAuthUnion); internal/api/vaultcredauth.go applyCredAuthUpdate, internal/api/vaultcredentials.go errCredentialNotFound and archivedCredentialRefusal; internal/api/vaults_test.go TestCredentialUpdateImmutability, TestCredentialPathScoping, TestCredentialUpdateErrors and TestCredentialWritesInAnArchivedVault* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the archived-credential refusal, the `token_endpoint_auth` arm switch and the archive route's 404); landed for #50.*
- **mcp_oauth_validate — probe mechanics (plan 12 D8)** — The probe needs no MCP client: an RFC 6749 refresh-token exchange when a refresh block and token exist (basic-auth halves form-urlencoded per §2.3.1; `client_id` in the body for `none` and `client_secret_post`), then a streamable-HTTP JSON-RPC `initialize` (protocolVersion 2025-06-18) under the possibly-refreshed bearer token. Status mapping per the docs' prescription, matrix inferred: a definitive 4xx (except 429) from either exchange → `invalid`; else a clean 2xx probe with a non-failed refresh → `valid`; else `unknown`. Captured `http_response` bodies are truncated at 4096 bytes (our cap; the docs promise truncation, not a size) and scrubbed of every secret value including freshly-rotated ones; `http_response` renders null for `no_refresh_token` and connect failures (per the docs' example). A successful refresh persists the rotated tokens and derived `expires_at` (our decision, consistent with the reference's managed re-resolution; whether *its* validate endpoint persists is unobserved). Validate on a non-`mcp_oauth` credential is a 400, as is an archived one. Because the probe dials credential-supplied URLs from the control plane and returns their bodies, it carries an SSRF guard the reference's managed egress makes invisible: connections to loopback, link-local (cloud metadata), unspecified, and multicast addresses are refused at connect time on the resolved IP — evaluated on every dial, so a DNS-rebinding hop is re-checked — while RFC 1918 private ranges are deliberately allowed — a self-hosted platform's MCP/OAuth endpoints legitimately live on the operator's own network (CLAUDE.md's on-prem premise). A blocked target reads as a connection failure, never confirming an internal host. The probe also refuses to **follow** HTTP redirects at all (`CheckRedirect` → `http.ErrUseLastResponse`): neither an OAuth token exchange nor an MCP `initialize` legitimately redirects, and a followed 307/308 would replay the POST body — the `refresh_token` and a `client_secret_post` secret — to the redirect target, an exfiltration the per-dial IP guard cannot prevent because it only vets where a hop lands, not that a hop should happen; a 3xx is instead pinned as the final response and captured as a failure. *Evidence: internal/api/vaultvalidate.go; internal/api/vaultvalidate_test.go; platform.claude.com vaults guide (status mapping, null http_response example)* **Two recording waves have now failed to make the reference's probe run at all, and the reason is not the one first recorded.** Six `mcp_oauth_validate` calls — with `?beta=true` and without, with a body and without, against a reachable MCP server (`mcp.deepwiki.com`, which answers `initialize` and served six other probes in the same wave) and an unreachable one, before and after `expires_at` had passed — every one returned `mcp_probe: null` **and** `refresh: null`, alongside `status: "valid"` and `has_refresh_token: true`. The first wave concluded that `refresh` is null *because* the MCP probe fails; that was an over-reading of a field which is simply always null on this account, and the second wave disproved it against a server that does answer. What withholds both sub-probes is a feature flag, an account entitlement, or a code path the console cannot reach — **which of the three is not determined here, and is not guessed at.** The consequence for this entry is that the status matrix is not merely unrecorded but unreachable by this route: no amount of console-driven validation on this account will produce one. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch1.json and batch2.json, the `cred.mcp_oauth_validate.*` probes in each (batch2's are `te-400`, `te-429` and `te-200`, plus a `.beta` variant of each).*  *Analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §D, which corrects 2026-09-03/FINDINGS.md on this point.* *Tracked: #78 (the status matrix and whether validate persists rotated tokens — both need an account whose probe actually runs); landed for #50.*

- **Agent create/update caps (#66) — the caps are documented, the mechanics are ours** — The caps are the SDK create/update param doc comments' own numbers (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaAgentNewParams and BetaAgentUpdateParams; enumerated in the CONFIRMED documented-limits entry above) — bar the three string bounds, `name` 256, `description` 2,048 and `system` 100,000 characters, which are the spec's `maxLength` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCreateAgentParams.properties and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsUpdateAgentParams.properties; #665); the readings on top of them are ours. The 400 `invalid_request_error` statuses (the reference's unobserved, bar agent create's `system` and metadata-count rejects, recorded in the CONFIRMED entries above). Since #540 the messages are the reference's where it was recorded refusing: `system: maximum string length is 100000` and `invalid metadata: metadata map would have 17 entries, exceeding the maximum of 16` on agent create (2026-09-02 `agent.create.system-100001-ascii`, `agent.create.metadata-17-keys`), and the same maxLength sentence for `name` and `description`, the reference's generic template, recorded on a second resource too (a vault credential's `secret_name`). On update every such path carries the `agent.` prefix the reference's update route was recorded putting on the paths it reports (`agent.model.effort`, `agent.tools[0]…`) — for these fields an inference. **The string bounds bind only the value a create or update supplies**, never a stored one — the spec puts `maxLength` on the request params, and binding the stored value would strand what no agent update reaches, a roster's pinned member version and a session's snapshot. So an agent stored over one before #665 still resolves wherever it is read, and an update that does not resend the field lands; whether the reference would refuse such a stored value is unobservable, since it refuses to store one. They count code points: agent create's `é` probe rules out bytes, and code points rather than UTF-16 units past the BMP is the override's recorded unit, assumed for agent create. Update's reject shape, and everything about `name` and `description`, were never probed. "Maximum of 128 tools across all toolsets" counted as **entries of `tools[]`** — an `agent_toolset_20260401` counts once, not as its enabled built-ins, an `mcp_toolset`'s count being unknowable at write time and a mixed rule arbitrary. Tool-**name** uniqueness across the *expanded* names (a custom tool may legally reuse a *disabled* built-in's name); whether the reference rejects that set at create is unobserved. A session snapshot stored before this check can still pair a custom tool with an *enabled* built-in of its name. Such a session's calls on the name went to whichever of the two `tools[]` lists later, which owned the name's class, and the brain keeps that: at resolve time it offers only the later entry and drops the earlier with an operator note — so no request carries the name twice, which the Messages API refuses, the tool that runs is the one that ran before, and the name is marked built-in (`provider.Request.BuiltinTools`, #682) only when the built-in is the later entry. What the reference does with such an agent, if it ever stores one, is unrecorded. Metadata key/value lengths counted in Unicode code points, the SDK's "chars" unit being unobserved. All the rest binds the **resulting** spec: agent update's merge (so clearing `tools` while stored servers remain rejects, and the metadata cap binds the post-patch bag); the session's resolved agent on *every* resolve, not only under overrides, so a pre-#66 stored spec fails at session create rather than on every turn; and a session-update `agent.tools`/`agent.mcp_servers` patch (#287). Binding the *merged* spec there is itself ours — the SDK makes the two override fields independently replaceable — and visible: an override replacing `tools` without re-listing the stored servers' toolsets rejects unless it clears `mcp_servers` too, and since plan 29 slice 1 clearing `mcp_servers` alone rejects on the dangling rung, so a session turns MCP off by replacing both sides of the pair or neither. Whether the reference validates a merged spec there at all is unobserved. **Both MCP directions are now rejected** (plan 29 slice 1, correcting this entry): a dangling `mcp_toolset` — naming a server the spec does not declare — was once deliberately unrejected here and is now a 400 on every surface that resolves a whole spec, so neither direction is a divergence. Two documented `mcp_servers[]` bounds do stay unenforced, each checked only at its lower edge (empty rejected): `name` "1-255 characters" (the SDK's, restated as "1–255 characters" in the MCP-connector guide's field table) and `url` "up to 2,048 characters" — outside #66's scope, left for a recording to justify. *Evidence: create/update params, incl. the update's "resulting tools" and "stored bag is limited to 16 keys" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaAgentNewParams and BetaAgentUpdateParams), `name` 1-255 (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsURLMCPServerParams.Name); the override fields (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentWithOverridesParams.MCPServers and BetaManagedAgentsAgentWithOverridesParams.Tools); docs/plan/29_mcp-toolset.md § "Wire shapes" (the `url` bound's doc-only source); internal/api/wire.go, whose comments on maxAgentTools, validateAgentSpec and validateMetadataCaps carry the derivations cut from this entry; internal/api/sessions.go (resolveAgent, updateSession); internal/api/agentlimits_test.go, incl. TestAgentUpdateValidatesResultingSpec, TestAgentUpdateMetadataCapOnStoredBag, TestAgentSystemCap, TestAgentNameAndDescriptionCaps and TestStoredOverCapAgentGrandfathered; internal/api/sessions_test.go TestSessionOverridesValidateAgentSpec; internal/brain/mcptools.go resolveTools and TestALegacyNameCollisionKeepsTheLaterEntry (the resolve-time settlement); internal/api/mcptoolset_test.go, whose header quotes the guide's both-directions sentence (fetched 2026-08-08), TestAgentDanglingMCPToolsetRejected / TestSessionDanglingMCPToolsetRejected* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the reject shapes, the counting units, whether a custom tool may share an enabled built-in's name, and the two unenforced mcp_servers bounds); the caps landed for #66, the string bounds for #665, the resolve-time settlement for #682.*

- **Multiagent roster — resolution and snapshot mechanics past the documented rules (plan 35 slice 1, decision 10)** — The documented constraints (1–20 entries; distinct agents after resolving `self` and string forms; at most one `self`; members exist, unarchived, no nested `multiagent`) are enforced as 400s — since #540 a member's refusal in the reference's recorded words, naming the member rather than the entry (`Agent has invalid configuration: subagent agent_… is archived` and its siblings, 2026-09-02 `agent.create.roster.*`), the rest naming the entry in ours — and the roster is replaced as a whole on update, `null` clearing it. What is ours or inferred: a bare id string and a versionless `{type:"agent"}` reference **pin the member's current version at write time** (INFERRED from the resolved type's plain `int64` — the roster doc says only "an agent ID string"; by analogy with session create's "pins the latest version"); `self` resolves to the coordinator's own id and the version the write produces — and an update that keeps the roster re-pins the stored `self` entry to the new version, so `self` always names this coordinator at the version a session resolves (ours; a Codex review finding) — and is **exempt from the depth-1 check** (INFERRED — read literally the rule would forbid the documented feature); the depth check reads the spec that gets pinned; a member id is checked on shape first (the recorded `… subagent agent_… is not a valid agent ID`, the vault_ids precedent, by the shape *Malformed and absent ids — the one shape agent and thread ids are read by* below defines; a well-formed id naming no agent keeps ours) and a missing or archived member is a **400**, not a 404; two entries resolving to distinct agents that **share a name** are a 400 too, naming both members and their indices (ours — the documented constraint is distinctness of *agents*, but a coordinator spawns a member by name, so a duplicate would leave one member unspawnable; whether the reference refuses it is unobserved); `version: null` on a reference reads as omitted, as session create's `agent.version` does (the SDK admits no null there); a member is not re-checked for archive state at session create, nor when an update that omits `multiagent` carries the stored roster into the new version (the roster pins immutable versions — the same roster re-sent explicitly is re-resolved and would reject; the reference's behavior for a member archived after the roster was written is unobserved); **any entry naming the coordinator's own id is the `self` entry** — a bare id, or a reference at the version a GET renders for self or at the version the write produces (any other own version is a 400 pointing at `{type:"self"}`) — because the rendered roster carries no self marker and the echo of a GET must read back as what it rendered (ours, a review finding); two concurrent coordinator updates naming each other can deadlock on the member row locks, and the aborted one is a **409** to retry rather than a 500. The session snapshot is full `SessionThreadAgent` definitions with the roster not repeated inside a member, each member's stored spec answering to the whole-spec caps (#66) as the coordinator's own does at resolve — a pre-cap member spec is a **400** naming the member; the `self` member is the coordinator's resolved spec for this session, overrides applied (documented: overrides "apply to the coordinator and its `self` copies"), identified as the entry naming the coordinator at the version the session resolved; and a session update patching `agent.tools` / `agent.mcp_servers` **rewrites the `self` copy too** (ours — keeps that rule true after create; whether the reference's update reaches an already-snapshotted `self` copy is unobserved). a non-null `multiagent` inside `agent_with_overrides` is an explicit **400**, as the reference was recorded answering it — `Failed to parse request body: unknown field "multiagent"` (2026-09-02 `session.create.agent_with_overrides.multiagent`), its words here since #540 — while an explicit null — the value every single-agent session response renders — reads as absent, so a read-modify-write echo passes (ours: whether the reference admits that null is unrecorded); the plain `{type:"agent"}` reference ignores the key as it ignores every override key. *Evidence: the MultiagentParams doc comment (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsMultiagentParams), AgentParams (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentParams), SessionMultiagentCoordinator (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsSessionMultiagentCoordinator and BetaManagedAgentsSessionMultiagentCoordinatorAgentUnion), AgentReference (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentReference), MultiagentSelfParams (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsMultiagentSelfParams), SessionThreadAgent, "The multiagent roster is not repeated here" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsSessionThreadAgent); platform.claude.com managed-agents multiagent + agent-setup docs (roster snapshotted at coordinator create/update, "replaced as a whole … Pass `null` to clear it", overrides and `self` copies); internal/api/roster.go, agents.go (create/update), sessions.go (resolveAgent, the session-update patch); TestRosterCreateResolvesEntries, TestRosterUpdateSelfAndReplacement, TestRosterConstraints, TestRosterSessionSnapshot, TestRosterOverrideRejected, TestRosterMemberValidatedAtSessionCreate* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether a versionless reference pins the member's current version, whether `self` escapes the depth-1 check, whether the reference refuses duplicate member names or admits a null `multiagent` in overrides, how it treats a member archived after the roster was written, and whether its session update rewrites an already-snapshotted `self` copy).*
- **Session threads — the primary thread and the thread substrate past the documented rules (plan 35 slice 2, decisions 1, 2, 12)** — Documented and matched: every session lists one primary thread (`parent_thread_id: null`) on `GET /v1/sessions/{id}/threads` (default `limit` 1000, forward-only `page` cursor — the 1000 **maximum** is ours, the param documents no cap); `GET …/threads/{tid}`, `POST …/threads/{tid}/archive`, `GET …/threads/{tid}/events` (`limit`/`page` only) and `GET …/threads/{tid}/stream` (`event_deltas[]`); the thread's `session.thread_status_*` events carry `session_thread_id` and `agent_name`; a child's cross-posted tool call, or an answer to one, names its thread with `session_thread_id` on the session view and omits the key on the child's own (the SDK's "Empty on the thread's own events"; no recorded event renders it null on any surface, so the stored null is dropped at render on every thread-addressable event — #674), while a thread's own `session.thread_*` events carry their `session_thread_id` on every surface — it is a required field there, not a cross-post marker. What is ours or inferred: **the primary thread's id is derived from the session id** (`sthr_` + the session's token) so every pre-existing session has one by SQL backfill and no new id is minted (ours — the reference's derivation is unrecorded and ids are opaque); every `session.status_*` a primary-thread transition emits — the session's move, or a re-emit or the reclaim's forced pair, which move nothing — is recorded **in the same batch** as a pair with the primary's `session.thread_status_*`, on every session including single-agent ones: `session.status_running` ahead of the primary's event, the reference's order within the pair (#674 — every `session.status_running` recorded comes before the primary's `session.thread_status_running`: 162 across the recordings archive, the thirteen in the 2026-09-02 recording's `sessK`, `sessK2`, `sessT` and `sessW` event lists among them, the primary's event next in 157 and after session-start `session.error`s in the other five; 1 µs later in 131 of the 157 and up to 1.7 s later in the rest, each a session's first pair, and up to 63 s behind the errors), `_idle` behind it (the fact, then the rollup — recorded too), and `_rescheduled`, never recorded, behind it as idle is; the order keys on the session event emitted, whichever thread moved, and a child's move that wakes an idle session is not this pair ("Session threads — a child's resume of an idle session", among the deliberate divergences above); **where the pair sits in its batch is matched** (landed for #793): a wake by `user.message` or `user.define_outcome` writes the pair ahead of the waking event, as the reference does (all 115 message wakes on the event lists recorded through 2026-09-24 — 110 with the pair directly ahead of the message, five with session-start `session.error`s between its two events, none with the message first — every such wake a recorded live stream carries, and the one recorded `user.define_outcome` wake), at every wake site — a send, the interrupt-plus-message redirect, a create's `initial_events` (a recorded deployment run's list opens the same way) and a dream stage;
