a primary's tool answer precedes the pair on both (all 32 recorded answer wakes: 12 by a confirmation, 9 by a `user.tool_result`, 11 by a `user.custom_tool_result`), as it always did here; so is the pair of a thread an answer resumes (landed for #793): the send moves the thread itself, where the answer is consumed — in receipt order with any interrupt beside it, so an answer received ahead of an interrupt keeps the session from folding idle on the gate it cleared; one case is ours: a thread moves once per send, at its last processed answer, so when one thread's answers straddle another thread's interrupt in a single send the interrupt's fold still names a gate an answer listed ahead of it cleared, where a processing-order reference would re-idle the thread on the remaining gate first (TestAnswersStraddlingAnInterruptMoveTheirThreadOnce) — and lists the pair after the answer and ahead of what the resumed turn consumes — on the primary, a denial's `agent.tool_result`, where all seven recorded primary denials list it (six whose confirmation resumed the thread, read as the confirmation, the pair, the result — 2026-09-12-archived-threads/batch1.json `[5].body.data` idx 31–35 among them, the result 117 ms after the pair and 1 µs before the resumed turn's `span.model_request_start` — and one held behind a later custom call, read as that call's result, the pair, the denial's result: 2026-09-19-custom-order-followup/setup.json `ask-first-deny.final-audit.events` idx 15–18), and an input posted beside the answer, a message the resumed turn will read, say, which is the rule's prediction, since no recording carries an input beside an answer wake; an answer that leaves its thread parked on another gate re-idles it right after the answer, as the reference does (`ask-first-deny` idx 11–14: the denial, the thread's idle on the gate left open, `session.usage` — not built, above — and the session's idle), ahead of any input the send leaves pending; a child's resume, its denial's result included, is the deliberate divergence above; one placement stays ours: such a denial's `agent.tool_result` is written in the denial's own commit, beside its confirmation and ahead of the re-idle, where the reference holds that result alone back until a later answer resumes the thread and writes it behind that resume's pair (`ask-first-deny` idx 11–18) — deferring it would leave a processed denial with no result on the log between commits, which the ordered tool flow reads as a call released to run; and events already on the log keep the message-first order this platform wrote before (append-only; nothing backfills them) — and a session's **pre-slice history holds no thread events** (append-only; nothing backfills them); the primary thread **never emits `terminated`** (the archive is recorded, and leaves the primary `idle` with nothing on the session's event list announcing an end; INFERRED still for the transport no stored list can show, and for every other ending — only `_running` is asserted for every session, and the docs carve the primary's end out of the thread webhooks); its row carries its own status, stop reason and usage (the session's status is the fold over its threads' — slice 3, below), and nothing of the session's — the archive leaves the row alone, as the reference does (#713) — and only those: the primary's `updated_at` moves with its own status and usage, not with an archive, a session title, metadata or resources update, so a freshly patched session and its primary thread can read different `updated_at`s (ours; what moves the reference's thread `updated_at` is unrecorded, beyond a session archive moving neither it nor `archived_at`); the primary's `agent` is rendered from the session's resolved agent minus the roster, never stored (ours); thread `stats` and `usage` are `null` until the thread's first status transition and objects from then on (#674; the first-party console reads both as objects until managed-agent-console#184, so a console release carrying it deploys before a platform release carrying this), so a never-run primary renders both null, as the recorded one does, and a thread born running — a spawned child, the primary of a session created with `initial_events` — renders both from birth; whether a thread has moved is read from its row — a child, a status other than idle, or nonzero usage each says so — and, for an idle primary, the one case its row cannot tell, from a `transitioned` flag its first status change sets (migration 0045); that one rule keys both, and **for `usage` that follows the recordings, not the spec**: the spec has `stats` "Null until the thread's first status transition" but `usage` "Null until the thread's first idle transition" (the two citations in the evidence below), while no recorded thread renders one of the two null and the other not, and two children recorded running before their first idle carry `usage` objects (2026-09-12-child-lifecycle/ui-network.json idx 9; their first `session.thread_status_idle` is in idx 13–14) — following the spec instead takes a second flag, set on the first idle, and a change in `threadRow.moved`; what counts as a transition is ours where nothing is recorded: a move to the status the row already holds (the payload-only re-idle) sets nothing, so an interrupt on a never-run session leaves both null; two recorded sessions created with a `budget` render both as zeroed objects within half a second of creation with nothing on their event lists (2026-09-12/ui-network.json `rec91.ui.session.created` idx 10, 2026-09-12-child-lifecycle/ui-network.json idx 1), a move no session here makes, since budgets are registered, not built ("Reference surface that arrived in one SDK release", above); every thread that existed before migration 0045 is flagged, so one created before the upgrade and never run still renders objects (the conservative side, the owner's decision on #674); during a rolling upgrade a replica on an earlier build renders objects for every thread and sets no flag, so while both builds serve, a never-run primary reads as objects from an old replica and null from a new one, with no transition between the reads, and an idle primary an old replica both started and stopped, with no model request settling nonzero usage on it, renders null until its next transition — never, for a one-shot session — while every other thread an old replica writes or moves renders from its row; once present, thread `stats` render the empty shape (ours, the session-stats precedent) and thread `usage` the token buckets only — `active_seconds`/`list_cost`/`server_tool_use` are the unbuilt surface (#430–#433; since anthropic-sdk-go v1.62.0 — betasessionthread.go BetaManagedAgentsSessionThreadUsage.ActiveSeconds and BetaManagedAgentsSessionThreadUsage.ListCost and BetaManagedAgentsSessionThreadUsage.ServerToolUse); **the primary's events list and stream are the session view** (INFERRED from "the session-level stream is the primary thread"): one stored row per event with a `cross_posted` flag (ours) selecting what a child's row surfaces on the session view, the `session_thread_id` set there on tool-use kinds and the inbound answers to them; a child's preview frames reach only the child's stream, the frame envelope carrying the thread (ours); a thread's events list **rejects `order`, `types[]` and `created_at[*]` with a 400** rather than ignoring them — the typed params carry only `limit`/`page` and a silently ignored filter would hand back the wrong rows — and a descending `page` cursor minted by the session list is an invalid cursor there for the same reason (ours); a missing session is `session … not found` on every thread route, a missing thread `thread … not found`, a `{tid}` that is not a thread id at all the reference's 400, `Invalid thread ID: …` (recorded for an `sth_` prefix on the get, 2026-09-02 batch2 #337 `sessK2.threads.get.sth_-prefix`; #841; the rest is *The reference's recorded error sentences, answered where no recording reaches*, below), and a bad parameter on either is still the 400 every list here answers first; archive: the primary is a 400 with the reference's own sentence, "The primary thread cannot be archived; archive the session instead." (recorded, below — ours had said the same in fewer words, so nothing was lost adopting it), a non-idle child a 400 (the multiagent docs' "Archive only succeeds if the thread is `idle`"; `rescheduling` below), an idle child — one parked on `requires_action` included — has its unanswered tool calls closed first with the error results an interrupt writes (INFERRED by analogy with the interrupt, whose answer to a child parked on `requires_action` the same page spells out; each result on the surfaces its call was on), then becomes `terminated` with `archived_at` set and emits `session.thread_status_terminated` on both surfaces; archiving the session terminates every live child first (documented: a child terminates on archive and on the session's end) and leaves the primary exactly where it was — unarchived, its status and `updated_at` untouched, whatever they held — which is what the reference does (both recorded archives left an `idle` primary `idle`; every status `requireNotRunning` admits is archivable, so `idle` is the sample rather than the rule) (this platform mirrored the session's `archived_at` there until the 2026-09-12 recording refuted the match that mirror assumed, and #713 dropped it); session delete cascades to the thread rows and, since nothing can be appended to them, broadcasts each live child's `session.thread_status_terminated` ephemerally to the child's and the session's open streams ahead of `session.deleted` (ours — the precedent `session.deleted` set). **At `rescheduling` the thread and session rules part (#730)**: a child there is refused its own archive, yet ended by its session's archive or delete whenever no other thread, the primary included, is running, since `requireNotRunning` ("Session archive/delete" above) refuses only `running`. Each rule reads its own page literally, and each page admits another reading. The thread docs' idle sentence covers `rescheduling`, but their next clause, "only a running thread must be interrupted first", reads as if `running` were the only other status. The session docs name only `running`, but the recorded refusal's "Only pending or idle sessions may be archived" reads as an allow-list leaving out `rescheduling` and `terminated` — a loose one, since `pending` is not among the SDK's `BetaManagedAgentsSessionStatus` values. So what the reference does with a thread or session at `rescheduling`, or with a session already `terminated`, is INFERRED on both sides. Admitting the session keeps an auto-retrying session's archive or delete from waiting out its retry loop; refusing the thread pays that cost for a slot under the documented 25-thread limit, which the idle sentence asks for. Both are latent here: nothing leaves a thread at `rescheduling` across a commit, and nothing stores a session `terminated` until #577. On the reference `rescheduling` cannot be provoked on demand, so only a recording that happens on one could settle that half, while a session `terminated` by a context overflow (#577's recording) can be archived on purpose. Child rows existed only through tests until slice 4's delegation spawned them. *Evidence: the thread, its Stats and Usage, and its list params with "Defaults to 1000" and "Forward-only" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasessionthread.go BetaManagedAgentsSessionThread and BetaManagedAgentsSessionThreadStats and BetaManagedAgentsSessionThreadUsage and BetaSessionThreadListParams); The second session's complete 41-event list holds neither `session.thread_status_terminated` nor `session.status_terminated` while holding four `_running`/`_idle` pairs of each scope, so the absence is the endpoint's answer and not an empty sample; the first ran no turn and lists nothing. Every recording through 2026-09-19 carries `session_thread_id` as a value or not at all, on every surface and in every echo — never null — and 2026-09-02/batch2.json's `sessT.send.interrupt` echo carries none.* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02/batch2.json, probes `session.threads.list.fresh` and `session.threads.get.primary` (a never-run primary, `stats` and `usage` both null) and `sessK.threads.list` (both objects after the first idle); across every recording through 2026-09-24, 48 distinct threads render a `stats` and a `usage`: three never-run primaries both null every time, the other 45 both objects every time, 23 of them while running, and none one of each.* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02/batch2.json, probe `sessK.threads.primary.archive` (resolved by name): 400 `invalid_request_error`, "The primary thread cannot be archived; archive the session instead."* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle how the reference derives the primary thread's id, what moves its thread `updated_at` at all, whether the primary announces an end on a transport no stored list can show or on any ending but the archive, whether its primary's events list is the session view as the docs say its stream is, and what the reference's archive or delete does with a thread or session at `rescheduling`, which only a chance recording can catch, or with a session already `terminated`, which a context overflow can provoke, and whether a thread's `usage` is null before its first idle, as the spec says and two running children contradict — a recording of a primary polled during its first run would settle it); landed for #713's archive alignment, #730's record of the two archive rules, #674's primary-archive message, omitted null `session_thread_id` and `stats` and `usage` null until the first transition, #793's running-pair placement, a resume's included, and #841's malformed-thread-id 400.*
- **Session status as a fold over threads, and the thread-aware execution substrate (plan 35 slice 3, decisions 3, 4, 5, 9, 14, 15)** — On the wire this slice shows only as the `session_thread_id` acceptance above; child threads came from a test seam until slice 4's delegation spawned them, and a single-agent session's wire is unchanged. Session status is a **fold** over the session's live (unarchived, non-terminated) threads: any `running` → running, else any `rescheduling` → rescheduling, else idle with the highest-ranked idle `stop_reason` among the threads' — `requires_action ≻ retries_exhausted ≻ end_turn` (the ask-over-cap pair is documented; `end_turn`'s rank is INFERRED; `budget_reached`'s documented rank is recorded, not implemented — nothing writes it) — and a `requires_action` session's `event_ids` is the **union** across threads in log order (INFERRED); the session event is emitted when the fold changes, when an idle external wait's blocker set changes, or for the interrupt's payload-only re-idle and the reclaim's pair, so a child's idle under a running sibling is its thread event alone, a child's `terminated` moves the session's status only when the fold over the threads left differs from the status stored (never for an idle child under a live primary; the last thread holding the session at `rescheduling` leaves it `idle`), and every single-agent session keeps exactly the pairs slice 2 pinned (the single-thread reduction). The rollup is documented; this exact fold is ours, and the archived case falls outside it — a 2026-09-12 recording has an archived session reading `terminated` while its one thread, the unarchived `idle` primary, is live by this definition, so a fold over live threads alone would have read `idle`, and nothing on the session's own list records the difference: its status events end at the `idle` pair, and the archive appended none. No recorded active session contradicts the fold outside the child resume registered below ("Session threads — a child's resume of an idle session"), so what this refutes is the fold as the whole of the reference's rule, not the fold. What produces `terminated` there is still unrecorded; this platform renders the status rather than storing it (#710) — the archive projects `terminated` from `archived_at` when a session is rendered, and `sessionStatusExpr` mirrors that rule in SQL for the one path that renders nothing first, the list's `statuses[]` filter, so every archived session the recording showed reads `terminated` here too while the column keeps the fold's own value and no settlement has to reach a state this model has no path to; that the filter agrees with the projection is ours, the recording having probed an archived session's GET, threads and events and never a list filtered by status. `user.message` addresses the primary thread (INFERRED — documented only for `system.message`; the params carry no thread field). A child-scoped `user.interrupt` is stored on the child's log **and cross-posted**, so the session view shows the client's own event with the thread named and the child's own view omits it, as the answers to a cross-posted call do (INFERRED); it renders `processed_at` set from the start — the arm that ends the child's turn is what consumes it, and no later turn of that thread would stamp it (ours). Every exec driver — the executor's tool, web and MCP passes, the API's resume arms, the `tool_exec` stop re-arm — runs only the **runnable set**: authorized calls that also meet the ordered flow's execution rule: platform calls wait for their thread's earlier calls and processed approvals, while a self-hosted worker may execute a later call once its allow confirmation is received. An unanswered ask-gated sibling never runs (the reference runner enforces authorization client-side). Sandbox tool execution across sibling threads is serial — one session-keyed `tool_exec` item at a time, the shared sandbox's — (matches the reference runner's own comment; whether the reference server serializes is INFERRED); the web and MCP drivers, which never touch the sandbox, keep their own session-keyed items and may run beside it, as they did before threads (ours). MCP servers are discovered and dialed per thread's agent, catalog rows keyed `(session, thread, server)` and a server's failure announced once per thread that declares it (ours). Outcome grading runs on the primary at the session's quiescence — the last running thread's `end_turn` — never on a child's `end_turn` alone (INFERRED — the outcome docs predate threads); a child's quiescent `end_turn` wakes the parked primary for the grading turn before the child idles, so the session never idles between. A thread-scoped interrupt does not stop the shared exec item: the drivers skip a call answered before it starts, drop a late result, and cancel an in-flight call once its use is answered (ours) — the platform executor on a per-call watch beating at `LeaseTTL/3`, reading the log under its own connection; the BYOC worker on the wire twin of that watch, walking the session's events newest-first to the call itself every ten seconds, which is the same cadence against the work API's 30s default lease TTL. Neither rides its lease keeper: the keeper would have to be told which call is running, and it already answers for the lease and the stall budget. The worker cannot drop a late result under a lock the way the executor does, so it drops it on the post instead — a refused duplicate is a skip and the pass carries on, and the refusal is confirmed by re-reading the log and by the session not being archived, rather than by the refusal's wording — the status alone is too broad, since an archived session is refused with the same 400 before any validation runs. Every one of these reads stops at the call it asks about rather than at a fixed look-back, because a thread-scoped interrupt answers a whole thread's calls in one append and no fixed window covers a wide turn's oldest; the two that are optimizations are capped, the post's is not (#441). A `tool_exec` stopped through the work API (`POST …/work/{id}/stop`, once `stopped` — by the stop itself, or by the poll that finalizes a wind-down whose worker never finished) while runnable platform calls remain is re-armed as a fresh queued item with a fresh work id, of the kind the send trigger would pick — `mcp_exec` first — under the session row lock, in the same transaction that settles the stop, serialized with the settlements that append calls; a finalizing poll hands the fresh item straight out when the pick is the worker's `tool_exec` — a web/MCP pick is the internal executor's, which no poll serves (ours; a poller sees a new item where the reference's long-lived worker never stops early — client-visible only to a one-pass worker like ours). A turn stamps its own thread's queued input only; its replay sequence watermark independently detects inputs received during the model request, including tool replies already processed by the ordered flow (ours). A child's previews reach the child's stream alone, where the event they preview lands (decision 2, as slice 2 registered). Archiving an idle child whose stop reason the session's idle pick carried — its asks in the union, its `retries_exhausted` over a sibling's `end_turn` — re-advertises the pick payload-only with what remains; one the pick never carried re-advertises nothing (ours, decision 4). A session-agent patch prunes the primary's catalog rows alone; a child's rows belong to its own snapshot (ours). *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents multiagent docs (thread status, the session rollup, `requires_action` over `budget_reached`); `user.interrupt` "If present, interrupts only the named thread" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsUserInterruptEventParams.SessionThreadID); the result events' `session_thread_id` "Copy from the … event's" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsUserCustomToolResultEvent.SessionThreadID; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsUserToolResultEvent.SessionThreadID); internal/events/status.go (TransitionThread, foldSession, PreviewTransition), toolflow.go (runnableToolUse, RunnableExecClass, ResumableThreads), route.go (RouteInbound); internal/api/events.go (the per-thread trigger arms), threadstate.go, workapi.go (stopWork); internal/brain/grader.go (settleEndTurn); internal/executor/drain.go (settleDrain, answeredWatch), mcpwork.go; internal/worker/toolexec.go (answeredWatch, answeredOnLog, postToolResult); internal/api/sessions.go (renderSession, sessionStatusExpr); internal/store/migrations/0026_work_thread.sql; TestTransitionThreadFoldsOverChildren, TestArchivesCountEachStatusMoveTheirEndingsMake, TestTransitionThreadSingleThreadIsThePair, TestMarkProcessedThroughIsThreadScoped, TestConfirmationForAThreadWhileASiblingRuns, TestDenialForAThreadWakesThatThreadOnly, TestThreadScopedInterruptLeavesTheSharedExecItem, TestInboundThreadClaimValidation, TestWorkStopReArmsForRunnableCalls, TestWorkStopWaitsForTheSessionLock, TestExecutorRunsOnlyTheRunnableSetAcrossThreads, TestAnAnsweredCallIsCancelledOnTheKeeperBeat, TestAnAnsweredCallIsCancelledOnTheWatchBeat, TestACallAnsweredWhileASiblingRanIsNeverStarted, TestAnArchivedSessionsRefusalIsNeverSkipped, TestAChildsMCPServerIsDiscoveredFromItsOwnList, TestOutcomeGradesAtSessionQuiescenceNotOnAChildsEndTurn, TestEnqueueThreadKeysTurnsAndDedupesExecItems, TestCancelThreadLeavesSiblingsAndExecItems, TestAnArchivedSessionReadsTerminated* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-12-archived-threads/batch1.json, probes `rec.archived-session.after-model.get`, `.threads` and `.events` — one archived session reading `terminated` over a single `idle`, unarchived primary, its 41-event list ending at the `idle` pair with nothing appended at the archive.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle `end_turn`'s rank in the idle fold, whether `user.message` addresses the primary thread, and whether the reference server serializes sibling threads' sandbox calls); landed for #710's read-time projection.*
- **Coordinator delegation — the six tools, the message pair, and what one settlement schedules (plan 35 slice 4, decisions 6, 7, 8, 11)** — Documented and matched: a coordinator spawns its roster's agents as threads and they report back to it; at most 25 concurrent threads per session; a finished child goes `idle`, not `terminated`, and archiving it frees the slot. Everything else is ours or inferred, except where a clause says it was recorded. **The six tools are injected by the thread's role**, never declared by an agent: `create_agent`/`send_to_agent`/`list_agents`/`wait_for_agents` on the primary thread of a session whose snapshot carries a non-empty roster, `submit_result`/`send_to_parent` on any child, neither set on a single-agent session — so a child never reaches the coordinator four whatever its own snapshot carries (depth 1), and an agent's own `custom` tool named like one of the tools that thread is *offered* is dropped with an operator note rather than shadowing the platform's, while one named like the other half — a coordinator's `submit_result`, a child's `create_agent` — keeps the name, nothing platform-side being offered under it there. All six names are nevertheless classed as settlement work inside a rostered session, and classed **before** the agent's own tools are read, so a model that calls across the line is answered `is_error` in the commit that emitted the call — a sentence naming the tool its own half provides — rather than stranded on a call no driver runs; a genuine `custom` declaration of that name still wins the class back (ours; the reference's behaviour for a cross-half call is unobserved). Their descriptions and input schemas are ours, pinned byte-for-byte because the model reads them on every replay of the thread and a description that drifts moves the request prefix; so are the answers — `create_agent` → `{"session_thread_id":"sthr_…"}`, `list_agents` → an array of `{session_thread_id, agent_name, status}` over the session's unarchived, unterminated children, each carrying a `stop_reason` as well when the child is `idle` and has one (ours — a coordinator deciding whether to re-task a child has to be able to tell one that ended its turn from one that ran out of retries), `wait_for_agents` → `{"message":…,"timed_out":bool}`, `send_to_agent`/`send_to_parent` → `Message sent.`, `submit_result` → `Result reported.`, and every `is_error` a plain sentence naming the problem. **Design C**: a delegation call persists as an ordinary `agent.tool_use` on the *calling* thread's own log, stamped `evaluated_permission:"allow"`, and is answered by an `agent.tool_result` in the very commit that emitted it (ours — a recording shows what the reference renders, never what it stores). **No events list or stream renders either row** — not the session view, not the primary thread's, not a child's own (#675). On the lists this **matches the 2026-09-02 recording**: the six names occur nowhere in `batch2.json` or `free_batch1.json`, a coordinator's list running `span.model_request_end`, `session.thread_created`, `agent.thread_message_sent` with no call between (`sessK.threads.primary.events`) and a child's reaching its `agent.thread_message_sent` the same way (`sessK.threads.child.events`), while `bash` calls still appear — a child's cross-posted ask-gated one on both its own list and the primary's (`sessK2.threads.child.events.final`, `sessK2.threads.primary.events.final`). On the session **stream** it matches the 2026-09-19 recording: `2026-09-19-self-hosted-latest-cli/ui-streams.json` `adapted-coordinator-start`, a live coordinator session stream opened before the turn, carries two spawns (`session.thread_created`, `agent.thread_message_sent`, twice) and later `bash` `agent.tool_use` rows but none of the six names (the 2026-09-02 `sessK.stream.raw` was opened after its delegation, so it shows nothing either way). The thread streams, and a refused call on any surface, are unrecorded: hiding them there is ours, by the same rule. The filter keys on the call's **type and name** alone — an `agent.tool_use` naming one of the six, so a `custom` tool of such a name stays, as does every other call — and is taken only on a session whose snapshot carries a roster, the one kind whose brain classes the six as delegation calls: all six on every thread, whether or not that thread was offered them, so a call across the roles is hidden with its `is_error` answer (the refused-call entry below). On a single-agent session one of the names is an unknown tool like any other (#567), and its surfaces list without the filter. It sits inside the list query (`events.ListQuery.HideTools`, a result matched to its call through `tool_use_id`), so a page still holds `limit` rows and its cursor skips nothing; it binds a `self_hosted` session's widened view too, which would otherwise pull a child's `send_to_parent`/`submit_result` back in. A thread's replay reads the rows unfiltered. The six join the web tools in the platform-executed predicate below, so a client `user.tool_result` naming one is a 400 and no client can forge a child's report, and both exec drivers' scans skip the six names as a stray-case guard — the answer a six-tool runner would give is "unknown tool", which commits as a result telling a coordinator its spawn failed. The same six are excluded from the **runnable classification** (`events.RunnableExecClass`, where both exec kinds and the BYOC re-arm are picked), so a stray unanswered delegation call arms no exec item at all: classed as sandbox work it would arm a `tool_exec` whose pass filters it out and whose settlement re-arms the same kind, a stop → re-arm → nothing runnable → stop loop rather than a wrong answer. **One message is two rows, neither cross-posted**: `agent.thread_message_sent` on the sender's log, `agent.thread_message_received` on the target's. **The peer names match the reference (recorded 2026-09-02, #675)**: a received row always names its sender — the coordinator too, by its session agent's name — and a sent row names its target only when the target is a child, so a message to the coordinator carries no `to_agent_name` key at all, rather than a null. Neither is cross-posted because neither type declares a `session_thread_id` for a child's row to render under on the session view, so a cross-posted one would read there as a message the primary itself exchanged; the consequence is that a coordinator's task reaches the session view as the primary's own `sent` and a child's report as the primary's `received`, while each child's half stays on its own thread's list. A spawn projects four events, in the order they are appended — `session.thread_created` and the `sent` on the primary, then `session.thread_status_running` and the `received` on a child born `running` in the same transaction as its queued turn, so no window exists in which a turn is queued for a thread that is not yet running. That order is the reference's (#675): each of the twelve recorded children's own list opens with its running and then its task. A child spawned before that fix (release 0.4.0 appended its `received` first), or by an older brain replica during a rolling upgrade, keeps the inverse order for good: the log is append-only, lists in seq order, and is not backfilled. The session view shows three of the four: the child's status event cross-posts, its `received` stays on its own list. **Committing all four together stays ours** (recorded, not chased): the reference writes the creation and the `sent` a microsecond apart, the child's running 207–357 ms after them and its `received` 130–212 ms after that, where ours commits all four in the settlement that answers the call. Two session-view orders follow. The coordinator's next `span.model_request_start` comes before the child's running on the reference for ten of the twelve children, both lone spawns included; ours writes it only after that commit, so always after the running. And across two spawns in one turn the reference's order follows its timing: four of the five recorded two-child turns read both creations and sends before either running, while 2026-09-19-self-hosted-docker's reads the first child's running before the second creation, which is the order ours always writes. **A wake writes the status before the message** (landed for #793 item 3): every wake a delivered message causes — a child's report to a coordinator `idle` on `end_turn`, a `send_to_agent` that wakes an idle child, and every ending notice that wakes a coordinator — writes the target's `session.thread_status_running`, behind a `session.status_running` when the session was idle, before the `agent.thread_message_received` the woken turn consumes. That is the order each of the ten recorded report wakes lists, on the event lists and on the live streams (2026-09-12-console-followups/streams/ui-02.sse, and the worker-wire session streams 2026-09-19-self-hosted-docker/worker-wire/0042.sse and 2026-09-19-self-hosted-latest-cli/worker-wire/0034.sse), and the order a spawn writes a child's task in. The reporting child's own `agent.thread_message_sent` stays ahead of the wake, on its own log. The `send_to_agent` and ending-notice wakes are unrecorded, so their order is the rule's prediction rather than a recording. A client's interrupt of a child is laid out by the same rule within its send: the child's idle goes with the interrupt, and the notice follows the coordinator's running event whichever of the send's events woke the coordinator — its own ending, a sibling's, or a `user.message` beside it — or, when nothing woke it, follows everything else the send writes (internal/api/events.go, processingOrder). Sessions written before the change, or by an older brain replica during a rolling upgrade, keep the message-first order (append-only). Two differences stay. Nine of the ten recorded report wakes carry a `session.status_running` this platform's cannot, because the reporting child still holds the fold at running — the shape registered as deliberate in "Session threads — a child's resume of an idle session". And the reference writes the coordinator's `span.model_request_start` before the reporting child's idle, where this platform commits the child's idle with the delivery, so the idle lands first: the same shape as the spawn's committing-together gap above. `wait_for_agents` **answers at once and parks the coordinator `idle`/`end_turn`** (W1) when something can still wake it — a child `running`, or `idle` on `requires_action` and so waiting on external input. A child at `rescheduling` is not one, since nothing resumes a thread resting there (busyChild says why; #731). A wait with no such child **and nothing already waiting to be read** answers `{"message":"No agents are running and no reports are pending.","timed_out":true}` and the turn continues. Both halves are required: input already waiting — a report past the head this turn replayed, or a client's `user.message` that landed mid-turn and enqueued nothing — is answered with the wait-started payload instead, whether or not a child is still running, and parks nothing, because the turn chains and reads it. Telling a coordinator that no reports are pending while one sits unread on its log would be an invitation to conclude on work it has not seen. Having something to read beats having something to wait for, on the reasoning that a park is only ever safe when something else will move the thread. Several waits in one turn share the first's verdict. **The commit schedules what follows**: a turn whose every call was a delegation call, with no park and no report, hands its own work item straight back for the caller's next turn (a fresh enqueue would be swallowed by this item's live slot); a turn that also holds a call someone else must answer uses the shared ordered tool flow — `running` for executable platform work, `idle/requires_action` for external input — and resumes the model only after all its calls settle. A `wait_for_agents` sharing that turn cannot replace the tool wait with a parking `end_turn`; delegation calls still execute inline, with no confirmation gate of their own. The two settlements that do idle the thread — a parking wait, and the `submit_result` that ended the turn — first make the chain-or-idle check every other terminal settlement makes, so input that arrived past the turn's head is read rather than stranded on a thread nothing will wake: the message a coordinator sent a `running` child, which decision 6 promises it "reads at its next settle", is read at exactly that settle. A `submit_result` sharing its turn with a call someone else must answer is an `is_error` ("report after your tool calls have returned") and ends nothing. `send_to_agent` addresses by thread id, else by agent name when exactly one live child runs it — none, or more than one, is an `is_error` naming the candidates, and the coordinator's own id is refused; a target `idle` on `retries_exhausted` is an `is_error` too, because nothing re-enqueues such a thread and an acknowledgement would be a lie the coordinator acts on. A delivered message flips a target `idle` on `end_turn` — or on no reason at all — to `running` and enqueues its turn in the same transaction; a target on `requires_action` receives without waking, its own resumption replaying the message, and a `running` one chains at its next settle, detected by **seq** rather than `processed_at` — exact, since a report past the settling turn's watermark cannot have been consumed by it, and it still finds a report a build before #793 stamped at write. Two reports therefore cost one queued turn. A child's ending is delivered to the coordinator as `agent.thread_message_received` text on the primary's log, a bracketed line naming the agent and the next action open to it — its turn out of retries, an interrupt, an archive, or a turn that simply ended calling no `submit_result` (INFERRED, and ours: the reference documents no notice for a child that stops without reporting, but a coordinator parked on a wait has no other trigger, and decision 7's W1 exists precisely to leave no thread parked on something that will never come). Every ending — out of retries, ended without reporting, interrupted, archived — also **wakes** a coordinator `idle` on `end_turn` when the ending child was the last one it could have been waiting for: a wait parks only while some child is *busy* (`running`, or `idle` on `requires_action` — one that will still report once its human answers), so an ending that takes the last busy child away is the last chance to wake a coordinator parked on it, and a notice delivered without that wake would sit unread on a session folding idle with no turn coming. The two exclusions are as load-bearing as the wake: an ending that leaves another busy child behind wakes nothing (that child's own report or ending will), and neither does one that ends a child nothing was waiting on — an `idle` one a client tidies away — since a coordinator with no busy child was never parked on a wait at all (such a wait never parks — it times out in-commit, or chains on the input it has to read), and a turn it did not ask for costs a model call, blocks the session's own archive behind it, and puts the archive path's idle re-advertisement (decision 4's pick) out of reach. A `running` coordinator is not woken either — its own settlement chains on the notice by seq — and one on `requires_action` stays parked until its human answers. The wake is written **before** the ending thread's own transition, so the session never folds idle between the two; on the archive path that puts the coordinator's notice ahead of the child's closing results and its `session.thread_status_terminated`. A session-wide interrupt says nothing at all and wakes nothing: it ends the primary in the same batch. The 25-cap counts unarchived, non-terminated threads with the primary among them, so the spawn that would make a 26th is the `is_error`. Replay renders a received message as a **user**-role text block prefixed `[message from <name>]` (`your coordinator` when the sender is the primary, whatever the row's `from_agent_name` holds — a row written before #675 carries a null there and one written since the coordinator's name, and both render byte-identically, so naming the coordinator on the wire moved no child's request prefix) and renders a sent one as nothing — the sender's own call and its answer already carry that text — and the grader's transcript renders neither. Model-supplied text is sanitized and capped at 16 KiB before it reaches jsonb. Skills materialize as decision 11's **union**, the coordinator's own references then each member's in roster order, deduplicated by skill **id** with the first reference winning rather than by `(id, version)`: a skill's landing directory carries no version, so a member pinning a different version of a skill the coordinator also names runs the coordinator's. The 500-skill cap is still checked per agent spec rather than over the union, so a full roster can materialize more than 500 skills into one sandbox. **A run of consecutive settlement-only chains is bounded at 25 (ours, #442)**: such a turn has nothing to wait for, so it hands its own `model_turn` straight back with no delay, and every shape that reaches it — `list_agents`, a `wait_for_agents` answered `timed_out`, a `create_agent` the 25-thread cap refused, `send_to_parent` — is one a model repeats and none of which wakes another thread — a successful `create_agent`, `send_to_agent` or `send_to_parent` scheduled somebody else’s work, so it resets the count rather than adding to it; unbounded, the thread never idles, so the fold keeps the session `running`, archive and delete refuse it, and (`Claim` orders by `created_at`, which a requeue does not move) it starves every other session's turn on a single-brain deployment. At the cap the thread idles `end_turn` and carries, on its own log, a `session.error` of the platform-coined type `delegation_chain_exhausted_error` with `retry_status: exhausted` — an `end_turn` alone is indistinguishable from a coordinator that finished; on a child that error reads on the child’s stream, as a child’s failed turn already does, and the session view sees the thread’s status change. The chain this bounds counts a turn of any of three shapes the same way — a genuine delegation call, a name the model was never offered, or a call to the other half its own role was never offered (the MCP-offering entry below is what the first two look like in the log) — whatever mix a run holds, because every one of them is a turn resolved entirely by settlement-owned answers, with no external input and no newly woken thread, which is what the cap is for; the message describes that condition rather than naming a kind, for the same reason. A message, or a report from a child, resumes it; and a cut **child** delivers its coordinator the ending notice every other ending owes it, waking a parked one, so the cut cannot leave the W1 wedge behind. Input arriving mid-turn chains past the cap and resets the count, since the bound is for a thread nobody is feeding — which also means a peer agent’s message resets it, so two agents looping messages at each other are not bounded here — that is the session delegation bound recorded above, which counts on the sessions row precisely because this count cannot survive the idle a peer’s wake goes Child lists: batch2.json `sessK.threads.child.events` and `sessK2.threads.child.events.final`; child-lifecycle setup.json `alpha-persisted-events-after-interrupt` and `beta-persisted-events-after-interrupt`; console-followups setup.json `control.events.rec78f-alpha` and `control.events.rec78f-beta`; custom-mixed-tools setup.json `children.completed.rec19mixed-child-alpha.events` and `children.completed.rec19mixed-child-beta.events`; and, their names being shared, self-hosted-docker ui-network.json idx 192 and 198 and self-hosted-latest-cli ui-network.json idx 150 and 166.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle what the reference answers a cross-half call, whether it notices a child that stops without reporting, and how it bounds a run of settlement-only chains).*
- **Refused delegation calls — hidden like the rest (#675)** — A delegation call the settlement refuses is answered `is_error` in the commit that emitted it (the Coordinator delegation entry), and the list filter keys on the call's name alone, never on `is_error`, so no events list or stream renders either half of it. A failed spawn therefore leaves nothing on the wire: no call, no answer, and no `session.thread_created`, because no thread was made — a client learns of it only from what the agent says next, while the coordinator's replay still carries both rows. One refusal is recorded, and matches: sessK2's coordinator, told to message a child the client had archived through the threads archive route (`sessK2.threads.child.archive`), relayed an "Agent is archived" error in its own `agent.message`, and its list shows no call and no result between that turn's two model requests (`sessK2.send.message-archived-subagent`, `sessK2.events.after-archived-subagent-message`). The rest — a `create_agent` naming no roster member or past the 25-thread cap, an input missing a required field, a call to the other role's half — are unrecorded, so hiding them is INFERRED from that one refusal and from every successful call being hidden. *Evidence: 2026-09-02 recording (`batch2.json`, `free_batch1.json`: none of the six names occurs; FINDINGS.md §B items 1 and 4); internal/events/log.go (ListQuery.HideTools); internal/brain/delegate.go (createAgent, sendToAgent, wrongRole); TestNoEventsSurfaceRendersADelegationCall, TestListHideTools.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording of a refused spawn and of a call across the roles would show whether the reference renders either half); landed for #675.*
- **The `self_hosted` session view, and the coordinator-mode BYOC worker (plan 35 slice 4, decision 13)** — On a session whose environment is `self_hosted`, the session-level events list and stream — and the primary thread's, which are the same surface — carry every child thread's `agent.tool_use`, whatever its evaluated permission, and the `user.tool_result` / `agent.tool_result` answering it, on top of the documented condensed view — all but a delegation call and its answer, which no surface renders (the Coordinator delegation entry's Design C). Cloud sessions are unchanged, a child's own surface is unchanged, and the widening is a predicate on the environment kind over the one stored row rather than a second write. The docs are silent on self-hosted multiagent sessions; **a recording (2026-09-19) shows the widening**, and no refusal: the reference ran a coordinator on a `self_hosted` environment, and its session view carries each child's `bash` `agent.tool_use`, stamped `allow`, and the worker's `user.tool_result` answering it, all naming the child's `session_thread_id`, while none of the six delegation names occurs anywhere in the recording. What stays INFERRED is the widening past that sample: a child's call stamped `deny`, and an `agent.tool_result` answering a child's call — the recorded view held neither. One rendering asymmetry rides along, and is deliberate rather than a slip: the widened `agent.tool_use` and `user.tool_result` name the child with `session_thread_id` exactly as a cross-posted ask does on cloud, and the `agent.tool_result` does **not**, because the SDK's event declares no such field — so an interrupt's synthesized error result reaches the session view without naming its thread. (The reference runner marks answered only from the user-side results, so that synthesized result is invisible to it and it re-executes the interrupted call on reconnect; that is already today's behaviour on every single-agent `self_hosted` session, #429, and the view rule neither causes nor cures it.) **The BYOC worker stays thread-unaware**: the roster on the session it already loads for its liveness gate is the whole of what it learns about threads. A coordinator session makes its scan drop the #76 trailing-turn bound and walk to the log's start — that bound's two premises hold per thread, not per session, so a runnable call can sit arbitrarily far down with an answered pair above it and no stop condition short of the start finds it — and makes the run re-scan after answering the found set until a pass comes back empty, because a sibling thread can commit calls while this item is live and its enqueue is a no-op against it. The sandbox, skills and files are provisioned once ahead of that loop however many passes run. Single-agent sessions keep one bounded pass exactly. The scan also took the runnable narrowing that slice 3 gave the platform's own drivers, and on every session rather than coordinator ones alone: an `agent.tool_use` whose `evaluated_permission` is `ask` needs an `allow` `user.tool_confirmation` naming it, and one stamped `deny` runs on no confirmation at all, an absent field reading as allow the way the platform's SQL COALESCEs it — so a turn suspended on two asks and released one at a time no longer runs the one no human answered. The two halves of this scan change therefore have different scopes on purpose: the walk to the log's start and the re-scan are a coordinator session's alone, while the ask gate binds **every** `self_hosted` session, single-agent included, because a turn released one confirmation at a time is a single-agent shape too and running the unreleased sibling is a permission bypass wherever it happens. Delegation names are skipped beside the web names, for the same reason — feeding one to the six-tool runner would post an unknown-tool result the control plane then refuses — as a backstop now: a current control plane lists no delegation call a worker could run (the Coordinator delegation entry), and the check is kept for an older one that does. Our worker still ends with the item it claimed — one pass on a single-agent session, as many as find work on a coordinator's — where the reference's is a stream tail held until `MaxIdle`; client-side behaviour, recorded beside the #383 precedent, not a wire mismatch. *Evidence: `agent.tool_use`'s `session_thread_id`, "Empty on the thread's own events", and `agent.tool_result`, which declares none (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolUseEvent.SessionThreadID and BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolResultEvent); `user.tool_result`'s, "Copy from the `agent.tool_use` event's" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsUserToolResultEvent.SessionThreadID); the reference runner marks answered from the user-side results only (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasessiontoolrunner.go SessionToolRunner.reconcile); platform.claude.com managed-agents self-hosted-sandboxes + multiagent docs (the condensed session view; silent on the two together); internal/events/log.go (ListQuery.ThreadToolCalls, threadToolCallTypes); internal/api/events.go (sessionView, eventWire, threadAddressable), threads.go (resolveThreadEvents); internal/worker/toolexec.go (RunSessionTools, unansweredToolUses), lease.go (sessionLive); TestSelfHostedSessionViewCarriesChildToolCalls, TestCloudSessionViewOmitsChildToolCalls, TestSessionViewWithThreadToolCalls, TestSelfHostedSessionViewHidesAChildsDelegationCalls, TestSessionLiveReportsCoordinatorMode, TestCoordinatorScanWalksTheWholeLog, TestRescanIsCoordinatorOnly, TestAskGatedUseWaitsForItsVerdict, TestCoordinatorSkipsDelegationCalls, TestScanIsBoundedByTheTrailingTurn* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-19-self-hosted-docker/setup.json, probes `multiagent.events.during-worker-execution`, `multiagent.events.after-child-results` and `multiagent.events.final-before-archive` — the session view of `fixture.multiagent-session`, a coordinator with a two-agent roster on the `self_hosted` environment, listing both children's `session.thread_created`, their `allow`-stamped `bash` calls and the `user.tool_result` answering each; no delegation name occurs in any file of that directory (FINDINGS.md, "Coordinator and children on the self-hosted environment").* *Tracked: #78 (a `self_hosted` coordinator session whose child makes a `deny`-stamped call, or one the reference answers itself with an `agent.tool_result`, would settle whether the reference's view carries those too).*
- **POST /v1/skills (+ …/versions) — upload-form detection and rejection surface** — **The zip form is detected here by magic bytes on a single files[] part (PK\x03\x04); the reference selects archive handling by the filename's extension instead, and a recording has now refuted the inference.** A four-cell matrix separates the three candidate signals: the same archive bytes are expanded under `archive.zip` and refused under `archive.txt` with `SKILL.md not found in uploaded files`, while plain Markdown named `archive.zip` or `archive.skill` is *attempted* as an archive and fails with `archive must be a readable, unencrypted zip using "DEFLATE" or "STORE" compression`. The extension alone picks the handler and `.skill` sits in the archive set beside `.zip`; the declared type never moved that choice (`text/plain` rode both an expanded archive and a refused one), and the leading bytes decide only whether the unzip the extension already committed to then succeeds. The outcomes part in one direction only: a real archive named `archive.txt` we expand and the reference refuses. Its mirror agrees by accident — a plain manifest named `skill.zip` fails the reference's unzip and fails our path-qualification rule instead, so both 400 for unrelated reasons, and the reason ours gives is #630's flat-basename divergence rather than this one. The mechanism is #630's to converge or register. A files[] part without a Content-Disposition filename is rejected 400 on both routes, `files[].0: Expected UploadFile, received: <class 'str'>` — neither tolerated nor named `anonymous_file`, and since #540 in those words here too, the index counting files[] parts (only 0 is recorded); flat basenames, mixed top-level directories, and the other frontmatter/size violations reject with this platform's own 400 messages. The reference's own strings were unobserved until the console-cookie lane reached the upload form, and its frontmatter length pair reads `` `name` and `description` must resolve from `SKILL.md` frontmatter or its fallbacks, within their length limits ``, refusing a 65-character name and a 1,025-character description against caps of 64 and 1024 that this platform already shares — the sentence both refusals answer here since #540, the field and its cap going to the operator's log. Its total-size limit is still unstressed. **A frontmatter with no `name` is accepted, the name falling back to the skill directory's**: `rec-followup-upload/SKILL.md` carrying only a description became a version named `rec-followup-upload` on both routes. This platform refused it with `SKILL.md frontmatter is missing name` until #842 and now resolves it the same way. The fallback is the directory as the directory-name rule reads it — lower case, `_` as `-` — so a directory that would name a SKILL.md carrying a name names one without it (`My_Skill/` is `my-skill`), an inference, the recorded directory being lower-case already; it is held to every rule a frontmatter name meets (the pattern, the 64-character cap and its sentence, the reserved words), and a refusal of the pattern or a reserved word adds that the name was taken from the directory, in this platform's words. The sentence's "or its fallbacks" suggests a description has one too, but no upload lacking a description was recorded, so that refusal keeps this platform's words (`SKILL.md frontmatter is missing description`). Unknown multipart parts are **ignored** rather than rejected (plan 39 decision 8), which is what the reference does: a create carrying a stray `display_title` part succeeded, with the name derived from the frontmatter as if the part were absent. That rested on exactly one field name until 2026-09-12 put two more through both routes — parts named `description` and `xyzzy` are each accepted and ignored, the version's description still resolving from frontmatter — so the tolerance now rests on a sample of three names rather than on one, which is a weaker extrapolation and not yet a rule about every part name, and the entry it replaces went the other way, rejecting unknown parts like unknown JSON keys, which special-casing `display_title` alone would only have half-corrected. `files[]` is both required and the exact part name: a part named bare `files`, and a form carrying no file part at all, each answer `files[]: Field required`, which this platform now returns verbatim. A zip whose root `SKILL.md` entry carries non-regular Unix type bits (a symlink or FIFO entry) is rejected 400 (plan 35 slice 0) — a symlink in the reference's words since #540, below, a FIFO in ours, `SKILL.md must be a regular file`: extraction — the reference worker's (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.63.0 — skillarchive.go zipEntryIsPlain) and this platform's alike — skips such a member, so the archive would validate and then materialize without its manifest. **The reference splits the pair our one predicate joins**: it refuses a symlink manifest, with `archives must not contain symbolic links` — naming the archive rather than the file, and by its wording refusing a symlink anywhere in it, where ours checks only the manifest — and **accepts a FIFO one**, the version's name and description resolving from the manifest bytes. Ours is therefore one rejection wider than the reference, on a shape only a crafted upload produces. Only the reference's *upload* was exercised, so what its hosted materialization then does with the FIFO manifest it accepted — the concern this rule was written for, and the one the worker's source answers only for the worker — is still unobserved, and the symlink result must not be generalized to every non-regular Unix file type. A version stored before this rule with such a manifest (a deliberately crafted upload only) now materializes without its `SKILL.md`, and nothing re-validates stored archives. *Evidence: docs/plan/06_skills.md "Multipart upload" + "Recording checklist"; the 2026-09-04 recording [5] (the ignored `display_title` part), [8] (a bare `files` part), [11] (no file part at all); docs/plan/39_skills-ga-shape.md decision 8; skills-guide upload rules; internal/api/skillsupload.go, internal/skills* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-12-followups/skills-api.json [26..33] (the extension/MIME/magic matrix, corroborating 2026-09-05/batch1[8..11] and extending it to the version route), [2, 12] (the unnamed part), [3, 4, 13, 14, 22, 23] (`description` and `xyzzy` ignored), [5, 6, 15, 16, 24] (the symlink refusal and the FIFO acceptance, against hashed zip fixtures), [8, 9, 18, 19] (the frontmatter length refusals), [7, 17, 25] (the directory-name fallback); ui-network.json [6] (the native version request, `files[]` only, no display_name); 2026-09-12-skill-directory/ui-network.json [12] (a dropped directory sent as separate path-bearing parts, not zipped client-side).* *Tracked: #78 (whether the reference's extraction skips a FIFO manifest, its total-size limit, and what a SKILL.md lacking a description resolves to), #630 (converging or registering the extension-versus-magic detection and the FIFO width); landed for #54 and #842.*
- **GET …/versions/{version}/content — the response headers** — The download responds with Content-Type: application/zip and Content-Length and streams the stored archive verbatim (the SDK sends Accept: application/binary and treats the body as opaque). The reference's own media type and length on this route are still unrecorded — the 2026-09-04 recording captured each download's bytes, its zip members and its `Content-Disposition`, but not those two headers. What it does settle is the route's shape: `/content` is served on the bare path and on `?beta=true` alike, so it is not beta-only server-side. It also settles one header this platform had not been sending: every recorded download carried `Content-Disposition: attachment; filename*=utf-8''<skill name>.zip`, and plan 39 decision 13 now sends exactly that — the version's own immutable slug plus `.zip`, carried only as an RFC 5987 `filename*` with no bare `filename` fallback beside it, which is why `mime.FormatMediaType` is not usable (it reaches for the extended form only when it finds a non-ASCII value). Nothing between these two processes reads it — the SDK's `Download` hands back an opaque body and the BYOC worker names its own temp file — so it buys a browser save its filename and nothing else; a recorded header is copied because matching one costs less than carrying the explanation for its absence. This platform additionally sends `x-skill-archive-sha256`, the lowercase-hex digest the registry recorded at upload, so the BYOC worker — which never touches the database — can verify what it downloaded before extracting it into a sandbox; the header is omitted for a version whose row predates that column. That is an additive platform surface in the mould of the CONFIRMED traceparent-on-/work/poll entry: reference clients ignore unknown headers, so nothing on the wire breaks in either direction. Whether the reference advertises a digest of its own, and under what name, is what stays inferred; RFC 9530 `Repr-Digest` was considered and rejected as a partial implementation of a standard nothing between these two processes consumes. **Two halves of the entry this replaces are retired rather than answered.** It had a skill whose every version was deleted rendering `latest_version` as the empty string, and asked what the reference echoes in that state; the state is now unreachable through either API — the reference refuses to delete a skill's only version, and this platform refuses too (plan 39 decision 6). It is not unreachable in a *database*, though, and the entry that claimed otherwise was wrong: the previous release allowed that delete and left `latest_version` NULL, and migration 0033 drops an index and touches no rows, so a skill carrying zero versions survives an upgrade across this change. Such a row renders `latest_version_id` as the empty string while `/versions/latest` answers 404, until a version is added to it — which is the recovery, and the reason 0033 does not delete the row. So `renderSkill`'s placeholder is retained for a legacy row rather than for a state the API can still reach, and the docs' "Always set" holds for every skill this platform creates. And the `{version}` slot's accepted forms are no longer a question: they are recorded, and the divergence they leave is the CONFIRMED entry on the numeric and `skillver_` aliases. *Evidence: the 2026-09-04 recording [134, 135] (`/content` on both paths), [134, 137, 138] (the `Content-Disposition` and the zip members), [111] (the only-version refusal); docs/plan/06_skills.md "Endpoints" + "Recording checklist"; docs/plan/39_skills-ga-shape.md decision 6; Download, Accept header (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaskillversion.go BetaSkillVersionService.Download); internal/api/skills.go (the content handler's four headers, skillContentDisposition), internal/skills/extract.go ArchiveDigestHeader* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the reference's own Content-Type and Content-Length on this route, and whether it advertises an archive digest and under what name); landed for #54.*
- **Agent skills[] refs — version accepted on both anthropic and custom types** — The managed-agents docs' field table marks version "Custom skills only", but the SDK gives both union variants the same optional Version ("Version to pin. Defaults to latest if omitted.") and both resolved response types require it — so validation accepts version on both types (as parseSkills always has). Docs-vs-SDK discrepancy; a recording decides. *Evidence: checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAnthropicSkillParams.Version and BetaManagedAgentsCustomSkillParams.Version; platform.claude.com managed-agents/skills field table* *Tracked: #78 (the recording that decides the docs-vs-SDK split over version on anthropic-type skill refs); entry landed for #54.*
- **Skill references — the 500-skills-per-session cap's reject shape** — The 500-skills-per-session cap value is documented, but the reference's reject status and message for exceeding it are unrecorded — this platform answers 400 invalid_request_error. The 2026-09-04 recording never built a session carrying 500 references, so it settles nothing here. (The other half of the entry this replaces — whether the reference also accepts a dangling skill reference at create — the recording does settle, in the opposite direction to the guess, and it is now the CONFIRMED entry on a dangling reference at agent create.) *Evidence: docs/plan/06_skills.md design decision 7 + slice-4 inferences; docs/plan/39_skills-ga-shape.md § "Out of scope"; internal/api/wire.go parseSkills* *Tracked: #78 (the reference's reject status and message for a session exceeding the 500-skill cap); landed for #54.*
- **Level-1 skill injection — the reference's template is recorded, ours is unaligned; only the placement stays inferred** — The reference includes skill metadata "in the system prompt" at startup and notes skills add "instructions and metadata" to the context window, and no *source* captures the exact template (the SDK worker emits no prompt text; claude-code-source is a design reference for budgeting, not wire behavior) — but a 2026-09-03 recording does, quoted below, so the template is no longer inferred and only its placement in the prompt is. This platform's brain resolves each `agent.skills[]` reference at request-assembly time three ways — the literal `latest` through the skill's latest version, a `skver_`/`skillver_` id through its own row, and a digit version verbatim — reads name/description from the resolved version, and appends a block — a lead line plus one `name - description (skills/<dir>/SKILL.md)` bullet per skill, `<dir>` being the materialization directory (`skills.TargetDir`, so the injected path matches where the executor/worker land the files) — after the agent's own system prompt and before any runtime `system.message` text. An unresolvable reference (dangling id/version, or a store blip) is a logged miss counted by `skills.resolve.misses`, never fatal to the turn — the same late-bound tolerance materialization applies. The block wording and whether the reference names a path at all were inferences until this recording closed both; its placement relative to runtime system messages still is, the model having reported the block's content and not its position. *Evidence: platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/skills ("included in the system prompt"; context-window cost note); docs/plan/06_skills.md "Level-1 injection is brain-side and required"; internal/brain/skills.go (resolveSkillsBlock, renderSkillsBlock), internal/brain/replay.go (buildRequest placement)* **A session was asked, and it answered.** An agent carrying the `xlsx` and `pdf` anthropic skills returned a block headed **"Skills usage policy"**, containing a `<system-reminder>` tag and an `<available_skills>` list whose entries are `<skill><name>…</name><description>…</description></skill>`, and stating that skills are available under `/workspace/skills/`. So the reference's template is XML-tagged rather than prose, and level-1 injection is **not defended**: the model quoted its own instructions on request, which settles the level-1 half of the injection question in the plainest way available. **Ours matches the template not at all, and names the same place by another route.** `renderSkillsBlock` emits a prose lead (`Available skills. Each skill's full instructions live in its SKILL.md file…`) and `name - description (skills/<dir>/SKILL.md)` bullets, the ` - description` dropped when it is empty — markdown rather than tags. The path half is thinner than it first looked: the bullet is relative, but relative to the sandbox workdir, which the built-in file tools (`toolset.resolve` joins any non-absolute path onto it) and bash (the exec's working directory) both resolve against, and which defaults to `sandbox.DefaultWorkdir` = `/workspace` — so a path copied verbatim out of the block lands on exactly the `/workspace/skills/<dir>/SKILL.md` the reference advertises, and the difference is one of expression, not of place. Nor could it simply be made absolute: the workdir is executor/worker configuration (`EXECUTOR_WORKDIR`/`WORKER_WORKDIR`) and the brain holds no value for it. What argues for changing it anyway is that the sibling files, repository and memory blocks all name absolute paths, leaving skills the odd one out. #581 owns choosing between adopting the template and registering ours, and correcting `skills.go`'s comment that "the exact reference template is captured by no source", which this recording falsifies. The path the block names is true of a real directory — `/workspace/skills/` holds the *extracted* `pdf/` and `xlsx/` trees, root-owned and not a symlink — while the *zips* live at `/mnt/skills` (`pdf.zip` 21,600 bytes, `xlsx.zip` 154,243 bytes, owned `claude:ubuntu`), which is the read-only FUSE mount the file-mount entry records. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `session.events.list.skill-prompt` and `session.events.list.skill-paths`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §H.* *Tracked: #581 (the template, and whether the bullet should name the workdir-absolute path its sibling blocks do; the block's placement within the system prompt is still inferred, since the model reported its content and not its position); landed for #54.*
- **GET /v1/sessions/{id}/events — limit cap** — maxEventLimit = 1000 for the session-events list, as the deployment-runs, files, session-resources, skills and threads lists also cap at 1000; the rest cap at 100. Chosen because 1-to-1000 is a list convention on the reference (documented on several SDK list params, though the agents list and several other managed-agents lists document a maximum of 100), the real ant beta:worker requests exactly limit=1000 (a 100 cap 400-rejected it and broke the slice-8 acceptance), and the SDK's event-list param documents no explicit cap. A compatible upper bound, not a proven reference cap; some cap is needed as a query-cost guard. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "Slice-8 acceptance — real `ant beta:worker` end to end (run 2026-07-16) — ✅ passed" (slice-8 acceptance record, bug found and fixed); the list-param docs — 1 to 1000, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betafile.go BetaFileListParams.Limit and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaskill.go BetaSkillListParams.Limit, a maximum of 100, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaAgentListParams.Limit, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeployment.go BetaDeploymentListParams.Limit and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betavault.go BetaVaultListParams.Limit, and no cap, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaSessionEventListParams.Limit; the SDK's SessionToolRunner, which `ant beta:worker` runs, listing a session's events at limit=1000, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessiontoolrunner.go SessionToolRunner.reconcile.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the reference's own cap on this event list, which our 1000 infers from the 1-to-1000 convention several SDK list params document).*
- **POST /v1/sessions/{id}/events — duplicate tool-result re-delivery** — A tool result must reference an existing, kind-matching, not-yet-answered tool use or the send is a 400 — so a worker that loses the HTTP response and re-delivers an identical result gets a 400 rather than a benign success (re-delivery is not idempotent). Whether the real backend rejects or accepts-and-dedups a duplicate reference is explicitly unresolved: neither the SDK types nor the ant CLI reveals it; settling needs a recording against a real managed-agents endpoint. The strict reading is taken because a rejected send is recoverable and a poisoned append-only log is not. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/events` + events API — append-only log, send/list, SSE stream (slice 3)" ('Known cost, unresolved against the reference')* *Tracked: #58.*

- **POST /v1/sessions/{id}/events — a client result for a platform-executed web call** — A `user.tool_result` whose referenced `agent.tool_use` names a web tool (`web_search`/`web_fetch`) is a 400 even while the call is still unanswered — under environment credentials, the only ones that reach the check, since a management credential is refused 403 first (the entry "POST /v1/sessions/{id}/events — who may post a `user.tool_result`") — because web calls are platform-executed on both environment kinds (`queue.Claim` restricts only `tool_exec` to cloud environments — `web_exec` is served to the platform executor for self_hosted sessions too), so the platform's web driver is their only legitimate writer, and accepting a client's answer inside the executor's scan-to-commit window would land two answers for one `tool_use_id` on the append-only log (#222). The reference's behavior is unobserved — the public docs (managed-agents self-hosted-sandboxes / tools / events-and-streaming, checked 2026-08-01) document no result-validation rules, and the reference's own worker never sends one: its session tool runner answers by per-name registration and posts no result for a name outside the six sandbox tools it registers, logging "tool not owned by this runner; leaving the tool_use_id pending for its owner" — the mechanism the plan-15 acceptance run measured (docs/HISTORY.md), independent of any enqueue ordering — so, like the duplicate-re-delivery 400 above, the strict reading is taken: a rejected send is recoverable, a poisoned log is not. The pre-existing already-answered arm still runs first (a late re-delivery reads "already has a result", exactly as before), and the sandbox six stay client-answerable via `user.tool_result` — the BYOC pull protocol is untouched. The documented escape for a genuinely stuck web call remains `user.interrupt`. *Evidence: internal/events/toolflow.go (ValidateToolResults, the platformOwned arm); internal/api/events.go (platformExecuted, the predicate injected — since plan 35 slice 4 the delegation six share it, above); docs/plan/16_one-answer-per-tool-call.md* *Tracked: #78 (only a probe under environment credentials, answering a web call still unanswered, can settle whether the reference refuses a client result for a call it executes itself: its credential gate refuses a management-auth result before the tool is consulted, and the one recorded attempt, `sessT.send.user.tool_result.for-platform-call`, drew that 403 under a Console session while naming a `bash` call answered minutes earlier rather than the `web_search` one beside it); landed for #222.*
- **POST /v1/sessions/{id}/events — an environment credential's `user.tool_result` on a `cloud` session** — No request reaches the batch. A `cloud` environment's key, which the console issues as the reference's does (*The console-API dialect's edges*), is refused at the lane with a 404 before the session is read (*A `cloud` or archived environment's key — what it reaches*), and a sessions token never names a `cloud` session, since only a poll mints one and a poll serves `self_hosted` work alone. Behind both, the environment-kind check `NormalizeInbound` makes after the credential check in the same arm — a 400 `invalid_request_error`, "user.tool_result is only valid on self_hosted environments" — stays as a backstop. Since #662 only an environment credential could reach it, the credential check refusing every other caller first (the entry "POST /v1/sessions/{id}/events — who may post a `user.tool_result`"). What the reference answers such a request is unobserved, because every recorded `cloud` probe was a Console session its own gate refused before the environment was consulted. *Evidence: internal/api/envauth.go requireEnvironmentKeyForSession; internal/events/inbound.go normalizeOne; internal/events/inbound_edge_test.go; internal/api/events_test.go TestToolResultOnACloudSession (a key issued for a cloud environment, refused at the lane).* *Tracked: #78 (an environment credential posting a result on a cloud session would settle what the reference answers).*
- **Replay — the tool_use id the model sees** — tool_use blocks are rebuilt under their sevt_ event ids; the provider-side tool id is discarded at emission and result events reference the event id. An assumption to revisit against observable reference behavior. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/brain` + `internal/queue` + state machine — the orchestration loop (slice 5)" (brain/replay.go), docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/brain` + `internal/queue` + state machine — the orchestration loop (slice 5)" (slice-5 'documented assumptions, revisit against reference behavior when observable')* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference replays the provider-side tool id rather than the sevt_ event id we substitute).*
- **session.status_idle stop_reason for max_tokens / stop_sequence** — Provider stop reasons max_tokens and stop_sequence settle the session as end_turn — the brain flattens every non-tool_use stop reason to end_turn in v1 (the provider-level distinction is telemetry-only), and the same is true of the remaining values the published enum carries: pause_turn (no paused server-tool loop is continued), refusal (see the next entry) and model_context_window_exceeded. **Scope: a turn that called no tool** — a response carrying tool blocks is classified on the blocks, not on this label (next entry, #181). Assumption about how the reference maps model stop reasons onto session stop_reason. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/brain` + `internal/queue` + state machine — the orchestration loop (slice 5)"; docs/history/2026-07.md § "OpenAI-compatible adapter (`internal/provider/openai`)" (openai adapter mapping note); platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/handling-stop-reasons (the seven-value quick-reference table)* **The `max_tokens` arm is settled, and the alternative this entry offered turns out not to exist.** A recorded haiku session asked to print 1..200000 stopped on a round `output_tokens` of 64000 with its single `agent.message` truncated mid-token at 118,616 characters, and both `session.thread_status_idle.stop_reason` and `session.status_idle.stop_reason` read `{"type":"end_turn"}` — the reference flattens, as this platform does. The second half of the question was whether the provider-level distinction survives as telemetry instead. **It survives nowhere**: `span.model_request_end`'s key set is exactly `id, is_error, model_request_start_id, model_usage, processed_at, type`, which is exactly what `ModelRequest.EndEvent` emits here, so nothing on the wire records that the turn was cut off and a client must infer it from the token count sitting on the ceiling and the text ending mid-token. `stop_sequence` and `refusal` remain unobserved as stop reasons; `model_context_window_exceeded` is now known not to appear as one either, because such a turn terminates rather than idles (the retry-semantics entry).
