One shape worth matching deliberately came with it: `span.model_request_end.model_usage` uses a flat `cache_creation_input_tokens` while `session.usage.usage` uses a nested `cache_creation: {ephemeral_1h_input_tokens, ephemeral_5m_input_tokens}` — the two coexist on one session, which is the `cache-creation-usage-bucket` item #78's checklist marks recorded-but-unverified. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probe `session.events.list.stop-reason`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §B.* *Tracked: #78 (whether `stop_sequence` and `refusal` flatten the same way).*
- **Turn classification — the tool blocks, not the stop_reason label** — A response carrying tool blocks is a tool turn whatever stop reason came with it (max_tokens, stop_sequence, model_context_window_exceeded, a non-compliant end_turn): the brain suspends on the blocks and routes their intents exactly as a tool_use stop's. **refusal is the one carve-out** — its blocks are dropped and the turn settles on its text alone (the SDK client goes further, see below). Classifying on the label instead strands the call, since `turnEvents` commits one intent per block either way: the session idles with a tool_use nothing enqueues and no result answers (#181). Reachability is not hypothetical **on either side of the seam**: the docs put an *incomplete* tool block under `max_tokens` and `refusal` under a normal 200, while pairing a *complete* client call with tool_use — "A response that leaves a client tool_use block waiting on you never has a stop_reason of `pause_turn`: when Claude stops to call your tools, stop_reason is `tool_use`", this entry's own strongest counter-evidence — and design principle 4 points this provider at **any** endpoint speaking Messages, which need not honor that pairing at all (see the provider/openai stop_reason entry above). Nothing in the schema ties the label to the blocks either. Three rejected alternatives stay recorded here — comment and changelog both defer to this file for them: failing the turn as the converse tool_use-without-blocks guard does (it discards a complete, well-formed call, making a recoverable turn a session.error); dropping the blocks for *every* stop reason (the same loss, silently — right only for refusal, where it is done); and answering a refusal's intents with synthesized is_error results as a denied confirmation does (a denial is the user's decision the model should see, a refusal is the model's own; telling it the call errored invites a retry of the refused action). The drop goes deliberately past the SDK, which declines to *execute* those calls but keeps them in a history it never re-sends: this log is durable and replayed every turn, so a retained intent would be #181 re-opened for that one stop reason. The SDK's client loop (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.62.0 — betatoolrunner_test.go TestExecuteToolsSkipsMaxTokensTurn) also declines to execute *any* call of a cut-off turn — `max_tokens` and `model_context_window_exceeded`, "a cut-off turn left its last call's arguments incomplete" — complete calls included; the platform keeps running the complete ones, because its truncation guard is per block (next sentence) rather than per turn, and a complete call the model made is not one to strand (#181). The client loop has also run tools on a `tool_use` stop alone since anthropic-sdk-go v1.67.0 — betatoolrunner.go determineNextStepFromStopReason, so it leaves the complete calls of an `end_turn` or `stop_sequence` turn unexecuted too; the platform runs them, for the same reason. The log thus loses what the model asked for — logged, not silent — while the wire is unchanged (the stop reason flattens per the entry above). A mid-input truncation never reaches the classification; a block cut before its first input delta arrives as `{}` and runs. What the reference *server* does with any of this stays unobserved. *Evidence: internal/brain/brain.go — runTurn's refusal arm and commitTurn's classification comment now hold the derivation cut from here: the SDK's "dead conversation" rationale, the reachability argument, the truncation case (with internal/brain/stream.go normalizeToolInput); executeTools + NextMessage — the refusal early return, joined by a max_tokens / model_context_window_exceeded early return pinned by TestExecuteToolsSkipsMaxTokensTurn, and history appended before executeTools runs in every case (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betatoolrunner.go betaToolRunnerBase.executeTools and determineNextStepFromStopReason and BetaToolRunner.NextMessage; since anthropic-sdk-go v1.62.0 — betatoolrunner_test.go TestExecuteToolsSkipsMaxTokensTurn) — and StopReason, a bare enum with no stated relation to content blocks (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — message.go StopReason); platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/handling-stop-reasons, § "Incomplete tool use blocks" for the max_tokens shape; docs/changelog/0.2.0.md § the #181 entry; internal/brain/brain_test.go TestNonToolUseStopWithToolBlocksRunsThem / TestNonToolUseStopKeepsCustomAndAskRouting / TestRefusalDropsItsToolBlocks / TestToolUseStopWithoutBlocksFails* **The cut-off arm is recorded and this entry's core claim holds.** A haiku session was asked to call `bash` with a 300,000-character `command`, so that generation would hit the output ceiling mid-argument. The `agent.tool_use` event **was emitted**, with `input` = `{}` — the partial JSON discarded rather than repaired or the block dropped — the call **was dispatched anyway**, and it came back `is_error: true` carrying a schema-validation array as its text (`[{"expected":"string","code":"invalid_type","path":["command"],…}]`), after which the turn continued into another model request. So a response carrying tool blocks is a tool turn whatever stop reason arrived with it, and **the reference does not pre-validate a call's arguments before dispatch**: a malformed argument surfaces as a *tool-level* result error, never a turn-level one. One field came free and this platform already emits it: `agent.tool_use` and `agent.mcp_tool_use` both carry `evaluated_permission` (`"allow"` on every observed instance), the policy decision written onto the event itself. The refusal arm stays unobserved. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probe `session.events.list.tool-blocks-final`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §C.* *Tracked: #78 (what the reference does with the tool blocks of a `refusal` turn).*
- **Tool-only turns — empty agent.message** — A turn that only calls tools emits no empty agent.message event. Assumption about reference event emission. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/brain` + `internal/queue` + state machine — the orchestration loop (slice 5)"* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference emits an empty agent.message on a turn that only calls tools).*
- **Session usage — cache-creation token bucket** — The provider's flat cache-creation counter folds into the ephemeral_5m bucket of the wire usage shape. Assumption about reference usage accounting. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/brain` + `internal/queue` + state machine — the orchestration loop (slice 5)"* **Recorded, and it folds into `ephemeral_5m` as this entry assumed** — with the nested pair a running session total rather than the per-turn figure a first reading took it for. `cache_creation.ephemeral_1h_input_tokens` is `0` in all 63 recorded usage objects that render the pair, while `ephemeral_5m_input_tokens` equals the sum of every flat `span.model_request_end.model_usage.cache_creation_input_tokens` in the session *to date*: the multi-turn listings separate the two readings decisively, the mixed-permission session going 5,408 then 5,671 where its second turn's span was only 263, and the repository-inspection session 6,294 then 7,616 where the delta was 1,322. `cache_read_input_tokens`, `input_tokens`, `output_tokens` and `active_seconds` accumulate identically, and the same running figure is mirrored onto the session resource's own `usage`. So the two shapes coexist on one session, the flat counter is the span's own and the nested pair the session's cumulative, and a provider count with no TTL of its own lands in the 5-minute bucket. **This platform already accumulates the same way**, so this confirms rather than accuses: `domain.Usage.Add` folds each turn's `ModelUsage` into the stored session totals with `+=`, which is what the event log calls when it commits a turn. One cosmetic difference remains: before its first turn the reference renders `cache_creation` as `null` where this platform renders a zeroed pair — indistinguishable to its own SDK, which types the bucket pair as a non-nullable struct, unlike the sibling `list_cost` and `server_tool_use`. This is #78's own `cache-creation-usage-bucket` checklist item, which was marked recorded-but-unverified. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch1.json and batch2.json — 63 usage objects render the bucket pair and `ephemeral_1h` is `0` in every one; the 37 carried inside an event listing were each checked against that listing's running span sum and all 37 matched it, the multi-turn probes `session.events.list.mixed-perm-final`, `.repo-inspect`, `.skill-paths`, `.blocked-status` and `.cred-conflict-3turns` being the ones that can tell cumulative from per-turn. Two listings are in reverse chronological order, which a naive walk misreads as a mismatch.* *Tracked: #78 (whether a request cached at 1h splits into `ephemeral_1h` — no recorded surface carries a cache-TTL knob at all, so the recording cannot say which TTL any turn asked for; that `ephemeral_1h` read `0` throughout is the only basis, and the other arm stays inferred from the field's existence).*
- **session.error retry semantics (model request failure)** — No automatic model retry: one failed model request ends the turn visibly with session.error (model_request_failed_error) and the session idles with stop_reason retries_exhausted — unless pending mid-turn input chains a fresh turn instead. retry_status reports the decision, not the code path: 'exhausted' when the session idles, 'retrying' when a chained turn is about to run. Reference retry policy and error-event shape not observed. *Evidence: docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/brain` + `internal/queue` + state machine — the orchestration loop (slice 5)" (brain/brain.go settle), docs/history/2026-07.md § "`internal/brain` + `internal/queue` + state machine — the orchestration loop (slice 5)"* **Recorded, and the no-retry half holds while the shape does not.** A 1.35 MB `user.message` was accepted by `POST /v1/sessions/{id}/events` with a 200 — there is no body-size refusal at that size — and the turn ran `status_running | thread_status_running | user.message | model_request_start | model_request_end | session.error | session.status_terminated`, carrying `{"type":"unknown_error","retry_status":{"type":"terminal"},"message":"prompt is too long: 335390 tokens > 200000 maximum"}`. Three corrections follow. `error.type` is **`unknown_error`** — the reference does not classify a context overflow at all, neither as `retries_exhausted` nor as `model_context_window_exceeded`; it passes the provider's sentence through under a generic type. `retry_status` is **`terminal`**, the only `terminal` observed anywhere in either recording wave, so the union has at least two arms and this is the one that means stop. And the session **terminates rather than idling**: `session.status_terminated`'s payload is just `{id, processed_at, type}`, so a terminated session records *why* only in the preceding `session.error`, and no `stop_reason` is written anywhere. The half that holds is the important one — **no automatic retry**, exactly one `span.model_request_start`/`end` pair. That end event is the only one in either wave with `is_error: true`, its `model_usage` is all zeros, and **no `session.usage` event was emitted at all**, so an overflowed turn is not billed. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probe `session.events.list.context-overflow-result`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §G.* **This platform matches none of the first three.** `commitFailure` settles with `domain.StopRetriesExhausted` and emits `model_request_failed_error` carrying `retry_status: exhausted`, going **idle** — so where the reference writes `unknown_error`/`terminal`/`status_terminated`/no stop reason, we write a classified type, a different retry arm, an idle status and a `retries_exhausted` stop reason. #577 owns aligning the unchained arm. The chained arm is deliberately different here and the recording does not reach it: `commitFailure` reports `retrying` when a chained turn follows, because calling it terminal "would tell a client the session is dead while it is still producing events", and no recorded turn had pending input behind a failure. *Tracked: #577 (align the unchained failure's type, retry arm and terminal state), #78 (whether a transport-level model failure, as opposed to a context overflow, is classified or retried differently).*
- **Denied tool confirmation — the result a denial writes: recorded for `bash`, inferred for every other tool** — A denial is answered with an `is_error: true` result carrying one text block, the reference's sentence `Permission to use <name> has been rejected.`, followed by ` Rejection message: <deny_message>` when the client gave one, and written in the family of the call that was refused: `agent.tool_use` → `agent.tool_result` and `agent.mcp_tool_use` → `agent.mcp_tool_result`, keyed by that family's own reference field. The built-in half is recorded and matched (#60; this platform used to write the bare `deny_message`, or a default sentence of its own): every denial the archive holds refuses a `bash` call, and every one carries exactly one of those two forms. What stays inferred is the sentence for any other tool — this platform names the tool as the model was offered it, which for an MCP call is the composed `mcp__{server}__{tool}` that "Offering an MCP tool to the model" (below) records the reference showing its model, not the event's bare `name` — and the MCP family's answer as a whole, which no recording carries; its family is the interrupt entry's mapping above, since answering across families would hand a client watching an MCP tool a result it never reads. Two readings of the message are ours as well: it is appended verbatim (both recorded messages already end in a full stop, so a reference that normalised punctuation would read the same), and an empty `deny_message` is read as none given (no recorded denial sends one). **Which calls may be gated is not inferred**: the SDK ties the confirmation to both families in two directions — `tool_use_id` is documented as "The id of the `agent.tool_use` or `agent.mcp_tool_use` event this result corresponds to", and the MCP tool use's own `session_thread_id` as the value to "Echo this on a `user.tool_confirmation` event to route the approval back" — so an MCP tool carrying an `always_ask` policy stops for a human exactly as a built-in does, and a gate that admitted only built-ins would run an MCP call the agent's own policy said to ask about. What stays inferred is only the answer to a call other than `bash`, as above. A consequence worth naming, since it is a live behavior change in a package the gate does not otherwise touch: a session parked on an ask-gated MCP call is excluded from idle-TTL sandbox reaping like any other pending approval, even though the MCP call itself needs no sandbox — the session is mid-turn, and its built-in tools resume into the workspace the reap would have destroyed. Related: the `deny` EvaluatedPermission value is carried in the wire enum but never produced by any configurable policy. *Evidence: the confirmation's ToolUseID and SessionThreadID doc comments (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsUserToolConfirmationEvent.ToolUseID and BetaManagedAgentsUserToolConfirmationEvent.SessionThreadID); the MCP tool result — `mcp_tool_use_id` required, no `session_thread_id`, no `mcp_server_name` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentMCPToolResultEvent); internal/events/toolflow.go (confirmableToolUseTypes, toolUseAnswer, DenialResults, denialText, deniedUse.modelName); internal/domain/mcpname.go (MCPModelName, shared with replay); internal/events/denial_test.go (TestDenialAnswersInTheDeniedCallsOwnFamily, TestDenialWithoutAMessageStillCarriesText), internal/api/confirmation_test.go (TestConfirmationDenyAnswersWithErrorAndResumesBrain, TestConfirmationDenyOfAnMCPCallAnswersInItsOwnFamily, TestConfirmationDenialsKeepEachCallInItsOwnFamily, TestMCPCallBlocksTheRequiresActionGate).* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings — 2026-09-12/batch1.json `rec91.denial.events.with-message` (idx 82) and `rec91.denial.nomessage.events.result` (idx 90); 2026-09-12-followups/budget-runtime.json `rec.budget.confirmation.events.after-denial` (idx 16); 2026-09-19-custom-order-followup/setup.json `ask-first-deny.final-audit.events` (idx 63); 2026-09-19-custom-mixed-tools/setup.json `ask-custom-first-deny.final-audit.events` (idx 198) and `ask-deny-first-custom.final-audit.events` (idx 201).* *Tracked: #78 (a recorded denial of an MCP call, or of a built-in other than `bash`, would settle whether the sentence names it the same way; one with an empty or unpunctuated `deny_message`, what the suffix does); the MCP-family half landed for #45, the recorded `bash` text for #60, and the result's place on the log for #793.*
- **Tool-result content — the empty text block is refused, and a silent success posts `(no output)` (plan 35 slice 0)** — Every inbound text block — in `user.message` (its plain-string content form included: `content: ""` is refused too, since replay renders it as one text block), `user.tool_result`, `user.custom_tool_result`, a `search_result`'s nested content, and `system.message` (whose text folds into the system string, where empty is harmless — it shares the rule for uniformity) — must be non-empty (400 `text block text must not be empty` / `content must not be empty` — save an empty block in a sent `user.message`, which since #540 is refused in the reference's recorded words, `events.0.user_message.content.0.text: value is required` (2026-09-02 `sessT.send.user.message.empty-text-block`)), and both platform halves answer a tool that succeeded with no output with the text block `(no output)` (`toolset.NoOutput`; the executor's `agent.tool_result` and the BYOC worker's `user.tool_result` alike). The rule and the wording are the reference runner's own — "The Sessions API rejects empty text blocks; a tool that succeeds silently must still produce a postable result" — but the SDK witnesses it for tool results only and records no status; extending the refusal to `user.message` and `system.message` is the platform's inference, made because every user-side block is replayed to a Messages endpoint that rejects an empty text block, so accepting it would wedge the session on every later turn (the same hazard the MCP path already guards). **All three of those are recorded now** (2026-09-02): the reference refused an empty text block inside a `user.message` with 400 `invalid_request_error`, "events.0.user_message.content.0.text: value is required". So the status and type match ours, the refusal does reach `user.message`, and the platform's extension of the rule to that event is confirmed rather than inferred — the hazard argument above turns out to describe the reference's own behavior. Only the wording differs, and in a way worth keeping on both sides: the reference names the offending field path, ours names the rule. *Evidence: toToolResultContent, the "(no output)" substitution and its inline comment, and the function's doc comment that states the rule outright — "Empty text blocks are posted as `(no output)`" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasessiontoolrunner.go toToolResultContent), and the two placeholder tests (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betasessiontoolrunner_test.go TestSessionToolRunner_EmptyTextResultPostsPlaceholder and TestToToolResultContent_EmptyTextBecomesPlaceholder); internal/events/inbound.go validateBlock; internal/executor/executor.go toolResultEvent; internal/worker/toolexec.go postToolResult; TestNormalizeInboundRejections ("text empty in …" rows), TestEmptyToolResultPostsPlaceholder (both packages)* *Tracked: #78 (the status, type and the refusal's reach into `user.message` are settled; what no probe covered is an empty `system.message`, whose text this platform refuses for uniformity alone — the recording refused a well-formed one on model grounds instead, "`system.message` events are not supported on model …", which this platform accepts unconditionally and #553 owns).*
- **Session resource — `budget` rendered, always null (plan 35 slice 0)** — the SDK added `budget` to `BetaManagedAgentsSession` as `api:"required"` without `nullable` (since anthropic-sdk-go v1.62.0 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsSession.Budget); the platform has no budgets (#432) and renders the key as `null`, the way it renders `archived_at` (also required, also non-nullable in the tag) and the way the SDK's own deployment twin declares its `budget` (`api:"nullable"`). The reference renders `null` for a session created without one (recorded 2026-09-12), so the empty case agrees and the shape question this entry opened is answered; it stays here because the code is not — the key is null unconditionally, where a reference session carrying a budget renders it, and nothing here can yet produce that value. *Evidence: Session.Budget and the nullable SessionUpdatedEvent.Budget (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsSession.Budget and BetaManagedAgentsSessionUpdatedEvent.Budget), and the nullable Deployment.Budget (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeployment.go BetaManagedAgentsDeployment.Budget); internal/api/sessions.go sessionJSON.Budget; sessions_test.go sessionRequiredFields* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-12/FINDINGS.md § "Budgets and usage" (a session created without `budget` returns `budget: null`).* *Tracked: #432 (budgets landing would give the key a value to render in place of the unconditional `null`).*
- **POST …/work/{work_id}/stop — a graceful stop of work already stopping answers it unchanged (#804)** — A graceful stop of `stopping` work answers 200 with the item as it stands and changes nothing: no timestamp moves and nothing is re-armed, as with every other stop that moves nothing (*POST …/work/{work_id}/stop — response shape*). No recording reaches this case. The recordings' 27 stops include 13 of already-`stopped` work, graceful and forced alike, all answered unchanged, and one forced stop of `stopping` work, but no graceful one, so the reference may yet refuse it, or re-stamp `stop_requested_at`. The owner chose the idempotent answer (2026-09-26, on #804) because every recorded repeat stop is one. *Evidence: internal/queue/lifecycle.go StopWith (the graceful arm moves only queued, starting and active items), internal/api/workapi.go stopWork (the re-arm, owed only by a stop that moved the item); internal/api/workapi_test.go TestWorkStopAnswersTheWorkObject.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording of a graceful stop of `stopping` work); landed for #804.*
- **POST …/work/{work_id}/stop — a graceful stop of queued work stops it outright (#810)** — A graceful stop of `queued` work, polled or never handed out, moves it straight to `stopped`: `stop_requested_at` and `stopped_at` stamped, any poll reservation released, and the session re-armed as by any move to `stopped`. No recording reaches this case — the recordings' three graceful stops of unfinished work are all of acked `starting` work, answered `stopping` — so the reference may park queued work in `stopping` too. This platform does not, because nobody has acked a queued item: there is no worker to tell or to finish the wind-down, and parked it would wait non-terminal for a poll to finalize it, one an emptied environment may never see (#25). *Evidence: internal/queue/lifecycle.go StopWith (the graceful arm's CASEs park only starting and active work); internal/queue/lifecycle_test.go TestGracefulStopOfQueuedWorkStopsOutright.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording of a graceful stop of queued work, polled and not); landed for #25.*
- **POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — a claim on once-claimed stopping work is refused 412 (#810)** — A `NO_HEARTBEAT` claim on `stopping` work that a beat once claimed answers 412, as a claim on any claimed item does, and writes nothing. The one recorded claim on `stopping` work (2026-09-19 custom-mixed-tools #44) was on work no beat had reached, and answered 200 `stopping`; this platform answers that case the same way (*POST …/work/{work_id}/stop — graceful vs force semantics*) and keeps the 412 for this one, which no recording reaches — the owner's scope decision (2026-09-26, on #810) took the recorded case and no more. The worker of once-claimed work learns of the stop from its echo beat, so this claim can only be a repeat of one that already landed, a retry after a lost response, say. Either answer is safe for both workers: ours and the reference's cancel the run on a 412 and on `stopping` alike, and a 412 only leaves the finishing to the finalizer instead of the worker's force stop. *Evidence: internal/queue/lifecycle.go Heartbeat (a failed claim answers the stop only on stopping work with no last heartbeat); internal/queue/lifecycle_test.go TestClaimRefusalsAroundAStop; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go runHeartbeat.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording of a claim on stopping work that a beat once claimed); landed for #810.*
- **POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — a claim on stopped work is refused 412 (#810)** — A `NO_HEARTBEAT` claim on `stopped` work answers 412 and writes nothing, whether a beat ever reached the item or not: force-stopped while `starting`, stopped after its claim, or settled by the finalizer after its worker never claimed it. No recording reaches it. The recorded claim that answered 200 was on `stopping` work (2026-09-19 custom-mixed-tools #44), and the owner's scope decision (2026-09-26, on #810) extends that answer no further. The nearest recording is not a claim: 2026-09-02's `work.heartbeat.after-stop` (batch2 idx 297) sent no precondition at all to claimed, stopped work and was answered 200 `stopped`, lease not extended — the omitted form this platform refuses with 400 (*POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — `expected_last_heartbeat` is required here, optional on the reference*). Either answer is safe for both workers: both cancel the run on a 412 and on `stopped` alike, and the force stop a 200 would provoke moves nothing and re-arms nothing. *Evidence: internal/queue/lifecycle.go Heartbeat; internal/queue/lifecycle_test.go TestClaimRefusalsAroundAStop and TestPollFinalizesANeverClaimedWindDown.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording of a NO_HEARTBEAT claim on stopped work, never claimed and once claimed); landed for #810.*
- **POST …/work/{work_id}/stop — graceful vs force semantics, and who completes the wind-down (what may enter stopping reversed on the 2026-09-19 recordings, #810)** — force:false/empty is read as 'ask the worker to stop' and force:true as 'stop now' (-> stopped). The wire exposes only {force?} and the state enum without spelling out the two-step wind-down; the recordings have since settled what may enter it and how a worker is told, and the rest is still ours to define. **What may enter stopping — reversed 2026-09-26, confirmed by recording:** an item a worker has acked, `starting` or `active`. A graceful stop of acked `starting` work parks it in `stopping` — `stop_requested_at` stamped, `stopped_at` and `latest_heartbeat_at` null, the ack's stamps untouched — as each of the three recorded graceful stops of such work answered (latest-cli #2 and #5, mixed-tools #23). A queued item, polled or not, has no worker to ask and still stops outright (*POST …/work/{work_id}/stop — a graceful stop of queued work stops it outright*). **The channels a worker is told on:** an echo heartbeat on once-claimed `stopping` work matches but does not extend the lease and reports the state; and a `NO_HEARTBEAT` claim on `stopping` work no beat has reached — the one beat a worker stopped between its ack and its claim still sends — answers 200 `{lease_extended: false, state: "stopping", last_heartbeat: ""}` and writes nothing, as recorded (mixed-tools #44, its `ttl_seconds` aside: *POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — the stopping claim's `ttl_seconds`*). Every other failed claim keeps its 412 (*POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — a claim on once-claimed stopping work is refused 412*, *POST …/work/{work_id}/heartbeat — a claim on stopped work is refused 412*). **Who leaves stopping:** the worker that was asked to stop — it winds its tools down and then force-stops the item itself (stopping -> stopped, stopped_at stamped, lease cleared, the first stop_requested_at kept), as the recorded worker did right after its claim (mixed-tools #47, `latest_heartbeat_at` still null). The reference worker reaches the same end state by force-stopping when its runner and heartbeat exit, unless its lease was lost (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — worker.go EnvironmentWorker.handleItem); ours must tell that stop apart from a lost lease too, because it also rotates work identities per re-hand-out (#62) and must never stop an item another worker may hold. Like the reference, it takes the claim's answer, and any lease not extended, for the stop rather than a lost lease (#813). The move to `stopping` re-arms nothing; the finished stop owes one re-arm, from the worker's force stop or the finalizer below. **Abandoned wind-downs:** a stopping item whose lease has lapsed and whose stop was requested more than `queue.WindDown` (60 s) ago is finalized to stopped by a poll of its environment, never re-offered, its session re-armed in the same transaction. A graceful stop keeps the lease it finds, so a never-claimed wind-down is finalized on the startup lease its ack installed (30 s from the ack) and WindDown together, and a claim as late as the recorded one, 59 s after its stop, still finds the item `stopping`; clearing the lease instead would have met the finalizer's null-lease arm at the next poll. The price, accepted to match the recordings: a graceful stop of `starting` work whose worker has already died no longer settles and re-arms at the stop, but at the first poll of its environment once the lease and WindDown have both run out, a minute or more later, and never if nothing polls. Until then the item is undrained work, so an environment delete answers 409 unless it passes `?force=true`, and a `force: true` stop of the item settles it at once. What finalizes an abandoned wind-down on the reference, and when, stays unrecorded. **The rule this reverses, kept so the change stays auditable:** from #25 until #810 only an `active` item entered `stopping`. A graceful stop of a `starting` one completed outright (-> stopped, and re-armed at once), on the argument that its worker had no channel left to be told on — its only remaining beat being the claim, which the heartbeat refused once the row was no longer starting — so that parking it in `stopping` would strand it; the claim that followed such a stop was answered 412, which our worker read as a lost lease. The recordings answered that argument: on the reference the claim *is* that channel. *Evidence: internal/queue/lifecycle.go (StopWith, Heartbeat), internal/queue/queue.go (Poll, ListAbandoned, FinalizeAbandoned), internal/api/workapi.go (stopWork, finalizeAbandoned), internal/worker/lease.go (hbExit); internal/queue/lifecycle_test.go TestGracefulStopOfStartingWorkWindsDown, TestClaimOnNeverClaimedStoppingWorkLearnsTheStop, TestPollFinalizesANeverClaimedWindDown and TestPollFinalizesAnAbandonedWindDown, internal/api/workapi_test.go TestWorkGracefulStopOfStartingWorkWindsDownThroughTheClaim and TestWorkStopAnswersTheWorkObject, internal/api/threadexec_test.go TestWorkGracefulStopOfStartingWorkReArmsOnce, internal/worker/lease_test.go TestWorkerStopBeforeTheClaimWindsDown, TestSDKTypedHeartbeatDecodesAStoppingClaim, TestWorkerControlPlaneStopWindsDown, TestWorkerLeaseNotExtendedStopsTheItem and TestWorkerLeaseLossDoesNotStopTheItem; the 2026-09-19 recordings' `worker-network.json`: `-self-hosted-latest-cli` #2 and #5 and `-custom-mixed-tools` #23 (graceful stops of acked `starting` work answered `stopping`), `-custom-mixed-tools` #22, #44 and #47 (that item's ack, its claim and its worker's force stop); docs/plan/58_work-stop-starting.md; docs/history/2026-07.md § "work API — the work-item lifecycle: get / ack / heartbeat / stop (slice 8, second part)"* *Tracked: #78 (what finalizes an abandoned wind-down on the reference, and when); landed for #25 and #810.*
- **Request bodies, path IDs, and query parameters — U+0000 (and any unstorable byte) rejected before it binds** — Postgres stores U+0000 in neither text nor jsonb, so the alternative to rejecting it is a 500 at bind time; we reject it in the shape an absent or unknown value already carries, on three surfaces. (1) **Bodies and inbound event payloads → 400:** every endpoint that decodes a JSON object body rejects U+0000 anywhere in it, key or value — not only the metadata-accepting ones; this covers a metadata *delete* key too, which the Go-side merges used to accept as a silent 200 no-op (the work endpoint 500'd on the identical patch) — the one genuinely wire-observable change in this family, every other surface merely converting a 500 into the status the wire already carried, accepted deliberately since that asymmetry was never a contract. (2) **Path IDs → 404, id-shaped query filters → 400:** a percent-decoded `%00`, like any non-alphabet byte, fails `domain.ID.Valid` before the bind and cannot name a stored row: 404 on every path id it validates, 400 on `agent_id` and on the `page` cursor's decoded id. (3) **Free-form `types[]` → 400 on the unstorable byte only:** an unknown-but-storable event type still filters to empty. The reference's behaviour is undecidable from the typed schema — opaque server-generated IDs, every other field (metadata included) a plain string — and no `ant` recording covers an unstorable byte, so whether it 400s, 404s, 500s or strips is unknown: stricter-than-schema by storage necessity, to confirm against a recording. **Outbound (#223):** a NUL a *tool* emits is stripped rather than rejected, so a stored `agent.tool_result` can differ from the tool's raw bytes by exactly its NULs; stripping is our choice, INFERRED to be at least as safe as the reject this entry records on the inbound side. **Model-side (#228):** the brain strips at its event-construction boundary — text deltas, the `tool_use` name and raw-JSON input, `failTurn`'s message — and that input's decode/re-encode also launders what only raw bytes can smuggle, a lone surrogate escape and invalid UTF-8, to U+FFFD. Both strips are ours and INFERRED: no recording shows what the reference does with NUL-bearing tool or model output — strip, replace, or fault, none of the three is covered. *Evidence: the derivations live in code comments — internal/api/wire.go rejectNULBody / checkID / storableText (SQLSTATEs, the single-chokepoint argument, the merge asymmetry), internal/domain/id.go Valid, internal/events/inbound.go rejectNUL, internal/toolset/toolset.go SanitizeText, internal/executor/executor.go toolResultEvent, internal/brain/stream.go normalizeToolInput, internal/brain/brain.go failTurn; pinned by internal/api/edge_test.go TestMetadataRejectsNUL / TestStringFieldsRejectNUL / TestPathAndQueryRejectNUL and internal/brain/nul_test.go; opaque-string IDs and map[string]string metadata (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaagent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgent.ID and BetaManagedAgentsAgent.Metadata, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsSession.ID and BetaManagedAgentsSession.Metadata, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betaenvironmentwork.go BetaSelfHostedWork.ID and BetaSelfHostedWork.Metadata); docs/changelog/0.2.0.md § Fixed.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference rejects, strips or faults on a NUL); the outbound and model-side strips landed for #223 and #228.*
- **Query strings that do not parse — a 400 `invalid_request_error` wherever a lost pair would widen, swap or unguard the answer** — Go's query parser drops a pair it cannot decode — an invalid escape such as `%zz`, or a pair holding a bare `;` — and, past its default ceiling of 10,000 pairs (GODEBUG `urlmaxqueryparams`), the whole query, so a parameter sent that way read as never sent: a filtered list answered with everything it holds, `GET /v1/agents/{id}?version=1%zz` served the latest version, and `DELETE /v1/memory_stores/{id}/memories/{mid}` ran without its `expected_content_sha256` precondition. Those routes — the memory delete, the agent read, and the agent, session, session-event, deployment, deployment-run, dream, skill, file, memory-store, memory and memory-version lists — now refuse such a request with 400 `invalid_request_error` ("malformed query string: …"). So does the thread-event list, although its own parameters only page: it shares the session-event list's handler, which answers a filter key sent to a thread with a 400, and a filter key lost on the way would have answered unfiltered instead. The status, the message and where the line falls are ours: no recorded request's query string holds an undecodable escape or a `;` (every `path` value carrying a `?` in the recordings' JSON files, 5,475 of them), and the SDK cannot send one, since it builds its query strings with `url.Values.Encode`. It can pass the ceiling, though: 10,000 `types[]` values on the session-event list, beside the `beta=true` its path carries, are 10,001 pairs, and that request, which used to answer unfiltered, now draws the 400 too. The 400 is argued against the drop only, which answers a question the caller did not ask. A reference that read the bad pair literally, as WHATWG `URLSearchParams` and Python's `parse_qsl` do, would answer some of these requests as asked — `path_prefix=/notes;v2/` names a real prefix, since a memory path may hold a `;` — and against such a reference the 400 is stricter, not safer: it refuses a request its caller can repair only by percent-encoding the pair. The other routes still read their query leniently, since a pair lost there can change the page, the view, the preview frames or a timing, hide archived rows, or leave a refusal standing, but never answer with rows a filter excludes, serve another version, or let a guarded write through: paging alone, a view, `event_deltas`, the work API's timing knobs, `include_archived`, environment-delete `force`, and the heartbeat's `expected_last_heartbeat`, whose absence is already a 400. *Evidence: internal/api/page.go queryValues and the handlers that call it; internal/api/events.go listEvents (the thread-event list's filter-key refusal); internal/api/query_test.go TestMalformedQueryStringIsRefused / TestMalformedQueryStringKeepsTheDeletePrecondition; the SDK's query assembly (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — internal/requestconfig/requestconfig.go NewRequestConfig) and its event-list path and `types[]` spelling (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaSessionEventService.List, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaSessionEventListParams.URLQuery); internal/memsync/memsync.go ValidatePath, which has no rule against `;`; managed-agents-wire-recordings, all eighteen sessions, searched for a request path whose query string holds a `%` without two hex digits or a `;`.* *Tracked: #78 (whether the reference answers a query string that does not parse with a 400, reads the bad pair leniently, or drops it).*
- **GET /v1/files — what the id cursors mean, and the unknown-cursor page** — Public docs settle more of this route than the SDK's types can, because the two dialects it serves are a documented compatibility affordance rather than a leftover: a request under `managed-agents-2026-04-01` gets a list that "includes `has_more`, `first_id`, and `last_id` alongside `next_page`", keeps `before_id`/`after_id` "still accepted (not combinable with `page` or `ids[]`)", and comes back "newest first" — the envelope, the exclusivity and the ordering all as implemented, and none of them this platform's invention. What the docs do not say is what the id cursors *mean*, and that half is still ours: `after_id` returns the rows immediately older than the cursor, `before_id` the rows immediately newer (fetched ascending, reversed to newest-first), an unknown one yields an empty page rather than a 400, and `next_page` is the position after the page in that same newest-first order — which on a `before_id` page is the cursor row's own, the one arm where `has_more`, answering the id lane's question instead, does not decide whether it is null. An **empty** page still sends the key and sends it `null`, as the one recorded empty response does; what it has no value for is the continuation, so a `before_id` that matched nothing reads as end-of-list where it is in fact the top of it; nothing can act on the difference, because a cursor pager stops on the empty `data` before reading the cursor. *Evidence: public docs https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/files § "Migrate from `files-api-2025-04-14`" (the managed-agents affordance) and § "List files"; docs/plan/08_files.md decision 8; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betafile.go BetaFileListParams; internal/api/files.go listFiles* **`ids[]` is implemented and one reading of it is ours.** The docs settle the bound (100, counted after de-duplication), the exclusivity (`page`, `limit`, and the id cursors), the terminal page, and that ids which "do not resolve to a visible File — including deleted Files — are silently omitted". What they do not say is what a *malformed* id does. The scalar filters beside it — `deployment_id`, `memory_store_id`, `scope_id` — reject a bad shape with a 400 under #135's shape-first rule, but this one drops the entry instead, on the reading that an id of no valid shape resolves to no visible file by definition and that a batch parameter documented to tolerate misses should not fail a hundred-id request over one typo. The cost is that an all-malformed request answers 200 with an empty page rather than telling a client its ids are wrong. The grammar check is load-bearing for a second reason regardless: it is what keeps an unstorable byte out of the bind parameter, which #135 requires whatever the status code would be. The wire spelling is `ids[]` — `BetaFileListParams.URLQuery` pins `ArrayQueryFormatBrackets`, whose encoder appends `[]` to the key once per element (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betafile.go BetaFileListParams.URLQuery, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — internal/apiquery/encoder.go encoder.newArrayTypeEncoder) — and the bare `ids` is accepted too, through the same `listParam` helper the other repeatable parameters use. An **empty** value is not an id here: `?ids=` reads as no filter rather than as a filter matching nothing, matching how every other empty parameter on this route reads, and the SDK's encoder emits no pair for an empty slice so it cannot produce one. The 100 bound counts entries the caller sent, de-duplicated, which means a malformed entry consumes cap and a hundred good ids plus one typo is a 400 rather than a quietly shortened page — the conservative side of a behavior no recording covers. *Evidence: the quoted contract is `BetaFileListParams.IDs`'s own doc comment, verbatim, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betafile.go BetaFileListParams.IDs; the id-cursor sentence is the public-docs quote at the head of this entry; internal/api/files.go listFiles; internal/api/files_test.go TestFileListIDs.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording of a paged file list would settle the id cursors' direction and the unknown-cursor page, and what the reference does with a malformed entry in `ids[]`); landed for #55, #544 and #652.*
- **Lists recorded only on their last page — `next_page` omitted when no page follows, a cursor in it when one does (#676)** — The archive holds each of these lists only as a last page, and never with `next_page` on it, null or otherwise: agents (35 bodies), agent versions (12), deployments (16), deployment runs (12), session threads (1,027), thread events (759), session resources (5), vaults (16) and vault credentials (22). Dreams have no recorded list at all, the recording organization not being enrolled for them. This platform omits the key on their last page, matching what was recorded, and sends its cursor there when a further page exists — what the five omitting lists recorded with a page to come do, and what the SDK's pager reads, all ten being `PageCursor` lists. The inference is that a page with more behind it names its successor in `next_page`, rather than these lists never sending the key; a recorded walk past the first page settles it, list by list. *Recording: the census in the keyset-cursor entry among the architecture notes.* *Evidence: the key the pager reads, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — pagination.go PageCursor.NextPage; internal/api/page.go pageJSON; internal/api/page_test.go TestListTerminalPageEnvelope.* *Tracked: #78 (a recorded walk past the first page of each of these ten lists); landed for #676.*
- **POST /v1/files — multipart strictness and MIME derivation** — The upload requires exactly one part named `file`; extra, unknown, or duplicate parts are rejected 400. One parameter part is admitted beside it: `expires_in_seconds` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betafile.go BetaFileUploadParams.ExpiresInSeconds), bounded 3600 to 7776000 as the public docs and the API reference both state, in either part order (#655, plan 49). **What is inferred is the refusal surface, not the parameter**: the docs publish the bounds and say nothing about what a value outside them, a non-integer, a padded one or a duplicate part reads as, so the 400 and its wording are this platform's, chosen to match the strictness the rest of this form already has. The filename follows the API reference's rule for the `file` part (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betafile.go BetaFileUploadParams.File): only its final path component is kept, and an absent or empty one becomes `unnamed` plus the extension for the stored `mime_type`, when known. The SDK sends an empty one when `File` is given no name over a reader that has none, and `anonymous_file` for a bare reader. What the rule leaves open is ours: `/` and `\` both separate components — a `\` comes from the SDK itself, which on Windows names an open file by a `path.Base` that cuts only at `/` — so `dir/` and `dir\` leave an empty name, and the other documented forbidden characters apply to what remains; and the extension is the one the pinned table pairs with the type — `.jpg` for `image/jpeg`, `.txt` for `text/plain`, `.bin` for `application/octet-stream` (the one Python's `mimetypes` gives it too) — while a type the table does not list leaves the name a bare `unnamed` (plan 55). The stored `mime_type` is the part's declared Content-Type when specific, else the filename extension through the platform's pinned table (`internal/mimetab` — shared with the outputs harvest, so the fallback is host-independent and both writers of the `files` registry derive the same value for the same name, #264/#277), else `application/octet-stream`. A part with no Content-Type is taken whatever the beta header: the Files guide calls it "Required" under `files-api-2025-04-14`, a header this platform ignores like every beta header and which, the same guide says, the Go SDK stopped sending at its 1.68.0 release. The reference's exact rejection surface and error strings were never observed, and of its MIME derivation the Files guide says only that an omitted part Content-Type "is detected"; this platform sniffs nothing, so a nameless part without one — the SDK's `File` given neither a name nor a type — is stored as `unnamed.bin`, `application/octet-stream`. *Evidence: docs/plan/08_files.md slice-1 inferences + "Recording checklist"; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betafile.go BetaFileUploadParams; the SDK's filenames, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — field.go File, and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — internal/apiform/encoder.go encoder.newReaderTypeEncoder; platform.claude.com build-with-claude/files (fetched 2026-09-24 — the forbidden characters, and the migration table's part Content-Type row: "Required" with `files-api-2025-04-14`, "Optional; the type is detected when omitted" without); internal/api/filesupload.go (parseFileUpload, finalPathComponent); internal/mimetab/mimetab.go (ExtFor, preferredExt); internal/api/filesunnamed_test.go* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the multipart rejection surface, the extension a nameless upload takes, whether `\` separates path components, and the MIME fallback); landed for #55, for the parameter and the lifecycle behind it #655, and for the filename rule #738.*
- **GET /v1/files/{id}/content — download response headers and the not-downloadable status** — A downloadable file streams with Content-Type = its stored MIME type, Content-Length, and Content-Disposition naming the original filename (the CLI parses that filename; the exact header shape is inferred). An uploaded file (`downloadable:false`) is refused 400, matching the public docs ("Downloading a file you uploaded returns a 400 error"), and since #540 in the reference's recorded words, "File `file_…` is not downloadable. Only files generated by a tool (for example, the code execution tool) can be downloaded." (2026-09-02 `file.content.download`, 2026-09-03 `cookie.files.content-original`). *Evidence: docs/plan/08_files.md decision 3 + slice-1 inferences; platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/files ("Downloading a file"); Content-Disposition parsing, checked against anthropic-cli v1.30.0 — cmdutil.go createDownloadFile; internal/api/files.go downloadFile* The refusal string is recorded now, from the console lane: ``File `file_…` is not downloadable. Only files generated by a tool (for example, the code execution tool) can be…`` — a 400, and it says something the plan did not assume, that **only tool-generated files are downloadable at all**, so an uploaded file is never a download whatever headers it would carry. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probe `cookie.files.content-original`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §A.* **A third status now shares this route and its precedence is inferred**: past `expires_at` the docs say plainly that "Downloading its content (`GET /v1/files/{file_id}/content`) returns a 404 error", but not where that check sits against the not-downloadable 400 and this platform's own environment-key mount gate. It is answered first here, so an expired file reads as absent on both lanes rather than as "not downloadable" on one and as bytes on the other; a recording of a content GET for an expired *upload* — where the reference must choose between 400 and 404 — would settle it (#655, plan 49). *Tracked: #78 (the Content-Disposition shape, which needs a download that actually succeeds — a tool-generated file, not the upload this probe used; and the expired-upload precedence above); landed for #55.*
- **DELETE /v1/files/{id} — dangling references tolerated** — Deleting a file still referenced by a session's `resources[]` is allowed (hard delete, no cascade or RESTRICT); later materialization records the missing file per-resource and the agent sees an absent mount, rather than the delete failing. The `files` row is authoritative for existence on both injection points: the brain's `resolveFilesBlock` joins the row and the executor's `materializeFile` checks it before streaming, so on any later injection or materialization pass a deleted file is dropped from the brain's next-turn block and its owed-to-the-drain object is not mounted onto a fresh or reprovisioned sandbox. It does not retract bytes an earlier pass already materialized into a live sandbox — those persist until the mounted set changes (the sentinel-idempotence residual below). **Expiry inherits that residual rather than tightening it** (#655, plan 49): past `expires_at` no pass mounts the file again on either half of the pull protocol, but a sandbox already holding the bytes keeps them, which is what the docs describe for deletion too — files "may persist in active Messages API calls and associated tool uses". The reference tolerates the delete too: an upload a live session mounted was deleted with 200 `file_deleted`, a later read of it answered 404 and the session's `resources[]` came back unchanged (2026-09-02 batch2 idx 405–407). Neither side leaves a dangling mount there, because the resource names the session's own copy, which survived (idx 408) and here keeps its bytes — the per-resource-copy entry below (#578). What dangles is a deleted copy, and here also a deleted upload that a session created before #578 still names; what the reference does when the copy is the file deleted is unrecorded. *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording above; docs/plan/08_files.md decision 2 + "Recording checklist"; internal/api/files.go deleteFile, internal/executor/files.go materializeFile, internal/brain/files.go resolveFilesBlock* *Tracked: #78 (what deleting the id a session's `resources[]` names — on the reference, the session's copy — does to its mount); landed for #55 and #578.*
- **POST /v1/sessions (create) and POST …/resources — file resource validation** — Rooting, the already-rooted pass-through and the `/uploads/<name>` alias are CONFIRMED by recordings (#323, #848), the rest INFERRED. Every supplied `mount_path` roots under the session's uploads directory — `/data.csv` and `data.csv` alike land at `/mnt/session/uploads/data.csv`, and `/tmp/elsewhere.txt` was mounted at `/mnt/session/uploads/tmp/elsewhere.txt` (2026-09-02 batch2 idx 389) — an omitted one is `/mnt/session/uploads/<file_id>`, parent directories are created automatically, and create and add resolve alike. A path already under `/mnt/session/uploads` passes through (idx 388, read back at idx 397). `/uploads/<name>` lands at `/mnt/session/uploads/<name>` (#848), recorded on session create (2026-09-12-console-141 api-fixtures idx 13), resources add (ui-network idx 288) and a deployment fire (api-fixtures idx 15's deployment, run at ui-network idx 266, its session read at idx 268); a deployment resolves it the same way at create and update, stores that path for its fire to mount, and echoes the spelling as sent (the deployment `mount_path` entry, above). #848's other reading, that every path roots under `/mnt/session/`, is refuted by idx 389. Inferred: (1) **the alias past the recordings** — the relative `uploads/<name>` is the same alias; `/uploads` or `uploads` alone mounts the file at `/mnt/session/uploads/<file_id>`, as an omitted `mount_path` does; a relative `mnt/session/uploads/x` still roots like any other. (2) A referenced file is proven to exist inside the create/add transaction — a missing file is a 404 (on session create the reference's recorded one since #540, "One or more files not found. Check that each `file_id` exists and is accessible: file_…", 2026-09-02 `sessF2.create.deleted-file-mount`, as is a malformed `file_id`'s 400, "Invalid file resource: invalid file_id: …"), not a deferred materialization miss, and an **expired** file is missing for this purpose (#655, plan 49): its content is contractually gone, so mounting it would place a file in the sandbox the content route refuses to serve. The docs' nearest sentence is about a different API — "A Messages request that references the file fails before inference" — which this platform does not serve, so that the reference refuses the mount too is an inference. (3) The **resolved** path must be ≤1024 bytes, storable text, and unique per session — a duplicate is 400, judged on the *cleaned resolved* path, so aliases of one mount collide (a pre-#323 non-canonical literal included); the deployment routes judge it on the resolved paths too, store the resolved path and echo the caller's spelling, which they hold to 1024 bytes as well (the deployment `mount_path` entry, above). (4) A path naming the root — `/`, `.` or `/mnt/session/uploads` — or one that still climbs above it after cleaning is a 400. **No backfill for #848:** a mount stored at `/mnt/session/uploads/uploads/<name>` before it stays there, since it may be meant. A deployment created then with `/uploads/<name>` keeps firing at that path, which is the one it stores, and a GET-then-POST of its echoed resources keeps it too, the echo of an element stored before #849 being that rooted path; re-sending `/uploads/<name>` moves it. An add retried across the upgrade with the same `/uploads/<name>` body is no longer refused as a duplicate of the doubled mount, so the session holds both. Rooting also means a `mount_path` created from now on cannot *name* a location outside the sandbox's writable set — lexically and for new resources only (no backfill; the agent-writable uploads directory still admits an intermediate symlink redirecting a mount's bytes, the mount sentinel's accepted single-tenant tampering residual, below). *Evidence: public docs https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/files § "File paths" and https://platform.claude.com/cookbook/managed-agents-data-analyst-agent; the recordings 2026-09-02 batch2 idx 388, 389 and 397 and 2026-09-12-console-141 (api-fixtures idx 13 and 15; ui-network idx 266, 268 and 288; the attach dialog in screenshots 33 and 62); checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsFileResourceParams, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionresource.go BetaManagedAgentsFileResource, and the `/uploads/…` spellings in the SDK's tests (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession_test.go TestBetaSessionNewWithOptionalParams; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionresource_test.go TestBetaSessionResourceAddWithOptionalParams; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeployment_test.go TestBetaDeploymentNewWithOptionalParams and TestBetaDeploymentUpdateWithOptionalParams) and the prompt in the SDK repository's examples/agents-with-files/main.go, a separate module the pin does not fetch (read in the checkout 2026-09-17); internal/api/sessionresources.go, whose resolveMountPath comment now holds the rooting, style-not-root, pass-through, `uploads` alias, clean-before-rooting and lexical-containment derivations dropped from here, pinned by TestResolveMountPath and TestMountPathTakenCleansStoredPaths (internal/api/unit_test.go) and TestSessionFileResourceRoundTrip, TestDeploymentFireResolvesTheUploadsAlias and TestSessionResourceValidation (internal/api/sessionresources_test.go) and TestDeploymentRowsStoredResolvedFireWhereTheyDid (internal/api/deploymentruns_test.go); containment's two limits in docs/self-hosted-security.md; the pre-#323 behavior corrected here in docs/changelog/0.2.0.md. **docs/plan/08_files.md decision 6 is superseded on this point, not authority** — it still reads "must be absolute, NUL-free, ≤ 1024 bytes"; left unedited, an archived plan being a record, not a live spec.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording of a relative `uploads/x` or `mnt/session/uploads/x`, or of `/uploads` alone, read back from session GET, would settle inference (1)); landed for #55 and #848.*
- **Session file resources — a per-resource session-scoped copy, minted as the reference mints it (converged for #578)** — **Recorded, and matched.** Every path that mounts a file mints a fresh `file_id` per resource, and `resources[].file_id` echoes it: session create (2026-09-02 batch2 idx 388 and 389; 2026-09-03 batch2 idx 17; 2026-09-12-console-141 api-fixtures idx 13), resources add (console-141 ui-network idx 288) and a deployment fire (ui-network idx 266, the session read at idx 268 and api-fixtures idx 18), two resources naming one upload getting two copies (idx 388). The deployment itself keeps echoing the upload's id (api-fixtures idx 15; ui-network idx 232 and 305), and an omitted `mount_path` still defaults to `/mnt/session/uploads/<the requested id>` (idx 388; 2026-09-03 idx 17). The docs say it outright: "A new `file_id` is created that references the instance of the file in the session. These copies do not count against your storage limits." A copy is a `files` object of its own: `GET /v1/files/{copy}` answers 200 with the upload's `filename` (kept when the mount renames it, ui-network idx 292), `size_bytes` and `mime_type`, its own `created_at`, `downloadable:false` and `scope: {type:"session", id}` (idx 396 and 408; ui-network idx 243) — here on every read, where the reference sends `scope` only under the managed-agents beta header, as the `anthropic-beta` headers entry above records. Copies list under `?scope_id=<session>` (ui-network idx 243, 274 and 292) and in neither unfiltered list (2026-09-02 idx 391, taken while they existed; api-fixtures idx 30, taken after the archive). Deleting the upload leaves them: `DELETE` 200 (idx 405), the copy still 200 (idx 408), `resources[]` unchanged (idx 407), the agent still reading the bytes in its live sandbox (idx 413). Archiving a session leaves `resources[]` naming its copies (api-fixtures idx 25; ui-network idx 301), and each copy still answered `DELETE` with 200 afterwards (api-fixtures idx 33–35). An environment key gets 404 on a copy's `/content` as on the upload's (2026-09-03 idx 19); this platform's worker lane serves it, the copy being what a session in its environment mounts (the content-lane entry above). **How, ours:** a copy aliases its source's stored object rather than copying the bytes, so "do not count against your storage limits" holds without a quota to exempt it from (the storage-quota entry above). Migration 0046 lets a row name the key its bytes are at, `object_key`, which a copy sets and every row that owns its object leaves NULL, its id still deriving its key; every reader of a file's bytes reads that key instead of deriving one from the id it was asked for, and a copy names its source in `source_file_id`. The object-delete queue counts references: a key some row still names is dropped rather than owed, so deleting the upload leaves its copies' object, and deleting the last row naming it owes the object to the drain. **Inferred, ours** — what no recording settles: (1) deleting a session deletes its copies with its outputs, though the docs delete only "files the session itself produced", and a copy is scoped to the session without being produced by it; the shared object stays while another row names it. (2) Deleting a file resource leaves its copy, listed under `?scope_id=` and deletable, as an archive does. (3) Deleting a copy is allowed and dangles its mount, the dangling-references entry above. (4) A copy inherits its source's `expires_at` — the same content, the same lifetime — so it stops mounting and is swept with the upload. (5) A copy's `/content` under a management credential is the `downloadable:false` 400 every upload gets. (6) The unfiltered list leaves every session-scoped row out, harvested outputs included (the outputs-harvest entry below), and `ids[]` sent without `scope_id` meets the same default. (7) A copy, or a harvested output, can itself be mounted, minting a copy of the same object. (8) A dream mounts its transcripts as themselves, never through a copy (the dream-transcripts entry below). (9) No backfill: a session created before #578 keeps echoing and mounting the upload's id, and deleting that upload still dangles its mount. **Rolling upgrade:** binaries built before 0046 are not supported against its schema, so rolling back below 0046 is unsupported (#856); migration 0047 dropped the copy-delete guard and the tombstone trigger 0046 had added for them, and a copy is deleted like any other row. *Evidence: the managed-agents-wire-recordings entries above; public docs https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/files § "Mounting files in a session" (the copy sentence) and § "Listing and downloading session files" (`scope_id` needs the managed-agents header), and https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/session-operations § "Deleting a session" (both fetched 2026-10-02); the SDK documents neither the copy nor which id the default path names (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionresource.go BetaManagedAgentsFileResource; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsFileResourceParams.MountPath) and describes a scope only as "the context in which it was created" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betafile.go BetaFileMetadata.Scope and BetaFileListParams.ScopeID); internal/executor/files.go materializeFile; internal/executor/harvest.go settleHarvest; internal/brain/grader.go deliverablesSection; pinned by internal/api/filescopies_test.go and filescopieslocks_test.go, internal/store/files_object_key_test.go, drop_file_copy_guard_test.go and files_deletes_test.go, internal/executor's TestMaterializesASessionCopy and TestReHarvestLeavesTheSessionsCopies, and internal/brain's TestOutcomeGraderLeavesTheSessionsCopiesOut.* *Tracked: #78 (what the reference does to a session's copy when the session, the resource or the copy is deleted; whether a copy inherits a non-null `expires_at`; whether its unfiltered list hides harvested outputs and what `ids[]` does with a copy; and anything about dreams, which the recording organization cannot start); landed for #578 and #856.*
- **POST /v1/sessions and POST …/resources — the documented 500-files-per-session maximum is not enforced** — The docs state "A maximum of 500 files is supported per session." The spec bounds a deployment's `resources` at "Maximum 500", which this platform enforces at deployment create and update (`deploymentResourcesMax`), but gives a session create's `resources` no maximum, and the add endpoint takes one resource at a time. Here a session caps memory stores and repositories (`maxMemoryStoresPerSession`, `maxReposPerSession`) and not files: create and resources add accept any number of file resources, bounded only by the 4 MiB request body. It is left unenforced rather than enforced with a refusal of our own wording, because how the reference refuses is unrecorded — the status and message, whether adds and fires count against the same 500 as create, and whether the count is of resources or of the copies they mint; no recording mounts more than two files in one session. *Evidence: public docs https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/managed-agents/files § "Multiple files" (fetched 2026-10-02); checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCreateDeploymentParams.properties.resources and checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — spec components.schemas.BetaManagedAgentsCreateSessionParams.properties.resources; internal/api/deployments.go deploymentResourcesMax; internal/api/sessionresources.go parseResources, maxMemoryStoresPerSession and maxReposPerSession; internal/api/wire.go maxBodyBytes* *Tracked: #78 (the reference's refusal past 500 files — its status and words, and what it counts — which enforcing the cap here would have to match).*
- **POST /v1/sessions (create) — github_repository resource validation and sealed token storage** — A `github_repository` resource is validated create-time-local, no network call proving the repo or token: `url` must be the exact canonical `https://github.com/{owner}/{repo}` (optional `.git`; no userinfo, port, query, fragment — a bare trailing `?` or `#` included — extra segments, or trailing slash — the url is a rendered, logged field, so every credential carrier is refused; both segments are bounded to `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` and a repo name `path.Clean` would rewrite (`.`, `..`) is refused, so the derived default mount path is clean and storable by construction — and the derived default is validated like a supplied one); `authorization_token` must be non-empty and at most 8 KiB (the cap keeps every `secrets.Cipher` backend inside its plaintext ceiling); `checkout` is a strict `{type:"branch",name}` | `{type:"commit",sha}` union with `sha` exactly 40 hex, stored and rendered **as given — `null` when omitted**, the default branch resolving at clone time; `mount_path` defaults to `/workspace/<repo-name>` (last URL segment, `.git` stripped) and, when supplied, must equal its `path.Clean` form, not take a reserved target (`/`, `/tmp`, and from plan 36 slice 3 `/mnt/memory` or any path below it — the memory stores' parent, decision 8), not nest with another repo mount, and no resource may sit at a proper ancestor of a repo mount (a resource inside a repo mount stays legal; the cross-resource rules compare the file arm by its uploads-resolved path, #323); a session mounts at most eight repositories. The token itself never enters the verbatim-echoed `sessions.resources` jsonb — it seals through `secrets.Cipher` into `session_resource_credentials` (ciphertext + key id, session-cascade lifetime), and a cipher-less deployment refuses the create rather than storing anything. The SDK pins only the required fields, the mount-path default string, and the checkout variants; every strictness above, the as-given checkout rendering and the cap values rest on that. Several refusals were recorded on 2026-09-03 (batch1 `session.create.repo-*`) and answer in the reference's words on session create since #540 — the URL's ("Invalid `github_repository` resource: invalid github_repository url: must be https://github.com/{owner}/{repo} with no .git suffix"), the overlapping mounts' and a missing or empty token's — and two of them differ in more than words: the reference refuses the `.git` suffix this grammar accepts (that sentence is said here only of a URL refused for another reason), and caps the token at 4,096 characters where this platform takes 8 KiB, so that refusal keeps ours. A deployment's resources were recorded with their own sentences (2026-09-05 batch3 `rec84.repo.*`) and take those. *Evidence: docs/plan/25_git-repo-mounting.md decisions 1-3 + slice-1 inferences; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsGitHubRepositoryResourceParams and BetaManagedAgentsGitHubRepositoryResourceParamsCheckoutUnion, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionresource.go BetaManagedAgentsGitHubRepositoryResource, "write-only and never returned" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betadeployment.go BetaManagedAgentsGitHubRepositoryResourceConfig); internal/api/sessionresources.go parseRepoResource / parseGitHubRepoURL / insertSessionResourceCredentials; internal/store/migrations/0020_session_resource_credentials.sql* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the validation strictness, the as-given checkout rendering, the cap values and the refusal shapes); landed for #55.*
- **Session resource sub-endpoints — mutation gate, deletion semantics, and the token-rotation reject** — `sesrsc_` resources live in the `sessions.resources` jsonb array; the list endpoint returns all when `limit` is omitted (SDK: "if omitted, returns all") and paginates by the last-id cursor otherwise. Add and delete are mutations, rejected on an archived session (the session-update gate) but allowed regardless of running/terminated status; an added file also obeys create's no-resource-above-a-repo-mount rule; deletion removes the reference only and never unmounts a live sandbox. `POST …/resources/{rid}` (the token-rotation update) is **live for `github_repository` resources since plan 25 slice 1**: the new token re-seals over the stored ciphertext, the resource's `updated_at` bumps inside the echoed jsonb, and the full rendered resource returns; a file resource keeps the "only github_repository resources support token rotation" reject, an archived session the archived-mutation reject, and a cipher-less deployment refuses the rotation outright. `DELETE …/{rid}` on a repo resource is likewise rejected — "Repositories are attached for the lifetime of the session" (the public guide); the SDK's generic Delete proves only that the route exists. None of the rotation response shape, the repo-delete rejection, or their error strings are recorded. Two neighbours are, and answer in the reference's words since #540: the add endpoint judges the type before reading the variant and refuses a non-file one `Failed to parse request: type: "memory_store" is not a valid value` (2026-09-02 `session.resources.post.memory_store`), and an unknown resource id on get, delete or rotation is `Resource not found: …` (`session.resources.get.bogus-sesrsc`, `.get.by-memory_store_id`, `.delete.by-memory_store_id`). *Evidence: docs/plan/08_files.md slice-2 inferences + "Recording checklist"; docs/plan/25_git-repo-mounting.md decisions 2-3; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionresource.go BetaSessionResourceUpdateParams; internal/api/sessionresources.go rotateResourceTokenTx / deleteSessionResourceTx* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the rotation response shape, the repo-delete rejection and their error strings); landed for #55.*
- **`github_repository` materialization — clone timing, contents, failure surface, and the Mounted-repositories block (plan 25 slice 2)** — The reference documents only that repositories are cloned into the sandbox; every operational property below is ours (INFERRED). **Timing:** the clone runs in the executor on the session's first work item, on our lazy-provision lifecycle (nothing exists to clone into before the first tool call); the reference may well provision eagerly at create. **What is fetched:** a full, non-shallow clone, no submodule recursion, no LFS smudging, with `checkout` resolved *here* rather than at create. Two descriptors create accepts are refused only at clone time, both ours: the **null commit id** (forty zeros) and a **branch name go-git cannot build a refspec from** (containing `:`); both report reason `checkout`. **Credentials:** the token rides each request's `Authorization` header (`x-access-token` basic auth), never the remote URL, so the stored `.git/config` is token-free and the sandbox never holds the credential in any form (a security property, but agent-observable). Consequently a **rotation is not retroactive**: future clones only, never a checkout already on disk. **Idempotence** is probe-only — `<mount>/.git` present means materialized, no sentinel file; the probe is a `test -e` exec, then, where that does not answer, a stat through the file API (on Kubernetes the read exec's own exit status, which an image's startup output cannot displace), asked twice — and a pass where neither answers clones nothing, records no `session.error` (nothing says the repository is missing), counts the mount `unprobed`, and asks again next pass, never re-cloning over a checkout it could not see (#860). **Failure:** a repository that will not clone records a `session.error` of payload `type` `github_repository_clone_error`, carrying `resource_id`, `mount_path`, `url`, a fixed human-readable `message`, a `reason` from `auth`/`not_found`/`network`/`checkout`/`too_large`/`timeout`/`internal`, and `retry_status` always `{"type":"retrying"}`, `auth` included; it is deduped per (resource, reason), and the session runs on with its other mounts. The variant name, its fields — bar `retry_status`, required on every variant of the reference's error union and carried by every other `session.error` this platform writes, so only its always-`retrying` value is ours — the dedupe and the tolerate-don't-fail stance are all ours (the reference surfaces clone failure in a way nothing records, possibly not as an event at all); `too_large`/`timeout` are ours twice over, since the budgets they report (`EXECUTOR_REPO_CLONE_MAX_BYTES`, `EXECUTOR_REPO_CLONE_TIMEOUT`) have no wire surface. **The "Mounted repositories" block** the brain appends to the system prompt — heading, `- <path> (<url>, branch X | commit SHA | default branch)` lines, placement after the skills and files blocks, `cloud`-only gating (the self_hosted entry above) — is inferred, as its files twin's wording was until a recording settled it (*Uploads pointer*, below): the reference must tell the model where the checkout is, but nothing records how. *Evidence: docs/plan/25_git-repo-mounting.md decisions 1, 4-8 + slice-2 inferences; platform.claude.com managed-agents/github (fetched 2026-08-06 — mounting documented, mechanics not); the derivations cut from this entry live in comments in internal/executor/repoclone.go (per-checkout clone shape, null-sha substitution, refspec blame in cloneReason) and internal/executor/repos.go (materializeRepos, extractRepo, emitRepoCloneError), with internal/brain/repos.go for the block; pinned by internal/executor/repos_test.go's TestRepoZeroShaIsRefused, TestCloneReasonBlamesTheRightSide, TestRepoTokenNeverEntersTheSandbox, TestRepoRestoredCheckpointSkipsClone, TestRepoInterruptedExtractMidTarLeavesNoTree, TestRepoCloneFailuresSurface and TestRepoCloneErrorDedupe* **What the reference fetches, and its failure surface, are now recorded.** `authorization_token` is **required** — omitting it on a public repository is a 400, `resources.0.github_repository.authorization_token: value is required` — and **never verified**: with a deliberately wrong token, `https://github.com/anthropics/skills` cloned in full and **no `session.error` was emitted at all**, so a required field that is never checked invites callers to pass anything and a wrong token fails silently rather than loudly. From inside the checkout: `git rev-parse --is-shallow-repository` is **`true`**, so the clone is shallow; there are **two remotes**, `github` and `origin`, both the plain `https://github.com/anthropics/skills.git`; there is **no credential in the remote URL**; and `mount_path` is `/workspace/skills`, the repository name rebased under `/workspace`, which matches what this platform derives. **The first two do not match us** and were recorded here before they were checked: `repoclone.go` builds `git.CloneOptions` with no `Depth`, so our clone is **full**, and go-git's `Clone` creates the single remote `origin` where the reference carries two. #582 prices both — the shallow default against our commit-checkout case, which needs arbitrary history, and the second remote, which costs one call and makes a script written against the reference work here unchanged. One observation is recorded as an anomaly rather than a conclusion, because it is the opposite of what a fresh clone into a fresh sandbox would leave: the working tree's mtimes predate the session by eighty minutes while `.git` is stamped at clone time, which points at a shared or cached checkout — plausibly the same per-session filestore mount the skills entry records, unproven here.
