Clone **timing** is therefore the half still open, and the anomaly is what an eager-versus-lazy probe would have to explain. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `sess.create.repo-mount-anon` and `session.events.list.repo-inspect`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §I.* *Tracked: #78 (clone timing, the cached-checkout anomaly, and the Mounted-repositories block are unsettled; a valid read-only PAT would sharpen all three), #582 (price the shallow default against our commit-checkout case, and the second remote); landed for #55.*

- **Uploads pointer — the reference's sentence is recorded and matched; its gating and placement stay inferred** — Both execution halves materialize a session's file mounts into the sandbox (the executor for platform-managed sessions, the BYOC worker for self-hosted ones), and the system prompt describes none of them: the brain appends the reference's sentence as recorded, "User uploads (files uploaded to the session by the user) are available at `/mnt/session/uploads`. Use `ls` on that directory to see available files.", and no filename, MIME type or size. The one exception is ours: a live mount whose stored `mount_path` lies outside `/mnt/session/uploads` — possible only for a resource written by an unreleased build between file resources landing and #323 rooting every path, 2026-07-23 to 2026-08-07, since no migration rewrote them and the executor still materializes them where they were stored — is named by path alone, quoted Go-style so a newline or comma in it cannot change what the line says, because `ls` on the uploads directory would never find it: after the sentence ("Files are also mounted at: …"), or in place of it ("Files are mounted at: …") when no live mount lies under that directory, which the sentence would otherwise send the agent to list with none of its files in it. It replaces a "Mounted files" block of our own, a lead line plus one `<mount_path> (<filename>, <mime_type>, <size> bytes)` bullet per mount, which the recording refuted; the owner accepted that a mount at its default path, named `file_<id>`, no longer tells the model the original filename or MIME type. **Still inferred:** the gating — the sentence appears only while the session has at least one live file mount, a dangling or expired one being a logged, counted miss and never fatal, because the one recorded quote came from a session with uploads; and the placement — the reference carries the sentence as one bullet of a "You have been invoked in the following environment:" section, beside the working directory and the `/mnt/session/outputs` instruction, which this platform does not render, so ours stands alone after the Level-1 skills block and before any runtime `system.message` text. The quote is model-mediated: the same session refused a direct request to echo its prompt (`sessF.send.mounted-files-prompt-echo`), so a listing elsewhere in it is not excluded with certainty, though "use `ls` … to see available files" is hard to reconcile with one. *Evidence: internal/brain/files.go (uploadsPointer, resolveFilesBlock), internal/brain/replay.go (buildRequest placement); pinned by internal/brain/files_test.go's TestResolveFilesBlock, TestResolveFilesBlockListsLegacyMounts and TestResolveFilesBlockNamesOnlyLegacyMountsWithoutThePointer and internal/brain/filesinject_test.go's TestFilesInjectedIntoModelRequest and TestFileAddedMidSessionLeavesSystemPromptUnchanged.* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02/batch2.json, probe `sessF.events.after-ls-after-delete`, agent message `sevt_01KeyMJpN1xZhVefHqySMkx3`, in a session mounting `/mnt/session/uploads/file_014N6apS35oRsarsf8eAjXKN` and `/mnt/session/uploads/custom/notes.txt`.* *Tracked: #78 (whether the reference includes the sentence in a session with no uploads, and where it sits relative to the skills block and runtime system messages); landed for #55, and the pointer for #681.*
- **File-mount materialization — sentinel idempotence, extended to mounts** — Both halves stream each mount's bytes to its `mount_path` before tools run — executor from object storage, BYOC worker over the content lane — recording a `{workdir}/.files_materialized` sentinel of the sorted `{file_id, mount_path}` pairs that landed; re-provisioning the same live sandbox re-streams only the mounts the marker does not record under the file the set names at that path now, and those a presence probe finds gone — the rest stay as the agent left them, so a mount that failed to land is retried alone and one the agent moved away lands again alone, while a marker that cannot be read or parsed re-lands the whole set. Two deltas from the skills sentinel, forced by arbitrary-sized mounts: presence is a `test -e` probe that lists the absent paths in one framed exec per ~120 KiB of paths, not a read-back, so it checks existence, not content — a mount an agent truncates or overwrites in place is kept until the mount is reassigned or the marker is lost (skills' accepted single-tenant tampering residual); and bytes go through `Sandbox.WriteFileStream`, not the buffered `WriteFiles` skills use. A dangling mount is skipped, never fatal, counted in `files.materialized` (`not_found`) and outside the `files_materialize` span's landed count — the skills precedent. Two accepted residuals, both single-tenant and self-healing: (1) **closed by #71** — both backends now stream to a temporary sibling and rename only once the byte count validates, so a stream that failed partway (a short body, a wrong `Content-Length`, a cancelled context, a full disk) leaves the target as it was rather than exposing truncated or previously-mounted bytes to the current work item, and the sentinel still excludes that mount for a later pass; (2) a mount at the sentinel's own path is honored (the file wins) but suppresses the sentinel: a `mountAtPath` guard disables the marker on **both** the skip-read and the write, so marker-equal bytes there cannot short-circuit re-materialization and the marker cannot clobber the file — that session re-materializes every pass. **Since #323 the guard is narrower than it reads:** mounts resolve under `/mnt/session/uploads` and the default workdir is `/workspace`, so only a path stored before #323 or a workdir configured under the uploads root can still collide. The reference worker has no file materialization at all — the mechanism is wholly ours. *Evidence: docs/plan/08_files.md decision 7, slices 3–4; derivations cut from here are comments in internal/{executor,worker}/files.go (materializeFiles/SetupFiles, keptMounts, mountAtPath) and internal/sandbox/probe.go (ProbeEach), pinned by TestFilesMaterializeIdempotent, TestFilesRematerializeAfterMountDeleted, Test{Files,SetupFiles}{RelandOnlyTheMountThatIsGone,FailedMountDoesNotRelandTheOthers,ReassignedMountRelandsAlone,UnreadableSentinelRelandsTheSet} and Test{Files,SetupFiles}SentinelPathCollision; atomic write: internal/sandbox/sandbox.go's WriteFileStream doc, #71 argument in sandboxtest/contract.go WriteThatFailsLeavesTheTargetAlone; #323 rooting in internal/api/sessionresources.go resolveMountPath* **The recording dissolves this question rather than answering it, and the entry is kept only to say so.** The reference does not stream a file mount's bytes into a container at all: it mounts session-scoped object storage over rclone FUSE, keyed by the session id — `rclone-filestore:sesn_…:/mnt/skills` → `/mnt/skills` (ro), `…:/mnt/session/uploads` → `/mnt/session/uploads` (ro), and `…:/mnt/session/outputs` → `/mnt/session/outputs` (rw). So "does a re-provisioned sandbox re-stream a mount it already placed" has no referent there: a fresh sandbox re-mounts the same filestore path and the content is simply back. Skills are the one place bytes still land in the workdir — extracted out of `/mnt/skills` rather than fetched, as the skills-materialization entry above now records — so the mount removes the *fetch* everywhere and the in-container copy only here. The sentinel answers a question the reference does not have, which is a better argument for it than the one this entry was opened with, and the same mount model explains two things recorded elsewhere in the wave — that the outputs harvest reads `/mnt/session/outputs`, the one read-write mount, and that a `github_repository` checkout carries mtimes older than its session. One further fact fell out of the same probe and is not in the analysis file, read straight off the create response: **a `file` session resource echoes a different `file_id` than the one requested** — `file_01W2uSvi…` went in, the resource came back carrying `file_01UfkHfJ…`, while `mount_path` kept the *original* id as its filename (`/mnt/session/uploads/file_01W2uSvi…`). The reference copies the upload into a session-scoped object before mounting it, so the id a caller passes and the id the session holds are not the same id, and neither of them is downloadable on the environment-key lane. This platform now mints that copy too, as an alias of the upload's object rather than a copy of its bytes (the per-resource-copy entry above, #578), so both halves read a copy's bytes from the object the upload stored. What stays ours and unrecorded is the dangling-mount arm. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `session.events.list.skill-paths` and `session.create.cloud-with-file` (entry 17's response body), analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §H.* *Tracked: #78 (what the reference does with a dangling mount; whether we adopt the mount model or keep staging is a design question rather than a recording one); landed for #55 and #578.*
- **`credential_host_unreachable_error` — the reference recorded, our emission point and policy lifetime not yet aligned (plan 12 slice 4c-2c)** — The SDK pins the shape (a variant of the `session.error` event's error union: `credential_id`, `vault_id`, `message`, `retry_status{type: retrying|exhausted|terminal}`, `type`) and the trigger ("An `environment_variable` credential's `auth.networking.allowed_hosts` includes a host the environment's network policy does not permit" — a *configuration conflict*, not a runtime substitution miss; runtime non-substitution on a non-allowed host is separately documented as normal, non-erroring behavior). Everything else is this platform's inference: **when** — detected at gate-config render (`GET /internal/v1/gate/config`), the first point that observes both configs for a session whose gate is live, re-evaluated every fetch so vault/policy edits track without a restart (the reference's detection point is unrecorded — create-time validation would also be defensible), and the append itself is detached from the fetch response — post-commit, its own 10-second budget, at most one in flight per session, never failing or delaying the fetch — so the event lands shortly after the config render that triggered it (externally observable in event ordering; the reference's delivery timing is likewise unrecorded); **cadence** — appended best-effort once per (session, credential) (an events-table check-then-append dedupe — a concurrent duplicate fetch can rarely double-emit), so a conflict is a signal, not a per-fetch drumbeat; **`retry_status`** — `{"type": "retrying"}`, chosen because detection re-runs and an edit heals the conflict (nothing is observed about when the reference reports `exhausted` or `terminal`); **`message`** — our own wording, naming the uncovered `allowed_hosts` entries (hostnames are non-secret); **entry coverage** — an exact entry is covered iff the policy matches it, while a `*.D` wildcard entry (a whole subdomain family) is covered only by a policy wildcard at or above `D` — a policy's exact host can never cover a family, and a broader wildcard is not covered by a narrower one (`egress.HostSet.CoversEntry`; the SDK sentence never says how a wildcard *entry* compares against a wildcard *policy*). A recording of the reference with a deliberately conflicting credential would confirm the emission point, cadence, `retry_status`, and message. *Evidence: BetaManagedAgentsCredentialHostUnreachableError and its doc comment (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsCredentialHostUnreachableError) and the networking docs (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betavaultcredential.go BetaManagedAgentsLimitedCredentialNetworkingParams and BetaManagedAgentsLimitedCredentialNetworkingResponse), the cited types unchanged since anthropic-sdk-go v1.59.0 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsCredentialHostUnreachableError and since anthropic-sdk-go v1.59.0 — betavaultcredential.go BetaManagedAgentsLimitedCredentialNetworkingParams and BetaManagedAgentsLimitedCredentialNetworkingResponse; internal/api/gateconfig.go emitUnreachableCredentials, internal/egress/hostset.go CoversEntry* **All four are now recorded, and two of them this platform does not match.** On the reference, emission is at session start, **before `thread_status_running`**, and it is **once per session, not once per turn** — three messages produced exactly one error and the second work cycle produced none, which is the sharp contrast with `mcp_connection_failed_error`'s every-cycle cadence and is coherent with it: a policy conflict is a static session-scoped fact, a dial is retried. It is **one error per offending credential**, two arriving back to back where a vault held two. `retry_status` is `retrying`. The key set is `credential_id, message, retry_status, type, vault_id` — no `mcp_server_name`, so the two error families do not share a shape. The message states a contract worth adopting verbatim: *a session's network policy is captured at creation and is not affected by later environment changes* (which is the question #297 is deciding for cloud environments). Coverage was probed as a truth table across four session creates, and it confirms `CoversEntry` rather than plain matching: an environment on `api.rec78.invalid` fails to cover a credential on `other.rec78.invalid` **and** one on `*.rec78.invalid`, while an environment on `*.rec78.invalid` covers both — so **a credential's wildcard is not satisfied by an exact host in the environment policy**, and the environment's patterns must cover the credential's entries using the same wildcard semantics the allowed_hosts entry records. **Where this platform differs**, and it is the emission rather than the classification: `emitUnreachableCredentials` runs from a **gate-config fetch**, in a goroutine `getGateConfig` detaches, so a session that never provokes one emits nothing at all — where the reference emits at session start regardless. And the conflict is computed against the **live** environment row, deliberately (*"resolution is read-time, so an edit heals or introduces a conflict without a restart"*), where the reference's own message says the policy is captured at creation and later environment edits do not reach a running session. Ours is also "best-effort once" per (session, credential) via a check-then-append that admits a double-emit under concurrent fetches, against a once-per-session the reference states outright. The classification, key set, `retry_status` and per-credential multiplicity do match. #579 owns the emission point and the cadence; the policy-lifetime half is the question #297 is already deciding for cloud environments, and the recorded contract is now attached there as evidence. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probe `session.events.list.cred-host-conflict` and the four creates beside it, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §F.* *Tracked: #579 (emit at session start against a creation-time snapshot, or argue the read-time model as a divergence); landed for #50.*
- **web_search's date line — adopted by owner decision, rendered per request in UTC (the zone inferred), and the cached prefix turns over daily** — The reference's `web_search` description ends its `<query_guidelines>` with `- Today's date is 2026-09-02` on the recorded day: an ISO 8601 date with no time, as the last bullet. The owner chose on 2026-10-01 (#682) to adopt it, so `toolset.Tools` renders the line from the request's clock and the brain builds the tool list for each turn, from its clock at assembly. **The cost is deliberate and accepted.** The tool definitions head every model request, so any prompt cache keyed on the request prefix — a provider's automatic prefix caching, or a breakpoint a gateway adds — loses that prefix at the first request after each UTC midnight, for every agent with `web_search` enabled, which is on by default. Within a UTC day two turns offer identical tool bytes, and across a UTC midnight the other seven built-ins do not move (TestWebSearchDateIsRenderedPerRequest); the delegation tools never do, being rendered once at load with no clock (internal/toolset/delegation.go renderDefs). **The zone is the inference.** The recorded request started at 2026-09-02T00:02:58Z and read 2026-09-02, on a session created at 2026-09-01T23:59:36Z. That rules out every zone behind UTC, where the date was still 2026-09-01, but cannot tell UTC from a zone ahead of it; and only under UTC does it also show the line rendered per request rather than at session creation. UTC is this platform's choice. An echo recorded late in a UTC day, after zones ahead of UTC have turned, would settle it. *Evidence: internal/toolset/webdescriptions.go (webSearchDescription); internal/brain/brain.go (Brain.now and the resolveTools call); TestWebSearchDateLineFollowsTheClock, TestWebSearchDateIsRenderedPerRequest, TestWebSearchDateComesFromTheWallClock* *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-02/batch2.json, probes `sessA.events.after-turn2` (the echo, and the `span.model_request_start` before it) and `session.create.five-stores` (the session's `created_at`).* *Tracked: #78 (an echo of web_search's description recorded late in a UTC day would settle the zone); landed for #682.*
- **Mixed web+sandbox turns — web-first hold-back ordering** — A turn calling both a web tool and a sandbox tool serializes web-first: the brain's settlement (and the confirmation resume) enqueues only the `web_exec` item, and the executor's web driver chains the sandbox `tool_exec` once every web call is answered — so on self_hosted the worker's item does not exist until then. The ordering is ours, not a recorded reference behavior; whether the reference orders a mixed turn this way or runs the two sides in parallel is unobserved. **The third possibility is not**: a 2026-09-02 recording caught the reference mixing them, one assistant turn emitting `web_search` and `bash` together (1 µs apart in the log) and both results landing together too — so "never mixes them at all" is ruled out, while the two live readings stay open, since that bash was an instant `echo` on a `cloud` session and so distinguishes neither ordering nor parallelism. One half of the original rationale has since been *measured* and softened: the slice-3 acceptance's mixed turn showed the real `ant` worker (checked against anthropic-cli v1.21.0 — worker.go handleWorkerPoll), on scanning a web `agent.tool_use`, logging "tool not owned by this runner; leaving the tool_use_id pending for its owner" and running only its own bash call — this client tolerates a non-owned call rather than answering it unknown-tool (its answered-diff also did not count the already-committed platform `agent.tool_result`, so the ownership check, not the hold-back, is what spared it). The hold-back is retained regardless: it makes the ordering deterministic rather than dependent on every client version's tolerance, and a client that *does* answer the whole set would otherwise wedge the call. An outstanding MCP call now sits ahead of both in the same ordering, for a reason the entry below on answering an MCP call records: only this platform's driver can answer one. *Evidence: internal/brain/brain.go (commitTurn's kind choice), internal/api/events.go (the confirmation-resume kind choice), internal/executor/webwork.go (the chained tool_exec), internal/worker/toolexec.go (the IsWebTool filter); docs/HISTORY.md § "Web-tools slices 2+3 acceptance" (the observed worker log line)* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference orders a mixed turn this way, runs the two sides in parallel, or never mixes them at all); landed for #47.*
- **user.define_outcome acceptance — everything past the typed schema and the documented rules (plan 21 slice 2)** — The SDK pins the shapes and the public docs pin the rules; the rest is ours, each item needing a recording. **Error shapes:** the one-at-a-time rejection, an out-of-range `max_iterations` (1–20; the docs say only "default 3, max 20"), a present-but-empty `description`, and an empty rubric `content`/`file_id` (the SDK pins only requiredness) are each a 400 `invalid_request_error` — its type the reference's, its message ours. **File rubrics (cap, scope policy and snapshot all ours):** the file must exist in the org scope (v1's single-tenant boundary) and fit a 256 KiB byte cap mirroring the SDK-documented 262144-character text bound, which we count in runes; accepted bytes are snapshotted at acceptance to an outcome-owned key (`outcomes/{outcome_id}/rubric`). **Interrupt settlement:** a non-terminal outcome flips to `interrupted` with a terminal `span.outcome_evaluation_end`: `outcome_evaluation_start_id` empty when no grading cycle had started (documented), the committed `span.outcome_evaluation_start` when the entry is `evaluating` (the lone carve-out of three non-terminal results); the entry's current `iteration` (0 with no cycle — emitted even on an end that had no grading cycle, the SDK tying the field to a start event this event does not have); our explanation text and a zero `usage`. All four are SDK-required fields, so only their values are ours. **Archive settlement (ours; the docs say nothing of a live outcome on archive):** archiving a session ends a live outcome the same way, with the same end event, explanation and flip, whatever the primary is parked on — a call the archive closes, a `wait_for_agents` on children the archive terminates, or nothing at all (`retries_exhausted`). **Wake semantics:** it wakes an idle session exactly as user.message does (status_running + a queued turn), is refused as one is while the primary awaits a response (*POST /v1/sessions/{id}/events — a message while the primary awaits a response*), and joins the mid-turn chaining set; the rendering is ours (the reference's agent-facing prompt is unobservable) — a user-role message of the description plus an inline text rubric, or a file rubric's description alone, each closing with a line naming the deliverables directory `/mnt/session/outputs/`. How the reference conveys that directory, and whether in this message, is unobserved. **initial_events:** a non-empty list creates the session directly in `running` (documented) with `session.status_running` and the primary's `session.thread_status_running` written ahead of the initial events, which its first turn consumes — processing order, as a recorded deployment run's list opens (#793; "Session threads — the primary thread …", above); the body bound is the platform's global 4 MiB / 413, stricter than the documented 32 MB. **Projection timing:** the entry is born `pending` and `completed_at` stays null until a terminal result — a field the SDK types as required, whose pre-terminal value no source shows. *Evidence: docs/plan/21_outcomes.md (the SDK pins and documented rules it quotes, 2026-08-02); the text-rubric cap and the end event's required fields (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsTextRubricParams.Content and BetaManagedAgentsSpanOutcomeEvaluationEndEvent) and the required `completed_at` (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasession.go BetaManagedAgentsOutcomeEvaluationResource.CompletedAt); the reasoning cut from this row survives as code comments in internal/events/{inbound,outcomes}.go, internal/brain/replay.go, internal/api/sessions.go, internal/api/events.go (the wake trigger and `snapshotRubrics`, whose comment holds the snapshot rationale) and internal/domain/outcome.go; shapes pinned by internal/api/outcomes_test.go (TestDefineOutcomeTextRubricCap, TestInterruptMidGradingReferencesStart, TestCreateSessionInitialEvents, and the archive's TestArchivingASessionParkedMidOutcomeInterruptsTheOutcome, TestArchivingASessionParkedOnAConfirmationMidOutcomeInterruptsTheOutcome, TestArchivingACoordinatorWaitingOnAGatedChildInterruptsTheOutcome, TestArchivingASessionIdleOnRetriesExhaustedInterruptsTheOutcome), internal/api/events_test.go TestSendValidationSweep and internal/brain/replay_test.go TestBuildRequestRendersDefineOutcome* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the reference's rejection messages and rubric bounds, its agent-facing rendering and where it names the deliverables directory, and its pre-terminal `completed_at`).*
- **The outcome grader — everything the reference keeps opaque (plan 21 slices 3–4)** — The docs pin the loop's observable shape only; each choice below is ours, INFERRED, pending a recording. **Call**: one request on the session's own resolved model config (the reference names no grader model); system = our grader charge + the rubric (a file rubric from its acceptance snapshot; no snapshot or no object store grades the description alone, with an inline note); user = the description + a `# Deliverables` section (cloud only) + a role-labeled plain-text transcript; no tools, no previews, no thinking. Bounds ours: 4,000 chars/item, 200,000/transcript, 20,000/persisted explanation, 64 KiB of inlined deliverables. **Verdict protocol**: the reply must end `VERDICT: satisfied|needs_revision|failed`; one without it reads as needs_revision carrying the whole text (the budget still ends the loop). **Entry lifecycle**: a `pending` entry flips to `running` in the commit of the primary's `span.model_request_start` for the request that reads its `user.define_outcome` (#793) — the SDK's pending/running boundary read as that request's start; entry state only, no wire event, and when the reference flips is unrecorded. **Trigger**: only a successful tool-less turn grades — a `requires_action` idle (HITL) or a `retries_exhausted` fault settles unevaluated and the outcome resumes on the next wake (the docs tie grading to the agent believing it has finished; the non-completion settlements are our reading). **Ordering ours**: grading is two-phase through the queue, so the committed start precedes the grader call — by queue latency, and on cloud by the deliverables harvest between them; heartbeats every 30s (cadence ours), never after the cycle's end event; an interrupt mid-grade wins and its verdict is discarded (the interrupt-settlement entry above). **Settlement**: satisfied/failed with budget left idle with plain `end_turn` (client input, or a live child's message, chains a turn first); needs_revision returns the entry to `running` with `iteration+1`; a final-cycle needs_revision is reported `max_iterations_reached` (boundary reading: up to max_iterations cycles total), and any verdict on the final cycle runs the one acknowledgment turn (the last-cycle entry below, #670) — every prompt replay-derived from the end event, no extra persisted event; a grader call that errors renders no end event at all, reverts the entry to `running`, and settles like a failed model turn (session.error + `retries_exhausted`, no automatic retry). **Usage**: the grader call's usage rides the end event and folds into the session's cumulative usage (whether the reference bills it is unrecorded). **Deliverables (slice 4)**: the docs show the grader scoring deliverables but never how they reach it, so the section's existence, format, inline rule and budget are ours; a storage-less deploy lists without contents, and self_hosted grades transcript-only. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/define-outcomes (fetched 2026-08-02); internal/brain/grader.go — its constants and doc comments hold every derivation cut from this row; internal/brain/brain.go (the pending→running flip), internal/events/span.go Heartbeat (the evaluating fence), internal/brain/replay.go (end-event injection), docs/ARCHITECTURE.md § "Outcome grading" (two-phase scheduling, the cloud harvest chain); internal/brain/grader_test.go TestOutcome{Satisfied,NeedsRevisionThenSatisfied,MaxIterationsReached,GraderError,InterruptDuringGrading,PendingFlipsRunningAtTurnStart,FileRubricSnapshot,GraderSeesDeliverables,GraderDeliverablesBudgetIsGreedy}* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the reference's grader model and prompt, when it flips an entry from pending to running, whether it bills the grader call's usage, and how deliverables reach the grader).*
- **An outcome's last cycle, and what a terminal grading settlement chains on (#670, #801)** — **The acknowledgment turn, reading (B) (INFERRED):** when no further evaluation cycle can run, `iteration + 1 >= max_iterations`, one acknowledgment turn follows the verdict whatever it is — `max_iterations_reached` as before, and now a `satisfied` or `failed` verdict on that cycle too; while budget remains, `satisfied` and `failed` still idle the session directly with plain `end_turn`. It is the one rule both sources fit. The recording: the archive's only `span.outcome_evaluation_end`, `satisfied` at `iteration` 0 of an outcome created with `max_iterations: 1`, is followed by an unprompted agent turn — `span.model_request_start`, `agent.message` "The outcome is complete. All criteria have been satisfied.", `span.model_request_end`, then the idle pairs — with no `user.*` event in the window. The SDK doc comment: "'satisfied': criteria met, session goes idle", and for `max_iterations_reached` "one final acknowledgment turn follows before the session goes idle" (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsSpanOutcomeEvaluationEndEvent.Result). **The confound:** that one verdict is both `satisfied` and the budget's last, so the bytes fit reading (A), a turn after every `satisfied`, as well as (B); (A) was never observed with budget left and is not adopted, and a `failed` verdict on the last cycle is on no recording at all. **What the turn is told (ours):** replay renders the verdict as a user turn from the end event — "The outcome grader reviewed your work and found it satisfies the rubric:", or for `failed` "The outcome grader found that the rubric cannot be applied to your work:", then the explanation and "Do not continue working. Briefly acknowledge the outcome." That is the least that tells the model the verdict, which the recorded acknowledgment shows the reference's model saw, and it keeps the request from ending on the assistant's own reply; with budget left nothing is rendered, so a later `user.message` replays as before. The closing "Do not continue working" — `max_iterations_reached`'s prompt's too — renders unless client input follows the verdict in its user turn: that input, such as a message posted around the verdict, the next outcome a client defines on seeing the end event, or the next message of a session whose acknowledgment never ran, is what the model must answer, so the verdict reaches it without the instruction. A child's row does not count: an ending notice the grading window held reads after the verdict, and the acknowledgment is still told to stop, a notice being no input worth a turn after a terminal verdict (#801). A turn the acknowledgment's reply closed keeps it on every later replay; a reply that persisted nothing (an empty `end_turn`, or thinking alone, which replay skips) closes no turn, so a later message joins the verdict's and the instruction goes, as that message joins any user turn such a reply left open. The rule reads the log, so it applies to sessions graded before it too: a session whose last cycle ended `satisfied` or `failed` now replays that verdict text, without the instruction, ahead of its next `user.message`. The acknowledgment is an ordinary model turn, so a provider failure there fails it like any other and idles the session `retries_exhausted` with the verdict already settled, where a verdict with budget left idles `end_turn` in its own commit. In the acknowledgment request the verdict comes before a notice or report that landed during the grading cycle, though that row has the lower seq, because a grading window holds input until the next start as a model request's does (the list-order entry). A cycle whose definition is missing from the log settles `failed` with no acknowledgment, there being no budget to have exhausted. **What a terminal grading settlement chains on (#801, ours):** a `satisfied` or `failed` verdict with budget left, and a failed grader call, chain the primary only on client input or on a message from a child that is still live — an unarchived child whose own `agent.thread_message_sent` to the primary, of the same content, is the send the row pairs with: it precedes the row with no other row the primary received from that child between them, since a send and its received row are appended together — and never on an ending notice, the received row nothing sent. The pairing, not the text, tells the two apart, so a notice that repeats word for word a message the child sent earlier, to the coordinator or to a sibling, still chains nothing. That holds wherever the notice landed: the scheduling commit's own "ended its turn without reporting" notice when a child's `end_turn` was the quiescence, or a client archive's notice before the grading claim or during the grader's call. Before, the last of these chained a coordinator turn while the other two idled past theirs. The notice stays `processed_at: null` until the primary's next request reads it: the acknowledgment turn when the verdict ended the last cycle, else the next `user.message`'s turn. The archived plans record the earlier rule and are left as written: plan 21's decision 6 idles every `satisfied`/`failed`, and plan 38's scope note excludes a verdict's settlement from the idle harvest, which now holds only with budget left (the outputs-harvest entry below). *Evidence: the 2026-09-02 recording, batch2.json `sessT.events.after-outcome` positions 83–92 (and FINDINGS.md: "the grader's verdict fed one more model request before idle"); internal/brain/grader.go (runGrading's lastCycle, settleVerdict, gradingChain, liveChildMessage) and replay.go (the end-event injection); internal/brain/grader_test.go TestOutcomeTerminalVerdictOnTheLastCycleRunsAnAcknowledgmentTurn, TestOutcomeSatisfiedAfterARevisionOnTheLastCycleIsAcknowledged, TestOutcome{Satisfied,Failed,MaxIterationsReached,NeedsRevisionThenSatisfied}; replay_test.go TestBuildRequestRendersATerminalVerdictOnlyOnTheLastCycle, TestBuildRequestDropsTheStopInstructionWhenInputSharesTheVerdictsTurn; delegate_test.go TestReportLandingDuringGradingChainsThePrimary, TestReportLandingDuringAFailedGradeChainsThePrimary, TestAnEndingNoticeAroundGradingNeverChainsThePrimary, TestAReportFromAChildArchivedSinceDoesNotChainATerminalVerdict, TestAnEndingNoticeRepeatingAMessageDoesNotChainATerminalVerdict; thread_test.go TestOutcomeGradesAtSessionQuiescenceNotOnAChildsEndTurn, TestOutcomeQuiescenceOnTheLastCycleAcknowledgesAndReadsTheNotice* *Tracked: #78 (a recording of a verdict with budget left, or of a `failed` or `needs_revision` one on the last cycle, would settle which reading the reference follows, and none shows how it treats an ending notice after a verdict); landed for #670 and #801.*
- **The outputs harvest — a session's deliverables into the files registry (plan 21 slice 4, plan 38)** — Docs give the `/mnt/session/outputs/` directory, the session-scoped Files-API fetch, and the sentence that deliverables appear "shortly after the agent finishes writing them, sometimes a few seconds after the session goes idle"; the rest is ours, pending a recording, bar shapes the SDK pins. **When**: two triggers, both cloud-only. A grading cycle's — a session's `end_turn` with an active outcome enqueues an internal `outputs_harvest` in place of the grading turn, which that harvest chains on completion, so the grader reads a current snapshot. And the session's own idle, no outcome required (#263): a settlement that folds the whole session idle enqueues one, which is the docs' sentence read literally. Five do it — a tool-less `end_turn`, a `retries_exhausted` fault, a `requires_action` confirmation gate, a delegated turn's park, cut chain or gated ending, and a delegation-bound claim refusal (`cutExhaustedRun`) — because the docs distinguish no stop reason. An idle harvest attaches to a live sandbox only, never provisioning one — a plain session that ran no tool, or one whose sandbox was reaped, has nothing to attach to and settles a no-op; with no sandbox there is no walk and so no whole-snapshot replace, so a sandbox-lost session keeps whatever an earlier harvest published rather than having it deleted (a grading harvest still provisions, since the grader needs a current snapshot). A transient attach fault (anything but `ErrNotFound`) is propagated so the lease reclaim retries it, never taken for no-sandbox; and when a grading cycle takes over an idle-tagged item that walked no sandbox, the settlement requeues it as a grading harvest rather than grade a stale snapshot (it provisions on its next claim). Reusing the one `outputs_harvest` kind, the idle trigger is not rolling-deploy-safe — an old executor claiming a new idle item drains it unread, self-healing on the session's next idle (#587). The two settlements that *end* an outcome cycle (a verdict with budget left, a grader error) fold a session idle too and deliberately do not re-harvest: that cycle opened with a harvest of the same tree and only a tool-less grader call ran since, so a second pass would republish identical bytes under fresh `file_` ids. A verdict on the budget's last cycle folds nothing itself: its acknowledgment turn (#670) ends in an ordinary `end_turn`, which harvests like any other, as the `max_iterations_reached` acknowledgment always has. Reference timing (continuous, per-cycle, per-idle or at session end) stays unobserved, and so does whether the reference re-harvests on a session that idles repeatedly. **Walk**: a sorted bash listing of the regular files under it — dotfiles in, symlinks and directories out, a missing directory an empty snapshot; an absolute, unclean, escaping or over-1024-byte path is excluded and logged, and each segment must pass the upload endpoint's own filename rule, so no harvested `filename` is one this platform's upload would 400 — but where an upload keeps only a name's final path component, the harvest keeps the whole `/`-joined path under that 1024-byte cap, the deliberate laxity. Past the exec output cap the listing degrades to its complete-entry sorted prefix, logged, never a fault. **Caps** (ours, greedy in path order; the reference documents none): 50 MiB per file, 200 files and 500 MiB per session — an over-cap or ineligible file is absent from the registry, never an error; a tree always yields the same snapshot. **Rows**: one per path — `filename` is the sandbox-relative path, `downloadable:true` (the seam 0008 reserved), mime from the extension alone via a pinned in-code table, never the host's (#264), `application/octet-stream` unknown; `scope` is the SDK's own `{id, type:"session"}`, so what is unobserved is whether the reference names, scopes or exposes harvested files this way, not the field. Rows list under `?scope_id=` and download byte-identically, and since #578 the unfiltered `GET /v1/files` leaves them out, as the reference's unfiltered list was recorded leaving out the copies a file mount mints (2026-09-02 batch2 idx 391, taken while they existed; 2026-09-12-console-141 api-fixtures idx 30, taken after the session's archive), which it listed under `?scope_id=` (console-141 ui-network idx 243 and 274). Whether it leaves harvested outputs out too is unrecorded, no recording having listed a session's outputs at all, though the docs call them "scoped to the session", as the recorded copies are; this platform scopes the list one way for both (the per-resource-copy entry above). **Snapshot**: each harvest that read the sandbox replaces the session's snapshot rows whole, leaving the copies its file mounts minted (#578) — delete-all + insert-all in one transaction, fresh `file_` ids per cycle (plan 21 said upsert), vanished paths dropped, the replaced snapshot's object keys enqueued for the control plane's drain inside the publishing transaction (#703); bytes stage to their final keys first, so a fault leaves the previous snapshot intact and only orphans behind. **Fence**: that transaction reads the session's live outcome state first and the item's own trigger second. An outcome still `evaluating` owns the pass whichever trigger enqueued it — the two flavors share one live item per session, so a cycle beginning while an idle-triggered item is still queued has its own enqueue swallowed by the dedup key, and refusing to serve it would strand that outcome in `evaluating` forever — served, it publishes and chains the grader when it walked a live sandbox, or requeues itself as a grading harvest when it found none, so the grader never reads a snapshot this pass did not freshly collect. Once nothing is evaluating, only the trigger separates a grading cycle that a `user.interrupt` settled mid-harvest (discard — nothing publishes, the item completes, staged bytes are discarded) from a session that never had an outcome at all (publish, chaining nothing). The trigger rides the work item's existing `metadata` jsonb rather than a second kind, so neither the schema nor the executor's dispatch grew. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/define-outcomes, and managed-agents/files § "Listing and downloading session files" for the idle sentence (fetched 2026-09-03); internal/executor/harvest.go, whose comments now carry the derivations cut from here (listing script, path validation, truncation, caps, the two-flavor fence) and internal/executor/executor.go sessionForRun's idle carve-out; internal/api/filesupload.go validateFilename; internal/mimetab (#264); internal/store/migrations/0017_outputs_harvest.sql (files_scope_filename_idx); internal/brain/grader.go settleEndTurn and enqueueIdleHarvest; internal/queue/queue.go EnqueueOutputsHarvest, RequeueAsGrading and Item.ChainGrading; docs/plan/38_idle-outputs-harvest.md; internal/executor/harvest_test.go (TestIdleHarvestPublishesSnapshotWithoutChainingATurn, TestFreshOutcomeGradingReclaimsAStaleIdleHarvestSlot, TestNoLiveSandboxGradingCollisionRequeuesInsteadOfGradingStale, TestIdleHarvestDeadSessionDrains); executor_integration_test.go TestHarvestRealSandbox / TestHarvestTruncatedListingRealSandbox; BetaFileMetadata.Scope is a BetaFileScope whose Type is constant.Session, default "session", beside Downloadable and the scope_id query param (checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.66.0 — betafile.go BetaFileMetadata.Scope and BetaFileScope.Type and BetaFileMetadata.Downloadable and BetaFileListParams.ScopeID)* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle the harvest's timing and caps, whether it re-harvests on every idle, and how the reference exposes harvested files, its unfiltered list included); landed for #77 and #578.*
- **The idle harvest trigger — five readings the docs' one sentence does not settle (plan 38)** — "Sometimes a few seconds after the session goes idle" is best-effort language about *when* files appear, not a specification, and the entry above records what this platform built on it. Five readings inside it are ours. **Which idles count**: every *brain* settlement fold outside an outcome cycle, whatever stop reason it carries — five of them: a tool-less `end_turn`, a `retries_exhausted` fault, a `requires_action` confirmation gate, a delegated turn's park, cut chain or gated ending, and a delegation-bound claim refusal. Whether the reference harvests only after a clean `end_turn` is unobserved; the docs distinguish no reason, so neither does this. Four idle folds are the deliberate exceptions, not oversights: an outcome cycle's own two — its verdict and its grader error — for the reason the entry above gives, and the two the *API* makes rather than the brain — a `user.interrupt` and the archive of a session's last running thread — a separate subsystem tracked by #586 (the interrupt also poses its own publish-vs-discard question). So the honest scope is every brain settlement fold, not every session-level idle fold whatever produced it. **Repeat idles**: a session that idles several times re-harvests each time, through the same delete-all/insert-all snapshot replace a multi-cycle grading session already gets. Whether the reference re-harvests, and with what semantics, is unobserved. **Child threads**: a multi-agent session harvests only at true session-level quiescence — a child idling under a still-running sibling, or a coordinator parking under live children, harvests nothing — mirroring the rule grading already follows. The reference exposes no thread-level file scope to compare against. **self_hosted**: excluded, for the reason its own entry above gives. **Latency**: nothing here promises the docs' "few seconds". The item is queued inside the settlement's transaction and picked up on the executor's own poll cadence, so the delay is a deployment's queue latency plus the sandbox walk, and no bound is claimed. *Evidence: platform.claude.com managed-agents/files § "Listing and downloading session files" (fetched 2026-09-03); docs/plan/38_idle-outputs-harvest.md § 2 ("What no source pins") and decisions 4 and 6; internal/brain/grader.go enqueueIdleHarvest, whose comment carries the gate's derivation; internal/brain/thread_test.go TestNoHarvestUntilTheSessionItselfIdles, internal/brain/delegate_test.go TestParkedCoordinatorWithLiveChildrenSkipsHarvest, internal/brain/brain_test.go TestRetriesExhaustedIdleEnqueuesOutputsHarvest / TestPermissionAskIdleEnqueuesOutputsHarvest, internal/executor/harvest_test.go TestReHarvestReplacesSnapshotPerPath* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle which idles the reference harvests on, whether it re-harvests on each one, and whether a multi-agent session harvests per thread or only at session quiescence).*
- **MCP client transport — Streamable HTTP only, no HTTP+SSE fallback (plan 29 slice 2)** — `internal/mcp` speaks Streamable HTTP and nothing else. What is documented: the 2026-07-28 spec classifies the separate HTTP+SSE transport of 2024-11-05 as **Deprecated** — "New implementations SHOULD NOT adopt it … It is eligible for removal in a future revision" — while also giving clients that want to reach older servers an exact detection procedure ("If it fails with HTTP status code `400 Bad Request`, `404 Not Found`, or `405 Method Not Allowed` **and** the response body is not a recognized modern JSON-RPC error … Issue a GET request to the server URL, expecting … an `endpoint` event as the first event"). Both halves are quoted here because they pull in opposite directions and the choice between them is ours: the fallback is split out of the client rather than shipped with it, so until it lands, a server that hosts *only* the deprecated endpoints is unreachable and surfaces as an ordinary connection failure. Nothing is silently degraded — the SDK negotiates every protocol version from 2024-11-05 up **over Streamable HTTP**, so an old server that hosts the modern endpoint is reached today, and only the transport-level fallback is outstanding. Two smaller readings ride along. The standalone SSE stream is **disabled** (`DisableStandaloneSSE`): 2026-07-28 removed the GET endpoint it used, and a per-work-item connection asks one question and closes, so it has no use for a server-initiated stream in any revision. Against a *modern* server the setting is a no-op — the go-sdk returns before opening the GET whenever the negotiated version is 2026-07-28 or later (checked against go-sdk v1.7.0 — mcp/streamable.go streamableClientConn.sessionUpdated) — so what it actually governs is an older negotiation, where the spec makes the GET optional and a `tools/list` answer arrives on the POST regardless — which is what the contract suite exercises rather than a case it cannot reach, since go-sdk serves 2026-07-28 only from a stateless handler and every fixture therefore negotiates 2025-11-25; a test counts the standalone GETs. Whether the reference's client opens one is unobserved. And the client identifies itself to servers as `managed-agent-platform` with no version; the reference's client name is unobserved, and an operator reading their server logs sees ours. *Evidence: modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/transports/streamable-http § "Backward Compatibility" and § "HTTP+SSE Transport (2024-11-05)" (fetched 2026-08-08); internal/mcp/mcp.go (Connect); internal/mcp/mcp_test.go* *Tracked: #78 (a recording would settle whether the reference's client opens a standalone SSE stream, and the client name it sends); the transport landed for #45, and the fallback itself is #348 — named by plan 29's architecture but by no slice, so it kept a home when #45 closed.*
- **MCP tool listing — what a malformed entry becomes, and the ceilings the protocol does not set (plan 29 slice 2)** — The reference makes its MCP connection server-side, so the exchange is invisible from the wire and only its outcomes are (`agent.mcp_tool_use`/`agent.mcp_tool_result`, and typed `mcp_connection_failed_error`/`mcp_authentication_failed_error`). Every reading here is therefore ours. An entry the platform cannot faithfully hand a model is **skipped**, never fatal to the listing — one bad entry must not deny an agent the rest of a catalog: an unusable name (empty, over 128 bytes, or outside `[A-Za-z0-9_.-]`, the SDK's own `validateToolName` charset rather than a guessed Anthropic rule), an `inputSchema` that is absent, null, not an object, or whose root `type` is not `"object"`, and a duplicate name (first wins — a malformed first entry leaves the name free, because the name is recorded after the schema check). A repeated pagination cursor **fails** the listing rather than looping; every cursor seen is remembered, not just the previous one. Five ceilings are platform policy and not protocol, which sets none: 100 pages, two minutes per listing, 30 seconds on any request the SDK detaches from the caller's context, 64 KiB on a single response's header block — the one a *legitimate* server can hit — and 8 MiB **accounted** across a connection, which is not the same as delivered: bodies count in full but header blocks only as far as they survive parsing to be counted, so the bytes actually on the wire can far exceed it (measured once at 91.8 MB against 3,900 bytes charged). Two readings are forced by the SDK's typed surface rather than chosen: a successful result carrying no `tools` field is recorded as "this server has no tools" rather than as malformed, and an empty-string cursor ends the listing — a real divergence from the pagination spec, which says an empty cursor is valid and MUST NOT be treated as the end, for a case no known server exercises. `"tools": [null]` panics the go-sdk (checked against go-sdk v1.7.0 — mcp/streamable_headers.go validateParamHeaderAnnotations) and is recovered at the call site, because the executor is shared by every session on the host. *Evidence: internal/mcp/mcp.go — ListTools, listPage, usableName, inputSchema, limitedTransport, headerBytes, and the constants ListTimeout, maxToolPages and maxHeaderBytesPerResponse, each carrying its own rationale, its measured boundary, and the record of the ceilings this entry published and had falsified (MaxResponseBytes's doc comment, including the rule that came out of it: do not multiply by a quantity nothing bounds); the detached-context close the 30-second bound covers (checked against go-sdk v1.7.0 — internal/xcontext/xcontext.go Detach; checked against go-sdk v1.7.0 — mcp/streamable.go StreamableClientTransport.Connect and streamableClientConn.Close); internal/mcp/mcp_test.go drives every boundary from both sides; MCP 2026-07-28 server/utilities/pagination; checked against go-sdk v1.7.0 — mcp/tool.go validateToolName; checked against go-sdk v1.7.0 — mcp/server.go Server.AddTool; checked against go-sdk v1.7.0 — mcp/protocol.go ListToolsResult.Tools and ListToolsResult.NextCursor.* *Tracked: #78 (pointing a reference session at a server with a malformed entry, or a repeated cursor, would settle whether it skips or fails the listing; an over-paginating or header-heavy server would settle what ceilings it sets where the protocol sets none); landed for #45.*
- **MCP-client egress and the tool catalog — what the reference leaves unobserved (plan 29 slice 2)** — The reference pins four things (that `limited.allow_mcp_servers`, default false, additively admits "MCP server endpoints configured on the agent"; that networking exists on **cloud** environments only; that session creation validates no MCP connectivity; and that a failed connection is "retried on the next `session.status_idle` to `session.status_running` transition") and leaves the rest unobservable, since its MCP exchange is server-side. **Where the policy is enforced is ours**: the platform dials from the executor's own process, structurally outside the per-session gate, so `limited` is enforced at that dial or nowhere — and before it, never after, since a refusal that still opened the connection would already have told the server the session exists. (Session create refuses the same hosts up front — the reference's own admission check, below — but the dial stays the enforcement point, since an agent patch can add a server afterwards.) Reading a documented *sandbox-egress* field as also governing the platform's own dial is our reading, and the one that gives it something to admit. **The policy's type decides, not the environment kind**: a `self_hosted` config carrying a networking block is one the REST surface cannot have written, so it came from an import or a hand-written UPDATE and is a policy somebody meant; an unrecognized type **refuses**, matching `gate.newPolicy`'s fail-closed direction, because the schema constrains `config->>'type'` but leaves `config->'networking'` alone and a config-preserving update carries a stored value forward unvalidated. A block naming no type reads as no policy — the one corner this cannot distinguish, since `domain.Networking` is a value struct — and it adds no reach a hand-written `unrestricted` would not. **The catalog** is a per-session snapshot keyed `(session_id, server_name)` — `(session_id, thread_id, server_name)` since plan 35 slice 3, each thread discovering its own agent's servers — rather than a URL-keyed cache, because two sessions may reach one server with different credentials and see different tools. Its row is the retry state, and the three states are ours: absent means never reached, `failed` is re-attempted next work cycle, `ready` is offered and not re-fetched. **Nothing disables a server for the rest of a session** — no source documents such a state. A `ready` row is matched on url as well as name, and rows an agent patch no longer declares are deleted in the patch's own transaction; the url comparison and a settle-time re-read under the session's row lock are deliberate redundancy against a missed or overtaken delete. **A server that cannot be reached is a row, not a fault** — an unreachable endpoint, a non-http(s) url, a policy refusal, and a server the pass's time budget did not reach all land as `failed` and the turn chains anyway, since faulting would reclaim-loop against a server that is simply down. Failures surface as `mcp_connection_failed_error`, or `mcp_authentication_failed_error` where a credential was refused (the connection worked well enough to be refused), **once per work cycle** rather than once per turn — a cadence that falls out of the row rather than being imposed. Two failed rows say nothing: one the pass timed out on, and one whose url is not http(s), since nothing was dialled. **What a row may hold is bounded by this platform, not the protocol**: a reason at 2,000 bytes, a listing at a quarter-megabyte with tools past it dropped whole. Slice 6b made the dials concurrent — declaration order never was a fact about a server, and serially one slow server kept those behind it out of the catalog for the session's life — while credential resolution stays serial, because an OAuth refresh run twice on one token can make an issuer revoke the family. The pass still runs on the goroutine that takes a host's work items, now for one server's dial rather than every server's, bounded by `EXECUTOR_MCP_PASS_TIMEOUT`. *Evidence: internal/egress/mcphost.go carries the type-not-kind derivation (MCPServerAdmitted), and internal/executor/mcpwork.go each other rule beside the code it governs (the settle-time re-read, the row-state machine, the concurrency split and why credentials stay serial); internal/store/migrations for the mcp_catalogs shape; internal/api/sessions.go for the patch-time delete (and internal/api/events.go for the idle→running clear of failed rows); the named tests in internal/executor/mcpwork_test.go and internal/api/mcptoolset_test.go pin the states and cadences.* **Both questions are now recorded, and the first one's mechanism is not what this entry assumed.** `allow_mcp_servers` is a **create-time admission check**, not a dial-time guard: a session whose agent declares an MCP server, created on an environment with `{"type":"limited","allowed_hosts":[],"allow_mcp_servers":false}`, is refused with a **400** and never exists — `MCP server host(s) blocked by environment network policy: "deepwiki" (mcp.deepwiki.com). Add these hosts to the environment's allowed_hosts, or set allow_mcp_servers=true.` The check extracts the host from each `mcp_servers[].url` and cross-validates it against the environment's policy, and both paths its message names do work: `allowed_hosts:["mcp.deepwiki.com"]` and `["*.deepwiki.com"]` each admit the session with the flag still false. It does **not** apply to `self_hosted`, whose config carries no `networking` block. This platform now refuses the same create with the same 400 (#571), asking the predicate the executor asks before every dial (`egress.MCPServerAdmitted`), and keeps that dial-time check because `mcp_servers` is mid-session-mutable. What goes past the recording is ours: several blocked servers are joined with `, ` in declaration order (one was recorded); only the session's own agent is checked at create, so a coordinator's roster members, and a server an agent patch adds mid-session, meet the dial-time check alone; the host is judged whatever the url's scheme, and a url with no host is left to the executor, which under the same `limited` policy refuses it before any dial; only a `limited` policy refuses at create, the one the message's advice can fix — a type nothing recognizes, or a `type` or `networking` that will not decode, is a row the API cannot have written, and the executor refuses it (or fails the session's first work item) as it did before; and a deployment run carries the create's message as its `mcp_egress_blocked_error` message. The second question settles as this entry read it: a failed server is **never** disabled, and the unit of its `session.error` is the **idle→running work cycle**, not the message — five messages across four cycles produced four errors, one cycle absorbing two messages — with the turn still reaching `end_turn`. Two incidentals came free: the error and the status change share a timestamp exactly on every cycle but the first, whose create path differs, and input arriving while a cycle is open **joins that cycle** rather than queueing a fresh turn. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `sess.create.mcp-host-exact-gate`, `sess.create.mcp-host-wildcard-gate` and `session.events.list.dead-five-turns`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §D.* *Tracked: #78 (how the reference joins several blocked servers, and whether it checks a roster member's servers, or an agent patch's, at all); landed for #45 and #571.*

- **Answering an MCP call — the execution locus, the result's blocks, and a failure's two halves (plan 29 slice 4)** — The reference's MCP exchange is server-side, so only the event pair is observable and everything between the two events is ours. **Locus**: the platform's own executor answers every MCP call on **every** environment kind, `self_hosted` included, because a BYOC worker's contract has no MCP surface — which makes MCP's precedence over the built-ins a correctness rule rather than a preference, applied at all six settlement sites. **A tool that ran and failed is a result, not a fault**: MCP asks servers to report it as an ordinary `is_error` result so the model can self-correct, and making it a work-item failure would reclaim-loop against a tool that fails identically every time. **A server that refuses is not a connection that failed** — an unknown tool, an `input_required` answer, unusable blocks, or a value that will not re-marshal all arrive over a working connection; the client marks server-answered errors (`ErrServerAnswered`) as a boundary rule rather than a list of cases. A JSON-RPC error is one of those answers only when the operation's most recent exchange was a **2xx** that this platform's response limit let through (#641): a non-2xx on the call is the HTTP layer failing whatever its body says, so it produces both events below. That exchange is not always the call's own — a reply the client posts to the server's mid-call request counts too — so a reply refused transiently turns the tool's genuine refusal into an extra `mcp_connection_failed_error`. The trade is ours and deliberate, and the reference cannot show its own because its exchange is server-side: reading only the call's exchange would instead report nothing when a refused reply ends the connection. **A call that never reached its server produces both events**: an `is_error` result so the turn continues and replay stays valid, plus a `session.error` naming the server so an operator sees a connection to heal. Its `retry_status` is **always `retrying`** — plan 29 specified `terminal` for an egress block and that is corrected here, because the union describes the *session* and this session neither terminates nor flushes; an operator can also widen `allowed_hosts` mid-session and heal the refusal. Messages are cut to `scheme://host`, since an `mcp_servers` entry may carry a credential. **Content blocks**: MCP's five types and the event's four do not correspond, so text and image translate, an embedded resource becomes a `document` titled with its URI, a resource link becomes text (never a url source, which would have the model's side dial an address this platform never vetted), audio is described rather than dropped, anything else is dropped, and an answer rendering to nothing gets one text block saying so. What a block may declare is the **intersection** of the event schema and the Messages schema the brain replays into — a block this platform can store but never send again fails every later turn on a log that cannot take it back. **Budgets are ours**, neither MCP nor the reference setting either: 100 KiB (`toolset.MaxOutputBytes`) charged per marshalled block, kept or dropped whole, with a trailing block naming what went and one exemption for a block whose text was already capped; and two minutes (`toolset.DefaultTimeout`) bounding the whole call, not one request. The execution pass is bounded as a whole too, by `EXECUTOR_MCP_PASS_TIMEOUT` (five minutes, operator-settable through Helm and compose) — checked *between* calls and never cancelling one, so its first call is always made; the reference documents no such limit either. No at-most-once guarantee is offered, because MCP has no idempotency key and the reference documents none. *Evidence: internal/executor/mcpexec.go and internal/mcp/calltool.go carry the reasoning beside the code; the claims above are each pinned by a named test in internal/executor/mcpexec_test.go, mcpwork_test.go, internal/mcp/calltool_test.go and internal/api/confirmation_test.go — the test names state the rules (TestMCPToolFailureIsTheModelsToRead, TestMCPProtocolFailureIsNotReportedAsAConnectionFailure, TestMCPBlobResourceRidesOnlyABlockTheWireCanCarry, TestMCPExemptionFollowsTheCappedBlockNotThePosition, TestToolResultResumeSchedulesAnOutstandingMCPCall, and the rest); both schemas, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentMCPToolResultEvent and BetaManagedAgentsAgentMCPToolResultEventContentUnion, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betamessage.go BetaToolResultBlockParam and BetaToolResultBlockParamContentUnion; modelcontextprotocol.io § tools for is_error's rationale.* **Two of the three questions are settled, and this platform matches on both.** The locus is the platform, inline: a recorded call runs `user.message | model_request_start | thinking | agent.mcp_tool_use | model_request_end | agent.mcp_tool_result | model_request_start | agent.message | model_request_end | idle{end_turn}`, so the result lands **between one model request and the next with no idle, no work item and no executor round trip** — against a `bash` call in the same wave whose result arrives only after `session.status_idle` and a confirmation. It holds **without a worker**: a session on a `self_hosted` environment with none running was served all the same, because the platform dials from its own side. `agent.mcp_tool_result`'s key set is `content, id, is_error, mcp_tool_use_id, processed_at, type` — `mcp_tool_use_id`, not `tool_use_id`, which is what this platform already emits — and an MCP **text** block maps to a `text` content block **verbatim**, unwrapped, the server's own JSON arriving as the text of one block. `retry_status` on a failed dial is `retrying`, as the connection-failure entry reads it. What stays unobserved is narrower than the entry opened with: the embedded-resource, `resource_link`, audio and empty-content-array cases, each of which needs a server whose tool returns one, and what the reference reports for a call answered with a non-2xx. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `session.events.list.mcp-real-call` and `session.events.list.mcp-self-hosted-locus`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §D.* *Tracked: #78 (a recording against a server returning an embedded resource, a resource_link, audio, or an empty content array would settle how each renders, and one answering the call with a non-2xx how that is reported); landed for #45, #641.*

- **Offering an MCP tool to the model — the name it is given, the ways it can be lost, and the turn that waits (plan 29 slice 4b)** — **The reference composes the name it shows the model and decomposes it on the event it commits**: a recorded session named its own tools `mcp__deepwiki__ask_question` and siblings, while `agent.mcp_tool_use` carries the bare `name` beside `mcp_server_name`. So `mcp__{server}__{tool}` is the reference's shape as much as ours, and the composition this entry was opened to record as a divergence is not one — the detail is in the recording paragraph below. No *source* documents the convention (`mcp__` appears in neither the SDK nor the `ant` CLI), which is why it was inferred the other way for as long as it was. What is genuinely ours is the *necessity*: this brain assembles one flat `tools[]` for an ordinary Messages endpoint, which carries a name and nothing else, so here the composed name is the only thing distinguishing two servers that both offer `search` — without it a call would be unattributable — where the reference has a field to fall back on. It is confined to the provider request, and to the text a denial answers the call with ("Denied tool confirmation", above) — the committed event carries the bare name and `mcp_server_name` as the SDK types them, and replay reassembles the pair so every later turn re-sends a name that turn actually offered. The composed name must fit `^[a-zA-Z0-9_-]{1,64}$` **here**, that being the Messages endpoint's constraint and not the reference's — the recording shows three composed names of 69, 75 and 76 characters offered and echoed back verbatim, with no truncation and no drop — while the mcp-connector page documents a server name of 1–255 characters and an `mcp_toolset` `configs[]` name of 1–128, both Anthropic's bounds, `configs[]` having no existence in the MCP protocol at all. So a pair legal on both wires can compose into one *this* endpoint rejects — and it would reject the whole request, every turn, forever. Bytes outside the class are **mangled to `_` one-for-one** rather than dropped, so the length checked before composition is the length produced. **Five ways a declared tool goes unoffered, each costing only its own tool and each a log line**: a name too long to compose; a name already taken (the agent's own `tools[]` wins over a third party's, and flattening is not injective, so two servers or two mangled names can collide — declaration order settles all three here, where the reference instead reserves the `mcp__` prefix at agent create and so makes the first of the three impossible by construction); a server whose listing failed or has not happened; a `configs[]` entry naming a tool the server does not report (the only one the docs settle — it warns without erroring); and a definition past the 256 KiB of MCP definitions one request carries. Two further drops are **silent by design** — a nameless tool and a repeated name are already refused where a listing is read, so warning about them would be noise about a state that cannot arise. The reports are log lines, not events, bounded at sixteen per turn; plan 29 proposed a `system.message` and that is corrected here, since the event is client-sent and replays into the system prompt, which one per turn would grow without bound. **A turn waits for a listing it has never had** (no model call; the item is handed back and the discovery driver chains the turn), while a *failed* row is an answer rather than an absence — the turn runs without that server. Failed rows are deleted on an idle→running transition, matching the reference's documented "retried on the next transition" cadence. An outstanding call is dialled only where its listing was read and only while the agent still declares the server there, since `mcp_servers` is mid-session-mutable and dialling a new address would ask a different party a question it never advertised. **A name the model was not offered is answered inline**: it still commits as an ordinary `agent.tool_use` (`evaluated_permission: "deny"`, `name`, `input`, `session_thread_id`) — `deny`, not `allow`, because a `self_hosted` worker built to the reference contract streams `agent.tool_use` directly and may own this very name in its own registered toolset regardless of what this agent's config disabled, and does not treat this platform's own `agent.tool_result` as answering it (it dedups only on `user.tool_result`/`user.custom_tool_result`) — an `allow` stamp would tell such a worker to run what the settlement already refused. The settlement answers it in the same commit with an `is_error` `agent.tool_result` reading `unknown tool %q: not offered to this agent` (the fully rendered text bounded to 16 KiB by `unknownToolAnswer`, which quotes the name exactly rather than trimming it as `delegationText` does) — both landing on the calling thread's own log, exactly as a delegation call and its answer do — corrected from treating it as client-executed, which parked the call as `agent.custom_tool_use` for a client that had declared no such tool and would never post a result, stranding the session `running` forever with no stop reason and no way for an operator to see why. The turn chains to the model's next request only when nothing else in it remains outstanding: mixed into the same turn as a gated `tool_exec` call or an `always_ask` one, it is answered all the same but the turn still waits on those, precisely as it would without it. A run of consecutive turns this settlement alone answers and that wake no other thread — unoffered names, genuine delegation calls, wrong-role calls to the other half, or any mix; a turn whose answer wakes a thread resets the count, as `chain && !woke` in `commitDelegatedTurn` says — is capped at the same per-thread `maxSettlementChain` (25) every shape already bounds, and the cap's own `delegation_chain_exhausted_error` fires the same way and reads the same kind-agnostic message regardless of the mix that built it: a run with no genuine call in it is cut exactly as one with only genuine calls is, so a coordinator parked on a child it capped is told and woken either way. What stays genuine-only is the session-wide delegation budget: it counts, and this run only spends, a turn that held at least one call the model was actually offered (#567). Declared custom calls use the shared external-wait processor described above (#375). `session.error` messages are bounded, since every string one is built from is capped nowhere it is stored. The agent's own tools are laid down before the MCP ones, which makes the collision rule decidable in one pass; ordering within `tools[]` is unobservable on the reference. An MCP call gates as an ordinary ask tool (`DefaultMCPToolsetPolicy` is `always_ask`), and work follows the first unresolved call on each thread. Across independently runnable threads the shared queue chooses `mcp_exec`, then `web_exec`, then `tool_exec`; this priority cannot pass an earlier call on the same thread. *Evidence: internal/brain/mcptools.go, internal/brain/replay.go, internal/brain/delegate.go (d.run's unoffered branch, answering the same way a delegation call is) and internal/toolset/mcp.go carry the reasoning beside the code; roughly thirty named tests pin the rules individually across internal/brain/mcptools_test.go, mcpturn_test.go, internal/api/mcptoolset_test.go, internal/executor/mcpexec_test.go and internal/toolset/mcp_test.go — the names state the claims (TestTwoToolNamesThatSanitizeAlikeContestOneName, TestAFailedListingRunsTheTurnWithoutThatServer, TestWakingASessionRetriesItsFailedMCPListings, TestMCPCallForARepointedServerIsNotDialledElsewhere, and the rest); platform.claude.com tool-use "Define tools" for the name regex, the mcp-connector guides for the committed event's bare-name shape and the two bounds; checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betasessionevent.go BetaManagedAgentsAgentMCPToolUseEvent and BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolUseEvent and BetaManagedAgentsAgentToolResultEvent, checked against anthropic-sdk-go v1.70.1 — betamessage.go BetaToolParam.* **The recording is what settled the composition question above, and the detail is worth keeping.** Asked to name its tools verbatim, a recorded session on `https://mcp.deepwiki.com/mcp` answered `mcp__deepwiki__ask_question`, `mcp__deepwiki__read_wiki_contents`, `mcp__deepwiki__read_wiki_structure` — **the name the reference shows the model is composed, not bare.** Both halves hold at once: the model sees `mcp__{server}__{tool}` while `agent.mcp_tool_use` carries the bare `name` beside `mcp_server_name`, so the reference composes outbound and decomposes inbound, and this platform's composition was never a divergence at all. Two consequences. The reference **reserves the prefix**: a `custom` tool named `mcp__deepwiki__ask_question` is refused at agent create with `tools.1.custom.name: custom tool name "…" uses reserved MCP tool prefix`, which makes a collision impossible by construction rather than settled by declaration order as it is here. And there is **no 64-character ceiling**: a 50-character server name produced composed names of 69, 75 and 76 characters — all three echoed back intact, with no truncation, no drop and a turn that ran normally, where `mcpNameFits` would not have offered the tool at all — the bound is the Messages tool-use guide's `^[a-zA-Z0-9_-]{1,64}$`, a constraint of the endpoint this brain assembles a flat `tools[]` for rather than of the reference's own protocol, so what the recording shows is that the reference is not subject to it. Both are #573. The waiting question settles as assumed: a session created with `initial_events`, its first turn due before any listing could have happened, shows a **single** `span.model_request_start` with the MCP tools already known — the turn waits for the listing before the first model call. *Recording: managed-agents-wire-recordings 2026-09-03/batch2.json, probes `session.events.list.mcp-tool-names`, `session.events.list.long` and `agent.create.mcp-name-collision`, analysed in 2026-09-03/FINDINGS2.md §D.* *Tracked: #573 (reserve the `mcp__` prefix at agent create; decide whether the 64-byte cap can be lifted or has to stay a property of the endpoint this brain assembles for), #78 (what the reference does with a tool name it cannot offer, the half of this question the recording did not reach — the listing-wait half it did); landed for #45.*

- **The console-API dialect's edges — rejections, paging bounds and the reserved org segment (plan 30 slice 2)** — The 2026-08-10 capture of the reference console showed the happy paths only; a 2026-09-05 capture (batch2 `rec83.*`) then drove the dialect's error paths, and since #820 we answer every edge it recorded as recorded but one. **Matched:** an unknown environment is a 404 and a malformed environment id a 400, each with the recorded `details` (*Error envelope — the reference's nested `details` member*), so the namespace is no better an enumeration oracle than `/v1`; an unknown or *foreign* `token_id` takes one 404 branch, as the reference's both do, so revocation can neither reach across environments nor confirm that an id exists elsewhere, and a malformed one is a 400; `limit=0`, a negative `offset` and a non-integer `limit` are 400s, in the recorded pydantic words since #540 (a non-integer `offset`, never sent, takes the `limit` sentence's form); an `offset` past the end is an empty page; 100 is the default page size, and a larger `limit` is taken and echoed in `pagination.limit` rather than refused (101 and 1000, idx 11–12). A key is issued on a `cloud` environment and on an archived one (200 both, idx 1 and 23); what such a key then reaches is *A `cloud` or archived environment's key — what it reaches*. A foreign organization UUID is a 401 `authentication_error` with `details` (idx 5), and any other value but `default` is the 400 `invalid_request_error`, without details, that the reference answers a segment that is not a UUID with (idx 6) — both before the environment is looked up, and both in the reference's words since #540: "Unable to authenticate session.", and pydantic's `path.organization_uuid` refusal quoting the uuid crate, which names the first character no UUID holds and its position. A segment the crate would refuse for its length or grouping instead, which no recording reaches, keeps ours.
